About the Digital Edition About the Digital Edition
Foreign Relations of the United States , 1981–1988, Volume XVI, South America is part of a subseries of the Foreign Relations series that documents the foreign policy decision making of the administration of President Ronald Reagan. This web page explains the current status of the volume’s digital edition.
To provide timely access to the volume’s documents, the Office of the Historian will publish chapters as they are cleared. To date, the following chapters have been published:
Title Publication Date
Ecuador September 18, 2026
Paraguay September 18, 2026
Uruguay September 18, 2026
Venezuela September 18, 2026
The volume’s landing page provides a complete list of its front matter and chapters, even those not yet published. When readers access portions of the volume that have not yet been published, they will encounter a note explaining this and linking to this page for further details.
Some front matter sections will be updated with each chapter. For example, Sources presents a list of the sources from the chapters that have been published.
The chapters and sections that have been published can be searched and viewed online and downloaded as ebooks and as TEI XML.
When the remainder of the volume is cleared for publication, a volume-length PDF will be released.
This page will be updated to note significant additions to the volume.
Last updated September 18, 2026

About the Series About the Series
The Foreign Relations of the United States series presents the official documentary historical record of major foreign policy decisions and significant diplomatic activity of the U.S. Government. The Historian of the Department of State is charged with the responsibility for the preparation of the Foreign Relations series. The staff of the Office of the Historian, under the direction of the General Editor of the Foreign Relations series, plans, researches, compiles, and edits the volumes in the series. Secretary of State Frank B. Kellogg first promulgated official regulations codifying specific standards for the selection and editing of documents for the series on March 26, 1925. These regulations, with minor modifications, guided the series through 1991.
Public Law 102–138, the Foreign Relations Authorization Act, established a new statutory charter for the preparation of the series, which was signed by President George H.W. Bush on October 28, 1991. Section 198 of P.L. 102–138 added a new Title IV to the Department of State’s Basic Authorities Act of 1956 (22 U.S.C. 4351, et seq.).
The statute requires that the Foreign Relations series be a thorough, accurate, and reliable record of major United States foreign policy decisions and significant United States diplomatic activity. The volumes of the series should include all records needed to provide comprehensive documentation of major foreign policy decisions and actions of the United States Government. The statute also confirms the editing principles established by Secretary Kellogg: the Foreign Relations series is guided by the principles of historical objectivity and accuracy; records should not be altered or deletions made without indicating in the published text that a deletion has been made; the published record should omit no facts that were of major importance in reaching a decision; and nothing should be omitted for the purposes of concealing a defect in policy. The statute also requires that the Foreign Relations series be published not more than 30 years after the events recorded.
Sources for the Foreign Relations Series
The Foreign Relations statute requires that the published record in the Foreign Relations series include all records needed to provide comprehensive documentation of major U.S. foreign policy decisions and significant U.S. diplomatic activity. It further requires that government agencies, departments, and other entities of the U.S. Government engaged in foreign policy formulation, execution, or support cooperate with the Department of State historians by providing full and complete access to records pertinent to foreign policy decisions and actions and by providing copies of selected records.
The editors of the Foreign Relations series have complete access to all the retired records and papers of the Department of State: the central files of the Department; the special decentralized files (“lot files”) of the Department at the bureau, office, and division levels; the files of the Department’s Executive Secretariat, which contain the records of international conferences and high-level official visits, correspondence with foreign leaders by the President and Secretary of State, and the memoranda of conversations between the President and the Secretary of State and foreign officials; and the files of overseas diplomatic posts. The records that constitute the Department’s central files, which were stored in electronic and microfilm formats, will be transferred eventually to the National Archives. Once these files are declassified and processed, they will be accessible. All of the Department’s decentralized office files from this period that the National Archives deems worthy of permanent preservation will also eventually be transferred to the National Archives where they will be available for use after declassification and processing.
Research for Foreign Relations volumes is also undertaken through special access to restricted Presidential Library and other U.S. Government agency records. While all the material printed in this volume has been declassified, some of it is extracted from still-classified documents. Archivists and declassification reviewers are processing and declassifying many of the documents used in this volume, but they may not be publicly available in their entirety at the time of publication.
Editorial Methodology
The documents are presented chronologically according to Washington time. Memoranda of conversation are placed according to the time and date of the conversation, rather than the date the memorandum was drafted.
Editorial treatment of the documents published in the Foreign Relations series follows Office style guidelines, supplemented by guidance from the General Editor and the Team Lead of the Editing and Publishing Team. The documents are reproduced as exactly as possible, including marginalia or other notations, which are described in the footnotes. Texts are transcribed and printed according to accepted conventions for the publication of historical documents within the limitations of modern typography. A heading has been supplied by the editors for each document included in this volume. Spelling, capitalization, and punctuation are retained as found in the original text, except that obvious typographical errors are silently corrected. Other mistakes and omissions in documents are corrected by bracketed insertions: a correction is set in italic type; an addition in roman type. Bracketed insertions are used to indicate omitted text that deals with an unrelated subject (in roman type) or that remains classified after declassification review (in italic type).
Words and phrases underlined in the original document are printed in italics. Abbreviations and contractions are preserved as found in the original text. In telegrams, the telegram number (including special designations such as Secto) is printed at the start of the text of the telegram.
The amount and, where possible, the nature of the material not declassified has been noted by indicating the number of lines or pages of text that were omitted. Entire documents withheld after declassification review have been accounted for and are listed with headings, source notes, and number of pages not declassified in their chronological place.
All brackets that appear in the original text are so identified in footnotes. All ellipses are in the original documents.
The first footnote to each document indicates the source of the document and its original classification, distribution, and drafting information. This note also provides the background of important documents and policies and indicates whether the President or his major policy advisers read the document.
Editorial notes and additional annotation summarize pertinent material not printed in the volume, indicate the location of additional documentary sources, provide references to important related documents printed in other volumes, describe key events, and provide summaries of and citations to public statements that supplement and elucidate the printed documents. Information derived from memoirs and other first-hand accounts has been used when appropriate to supplement or explicate the official record.
Some of the research for volumes in the Ronald Reagan subseries was done in Reagan Presidential Library record collections scanned for the Remote Archives Capture (RAC) project. This project, which was administered by the National Archives and Records Administration’s Office of Presidential Libraries, was designed to coordinate the declassification review of classified records held in various Presidential libraries. Throughout the course of the project many, but not all, such records at each Presidential library were scanned. As a result of the way in which records were scanned for the RAC, the editors of the Foreign Relations series were not always able to determine whether attachments to a given document were in fact attached to the paper copy of the document in the Presidential library file. In such cases, some editors of the Foreign Relations series have indicated this ambiguity by stating that the attachments were “Not found attached.”
Advisory Committee on Historical Diplomatic Documentation
The Advisory Committee on Historical Diplomatic Documentation, established under the Foreign Relations statute, reviews records, advises, and makes recommendations concerning the Foreign Relations series. The Advisory Committee monitors the overall compilation and editorial process of the series and advises on all aspects of the preparation and declassification of the series. The Advisory Committee does not necessarily review the contents of individual volumes in the series, but it makes recommendations on issues that come to its attention and reviews volumes as it deems necessary to fulfill its advisory and statutory obligations.
John C. Powers The Acting Historian
Kathleen B. Rasmussen General Editor

Ecuador Ecuador
368. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador
Washington, February 5, 1981, 1607Z
Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810055–0755. Secret; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information Immediate to Lima. Drafted by Eaton; cleared by Bushnell and in S/S, S/S–O; approved by Baltimore.
369. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador
Washington, April 14, 1981, 1654Z
Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810177–0671. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information Priority to Lima, Caracas, Mexico City, Brasilia, Buenos Aires, San Salvador, Bogota, USSOUTHCOM, and CINCLANT. Drafted by Hart; cleared in S/S–O and ARA; approved by Enders.
370. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador
Washington, June 10, 1981, 0236Z
Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810271–0467. Confidential; Priority. Sent for information Priority to Bogota, Lima, Caracas, La Paz, and Tel Aviv. Drafted by Hart; approved by Briggs.
371. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State
Quito, July 23, 1981, 1411Z
Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810344–1030. Confidential; Priority. Sent for information to Guayaquil and USUN.
372. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador
Washington, December 24, 1981, 0058Z
Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, N810010–0317. Confidential; Immediate; Nodis. Sent for information Immediate to Lima, San Salvador, and Guayaquil. Haig visited the Castries, St. Lucia, December 2–4, to attend the OAS General Assembly meeting.
373. Memorandum of Conversation
Washington, February 26, 1982, 11:40 a.m.–12:15 p.m.
Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S–I Records, Secretary Memorandums of Conversation (Memcons), Secretary Haig and Shultz, Lot 87D327, Secretary Shultz (Memcons) February 1981. Confidential. The meeting took place in Haig’s office. Drafted by Hart; cleared by Haig and Enders. Goldberg initialed for Haig.

368. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 368. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, February 5, 1981, 1607Z
30231. Subject: Conversation between Secretary Haig and Ecuadorean Foreign Minister Barrera.
1. S—Entire text.
2. Secretary Haig met for about 20 minutes at the OAS headquarters with Ecuadorean Foreign Minister Barrera on the morning of February 3. Accompanying Barrera was Ecuadoran Ambassador Crespo. Accompanying Haig were Bushnell and Eaton. The principal concentration of the conversation was on the Ecuador-Peru border conflict. 2 Other subjects discussed were Central America and the US attitude toward the internal politics of other countries.
3. Barrera opened by expressing Ecuador’s sincere thanks for the US positive approach to the current dispute. Ecuador was in favor of a role for the US, Brazil, Argentina and Chile in the achievement and consolidation of peace but Ecuador was also convinced that there must be an Organization of American States involvement. 3 Ecuador’s future attitude toward the OAS would be critically affected by whether or not it had a role. It wanted something in writing that would specify that the four countries presently involved in peacemaking were not acting solely (SIC) as guarantors but also as representatives of the OAS. It wanted to know the USG position on this point.
4. The Secretary noted that he had a special familiarity with this issue because an Ecuadoran by the name of Roca from Guayaquil had been his roommate at West Point in 1944. He said it was most unfortunate that this conflict had developed now when the hemisphere was faced with other problems that were so serious. He welcomed the ceasefire and said that he hoped there could be a decoupling of forces because of the inadvertent return to hostilities that could occur if there were not a disengagement. He hoped the two parties could reach a solution to the problem between themselves. He recognized the overlapping of or contradictions between the two international forums in which the dispute was being discussed. He said that the US would have to look into the issue more closely.
5. Barrera said—in apparent reference to the aggressiveness and vehemence of his presentation before the OAS the night before—that he would be speaking in the OAS today in as constructive a tone as possible. He wanted to underscore that we are talking about an area where the US has no legally binding position because the Rio Protocol does not establish what the border is. Since there is no agreed border, peace in the area is precarious. The keeping of that peace should not be confined to the guarantors. He, therefore, asked the US support for three points: (1) that it is acting in this issue as a friendly country, (2) that the OAS has a role in addition to the four countries presently involved, and (3) that whatever commission is established be continued for years if necessary.
6. The Secretary replied that he would wish to consider the three points made by Ecuador with care before responding. He thought it important to move carefully and not too rapidly in such matters. He lauded Barrera for his reputation as a diplomat and said that it had been confirmed by his presentation. 4
7. Central America. The Secretary said that in only two weeks the Reagan Administration had verified a massive Cuban intervention in El Salvador principally through Nicaragua. This kind of intervention is unacceptable to this administration. It will adopt fundamentally different policies with regard to it. In this respect it will want to have unusually close and cordial consultations with other countries of the hemisphere and especially with friends like Ecuador. Barrera replied with apparent conviction that he was in one hundred percent agreement. It was a fundamental principle of the hemisphere that there should be no intervention. Too often this was taken in Latin America to refer only to the US. Ecuador was convinced that it should apply to all countries. Ecuador does not see why any Latin American country should be exempt from this principle.
8. US attitude toward the internal politics of other countries. Secretary Haig said that the US had no preconceived ideas about the appropriate internal regimes of other countries and President Reagan would not try to impose the US model on other countries. Human rights are important but it is also important to concentrate on legal social change without foreign intervention.
Haig
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810055–0755. Secret; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information Immediate to Lima. Drafted by Eaton; cleared by Bushnell and in S/S, S/S–O; approved by Baltimore.
2 In telegram 19262 to Lima and Quito, January 24, the Department reported that Crespo had informed Eaton on January 23 that a “Peruvian helicopter had crossed border and shot at Ecuadorean helicopter on the ground, seriously wounding Ecuadorean pilot.” On January 24, Crespo telephoned Eaton and told him that “the situation looked bad,” as “Peru had mobilized on border and was threatening attack.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810036–0828) On February 10, Edward Schumacher wrote in the New York Times : “Ecuadorean soldiers were expelled from three border posts after five days of fighting in which Peru says one of its soldiers was killed and Ecuador says it lost eight, though the reliability of the figures and the reported ferocity of the fighting have been questioned. The outposts are up to nine miles past a line that the Peruvian and Ecuadorean armed forces informally drew as a cease-fire line after similar clashes three years ago. Sometime since then the Ecuadoreans secretly moved in and occupied the jungle posts. It was that occupation, discovered by Peruvian patrols more than two weeks ago, that led to the idea that Ecuador precipitated the incident.” (Edward Schumacher, “Behind Ecuador War, Long-Smoldering Resentment,” New York Times , February 10, 1981, p. A2) This five-day border war, known as the Paquisha War (or Fake Paquisha War), was a part of a centuries-old border dispute between Ecuador and Peru; see footnote 3 , below.
3 In 1941 Peru successfully invaded Ecuador. The United States, Brazil, Argentina, and Chile helped to negotiate a peace treaty. The 1942 Rio de Janeiro Protocol, with the United States, Brazil, Argentina, and Chile serving as guarantor nations, ceded Ecuadorean territory to Peru and determined the border in the Cordillera del Cóndor. For the protocol between Ecuador and Peru regarding peace, friendship, and boundaries signed at Rio de Janeiro on January 29, 1942, see Department of State Executive Agreement Series No. 288, or 56 Stat. (pt. 2) 1818. During the Paquisha War (or Fake Paquisha War) the four countries helped to negotiate a cease-fire. See “Peru Claims Victory; Ecuador Says Battle Goes On,” New York Times , February 2, 1981, p. A1; and “Peru and Ecuador Agree to Cease-Fire,” Washington Post , February 3, 1981, p. A27. See also Document 414 and footnotes 3 and 4 thereto.
4 In a March 6 memorandum to Allen, Fontaine noted: “For the moment, this issue has been put on the back burner, but we are not very close to a settlement—even a temporary one.” Fontaine added: “The U.S. is considering sales of the F–5 or approval of the Israeli Kfir to Ecuador. Other things being equal either would be o.k. But this is not a case of ceritus paribus. The sale of U.S. F–5s in particular at this time would be very provocative, causing endless political grief for Belaunde Terry, the pro-American President of Peru. This matter must be very carefully reviewed—with the most important consideration which is political—dealt with as the highest priority.” (Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Chronological File, [03/05/1981–03/06/1981])

369. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 369. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, April 14, 1981, 1654Z
95067. Subject: Conversation with President Roldos.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Summary: Ambassador Enders met with President Roldos for one hour and 45 minutes in Houston on April 12. The atmosphere was very cordial and constructive. Principal topics of discussion were the situations in Central America, the future of human rights and democracy in the hemisphere, the Peru/Ecuador border dispute, U.S. arms sales policy and the tuna issue. We believe the meeting was quite useful. End summary.
3. Ecuadorean President Jaime Roldos accompanied by Ambassador to the U.S. Crespo and OAS Ambassador Falconi received Ambassador Enders and ARA/AND Director Hart for one hour and 45 minutes conversation in Houston on April 12. The atmospherics of the talks were completely relaxed and positive. The main points which emerged are summarized below.
4. The future of democracy. Roldos opened by observing that there is no single Latin American view on what the proper U.S. or Latin American policy should be toward the promotion of democracy. Many Latin Americans have a superficial and emotional perception that the U.S. is allied with the “haves” in the hemisphere. They resent what they believe is overemphasis of the communist danger. Roldos does not think that Latin America and U.S. goals are contradictory. We both want democracy and reject the idea that anti-communism means denial of social justice. We share the concern about the efforts of the small but well-organized extremists of the right and left in Latin America to capture the initiative from the much more numerous (maybe 90 percent of the population) moderate forces. The extremes use any tactics to polarize societies rather than to work for gradual social, economic and political progress. Again, there is no difference with the U.S. on this approach. At the same time, there may be a wave of “new mercantilism” developing in the region to the detriment of democracy. The military play a powerful role in all the Latin American countries, including those with civilian constitutional governments. Because of the international economic crisis which all but the large oil exporting countries must face, governments are forced to concentrate on financial problems such as inflation and the balance of payments. Social programs to a large extent have had to be deferred despite the widening gap in the economic/social structure which had occurred as the result of economic growth in 1960’s and 1970’s. This plays into the hands of the communists and others who adopt pseudo-popularist rhetoric to fool the public. Where communism has been clearly posed as an alternative to the people, it has been rejected. Everyone knows that the experiment in Cuba has failed. In response to Ambassador Enders’ observation that there were also positive developments concerning democracy, and Roldos commented that Mexico, Venezuela and Brazil were the most important countries in the region for the U.S. and probably will serve Latin America as different models for democracy building.
5. Human rights. Enders outlined our new approach to human rights, using private rather than public diplomacy. Roldos did not respond directly, but talked about the need to lower the emotional content of the political debate within countries and to avoid the polarization that the extremists seek. Human rights in Latin America is linked mentally with the constitutional process. Protection of human rights is not a position of weakness; it’s a policy of strength on which we cannot turn our backs. Respect for human rights is needed to give credit to the center and discredit the extremes. In this respect, Roldos placed particular value on the direct dialogue he has established with Viola in Argentina. He mentioned that he may visit Brazil in October and is considering plans to meet soon with Herrera of Venezuela and Royo of Panama.
6. Central America. After Enders’ observation that it is precisely the need to strengthen the center which is at the heart of the problem in El Salvador and elsewhere in Central America, Roldos offered the opinion that many in Latin America see Duarte as being too closely tied to the junta. Duarte has a creditability problem because people see the Salvadoran political situation in black and white. Roldos mentioned his meeting with Duarte in Santa Marta.
7. The border conflict. Roldos thanked the U.S. warmly for its help in the recent crises. 2 In his view, there has been a change in Ecuador’s attitude on the settlement of this problem. Although there is no national consensus on the shape of the solution and the emotional content on the subject is very high, Ecuador has now lowered its sights on what can be achieved. It wants a friendly solution. Unfortunately, Peru has been very hard-nosed and has been unwilling to discuss even the more modest ideas Ecuador has put forward. On a strictly private basis, Enders suggested that a look at some of the European arrangements for river use might provide imaginative ideas which would give Ecuador an outlet to the Amazon.
8. Arms purchase by Ecuador. Roldos was told that the U.S. understood Ecuador’s feelings that it had to buy new aircraft at this time. Under our new arms policy, we had approved the sales of the Kfir and F–5. 3 At the same time, we believe it’s important that direct and frank dialogue be establish with Peru on delicate matters such as this to avoid a misinterpretation of motives. 4 Given Peru’s deep suspicion of Ecuador’s intentions, Peru probably would respond with large arms purchases of its own to the announcement of an Ecuadorean buy. Roldos replied that it is ridiculous to believe that Ecuador is a military threat to Peru. The planes would be purely for defense. Ecuador needs some kind of deterrent capability against Peru’s overwhelming military superiority. Roldos left the impression that Ecuador would buy the Kfirs fairly soon. 5 He did not react to the suggestion that communication with Peru on this and other subjects by someone like Galo Plaza is desirable.
9. Tuna. There was an agreement that both sides want to avoid having the tuna boat seizure problem interfere with our excellent overall relations. 6 We must search for practical solutions to avoid conflicts which could arise because of our different political and juridical position. Enders explained how we might soon be faced with a difficult situation in the Congress when a replenishment of funds for the Fisherman’s Protective Act is considered. There will be congressmen who will want to deduct payments to U.S. tuna boats seized by Ecuador from our foreign assistance. If the present situation continues it will be hard to say they are wrong. We want to continue aid to Ecuador and believe it would be very useful if we can begin soon to discuss an interim solution to our dispute pending the outcome of law of the sea negotiations. Enders mentioned the Micronesia Tuna Agreement as worth consideration as a way of having a modus vivendi while leaving our juridical positions intact. Negotiations between the U.S. and Ecuadorean Fishing Association might be the best way to look at the problem. They might find some contractual basis on which they want to do business. Roldos said he wanted to avoid a vicious circle of conflict over tuna. He had no problem with discussions between the US and Ecuadorean fishery industries. It was agreed that further talks between ourselves and Ecuador on how we wish to proceed should take place. Ambassador Crespo and Hart will be in touch.
Comment: Our main objective in this meeting was to establish a constructive personal relationship between Ambassador Enders and the President. We also wished to probe Roldos’ views on hemispheric problems and to provide our own thoughts on selective regional and bilateral matters. We are satisfied that both goals were accomplished.
Haig
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810177–0671. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information Priority to Lima, Caracas, Mexico City, Brasilia, Buenos Aires, San Salvador, Bogota, USSOUTHCOM, and CINCLANT. Drafted by Hart; cleared in S/S–O and ARA; approved by Enders.
2 See Document 368 and footnotes 2 and 3 thereto.
3 In telegram 43870 to Quito and Lima, February 20, the Department wrote about the sale of Israeli Kfirs to Ecuador: “Department informed Israelis on February 20 that we are favorably disposed toward to the sale and intends to notify Congress of intent to approve sale as soon as Peru-Ecuador situation permits. We briefed them on reasons for delay. We will seek GOI assurances that they will consult with the USG on the delivery schedule for the Kfirs (assuming sale is consummated) in order that the timing of their arrival does not come at a critical stage in the negotiations or a period of high tension between Ecuador and Peru.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810080–0879)
4 In telegram 43798 to Quito and Lima, February 20, the Department wrote: “We believe it is necessary to discuss frankly with the Governments in Quito and Lima the policy we are applying to their arms requests so long as the serious threat of hostilities between those countries persists. Therefore, the following talking points are provided for Ambassadors Corr and Gonzalez to use promptly at the highest level of their host governments: —Since the outbreak of hostilities, we have received requests from both countries for various kinds of military equipment and supplies (FYI principally from Ecuador). —We have been processing these requests in a routine manner. —Our policy of normal processing includes the application of the same criteria for case-by-case decisions to both countries and is intended to avoid any indication of favoritism. —We would like to impress on both governments how important we believe it is that they not increase military expenditures as a result of the unfortunate border incident. —Unusually difficult decisions are required when major items of hardware, such as combat aircraft, are involved. As you know, we have authorized in principle the sale of F–5E/F’s, A–37’s and T–37’s to Ecuador and Peru and conveyed an initially favorable response to the Israeli’s on sale of Kfirs to Ecuador. You should be aware, however, and you may so inform Peru and Ecuador, that we will take a close look at the prospect of conflict between the two countries before delivery of any major weapon system is actually carried out.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810082–0423)
5 A June 5 Department of State paper noted: “Ecuador signed an agreement with Israel in late May to purchase 12 Kfir fighters and is continuing negotiations for six more. Six Kfirs were being moved out of a storage area in the last week of May for probable sea delivery to Ecuador, according to the Defense Attaché in Tel Aviv. INR Comment : Ecuador probably chose the Kfirs because they can shipped more rapidly than US-built F–5’s; Ecuador’s military reportedly fears renewed conflict with Peru later this year. The deal is Israel’s first export of the Kfir. Latin America accounted for just over one-half of Israel’s total arms deliveries between 1976 and 1980.” (Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Subject File, Ecuador [January 1981–June 1981])
6 In telegram 82 from Guayaquil, January 16, the Consulate reported: “U.S. flag tuna boat Rosa D was seized by an Ecuadorian naval vessel January 15, 1981.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810023–0565) In telegram 20466 to Quito, January 27, the Department transmitted talking points for Gonzalez to use in a démarche to Roldós, indicating that “the USG is seriously concerned over the seizure of the Rosa D and the January 19 decision of the San Cristobal tribunal to penalize the Rosa D under Ecuadorean law as if it had been illegally fishing.” The evidence suggested that “the Rosa D was simply transiting the waters off the coast of Ecuador,” the talking points noted, “and a GOE decision to fine the boat and confiscate the fish on board the Rosa D would be viewed with great seriousness.” The Department also instructed Gonzalez to “mention to Roldos that in wake of this recent seizure and hopeful that a resolution can be worked out, it is perhaps a propitious time for USG and GOE representatives to meet to consider a solution to this problem. The USG is ready to engage in exploratory technical talks.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810038–1165)

370. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 370. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, June 10, 1981, 0236Z
151070. Subject: Conversation with Ecuadorean Ambassador Crespo.
1. (C—Entire text).
2. Ecuadorean Ambassador Crespo, who returned last week from Quito, discussed a number of subjects with ARA/AND Director Hart on Monday, June 8. The major points were as follows:
— Crespo has known Hurtado for some time. They had fairly lengthy talks while Crespo was at home attending the Roldos funeral. 2 Hurtado asked Crespo to remain at his post in Washington.
— Crespo reassured Hurtado that his being a Christian Democrat would not cause the US to be suspicious of him (something Hurtado said concerned him). Hurtado is not opposed to the private sector as many Ecuadoreans believe, notwithstanding some views he stated in a book some years ago. Hurtado has an open mind and is responsive. He is well disposed toward the United States but quite uninformed about it. Crespo described him as a typical Latin American politician whose lifelong orientation had been primarily toward Europe. His only visit here has been in Miami for medical treatment. It would be quite useful, Crespo believes, for Hurtado to come to the states and meet with President Reagan at an early date so that a personal relationship could be established. It did not have to be a state visit. Crespo will look for a chance to explore possibilities and be in touch with US.
— On Cuba, Central America and the Caribbean, Hurtado comes out in about the same place as Roldos. He is aware of Cuban attempts to stir up trouble, but he believes that the basic problem is the political, economic and social situations in such places as El Salvador.
— Hurtado is aware of the tuna issue with the US and wants to continue looking for practical bilateral solutions.
— Hurtado very much would like to have some assurances from the United States that we continue to support civil constitutional Government in Ecuador.
— His attitude toward the Peruvian/Ecuadorean border dispute is to seek a pacific solution. Hurtado will make sure that Ecuador does nothing to upset the status quo. Hurtado has decided that Ecuador will buy no new arms beyond those already contracted. (Crespo thought this would involve only 12 Kfirs, although he was not sure on this point.) He was not aware of the status of the radar contract. In Crespo’s opinion, the time still probably is not right for direct talks with the Peruvians.
3. Hart explained to Crespo why it had not been possible on such short notice for a high-level US mission to attend the Roldos funeral as we had hoped. Crespo was asked to pass to Hurtado our assurances that we continue to support democracy in Ecuador and elsewhere and that we look forward to the further strengthening of our already very close and friendly relationship with Ecuador. On the subject of a Hurtado-Reagan meeting, Hart explained the difficulty of scheduling an official visit over the short and medium term. We will, of course, give careful consideration to any proposals for a brief informal get together if an opportunity arises.
Haig

371. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 371. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 1
Quito, July 23, 1981, 1411Z
5289. Subject: Letter from President Reagan to President Hurtado.
Ref: State 174484 2
1. Entire text Confidential.
2. I delivered the signed original of the President’s letter (reftel) to President Hurtado on July 21. Hurtado, who had already seen the message which was transmitted through the Foreign Ministry upon receipt of the cabled text, expressed his personal satisfaction at receiving the original letter. He made two substantive comments regarding the President’s letter:
A. Hurtado stated he was determined to reciprocate President Reagan’s hope to eliminate obstacles hindering improved bilateral relations and he affirmed his intention to maintain the best possible relationships with the U.S. He commented that as Vice President he had followed our cooperation in development closely and that he would continue to do so from the presidency. These remarks offered me the opening to bring up our technical talks with the Foreign Ministry on fisheries, which I said constituted a good example of how both governments could work together to overcome bilateral problems. Hurtado said he was pleased at these efforts to find practical solutions to our tuna problems without prejudice to our juridical positions on LOS and repeated his determination to maintain good relations with the U.S.
B. President Hurtado also referred to President Reagan’s remarks regarding joint endeavors to encourage the development of more humane and secure societies elsewhere. He said he was very concerned about the situation in Central America; not only El Salvador but also Guatemala. Hurtado said we should talk about what can be done to alleviate suffering and violence in Central America while assuring the right of self-determination in those countries. I said the USG shares his concerns and referred to the Nassau conference in which the Governments of Canada, Mexico, Venezuela and the U.S. consulted on a Caribbean Basin development initiative. 3 We agreed that Central America and Caribbean Basin should be a topic for discussion when Ambassador Kirkpatrick calls on him on her forthcoming visit to Quito. 4
3. After our meeting, I sent the President the Spanish text of Ambassador Enders’ speech on El Salvador with a note referring to our conversation. 5

372. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 372. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, December 24, 1981, 0058Z
339302. Subject: Secretary’s bilateral meeting at OASGA with Ecuadorean ForMin Valencia, December 4, 1981.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Following is memcon of the Secretary’s bilateral with ForMin Valencia.
3. Summary. The Secretary met with Ecuadoran ForMin Luis Valencia afternoon of December 4 at OASGA in St. Lucia. Valencia sought continued US assistance in alleviating the border dispute between Ecuador and Peru. Valencia was concerned about reports that the US is planning to sell fighter planes to Peru. The Secretary replied emphasizing the importance to both the USG and GOE of reducing Soviet influence in Peru; Amb. Enders added that the US was not now processing any Peruvian request to buy planes, which the Ecuadoreans found encouraging. On the subject of seizure of tuna boats, Valencia mentioned new regulations on the innocent passage of ships and the possibility of a regional approach to the problem, which both sides viewed as positive steps. Participating were Assistant Secretary Enders and former GOE OAS Ambassador Galo Leoro.
4. Border dispute with Peru. After initial pleasantries, ForMin Valencia immediately raised the GOE’s primary concern over its continuing dispute with Peru. The GOE is anxious to initiate fruitful conversations with the GOP and feels the USG can be of assistance in facilitating such discussion. The Secretary assured the ForMin that the GOE can rely on the full assistance of the US.
5. Planes for Peru: ForMin Valencia expressed GOE concern that the USG may be planning to sell fighter planes to Peru, adding that he had hoped for a freeze on new acquisitions of arms in the area. The Secretary replied that the US is not anxious to contribute to increases in arms in Peru or anywhere, but we are anxious to have the GOP terminate its dependence on the Soviet Union. Amb. Enders underscored the extent of that dependence, noting that the Soviet Union has more advisers in Peru than the US has in the whole continent. The Secretary added that reduction in Soviet influence is particularly in the interest of the GOE, since the USSR is primarily interested in promoting conflict between Peru and Ecuador. Amb. Enders then pointed out that, although aware of interest in Peru, the USG has not received a request to purchase planes from the GOP and therefore is not even processing any such request. The Ecuadoreans found this news encouraging, Amb. Leoro noted that he thought a request was already being processed. The Secretary closed the discussion of Peru, saying that the USG is aware of and understands the GOE’s concerns, and will do its best to ameliorate the dispute.
6. Seizure of tuna boats: Before proceeding to specific bilateral issues, the Secretary commented that bilateral relations are generally quite good, with which ForMin Valencia wholeheartedly agreed. The Secretary then raised the problem of Ecuador’s seizures of US tuna boats. ForMin Valencia indicated that the GOE plans to introduce new regulations regarding innocent passage of ships and mentioned that there had been a determination that the recently seized boat Sea Wolf had been engaged in innocent passage. The Secretary informed Valencia that he had raised the tuna boat problem with the Mexicans. Amb. Enders noted that the Mexicans know there are problems with a regional approach, but will take another look at such a possible solution. Valencia responded that the GOE plans to discuss the tuna boat problem early next year in conference with Colombia, Chile, and Peru and perhaps in a broader framework thereafter. The Secretary and Amb. Enders commented on the positive nature of GOE steps.
7. NASA tracking station agreement: The Secretary expressed USG hopes for an extension of the agreement on NASA’s tracking station in Ecuador. ForMin Valencia understood that the requested 6-month extension had already been or was ready to be signed, so there was no longer any problem.
8. El Salvador: The Secretary stressed the importance of elections in El Salvador as a first step in a peaceful process and the importance of OASGA support for that process. When Valencia said it was difficult to support the GOES given the situation there, the Secretary replied that the alternatives are far worse. Amb. Enders cited the Andean Pact precedent of Venezuela, which went on fighting but held democratic elections. Amb. Leoro replied that that situation was not as critical as El Salvador. Amb. Enders agreed, but re-emphasized that a moderate resolution from the OAS could be quite helpful. Amb. Leoro replied that the GOE felt it would be better off working with the Andean Pact nations as a group; and that at the UN, it is planning to abstain on the El Salvador resolution as a group, as well as on human rights resolution regarding Chile.
9. The Secretary closed the meeting by thanking the GOE for its help and cooperation. Participating were Assistant Secretary Enders and former GOE OAS Ambassador Galo Leoro.
Haig
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, N810010–0317. Confidential; Immediate; Nodis. Sent for information Immediate to Lima, San Salvador, and Guayaquil. Haig visited the Castries, St. Lucia, December 2–4, to attend the OAS General Assembly meeting.

373. Memorandum of Conversation 373. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, February 26, 1982, 11:40 a.m.–12:15 p.m.
SUBJECT
Meeting with Ecuadorean Foreign Minister
PARTICIPANTS
United States
The Secretary
Assistant Secretary Thomas O. Enders
Samuel F. Hart (Notetaker)
Ecuador
Foreign Minister Luis Valencia Rodriguez
Ambassador Ricardo Crespo Zaldumbide
After an exchange of pleasantries concerning the last meeting between Valencia and the Secretary at St. Lucia, 2 Valencia proceeded to outline his thoughts on several issues he wished to discuss with the Secretary.
DEMOCRACY AND HUMAN RIGHTS
Valencia said Ecuador has followed with great interest US statements on the importance of democracy and human rights. As a young democracy with a coalition government, Ecuador wants to strengthen its relations and cooperation with the US on these grounds.
CBI. Ecuador welcomes the CBI and will watch its development with interest. It should promote a better political and economic atmosphere in the Caribbean Basin. However, it could have negative trade implications for Ecuador and other countries not included. Perhaps another program should be started which includes countries such as Ecuador, and he would be happy to talk with the US about developing this idea.
ECUADOR AND PERU BORDER DISPUTE.
Ecuadorean President Hurtado was very pleased with his visit to Brazil and his discussions with President Figueiredo about the border problem with Peru. Ecuador seeks a peaceful and permanent solution and welcomes the help of other interested countries in achieving this. There must be no unilateral placing of border markers. Figueiredo said that Brazil will cooperate with the other guarantor powers to find a solution acceptable to both countries which respects their national dignity. Valencia recalled the leading role the US played in helping resolve the border incident of a year ago and expressed the hope that the US would continue its active interest.
TUNA
Ecuador is concerned about the continuation of the US tuna embargo. It wants to solve the matter of US boats fishing within 200 miles of Ecuadorean territory. 3
LOS
Ecuador held consultations last week in New York about the renewal of the Law of the Sea Conference. It is looking for the cooperation of all countries, including the US, to conclude the negotiations successfully.
Secretary Haig responded that we are fully behind Ecuadorean democracy. Although democracies have trouble, the system offers the greatest benefit for the governed. Ecuador can rely on our support. Its record on human rights is impeccable, and we want to have close communications.
The Secretary continued that, since his talk with Valencia at St. Lucia about the border dispute, we have been very conscious of the matter and have followed it closely. It must be settled peacefully, and we are prepared to do what we can to bring about a satisfactory solution. Assistant Secretary Enders added that we expect to get together soon with Brazil and the other guarantors to see what we can do. However, our contacts with Peru concerning their position are not encouraging at present. It is a difficult problem also for Peru. Brazil will probably be in touch and we will proceed.
On the CBI, the Secretary commented that Ecuador’s concerns may be overdrawn. Any new program has its growing pains. The CBI is now on the launching pad. We have been encouraged by the reactions of a number of governments, including some Europeans, who might help us as well. We understand Ecuador’s concern about possible adverse effects on its agricultural exports. We should watch this closely and ask Ecuador to bring it to our attention if a real problem arises. We doubt that it will. Enders added that there is an impact on Colombia and Brazil but he doubts that Ecuador will be hurt. Ambassador Crespo agreed.
Moving to the LOS negotiation, Secretary Haig explained that early in the Reagan administration there was internal controversy on what to do about the LOS treaty. The Secretary said he is pleased that the US had decided to go ahead. In most areas there is no trouble. The major exception is on the deep-sea mining regime. We hope that when negotiations start again we can work from that end of the problem. It is vitally important that a successful outcome be achieved because it would be a major international accomplishment. We are going in with an open mind. The US Congress would not have ratified the old draft treaty without modifications of deep-sea mining provisions. The delays have not hurt, and we are more optimistic than pessimistic. The US negotiator will work closely with Ecuador and our other friends. Valencia added that there will be a meeting next week of the G–77 to consider the precise wording of changes the US wants. The Secretary commented that he hoped the Soviets would not cause us problems on the LOS.
The Secretary said that we are most concerned about foreign intervention in Nicaragua and El Salvador. We have hard proof of what is happening there. Our role is difficult historically, because President Reagan is dedicated to the idea that the US cannot be the policeman of the hemisphere. In response to Valencia’s comment that he was impressed with that part of the President’s CBI speech, Ambassador Enders commented that it was in the President’s own words. 4 Secretary Haig said it is important that we are not going into the CBI with excessive expectations. Our attitude toward the security problem is much the same. The development of the CBI concept is very important and Ecuador has been helpful.
Ambassador Enders said that we are about to announce the US delegation to observe the El Salvador elections. Senator Kassebaum, Father Hesburgh and Clark Kerr are among our six representatives. Valencia noted that Ecuador is sending two people from its elections board.
Ambassador Enders mentioned that the US was encouraged by the formation of the Central American Democratic Community and hoped that other countries would join eventually. Venezuela and Colombia are supportive. Enders suggested that maybe the Andean Pact as a whole would at some point express its approval of the organization. Valencia commented that the St. Lucia resolution on Central America and the Caribbean was a very good step. Secretary Haig said that there was a new spirit in the OAS. We must be wise enough to nurture it. The US is prepared to do all it can to assist and encourage.
1 Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S–I Records, Secretary Memorandums of Conversation (Memcons), Secretary Haig and Shultz, Lot 87D327, Secretary Shultz (Memcons) February 1981. Confidential. The meeting took place in Haig’s office. Drafted by Hart; cleared by Haig and Enders. Goldberg initialed for Haig.
2 See Document 372 .
3 The United States imposed an embargo on tuna imports from Ecuador on November 3, 1980. The fishery dispute hinged upon Ecuador’s assertion of offshore territorial claims of 200 miles and the U.S. recognition of 12 offshore miles of territorial rights for migratory species, including tuna. 374. Telegram From the Embassy in Colombia to the White House and the Department of State 374. Telegram From the Embassy in Colombia to the White House and the Department of State 1
Bogota, August 9, 1982, 2028Z
8491. Subject: Vice President Bush’s meeting with Ecuadorian President Oswaldo Hurtado, August 7, 1982.
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. The Vice President was accompanied by Admiral Murphy, National Security Advisor Nancy Bearg Dyke, DAS Everett Briggs, Don Barnes (interpreter), and Gerald Whitman (notetaker). Foreign Minister Luis Valencia accompanied President Hurtado.
3. Summary: The meeting was animated, frank, and very cordial, lasting more than 45 minutes. In response to the Vice President’s expression of strong U.S. interest in and support for the democratic process in Ecuador, President Hurtado said he had followed and appreciated the position of the U.S. with respect to that process. The discussion revealed considerable differences of view over the U.S. role in the Malvinas/Falklands crisis 2 and over its significance for Latin America; Hurtado expressed support for the administration’s human rights policies but called for more U.S. attention to Latin America, particularly in the economic area. End summary.
4. Hurtado said his late arrival in Colombia was due to preparations for the opening of Congress on August 10. He said Ecuador was passing through a critical moment in its history. The Vice President said the U.S. administration attached great importance to the democratic process in Ecuador and in its continuation; Hurtado said he had been following and appreciated the U.S. interest in Ecuador’s democratic evolution.
5. In response to the Vice President’s request for Ecuador’s views on U.S. relations with the hemisphere following the Malvinas crisis, Hurtado said he hadn’t noted any overt hostility toward the U.S. in Ecuador. However, it was evident that strong anti-U.S. sentiment existed there as well as elsewhere in the hemisphere. Hurtado asked for clarification to help him understand the U.S. position; it seemed the crisis had demonstrated that the U.S. gives higher priority to its ties with the North Atlantic countries than to its relationship with Latin America. Ecuador didn’t agree with Argentina’s aggression, but at the same time it could not ignore the juridical rights of a country to territory which had been taken by a colonial power.
6. The Vice President told Hurtado there had been much misunderstanding of the U.S. role. First the United States had used its good offices to attempt to negotiate a peaceful solution and Secretary Haig had worked hard to avoid bloodshed and avert military action. When the Argentines took military action the U.S. had no alternative but to support the UN resolution against aggression. The U.S. position reflected opposition to the use of force to resolve territorial disputes, but it did not signify support for colonialism. The U.S. has had a close historical relationship with Great Britain, but that did not mean the U.S. had assigned a low priority to Latin America. President Reagan from the beginning has emphasized the area as one of great importance to U.S. interests. In terms of priorities, this administration had tried to turn around what it felt was neglect by its predecessors. We sought to improve relations with Brazil and Argentina, adopting a more balanced approach toward the region on human rights. President Reagan reached out immediately to the President of Mexico. The reality was that the U.S. recognized its vital interests in the region, but the Latin American perception of this was different and that was the problem.
7. Hurtado said that the Malvinas episode had given the U.S. an opportunity to review its relationship with Latin America. An essential element of that review should be the international economic crisis. Never in the last thirty years has Latin America had an economic situation as dangerous to the stability of its institutions. He agreed with the Vice President that the strength of the U.S. economy was an important factor affecting Latin American economies; U.S. decisions could either favor or damage their development. One such favorable decision was the U.S. Caribbean Basin Initiative, but the U.S. needed to do more such as devoting more resources to multilateral financial institutions.
8. The Vice President emphasized that the health of the U.S. economy was the key to continued or expanded U.S. assistance. It was unrealistic politically to expect the U.S. Congress to expand assistance when the economy was suffering. A recovery in the U.S. was in process now, not as strong as we would like, but the worst has passed. The U.S. would do what it could, but present day economic realities precluded anything like a Marshall Plan for Latin America.
9. Hurtado said that he realized the initial impression by some that the Reagan administration would not care about human rights had been wrong. The U.S. had demonstrated its continued concern for these ideals in its support for Ecuador. He also acknowledged the more balanced approach the U.S. had taken world wide in applying its human rights policies.
10. Hurtado expressed concern over the greater U.S. tendency toward bilateral diplomacy, saying it was important also to conduct relations through multilateral fora as well, particularly as regards economic issues. He asked whether the U.S. would be receptive to a new multilateral forum which would address hemispheric economic issues. In response, the Vice President said the use of multilateral channels had been prejudiced by irrelevant political rhetoric. For example, violently anti-U.S. positions in the OAU and NAM had set back U.S. public support for multilateral organizations. However, if a Latin American proposal were advanced for a multilateral forum to address economic issues, the U.S. would give it serious consideration as a constructive use of multilateral diplomacy.
11. In closing, Hurtado thanked the Vice President for his deep interest in Latin America. He said the Vice President’s presence in Colombia was evidence that the USVP was indeed interested in Latin America, and such expressions of interest would solidify hemispheric relations.
Boyatt
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D820415–0348. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information to Brasilia, Buenos Aires, Caracas, La Paz, Lima, Mexico City, Panama City, San Jose, and Santiago. Sent for information Immediate to Quito. Bush visited Bogota, August 6–8, to attend the inauguration of President Betancur.

375. Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 375. Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 1
Washington, March 31, 1983
SUBJECT
Official Working Visit of President Osvaldo Hurtado of Ecuador
I. SETTING
President Hurtado visits Washington at the most troubled time since Ecuador returned to democracy in 1979. Economic activity is at its lowest level in years and has fueled discontent with the government in business, labor and military sectors. Hurtado’s courageous decision in March to impose essential austerity measures needed to come to terms with the IMF prompted a potentially dangerous general strike. U.S. political support has been important to Hurtado. He very much wanted and welcomed the White House invitation. 2 (He was scheduled for a visit in May 1982 but requested a postponement because of the South Atlantic conflict.) 3
Democracy’s roots in Ecuador are tender but Hurtado is dedicated to nurturing them. As a scholar and leader of the left-of-center Christian Democrats, his hope has been to wean Ecuador away from destructive cycles of personalism and militarism. His party’s minority position and the financial emergency, however, have hampered his efforts. Since mid-1982, his main goal has been to see Ecuador through to the elections in 1984.
The President draws some strength from the democratic structure he is trying to preserve. Most politicians and influential newspapers still back the constitutional order. Despite political unrest, Ecuador remains free of guerrillas and terrorists. Moderate military leaders realize they have no better proposals for dealing with the economic crisis than do the civilians. Helpful, too, have been the patience and understanding of foreign banks. Finally, widespread awareness that the U.S. and other hemispheric democracies would not look kindly on a coup has restrained plotters.
Hurtado faces formidable problems. Unfulfilled hopes raised by the oil wealth of the seventies haunt the country. Inflation, now 30 percent, pinches the man on the street. The business community is troubled and distrusts the President’s motives. The congress avoids responsibility for painful economic measures. Unions sometimes press unreasonable demands.
Some military leaders are restive and ambitious; even those who are not brood about the impact of the economic crisis on military income and hardware procurement. Should the armed forces not intervene effectively on Hurtado’s behalf in case of sustained popular unrest, the government could fall.
Hurtado’s style contributes to his troubles. Formerly the Vice President, he was thrust unexpectedly into the limelight when President Roldos died in an aircraft accident in 1981. 4 He is thoughtful, rather remote, and ill at ease with political infighting in a society which expects flamboyance and demagoguery from its leaders. While respected for his personal integrity and honesty, his seeming immobility in the face of difficult decisions leads to accusations of indecision.
II. OBJECTIVES
1. Support For Democracy
We want to demonstrate our strong commitment to Ecuador’s democratic system to Hurtado’s allies and adversaries and to the hemisphere as a whole. The U.S. already has played a key role through public support at nervous moments, visits of senior U.S. officials, CCC credits, the recent lifting of our tuna embargo 5 and, most importantly, in the Presidential invitation.
2. Ecuador’s Economic Problems
We have told the Ecuadoreans not to expect additional economic assistance from this trip, but they will probably seek greater support in dealing with their current problems and with hemisphere debt problems generally. We should commend Hurtado’s efforts to reach agreement with the IMF and indicate our willingness to convey to the commercial banks our support for his economic reform program.
3. Bilateral Relations
Our relations are generally excellent. Our economic and security assistance is modest but welcome. The Ecuadoreans are seeking reconsideration of the recent increase in Panama Canal tolls. Central America is not a major concern in Ecuador but the visit is a good opportunity to relate our strategy to Hurtado’s interest in hemisphere security. We should avoid any attempts to draw us into Ecuador’s historic dispute with Peru over access to the Amazon.

Enders sent the memorandum to Shultz under cover of a March 30 memorandum in which he wrote: “Hurtado’s working visit affords us the opportunity to reinforce our generally good bilateral relations with Ecuador; there are no urgently pressing issues in dispute.” See Document 376 and footnote 1 thereto.

4 See Document 370, footnote 2 .
5 In telegram 33460 to Quito, February 4, the Department wrote: “Dept. agrees with Ambassador that conditions now permit the U.S. to lift the embargo on Ecuadorean tuna products imposed in 1980” and instructed the Embassy to seek Ecuadorean participation in a regional licensing agreement. “In sum, Embassy may advise appropriate GOE officials that the USG is now willing to remove the embargo against Ecuadorean tuna products and is preparing the paperwork necessary to accomplish this, but that we do not want any public announcement concerning embargo at this time.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830066–0222) The “Removal of Prohibition on Importation of Tuna and Tuna Products From Ecuador and Peru,” was published by the Customs Service in the Federal Register , vol. 48, No. 76, April, 19, 1983, pp. 16798–16799.

376. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Clark) to President Reagan 376. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Clark) to President Reagan 1
Washington, April 7, 1983
I. PURPOSE
Our main objective should be to buttress President Hurtado’s effort to maintain democracy in Ecuador. Our political support has helped him in this regard. By demonstrating our strong commitment to the democratic system, we will give a clear message not only to Hurtado’s allies but to his adversaries as well and to the hemisphere generally. 2 President Hurtado probably will seek additional U.S. assistance in overcoming Ecuador’s current economic crisis. He may also comment generally on the hemisphere’s debt problems. Ecuador is also seeking reconsideration of the recent increase in Panama Canal tolls.
II. BACKGROUND
Hurtado assumed the Presidency in 1981 when President Roldos was killed in an aircraft accident. Hurtado has managed his portfolio as well as could be expected given Ecuador’s fledgling democracy, unstable economy, and erratic foreign policy. Economic activity, aggravated by a failure to adjust spending patterns to lower petroleum prices, is the lowest it has been in years. This has resulted in discontent in the business and labor communities and within the military. Inflation is 30 percent. The business community distrusts President Hurtado’s motives; and the military has been plotting to unseat him almost since he assumed power. Hurtado’s decision to impose austerity measures needed for a $170M stand-by agreement with the IMF prompted a potentially dangerous general strike last month. Despite political unrest and severe economic problems, Ecuador does not have a guerrilla or terrorist problem.
Because of the many problems he faces across a broad spectrum, Hurtado’s primary goal is to see Ecuador through to next year’s elections. Relations between our countries are generally excellent. We have recently lifted our tuna embargo and should indicate support for Hurtado’s economic reform measures and efforts to reach agreement with the IMF. 3
[Omitted here are a list of participants, a press plan, and a schedule.]

Attached as Tab C, but not printed, are background papers, papers on the political situation, the economic situation, foreign relations, and the Panama Canal tolls, and a CIA Special Analysis/Bio Brief. Tab D, a departure statement, was not attached. Reagan and Hurtado’s remarks are in Public Papers: Reagan, 1983 , Book I, p. 517. Hurtado visited Washington for an official working visit April 7–9. According to the President’s Daily Diary, on April 8 Hurtado met with Reagan in the Oval Office from 11:36 a.m to 12:17 p.m, followed by a working luncheon in the State Dining Room from 12:17 to 1:25 p.m. (Reagan Library, President’s Daily Diary)
