377. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 377. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, April 9, 1983, 2112Z
97975. Subject: Conversation between President Hurtado and Secretary Shultz, April 7, 1983.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Summary. In a very cordial 40-minute conversation, President Hurtado and Secretary of State Shultz discussed Ecuador’s economic situation and the general financial crisis in Latin America. Hurtado stressed the need for fresh capital flows to be resumed in the region to cover short-term liquidity problems. Secretary Shultz expressed admiration for the courageous steps Ecuador has taken. He agreed with much of the economic analysis of President Hurtado and pledged continuing US support for Ecuadorean democracy. End Summary.
3. The bilateral between Secretary Shultz and President Hurtado took place at the home of Ecuadorean Ambassador Crespo on April 7, 1983. It began at 9:40 a.m. and lasted approximately 40 minutes. Also present at the discussion were Foreign Minister Valencia, Ambassador Crespo, Assistant Secretary Enders, Ambassador Hart (notetaker), Stephanie Van Reigersberg (interpreter), and Paul Williams (interpreter).
4. Following Secretary Shultz’ words of welcome, President Hurtado launched immediately into a discussion of Ecuador’s economic problems and the general difficulties of the region. He said that Ecuador is now concluding the renegotiation of its public foreign debt, has started discussions to restructure the private sector debt and expects to reach agreement on a standby with the IMF soon. Changes in Ecuador’s debt profile will alleviate its liquidity problem but will not resolve the longer-term difficulties. The government is attempting to increase internal savings to finance development. In past years, Ecuador has relied on external savings for investment and has found this type of development to be very fragile. The GOE has recently raised taxes on consumption items such as tobacco and beer as well as public service and utility prices. In this way it hopes to extract savings from the more prosperous sector of society. At the same time, it has lowered subsidies on petroleum and other products of primary necessity. This has been very difficult politically because Ecuador has a weak presidency and under-developed institutions. Two weeks ago the country passed through a very serious political crisis following the latest increase in petroleum prices and the second devaluation of the sucre in a year.
5. Hurtado continued that these measures in themselves are insufficient to resolve Ecuador’s economic problems. Since last August the country has been cut off from loans from private international banks. No economy can stand such a shock when it has become used to large flows of bank credit. In New York yesterday Hurtado explained the country’s need for fresh credits to the banking community. The country’s recovery also depends on continued low international interest rates and no further fall in the prices of Ecuador’s exports. Hurtado noted that a one percent rise in the interest rate and a one dollar decrease in oil-prices costs Ecuador a total of 80 million dollars in foreign exchange per year. Ecuador’s problems are common to most of Latin America and their resolution is required to preserve democracy. Many think it is a miracle that Ecuadorean democracy has survived the recent stress. Its future will depend on international cooperation.
6. Secretary Shultz expressed his appreciation for Hurtado’s comments and offered his views on Ecuador and the international economic situation. The Secretary congratulated Hurtado on the success he has had in implementing measures which will be very beneficial over time. He expressed US support for what Hurtado has done and for Ecuadorean democracy. It is important that the democratic process be preserved in Ecuador both for that country’s own sake and as a demonstration to the hemisphere of what can be accomplished. The Secretary praised Ecuador’s efforts to raise domestic savings to finance development. This is a problem of world scale. President Reagan is also pursuing policies to this end in the United States. The US is heavily involved with the IMF and other institutions which will help our neighbors to cope with their debt. We are concentrating on the IMF as the major instrument to achieve this—recognizing that all measures taken are interrelated. The US believes all members of the world economy must pull together to resolve the present financial crisis. To achieve this, we need a general economic expansion so that increased trade will finance debt repayment. The US is trying to raise its growth rate and is urging other strong economies like Japan and Germany to adopt stimulative measures. We recognize the need to preserve liquidity and an open trading system. US policy is to support countries which are dealing with their short-term debt problem while we try to create an expanding world economy without excessive inflation—one of the reasons for our present difficulties.
7. President Hurtado responded that the interdependence of the world economy unfortunately is not understood elsewhere, such as in some private banks. The US must help to raise the consciousness of others to this. If Latin America’s problems are not resolved, it will cause other countries’ recovery to falter. He welcomed the recent good news about the US economy, but pointed out that it will take time for its benefits to be felt in Latin America. It could take one–three years before Ecuador’s export prices rise. Meanwhile, the short-term problem is serious and societies with weak institutions are in danger. The very survival of democracy in Ecuador is threatened. In such circumstances the IMF must realize that it cannot impose unreasonable conditionality on its loans. It must avoid applying mechanical formula without considering the social, political and cultural conditions in borrowing countries.
8. Secretary Shultz commented that the IMF can, through its conditions, help countries to do economically necessary things which are politically difficult. However, it must try to find the right mix between the politically possible and the economically desirable so that it does not go too far. If there are particular things in Ecuador’s negotiations with the fund that it would like the US to bring up, the Secretary said he would be glad to know about them.
9. President Hurtado commented that he knew the US did not control the IMF. The focus of present discussions between the Central Bank and the Fund is over the size of the fiscal gap. He did not know if this had been resolved in recent days. In any case, the IMF must recognize that the present crisis is different from those in the past because it is generalized and not limited to a few countries.
10. Secretary Shultz agreed with this point and said the IMF is increasingly aware of it. The world must also recognize that the locomotive idea of any single economy pulling along a world recovery is wrong. It will require a much more integrated process.
11. President Hurtado said that protectionist policies are obstacles to an economic recovery. Ecuador relies on foreign trade. Due to structural problems, import demand is rigid.
12. Hurtado then shifted the subject to Ecuador’s classification some months ago as a “sub-standard risk” by a United States Agency which advises US banks. Hurtado expressed the hope that Ecuador’s credit worthiness would be moved to a higher category as soon as it reaches agreement with the IMF and creditor banks. He asked Secretary Shultz if the committee could meet soon to reconsider Ecuador’s classification.
13. The Secretary said he was glad to be alerted to this problem. Assistant Secretary Enders stated that he would follow-up on the matter. The Secretary closed the meeting by reiterating US support for Ecuadorean democracy and pledging continued cooperation. He suggested to President Hurtado that he repeat some of the analysis on the world economic situation to President Reagan during the talks scheduled for April 8. 2
Eagleburger
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830211–0187. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information Priority to La Paz, Bogota, Caracas, Lima, and USCINCSO. Drafted by Hart; cleared by Enders and in ARA/AND, and S/S–O; approved by Shultz.
2 See footnote 1, Document 376 .

378. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Embassy in Ecuador 378. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Embassy in Ecuador 1
New York, October 5, 1983, 1325Z
Secto 10039. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with President Hurtado.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Summary. Hurtado’s principal focus was economic with a call for greater flexibility and understanding by the IMF and the IFI’s for the problems Ecuador faces and the tough austerity measures it has already undertaken. Despite its economic problems, the Ecuadorean democratic process remains on track. The Secretary was sympathetic to Hurtado’s concerns, especially the impact of austerity programs on international trade, but noted that the IMF had to be strict if long-term recovery is to be assured. Hurtado noted a more positive and pragmatic attitude in the NAM. He also said he would be proposing development projects with Peru to eventually resolve their border problems. End summary.
3. The Secretary and President Hurtado had a cordial 30 minute exchange at Hurtado’s New York hotel suite evening of September 30. Hurtado was accompanied by FonMin Valencia, Ambassadors Crespo, Falconi OAS and Albornoz U.N. On the U.S. side, Under Secretary Eagleburger, Assistant Secretary Motley, Ambassador Hart and ARA/AND Deputy Director Scott notetaker also attended.
4. The Secretary opened by regretting that poor weather had delayed his arrival in New York, forcing postponement of their meeting scheduled that morning. Hurtado said that weather seems to affect his visits to the U.S., recalling the delay in his arrival for his official working visit to Washington last April, a visit he termed very productive. 2 The Secretary recalled well their earlier meeting. He noted that the U.S. economy had strengthened in recent months even more than had been anticipated when he had talked with Hurtado last. While this should help the world economy, the Secretary recognized that Ecuador faced particularly difficult problems, including the El Nino flooding, but he admired and supported the GOE’s efforts to deal with the situation. Hurtado noted that indeed everything seemed to be conspiring against his government and the democratic system—except the Ecuadorean armed forces. Despite the problems, however, democracy was surviving, the election campaign was underway and the transition to the new government next year would be peaceful.
5. Hurtado then turned to the principal focus of his discussion—Ecuador’s difficulties with the international financial institutions. Hurtado asserted that while Ecuador still faces many problems in its domestic economy, there are hopeful signs as well. The economy is stabilizing, the private sector is growing, inflation is coming down and unemployment remains relatively low. Despite this progress, Hurtado wanted to express his concern to the Secretary about the attitudes of the IMF and the IFIs. Hurtado commended President Reagan’s strong support for increased funding for the IMF which reflected an understanding by the U.S. of North-South issues. However, the IMF and the IFI’s do not apparently share this understanding. The IMF is a “frozen” institution, insufficiently flexible in its criteria, and interventionist in its approach. Ecuador recognizes its own responsibilities for getting its economic house in order and has already taken tough austerity measures. For example, it has cut the GOE’s budget deficit from 7 to 4 percent of the GDP to meet IMF targets. But there are political limits to such measures and there must be an end to them in sight if the people are to accept them. The IMF’s difficult attitude also affects Ecuador’s access to international commercial credits.
6. Hurtado asserted that the IDB and the World Bank are similarly inflexible in their demands, often based on economic theories rather than political realities. The IDB, for example, demanded impractical positive sub-lending interest rates at least 60 percent at Ecuador’s current rate of inflation, in an emergency agriculture sector loan to rebuild flooded farmlands. Similarly, the GOE had to reject a 100 million dollars World Bank loan for electric power development because it required a doubling of electric rates—even though the GOE is already increasing the rate by 3 percent per month. When the Secretary noted that 3 percent would still be a negative rate, Hurtado argued that Ecuador’s current high rate of inflation is abnormal and temporary, due almost exclusively to the El Nino floods and the resulting sharp increase in food prices.
7. The Secretary agreed that Hurtado’s concerns were legitimate, although he noted he did not have all the details about Ecuador’s particular problems. However, IMF funds are limited and must be carefully allocated. While some adjustments by the IMF to individual country situations are possible, its programs must be fairly consistent if reforms needed to ensure long-term economic growth are to succeed. The Secretary did note that the austerity aspects of IMF programs are perhaps overdone. The U.S. faces a 70 billion dollars trade deficit this year; other economies must expand if we are to reduce that deficit. Nonetheless, the IMF has to have tough standards to carry out its responsibilities. In so doing, it must mind the business of others, an inevitable conflict. The Secretary again complimented President Hurtado on his government’s performance and termed the reduction in the GOE deficit remarkable. He assured Hurtado he would bear the President’s thoughts in mind during USG deliberations on development policy.
8. Turning to NAM, Hurtado said he had called on the leaders of India, Yugoslavia and Egypt during the UNGA and had attended the NAM Chiefs of State meeting September 29. He had told the NAM leaders that Ecuador and much of Latin America would welcome a return by the NAM to its original principles. Hurtado was encouraged by the current atmosphere in the NAM, less ideological and confrontational, more pragmatic. The Secretary said that we shared his assessment of a more positive attitude on the part of the truly non-aligned nations and are working hard to encourage it. The USSR, however, appeared to remain very confrontational in its approach.
9. Hurtado touched briefly on Ecuador’s relations with Peru. Ecuador wants to reduce tensions between two peoples who have so much in common. Hurtado hopes to propose soon a program of border development projects which will gradually create the conditions needed to settle the border issue. The Secretary welcomed Hurtado’s views which he termed constructive.
10. The Secretary concluded by wishing Hurtado well with his economic program and for the future of Ecuador’s democratic system. Hurtado asked that the Secretary convey his personal greetings to President Reagan.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830576–0951. Confidential; Immediate. Sent for information Priority to Guayaquil, Lima, and USUN.
2 See Documents 375 and 376 .

379. Memorandum of Conversation 379. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, June 26, 1984, 9:45–10 a.m.
SUBJECT
President’s Meeting with Ecuadorean President-elect Leon Febres Cordero
PARTICIPANTS
President Ronald Reagan
Vice President George Bush
Secretary of State George P. Shultz
Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs Langhorne A. Motley
Mr. Robert C. McFarlane, National Security Council
RADM John M. Poindexter, Deputy Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs
Constantine Menges, Senior Director, Latin American Affairs, NSC
Samuel F. Hart, U.S. Ambassador
President-elect Leon Febres Cordero
Vice President-elect Blasco Penaherrera
Congressman-elect Sixto Duran Ballen
President Reagan welcomed the President-elect and congratulated him on his recent electoral victory. 2 The President said that Vice President Bush would represent him at the August 10 inauguration in Quito. 3
Febres Cordero thanked the President and explained that he had campaigned on the same policies as President Reagan—i.e., use of the market to set prices. He then explained how Ecuador’s economic condition had worsened since 1979 when the country had returned to democracy from military rule. The size of the, budget, the deficit, the rate of inflation, the number of public sector employees and the external debt were all much greater today than five years ago. The Febres Cordero government has the political will to make the necessary changes to correct this situation, but they will need help from the US, he said. If the new government fails in Ecuador, the democratic system will fail. The new government takes office with a 100,000 vote margin of victory, but this could easily change. If the government succeeds, an historic victory will have been achieved and Ecuador can become the showplace for American democracy.
President Reagan responded that we wish to help, but we also have constraints. Secretary Shultz added that Peter McPherson has been discussing ways in which PL–480 and our housing program can be used early in the new administration to provide a political offset to politically difficult actions such as major increases in gasoline prices. 4 President Reagan continued that the deterioration in such matters as the size of the budget and inflation had occurred in the US as it had in Ecuador. One of the successes of this Administration had been to reduce the inflation rate to 1/3 of what it had been earlier. The biggest improvement, however, had been in putting people to work. Six million jobs have been created in the last 18 months through the activities of free enterprise.
The Ecuadorean President-elect outlined Ecuador’s difficulty in servicing its external debt. The country exports approximately $2.5 billion a year. In 1979, 15% of exports were used to service the debt. Now interest alone consumes 40% of exports. Yesterday’s rise of 1/2% in the US prime rate will cost Ecuador approximately $35 million.
President Reagan responded that the high interest rates are a great frustration to him and that he believes the problem is mainly psychological. The financial community still does not believe that the Administration will not revert to inflationary policies. The government does have control, President Reagan said, and he is mad about the bank actions.
Vice President Bush pointed out that Febres Cordero will be meeting tonight with the major international banks and the President-elect said that he would pass on the President’s thoughts.
The meeting closed with Febres Cordero’s extension of best wishes to President Reagan in the upcoming US elections. He said that the western world needs President Reagan’s leadership.
ACTION COMMITMENTS:
The Vice President will represent the President at the August 10 inauguration in Quito.

380. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 380. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, June 29, 1984, 0132Z
190917. Subject: Meeting between Secretary Shultz and President-elect Febres Cordero, June 26, 1984.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary: The Secretary told President-elect Febres Cordero that the United States will work for the success of its free market approach to Ecuador’s development. Febres said he hopes to end the government’s practice of subsidizing prices in his country but wants to move concurrently to provide food and housing. On relations with the communists, Febres declared that he has warned both the Cuban and Soviet Ambassadors that they must behave. End summary.
3. Participants: Secretary Shultz, Under Secretary Wallis, Assistant Secretary Motley, AID Administrator McPherson, Ambassador Hart, and ARA/AND Director Rondon were present on the American side; President-elect Leon Febres Cordero, Vice-President-elect Blasco Penaherrera, Congressman-elect Sixto Duran Ballen, as well as the President-elect’s two brothers: Nicolas and Agustin, represented Ecuador.
4. The Secretary congratulated President-elect Febres Cordero on his election, expressing enthusiasm for the ideas of the new Ecuadorean President. The Secretary said we want to be helpful.
5. Ecuador today. The President-elect compared today’s situation in Ecuador to 1979. He said the 70’s was the decade of the oil boom in Ecuador. By 1979 foreign reserves stood at 800 million dols, there was a seven percent rate of inflation, there were 150,000 civil servants, and it took about 15 percent of exports to service an external debt of 3 billion dols. Febres lamented that his predecessors did not take advantage of this to build the country’s agricultural infrastructure. Instead, by 1984, inflation has reached 50 percent annually, the bureaucracy has doubled, the public debt risen to 8 billion dols which takes 35 percent of exports just to service interest, and the fiscal deficit has ballooned. And now, he lamented, the U.S. prime rate has risen by one-half point which will cost Ecuador 33 million dols.
6. Economic plans. The new President spoke of his determination to end price subsidies, stating that if the price of gasoline were doubled to seventy-five cents the budget deficit would be eliminated. He cautioned, however, that raising the price of gasoline will cause social unrest. He warned that it would be very easy for Ecuador to slip to the left given the relatively-slim 100,000 vote margin by which he won over his presidential opponent. Febres said he wanted us to know that if Ecuador is helped, his new administration will show that the democratic/market mechanism process can develop a country.
7. The Secretary suggested that if the price of gasoline is increased, that it be done the first week in office. The Secretary recalled President Reagan’s decision to decontrol oil prices. This was done in the first days of the new administration and today is considered as one of the smartest actions of the administration. But the decision was a source of considerable upset at the time it was taken. Febres Cordero commented that the Ecuadorean economy as not as responsive as the American. He felt a need to compensate the working people for whatever steps he takes, mentioning a desire to provide housing.
8. U.S. assistance. AID Administrator McPherson expressed the hope that the USG can help through the housing investment guarantee program. We hope to be able to dedicate PL 480 proceeds to housing, McPherson continued. The creation of employment and visible housing would give political cover to measures such as raising gasoline prices. The idea, McPherson went on, is that the difficult steps be taken in tandem with improvement of the housing/food situation. McPherson said he would be disappointed if programs were not ready for implementation at the time of the new President’s inauguration.
9. The Secretary declared he would be disappointed too if we cannot get the programs rolling by then.
10. Central America/Cuba. The President-elect changed subjects, expressing his belief in self determination and non-intervention. He said that Ecuador will help work for peace in Central America. He referred in passing to the existence of a frontier problem with Peru but said no more.
11. The Secretary pointed to Soviet, Cuban and Nicaraguan intervention in Central America, including their attempt to subvert El Salvador. Such subversion stands in the way of the economic development we seek to promote in Central America. It will be hard to achieve these goals if Nicaragua is allowed to exist as a privileged sanctuary for guerrillas. The Secretary noted that the outgoing Hurtado administration had upgraded relations with Cuba. He asked how Febres Cordero would handle the Cuban connection.
12. The President-elect referred to conversations he has had with the Cuban and Soviet Ambassadors. With the former, Febres Cordero said he was very direct and told the Cuban Ambassador that he wanted no problems with Havana, as long as Castro keeps hands off Ecuador. Febres Cordero also reported that he informed the Soviet Ambassador that Ecuador would not invade the USSR, and that he expects reciprocity. Febres Cordero thought he had made his point to the Soviets.
13. In a closing comment, Febres Cordero stated that his victory broke the invincibility of the left in Latin America. This fact will unify Ecuador’s fractioned left against the Febres Cordero Government.
14. The Secretary repeated that we want the Febres Cordero administration to succeed and we will help it.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840417–0818. Secret; Immediate. Sent for information Priority to Lima. Drafted by Rondon; cleared by McPherson, Wallis, and McKinley, and S; approved by Motley.

381. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 381. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA 84–10082 Washington, August 1984
[Omitted here is a title page.]
Ecuador’s New Administration: Challenges and Prospects
The new conservative administration of President Leon Febres-Cordero faces an array of serious, interrelated problems, the most difficult of which is the struggling economy. He has inherited high inflation and unemployment, depressed manufacturing and construction sectors, and a foreign debt service burden that will consume 35 percent of export earnings this year.
Rising interest rates and a continued slump in the market for oil—one of Ecuador’s principal exports—will plague Febres-Cordero’s efforts to improve the country’s external accounts. Meanwhile, the stringent, free market adjustment measures he promises to employ to foster recovery in the domestic economy will produce negative political repercussions, such as labor unrest and congressional obstructionism. His plans to cut basic commodity subsidies, decrease regulation, and liquidate state monopolies will leave him open to attacks from opponents on the center-left and left, who control a majority of the seats in Congress. The President’s own coalition—six conservative groups out of Ecuador’s legally registered parties—is only a loose union.
Leftist subversive groups—relatively new to Ecuador—hope that Febres-Cordero’s conservative policies will swell the pool of alienated youth and leftists from which they draw recruits. Armed terrorist groups—including members of the Alfaro Vive Carajo (“Alvaro Lives, Damn It” or AVC) organization and militants from the Ecuadorean Socialist Revolutionary Party—are currently small but [ less than 1 line not declassified ] are planning increased violent activities this fall. Moreover, the latter is receiving Cuban assistance, and [ less than 1 line not declassified ] Havana may be considering offering similar aid to what we believe is the AVC.
In attacking these problems, Febres-Cordero has a number of advantages:
• Foreign bankers are reported to be pleased with his plans for economic recovery, and this will improve the chances for new lending and foreign investment.
• His economic philosophy and background as a businessman will garner strong political support from Ecuador’s private sector.
• The conservative armed forces, according to the US defense attache [ less than 1 line not declassified ] solidly favor the administration’s political and economic programs and its pro-US stance in foreign affairs.
• The President’s political skills and charismatic style—always important considerations in Ecuador’s fragmented political environment—will enable him to retain substantial popular backing.
• Febres-Cordero’s opponents are not united, ideologically or otherwise, and they are vulnerable to a variety of political carrots and sticks that are at the President’s ready disposal.
We believe Febres-Cordero will marshal these assets sufficiently well to keep opposition activity at controllable levels at least during his first year in office and thus continue the consolidation of Ecuador’s five-year-old democracy. We believe he will act promptly on economic matters while foreign lenders are sympathetic and before domestic opponents can organize their forces. Even under favorable circumstances, however, he will have recurring difficulty, in our view, meeting foreign debt payments, keeping Ecuador in compliance with IMF criteria, and paying for imports to reactivate the industrial sector.
We expect the President to use populist tactics—such as low-cost subsidized housing and job-creation programs—as well as patronage and legal sanctions to keep labor and leftist political challenges under control. We judge that security force countermeasures and public hostility toward violence will prevent the nascent terrorist movement from growing quickly enough to threaten the government’s stability over the next two years or so.
US interests in Ecuador will be served by the new administration’s intention to follow a moderate course in foreign affairs and to support US positions in international forums. This does not necessarily assure that Febres-Cordero will avoid cooperation with some of the more aggressive Latin American debtors at regional meetings on the debt question. Despite his nationalistic rhetoric, we doubt that Febres-Cordero will provoke tensions over Quito’s border dispute with Peru. He probably will not reverse his predecessor’s renewal of diplomatic ties with Cuba, since it could rally his leftist political opponents, unless he receives solid evidence that Havana is abetting terrorism against his government.
[Omitted here is the body of the assessment.]
1 Source: Central Intelligence Agency, Office of Asian, Pacific, Latin America, and African Analysis, Job 04T00367R: Intelligence Publication Files—Production Case Files (1984), Box 1, Folder 14: Ecuador’s New Administration: Challenges & Prospects, August 1984. 382. Memorandum of Conversation 382. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Guayaquil, February 28, 1985
SUBJECT
Secretary’s Meeting with Ecuadorean President Leon Febres-Cordero
PARTICIPANTS
United States
The Secretary of State
Assistant Secretary Langhorne A. Motley
Assistant Secretary Bernard Kalb
Ambassador Samuel F. Hart
Ambassador-Designate Richard T. McCormack
J. A. Purnell, Notetaker
Ecuador
President Leon Febres-Cordero
Foreign Minister Edgar Teran
Ambassador Mario Ribadeneira
Ambassador Marcelo Fernandez de Cordova
Ambassador Jaime Molestina
SUMMARY
President Febres-Cordero made an insistent appeal for additional U.S. assistance in his meeting with the Secretary. He emphasized the social costs which his economic rationalization policies have entailed. 2 Febres-Cordero said more U.S. aid would help him convince the Ecuadorean people that the temporary sacrifices he has asked them to bear will pay off. He asserted that he could not carry out his program without additional U.S. help. Febres-Cordero specifically mentioned PL–480, ESF, oil sales, and a multi-year Paris Club rescheduling agreement. The Secretary promised to look into how the U.S. might be able to increase assistance after he returns to Washington. END SUMMARY.
The Secretary stopped at the military portion of Guayaquil Airport for three hours on February 28 for a bilateral and lunch hosted by President Leon Febres-Cordero. The initial bilateral lasted almost 40 minutes.
The Secretary told President Febres-Cordero that he directed much of his time to arms control and the Middle East. Jordan, Egypt, and Iraq are responding to our concern that they come forward and actively seek progress toward peace. But President Reagan wishes to give primary foreign policy attention to our own neighborhood. We recognize that Febres-Cordero and we are on the same wavelength, and we appreciate that he is a democratically-elected president.
Febres-Cordero responded that his policies, both foreign and domestic, were coherent. He had done in his seven months in office exactly what he had said he would do. His policies were designed to support democracy and free enterprise—not only in Ecuador, but as a model for all South America. He had told us in June when he had met with the President, the Vice-President, and the Secretary that he needed our assistance. On taking office, he discovered that the economic situation of the country was worse than he had believed. He said he now needed our help desperately.
Febres-Cordero said he had to state very candidly, however, that so far he had received only sympathy rather than action. The aid he expected, which he thought he had a right to expect in view of his effort to promote shared policies and values, had not come. His policies were not conditioned on U.S. assistance, but we should understand that he lacked the resources to succeed on his own and that he was isolated from the rest of South America. He was thus shocked to get a report from the Foreign Ministry that for FY 1986, Ecuador was absolutely at the bottom of the list of U.S. aid recipients in Latin America.
Febres-Cordero said he hoped to make the Secretary understand that what was at stake was the stability of democracy in Ecuador. It will take time for his economic stabilization measures and encouragement of private enterprise to show results. In the meantime, he needed resources to convince the people that the current sacrifices will lead to greater well-being. Febres-Cordero noted that the U.S. would need oil for its strategic petroleum reserve. He hoped Ecuador could supply some reasonable portion, such as 40,000 BPD, to stabilize its production and income. 3
The Secretary said it might be useful if we reviewed each type of aid to see what could be done, although he didn’t have all the numbers in his head. The Secretary recalled that Febres-Cordero and he had discussed a housing program last May. 4 Febres-Cordero said that program seemed to be working well up until now, but that he understood that the Treasury Department had opposed a Housing Guarantee program beyond FY 1985. The Secretary explained that we are well-off as a country, but that President Reagan was concerned about reducing our budget deficit. Subsidies were being reduced in several areas, including housing, but so far as he knew our assistance to the Ecuadorean housing program was going forward. The Secretary said he would be glad to push for more help in Washington, but the Ecuadoreans had to single out particular areas and programs, rather than simply asking for more assistance in general.
Febres-Cordero responded that he had requested $25 million in PL–480, but we had agreed to only $15 million. The extra $10 million in assistance would make a tremendous difference to the growth of the agricultural sector. Furthermore, the GOE had agreed with the steering committee of the private banks on a multi-year rescheduling of official debts, but it would need our help in negotiating a similar arrangement with official creditors in the Paris Club. The Secretary agreed to look into both these matters, though he noted that questions of precedent and comparability would have to be considered in the Paris Club.
The Secretary returned to the question of PL–480, asking if ten million more in FY 1985 would satisfy the Ecuadoreans. Febres-Cordero said yes; Ambassador Hart noted that negotiations were set to begin on the $15 million that had been approved for FY 1985. Febres-Cordero noted that the USG was proposing $15 million in ESF for Ecuador in FY 1986 and asked that Ecuador receive at least that much. He tied his need for short term assistance to next January’s congressional elections, which he said would amount to a verdict on his entire administration and policy orientation. The Secretary again agreed to look into Febres-Cordero’s request on his return to Washington.
The President asserted that he has had to make extraordinary efforts himself, indeed to go beyond the limits of Ecuador’s obsolete drug laws, to make progress in the fight against illicit drug trafficking. The effort has been perhaps the most successful of all his Government’s initiatives, precisely because the U.S. had responded to Ecuador’s request for assistance in fighting drug traffickers. This kind of cooperation and mutual effort should be replicated in other areas. The Secretary expressed appreciation for the GOE’s interdiction efforts and noted that it was in Ecuador’s own interests to prevent the drug traffickers from getting out of control as they had in Colombia.
The Secretary agreed with Febres-Cordero’s emphasis on making a market economy work. But he told Febres-Cordero that Ecuador’s economy seemed to be doing better than the President was giving himself credit for. The Secretary praised the President’s prudent prediction of two and a half percent real growth in 1985, in spite of optimistic predictions of over four percent growth by some of his advisers. Better to predict two or three percent and achieve four percent than vice versa. The Secretary further noted that the U.S. must take into account economic performance in deciding how much help a country needed when allocating its assistance to various aid recipients.
Febres-Cordero rejoined that political concurrence among aid donors and recipients also should be considered. Moreover, he faced an unprecedented alliance between bitterly divided Moscow-line and Maoist-line communists. It was precisely his strong preference for a free market policy which had overcome the communists’ strong internal rivalries. He also faced intervention from Nicaragua, the Soviet Union, and other Communist countries, but he could handle their subversive activities. What he could not handle was a popular reaction to his economic rationalization measures during a transition to a thriving free market economy. The Secretary reiterated his willingness to push for more help for Ecuador on his return to Washington. The initial meeting then turned into a smaller bilateral, with only the President, the Secretary, the Foreign Minister, and Assistant Secretary Motley remaining. 5

383. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 383. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 1
March 1, 1985, 0157Z
Secto 4012. Subject: Memorandum for the President: Ecuador.
1. Secret entire text.
2. Memorandum for: The President
3. From: George P. Shultz
4. Subject: Ecuador
5. I met with President Febres-Cordero in Guayaquil on a three hour stop enroute to Uruguay. I extended your invitation for an official working visit in the second half of 1985 which he immediately accepted and indicated that November would be good for him. 2 We left it that our respective staffs would coordinate a mutually convenient date. I also gave him the agricultural report and read your letter to him in a joint appearance before his press. 3
6. Febres, in his usual forthright, energetic free market manner gave me a quick report of seven months in office. He focused on three main points. First that he was a much watched experiment of a free market society in our hemisphere, secondly he could not do it without our help, and finally that we had not been forthcoming as much as he had hoped.
7. We discussed the various U.S. AID levels in FY 85 and 86 in addition to his efforts with the commercial banks and Paris Club. The net result was a mixed bag as far as our support and I took his points. I will follow up on some of his suggestions such as the Paris Club, PL–480 and possible strategic petroleum reserve purchases of Ecuadorean oil. I did tell him of your determined efforts to cut our deficit and the attendant result on all programs. Overall, I was impressed with his sincerity, unemotional but effective presentation, and this reminded me of our need—if possible—to go the extra mile for one of the few genuine free market Presidents in Latin America.
8. At Febres-Cordero’s request I had a smaller meeting (his Foreign Minister, Tony Motley) with him where he raised the subject of Nicaragua, Cuba and President Betancur of Colombia.
9. Febres-Cordero is one hundred percent in accord with US in seeing the communist danger in our hemisphere. Especially the increasing danger in Nicaragua. He told me that Betancur shared his assessment (a reversal he felt on the part of Betancur whom he has known for many years) and that to avoid the risk of U.S. military intervention in Nicaragua it was necessary to remove the Cuban impact/influence in Nicaragua, thereby isolating Nicaragua, and causing it to wither away. He also mentioned that possibly it might be necessary to address U.S.-Cuban difficulties in order to achieve the Cuban withdrawal from Nicaragua. He then told me that Betancur not only had great insight into Castro but also considerable influence. He also emphasized that he was authorized by Betancur to relay this discussion to me.
10. I really am not sure what to make of this conversation. Febres-Cordero is one of us. He is strongly anti-communist, gutsy and recognizes the danger to all if the Sandinistas consolidate. At the same time, Betancur is known to us as one who flirts with Castro, is persuasive and loves a mediating role. We also know that Castro has recently launched yet another peace offensive and with it come “signals” from all quarters of Castro’s sincerity and determination. I have asked Harry Shlaudeman and Tony Motley to mull this over and report back to me.
Shultz

384. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) to Secretary of State Shultz, Secretary of the Treasury James Baker, and the Director of the Office of Management and Budget (Stockman) 384. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) to Secretary of State Shultz, Secretary of the Treasury James Baker, and the Director of the Office of Management and Budget (Stockman) 1
Washington, July 29, 1985
SUBJECT
FY 1985 ESF for Ecuador (C)
I have been impressed with the free market economic policies developed by Ecuadorean President Febres-Cordero and believe we have a stake in the success of his programs because of the positive influence this would have on other Latin American Countries. 2 I believe that the political support President Febres-Cordero has given to us has also been helpful on issues that are important to US interests—Central America, Cuba, and the increasingly politicized approach to the debt problem. (S)
In terms of strict economic need, Ecuador might not seem in as great a need for American help as other countries. Yet, I believe, that from time to time, it is both a legitimate and effective use of US power to send political signals to governments that are in sync with US goals and values. The Government of Ecuador fulfills that criteria. They have requested that we assist them, and I believe that ESF funding is appropriate for their current needs. (C)
I would appreciate it if the ad hoc group, chaired by William Schneider, that was established to deal with this type of problem could be promptly convened to explore the possibility of securing FY 1985 ESF for Ecuador . 3 (C)
Robert C. McFarlane 4

385. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 385. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M–85–10130 Washington, December 18, 1985
ECUADOR: The Febres-Cordero Administration After One Year
Summary
President Leon Febres-Cordero’s state visit to Washington in January comes at the one-and-a-half year point in his four-year term and at a time when he has made some significant progress on several fronts. 2 In particular, he has made great strides in setting the country on the road to a slow but steady economic recovery by adopting market-oriented strategies to promote entrepreneurial activity, stimulate investment, and revive growth. His economic and financial moves, some of them politically difficult, are already producing positive results in both external accounts and domestic recovery. The chief threat to this economic revival is the weakness of the world market for oil, Ecuador’s chief foreign exchange earner. Febres-Cordero also has launched a vigorous and successful drug enforcement campaign, with Ecuador accounting for 25 percent of the cocaine hydrochloride seized in South America in the first half of 1985. Despite his highly publicized and divisive legislative battles—often leading to charges that he has dictatorial tendencies—the President has consolidated his power base by gaining control of Congress and is close to attaining a majority in the Supreme Court. His principal shortcomings to date—failure to control the fledgling guerrilla organization Alfaro Vive, Carajo—is largely attributable to the military’s inability and unwillingness to develop an effective counterinsurgency capability.
During his scheduled meeting with President Reagan, Febres-Cordero is likely to request substantial increases in US assistance, particularly for counterinsurgency training, and added training and funding for drug enforcement efforts. He probably will argue that, as a strong proponent of democracy and free enterprise and because of his vigorous efforts to combat Communism, drug trafficking and terrorism, the United States should be more forthcoming with aid to what he sees as Washington’s staunchest ally in South America.
[Omitted here is the body of the paper.]

Secret; Noforn; [handling restriction not declassified]. Information available as of December 1985 was used in the preparation of the paper.
2 In a November 29 memorandum to Reagan, McFarlane noted “Shultz’s strong interest in upgrading the Febres-Cordero visit to a State visit,” and wrote: “We believe that President Febres-Cordero of Ecuador merits a State visit. He is one of the few Latin Americans vigorously implementing free market economic policies, has taken a strong stand against the Sandinistas, and has cooperated extensively with the U.S. on narcotics and other important issues. He is an open and enthusiastic friend of the United States. We would like to highlight this cooperation and friendship and strongly support the Department of State and Secretary Shultz’s request that his visit be upgraded from an official working visit to a State visit. The Department of State has requested expedited treatment of this request because of time constraints.” Reagan approved. (Reagan Library, NSC Records, Latin American Affairs Directorate, Country File, Ecuador (12/31/1985–03/31/1986))

386. Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 386. Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 1
Washington, January 8, 1986
SUBJECT
State Visit of Ecuadorean President Leon Febres-Cordero
I. WHERE DOES OUR RELATIONSHIP STAND?
○ Very close since August 1984 inauguration of conservative, businessman/President Febres-Cordero.
○ We helped preserve civilian rule under previous GOE administration, and to this one we stress our commitment to the process and practice of democracy.
○ As promised in his June 1984 meeting with you, Febres-Cordero has pursued market based, growth oriented policies.
○ Febres-Cordero agrees with us on Central America, narcotics and terrorism. — He broke relations with Nicaragua in October after exchanging harsh words with Ortega.
— He has taken a tough posture against narcotics traffickers and an increasingly dangerous local terrorist group.
II. WHAT DO WE WANT?
○ That democracy grow stronger. Febres-Cordero has given questionable interpretations to an admittedly ambiguous constitution. He needs to be reminded of our concern for strong democratic institutions.
○ That Febres-Cordero’s free market approach succeed.
○ That narcotics production be halted.
○ That the Alfaro Vive, Carajo! (AVC) terrorists be defeated.
III. WHAT DOES FEBRES-CORDERO WANT?
○ Anxious to have visible and tangible US support. This State Visit will play well at home. Our record of assistance gives substance to our words of support. — But, given his close affinity with us, Febres Cordero expresses surprise that our aid has not come more rapidly and in greater quantity.
— He has asked for PL 480 Title I to help continue agricultural sector reforms begun with the FY 85 Title I program. OMB and Treasury are resisting.
○ Participation in the U.S. Program for Sustained Growth. Ecuador follows most of the policies we support for debt management and growth. — Febres-Cordero will host a working breakfast for Secretary Baker, Chairman Volcker, myself and heads of international financial institutions in pursuit of this goal. 2
IV. WHAT CAN BE ACHIEVED FROM THIS VISIT?
○ A re-emphasis of US support for the consolidation of democratic institutions and practices in Ecuador.
○ Praise for a small South American country’s courageous effort to implement its market based philosophy 3 in a region long addicted to statist solutions.
○ A sharp contrast between Ecuador, a democracy with a solid market based economic program and a good human rights record, and Nicaragua, a totalitarian state with growing state control of its economy. — Febres-Cordero publicly denigrated the Sandinistas for not holding free elections. Ortega’s critical retort led to Febres-Cordero breaking relations.
1 Source: Reagan Library, NSC Records, Latin American Affairs Directorate, Trip File, Trip Book for Brazil (Tillman) 2/23/1986–02/26/1986 (1). Secret.
2 In telegram 31956 to Quito, January 31, the Department reported on the January 15 breakfast hosted by Febres-Cordero for Shultz, Baker, Volcker, the IMF and IDB Presidents, and the IBRD Acting President, noting “Secretary Shultz said the Ecuadorean program is bound to be successful and Secretary Baker said those present could help encourage a return to voluntary commercial lending.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D860081–0481) In a January 15 memorandum to Reagan, Shultz described the meeting, writing that “Febres-Cordero demonstrated clearly that Ecuador is a country that has already adopted the type of economic program we have been urging on debtor countries. The breakfast meeting will underscore publicly our private support for Febres-Cordero’s efforts to get the commercial banks to renew voluntary lending.” (Reagan Library, George P. Shultz Papers, Box 33, President’s Evening Reading January–March 1986)

387. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 387. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Ecuador 1
Washington, January 23, 1986, 0003Z
21120. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with Ecuadorean President January 13.
1. (S—Entire text).
2. Summary. Secretary met with Febres-Cordero at President’s suite in the Ritz-Carlton Hotel 4:30 PM, January 13. Discussion centered on Ecuador’s domestic politics with particular stress on human rights and terrorism, Ecuador’s economic performance and the Baker Plan, Central America, Ecuadorean relations with Peru and Cuba, and protectionism. End summary.
3. Febres-Cordero began meeting by stressing the importance he places on good relations with the U.S. He effusively praised Ambassador Rondon for his willingness to work closely with the Ecuadorean Government. He said he wanted to describe some of the problems he faces domestically. He has a problem with the press which falsely accuses him of having stopped elections. He said on the contrary he would veto any move by the Congress to extend its term by cancelling elections. He said that although he now has a working majority in Congress, that coalition of votes does not function on every issue. He will be calling for electoral reform, particularly to open up political participation to people who do not belong to political parties. Under the present system only the 20 percent of the populous which belongs to political parties can run for office and this tends to give a leftist tilt to politics. If the Congress defeats his reform proposals, he will ask for approval of his measures in a plebiscite to be held at the same time as the Congressional elections in June.
4. Human rights. The President claims he receives “a thousand letters a week” from as far away as Finland but always asking about the rights of terrorists, never expressing concern about the kidnappings of private citizens by terrorists. Upon arriving in August, Ambassador Rondon stressed the importance of the human rights issue. Febres-Cordero said he subsequently established an office to deal with the human rights question. He assured the Secretary his record will leave no doubt that his government respects and protects the rights of its citizens. The Secretary expressed admiration for Febres-Cordero’s human rights and narcotics policies.
5. Terrorism. The President quickly added that while respecting human rights, his government will be strong in the fight against terrorism within Ecuador and on international scene. Referring to the Isaias kidnapping, he said by refusing to negotiate with the kidnappers he had set in train events which ended with the death of one of his best friends. It was one of the most difficult decisions of his life but if he had not made it, no one in his country would have been safe. He thanked the U.S. for the assistance we have been providing. His government is having some successes in identifying the movements of the AVC hierarchy. The second in command was killed in a fire fight in recent days. The President specifically backs the U.S. effort to stop all international terrorism. He felt the U.S. was not receiving enough backing for its policy, but assured the Secretary that “big U.S. and little Ecuador will stand together”. The Secretary thanked Febres-Cordero for his several messages of support.
6. Central America. The President said he felt Contadora is “agonizing”. Knowing the participants, he had always been skeptical that anything would come out of it. The Secretary agreed that Contadora is in trouble but what must come out of the process is a pluralistic form of government in Nicaragua. The basic problem is that Mexico has difficulty accepting that essential ingredient. It is necessary for us to show that we are doing everything possible to come up with an agreement for peace. Meanwhile, we will continue to help the Contras. We want to do more and it would be better if it can be done out of the public limelight. In a month or so, we will go to Congress seeking new authorizations. We must help the other countries so the healthy economies provide a strong contrast to Nicaragua’s failures.
7. Relations with Nicaragua. The Secretary noted the Sandinistas are being discredited by the connection with the M–19. Unfortunately, President Betancur has trouble acknowledging the link because he started his term in office cast as a peacemaker and has never been able to change his role. Febres-Cordero said that if Betancur broke with Nicaragua, Lusinchi would follow suit. Unfortunately, if the Colombian President does not sever relations now with the clear evidence he has, he will never do it. He undertook to call Betancur to add to the pressures he is under from within his own party. Lusinchi had confirmed to Febres-Cordero during his visit to Caracas that weapons found in the Colombian Palace of Justice were originally given by the Venezuelans to the Sandinistas. The President feels the Sandinistas’ position is shaky. After he called for free elections in Nicaragua and the subsequent break in relations, the Sandinistas felt threatened to the point that they further restricted domestic liberties.
8. Cuba. The President frankly stated that in his initial months in office he had played with Castro and that this tactic did result in lessening Cuban support for Ecuadorean terrorists. But he is aware of U.S. concerns and has instructed his cabinet that there will be no dealings with the Cubans which results in a transfer of technology, specifically he mentioned the shrimp and the petroleum industries. The Secretary expressed his appreciation.
9. Peru. Febres-Cordero said he was perplexed at what Peruvian President Garcia might do. Money is flowing out of Peru to the extent that it is a factor disturbing Ecuador’s free market exchange rate. Garcia is a leftist and his policies are likely to contribute to inflation, unemployment and increasing scarcities. On the other hand, of all Peru’s parties, APRA had been historically the most friendly towards Ecuador and Peruvian Foreign Minister Wagner’s visit to Quito was an historic and positive step. For Ecuador, it would be a major disaster if Garcia’s bad domestic policies led to collapse of democratic government and the return to the belligerent military. The Secretary noted that Garcia does have wide popular support but he is going about things in the wrong way. In contrast, poor Bolivia is trying to straighten itself out and Ecuador has become a bright spot in the region.
10. The program for sustained growth. According to Febres-Cordero, Secretary Baker’s initiative is being distorted. Leftists have painted “No to the Baker Plan” on the walls in Quito. The positive effects of the US initiative are not understood. Unconsciously, Ecuador was already pursuing the type of stringent policy Secretary Baker called for. The President said now he needs help to make his policies show positive results in the short time remaining in his term of office. Businessmen are worried about what will happen after he leaves office. The World Bank is too slow and bureaucratic. It could not understand how cheaply the government has been able to build popular housing. The President felt that the IMF and President De Larosiere understood his policies better. The GOE also had good relations with the IDB. The Secretary said that the purpose of the January 16 breakfast would be to create an atmosphere indicating Ecuador is moving forward and deserves support.
11. The Secretary concluded by saying that President Reagan was looking forward to seeing Febres-Cordero the next day. He assured Febres-Cordero that he would encounter only friends in Washington. The U.S. was proud to be standing at his country’s side. 2
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S–I Records, Secretary Memorandums of Conversation (Memcons), Secretary Haig and Shultz, Lot 87D327, Secretary Shultz (Memcons) October 1985–present. Secret; Immediate; Exdis. Drafted by McLean; cleared by Abrams, McKinley, and Gelbard and S/S–O and S; approved by Shultz. Febres-Cordero made a State visit to Washington January 12–16.
2 In an evening report to Reagan, Shultz described his January 13 meeting with Febres-Cordero, writing: “In my meeting with him today he indicated the strongest possible support for our anti-terrorism strategy and his own determination to halt narcotics trafficking and block terrorist groups in his own country. He related how he was forced to make a difficult decision in refusing to negotiate with terrorists even though this led to the death of a close friend. He is also standing with us on Central America and will be urging Colombian President Betancur to take a bolder stand publicly against the Sandinistas’ aid to Colombia’s M–19. Finally, the Ecuadoran President is anxious to present his country as already having adopted the rigorous economic policies Secretary Baker called for in his Seoul speech. Your meeting tomorrow should be a lively and pleasant exchange.” (Memorandum from Martin to Wigg, January 14; Reagan Library, NSC Records, Latin American Affairs Directorate, Country File, Ecuador (12/31/1985–03/31/1986))

388. Memorandum of Conversation 388. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, January 14, 1986, 10:30–11:45 a.m.
SUBJECT
Meeting Between President Reagan and Ecuadorean President Febres-Cordero (U)
PARTICIPANTS
U.S.
The President
Secretary of State George P. Shultz
Secretary of the Treasury James A. Baker, III
Secretary of Defense Caspar Weinberger
Chief of Staff Donald T. Regan
Vice Admiral John M. Poindexter, Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs
Elliott Abrams, Assistant Secretary for Inter-American Affairs
Fernando E. Rondon, U.S. Ambassador to Ecuador
Jacqueline Tillman, NSC
Ecuador
President Leon Febres-Cordero
Foreign Minister Edgar Teran
Finance Minister Francisco Swett
Ambassador Mario Ribadeneira
The two presidents met privately after the photo opportunity for approximately five minutes. 2 The rest of the party then joined them in the Oval Office. (U)
President Reagan . We’ve been talking about entrepreneurs, small independent businessmen and what they have meant to our economy. It apparently is the same in Ecuador, people building their future by themselves. I met with some minority leaders yesterday 3 who were doing it on their own in public housing. They have been enrolling people to become owners of their own homes and what a difference that makes. (U)
President Febres-Cordero . That’s because its their own property to defend. Small entrepreneurs, small businessmen fight harder for their principles because they have gone up the hard way. (U)
President Reagan . There’s an entrepreneur, a young fellow who works right around the corner. He has a large life size cardboard photo of me and he charges five bucks for people to pose with me. Some have shown these pictures to me and they look very real. (U)
President Febres-Cordero . I saw it myself yesterday. You’ve got a very sporty jacket on (laughter). (U)
President Reagan . I saw one of me with this very attractive young lady, and she was holding my arm and was very pregnant and pointing at me. It made me tremble a little bit (laughter). (C)
President Febres-Cordero . I want to be very candid with you. I met yesterday with my advisers to organize our remarks for this meeting today. I decided to do away with everything that was suggested and made my own personal notes of things I felt should be discussed today with you. (S)
First, I want to express for myself, for my government and for the people of Ecuador our deep and sincere thanks for the help the United States has given us. For the friendship, for the sympathy, from every point of view, that you have made us feel that we are not alone, that our battles are shared by a great nation like the United States. (S)
I would not be sincere if I did not tell you how worried I am about Latin America. I know Ecuador’s problems and many of our problems are common to the other countries. Large countries, small countries, it doesn’t matter, the same patterns appear in all. Look at the Continent. Take South America. Venezuela has good leadership under Lusinchi, but I’m afraid that Carlos Andres Perez is going to be the next president. I was just in Venezuela, and met with him and I wasn’t all that nice to him. I feel that CAP will probably win and we will have problems in Venezuela. Colombia is a sound democracy. Barco and Gomez are good leaders and if either one wins, we won’t have problems. They will give Colombia stronger leadership than my good friend, Belisario Betancur, has given. Peru is in a very delicate point. The terrorists are a big problem in Peru. We have good relations with the Government of Garcia, from the point of view of overall diplomacy. The Secretary of State of Peru visited us recently and this was important because it was the first visit in a long time of a Peruvian official. But I’m very worried about the internal economic problems, the wrong handling of the debt and if terrorism gets out of hand it will affect us and cause large problems for Bolivia. I’m afraid that Peru is going radical. Chile. I confess a personal liking for Pinochet and believe he was backed by the electorate in a popular referendum. But if there were an election now I am afraid that the communists would win or the Christian Democrats, which is almost the same thing. If you investigate the campaign that elected Allende, the Christian Democrat that ran against Allende was so radical, I probably would have voted for Allende. I think Pinochet is sincere about holding democratic elections in 1989. But his lengthy stay in power may throw it to the left. (S)
President Reagan . I thought there were three factions: a moderate group and a communist faction. Are you saying that they are very close together? (S)
President Febres-Cordero . The democratic group is mainly headed by the Christian Democrats. We have had the same experience with our Christian Democrats and are paying the consequences now. I don’t like Christian Democrats. There are some good ones like in Italy or El Salvador, each country can have different kinds, but some are very radical like in Chile. So as the Chilean process moves to elections we have to watch it very closely. In Argentina, Alfonsin is doing an excellent job. They have the strongest middle class in South America and are against the left, so there is hope there. We have good relations with Sanguinetti but his economic problems are very serious. Brazil worries me. I was there in March. I was shocked when I went from the hotel to the Congress building for the inauguration. There were thousands of people waving the communist flag. It scared me to death. The minimum wage is $30 a month and inflation is 230% a month. Sarney is doing a good job but it’s such a large country with so many different regions and such a huge difference between the wealthy and the poor. On Central America, well, you are the expert on that. If I can sell you one idea today that would be that the United States has to look south with great concern. We feel that without the help of the United States, Latin America will get out of hand. (S)
President Reagan . Perhaps we’ve been too involved with Central America. The issues you have raised concern me. When I was at the United Nations in New York, 4 I sat near the Vice President of Bolivia. He told me they had more communists and more narco-traffickers there than anywhere in the hemisphere and asked for our help. We have constraints that come from the past. There is this image of the big colossus in the north and this of course bothers us. We are very concerned about Central America and Nicaragua and are determined to see it through. We are encouraged with the progress the elections have brought to El Salvador. I like to use this as an example when I am speaking and so forth about Central America. In El Salvador you have a government that offered amnesty and appealed to the guerrillas to participate in elections and to settle their differences with peaceful negotiations. And the guerrillas refused. In Nicaragua it is the guerrillas that have offered to lay down their arms and negotiate and the Sandinista government that has refused. It shows the similarity between the Sandinistas and the guerrillas in El Salvador. (S)
President Febres-Cordero . In my notes here I wrote down two things that worry me very much. That is that U.S. citizens are being killed. I am speaking of course about terrorism. We have had terrorism in Ecuador. A life-long friend, a banker, was kidnapped by terrorists and I had to sacrifice him. I feel so close to him that I have two nephews of his with me on this trip. But I believe the sacrifice was worthwhile because if I had negotiated with the terrorists I would only have had to face more kidnappings and more death. There was another case of attempted kidnapping quite recently. Fortunately, the man was armed and shot two of them and his son came out and shot too and they were able to escape. But your agencies are helping us. We now have a team of people equipped to deal against terrorism and put them on the defensive. I’m going to hit them as hard as I can. We are not violating human rights, although you may hear we are. But we are persecuting them. When I leave in 1988 I hope terrorism will be finished in Ecuador and I am making all efforts to that end. And have no doubts that we back up the United States against Libya. I saw Kaddafi on TV and I almost dropped dead. He is crazy! (S)
President Reagan . I saw him on the air too. He was saying we were threatening innocent civilians in their homes with bombs. This from a person who called it a “heroic deed” to kill an 11-year-old child. (U)
President Febres-Cordero . The narco-traffickers are working hand in hand with the terrorists to find armaments and move drugs out. (U)
President Reagan . Are they working together? (U)
President Febres-Cordero . Yes, and we are finding something very interesting. The guerrillas were used as a means of protection for transporting drugs but now we find they are going into the drug business themselves because of the profits. We pushed them underground to make their operations more expensive and inefficient. But it backfired and now they are robbing banks to get money. They need money and they are blackmailing by phone. Unfortunately many pay, the guerrillas get money, then they buy arms and we have problems. But we are fighting hard and we are receiving excellent help from you and are working well together. (C)
President Reagan . We increased our ability to confront terrorists by greatly improving our intelligence collection. We aborted worldwide 126 acts; these were headed off in advance. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . We have been very clear in the press about how we feel. I have said that we will back up any government, not just the United States, against terrorism regardless of how radical they go. (C)
Secretary Shultz . After the attacks in Vienna and Rome, the first message of solidarity and support that I received came from the Foreign Minister of Ecuador. On instructions from the boss, I understand (laughter). (U)
President Febres-Cordero . On Nicaragua and Contadora, I think we are in general agreement. I see Contadora agonizing. I never believed in Contadora, as long as the Mexicans are there. The Mexicans say privately they are all for the gringos but publicly never! (S)
President Reagan . Henry Kissinger passed on this analysis to me also. He said Mexicans will storm against you publicly and drink a champagne toast to you privately. (S)
President Febres-Cordero . Keeping Contadora alive is a stand I take publicly. I met with Ortega in Brazil for 45 minutes and I gave him a really hard time. I told him that I had asked his Counselor to leave and that if he kept it up I was going to tell his Ambassador to leave. I moved to break relations with him, it was my own initiative. I was tired of the drugs and the subversion and so I designed a plan to confront him. I hoped others would break too and I am trying to convince others that the only solution is democratic elections in Nicaragua. In 1984 I wrote to de la Madrid, Lusinchi, Betancur, all of them except Nicaragua, to stop these elections because these elections are a joke, I said. The only way is to isolate them through pressure. So Betancur will play a strategic role if he breaks with Nicaragua because of the weapons. But Ortega hasn’t said anything about me recently. I think Castro told him not to. I spent 16 hours alone with Castro and I told him to stop fooling around with us and I think I have seen an improvement. As I told Secretary Shultz and Ambassador Rondon, I have instructed all my ministers to boycott anything to do with Cuba. 5 Castro has requested some assistance with oil and technology with shrimp and we are riding him along. Ortega was very aggressive against me and suddenly it stopped. I traveled to Venezuela and the Dominican Republic and hit him hard, but he didn’t open his mouth. I think he was pressed by Castro not to fight with me. I think Castro told him that Febres-Cordero was close to Betancur, to Lusinchi, and to let it die out. We need to press the Latin Americans and I am pressing hard to see if they will pressure Nicaragua. (S)
President Reagan . If the OAS only could act. They made those promises about what the revolution was about to the OAS. Yes, it is the boil that needs lancing in the Western Hemisphere. We will not retreat but we have our problems with Congress. The Nicaraguans have developed quite a disinformation network. There is a good example of something that happened several years ago. An American Bishop was trying to get some refugees out. The press here said he was attacked by the Contras. I talked to him when he was in Iowa and asked him about it. He said the Sandinistas attacked the refugees and they had all been rescued by the Contras. (S)
President Febres-Cordero . The problem with the press is that they always change things and always advocate helping leftists. (C)
President Reagan . And we are always to blame. (U)
President Febres-Cordero . Two other things in Latin America. One is the public debt. Castro intelligently tried to improve his image away from subversion and violence in the minds of Latin Americans by saying something people could understand, “don’t pay the debt.” People don’t understand this issue. Mr. Baker’s idea is very interesting but is being mishandled in Latin America. The left is crying “no” to the Baker plan. The Foreign Ministers met recently in Montevideo and I told my Foreign Minister not to go because we don’t know yet what the Baker plan is. We have a feeling about it but it is being distorted in the newspapers. The communist party and labor unions that are controlled by the communists are trying to force me to reject the Baker plan. (S)
Secretary Baker . They are trying to politicize the problem so they want to move it in that direction. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . We will talk tomorrow about the Baker plan. 6 We look to inaugurate the Baker plan in Ecuador. There is one other thing. Sometimes I have the feeling to accomplish certain political things, some of the things we have do, we have to move away from sound economic principles. Housing for the poor is important and so for this, any type of subsidy is justified. I know this is unorthodox, especially coming from us. But you are a head of state and you know that sometimes you have to combine and compromise between economics and politics and we request that you help us so that the impossible can be fulfilled. Our experience is going beyond our frontiers. (C)
President Reagan . We have serious economic constraints ourselves but I want you to know that we are looking everywhere we can to help you succeed in what you are doing. Now that you mentioned housing I remember talking several years ago to D.K. Leonard. He showed me photos of buildings that he could put up at $2,500 a house and putting them up in a week with unskilled workers. Not here, overseas. These were prefab concrete jobs and the houses were attractive and sound. The only skilled workers you needed were for wiring and plumbing. Is D.K. still alive? We ought to find out how it was done because housing is a worldwide problem. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . We can take a shack and clean it up and in five days we can have a decent house that a human being can live in. And it only costs $1,800 with 10–year terms. (C)
President Reagan . That’s really wonderful and very interesting. You should have interrupted me when I was talking about old D.K. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . I was saying to Secretary Shultz yesterday that I thought the Baker plan was an excellent initiative. But I am concerned because it is tangled up with the World Bank as the center of operations. I have serious doubts about their efficiency. It’s hard to get something out of them. If the Baker initiative is to succeed the World Bank will have to move. And that’s going to be hard, it’s like moving a building (laughter). (C)
Secretary Baker . Of course part of the plan is exactly that, faster disbursements from the Bank and this will take reforms. That is part of the proposal. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . The IMF is tough but de Larosiere can talk intelligently. What bothers me is that the Bank doesn’t accept reasons and they impose worse conditions than the IMF. (C)
Secretary Baker . It is complicated and this is what we will be working on. (C)
President Febres-Cordero . I want to leave you with the tranquility of knowing that Ecuador is fully democratic. We have tough elections, but we live by the constitution and we have free speech and free press. The international press claims I stopped elections but I didn’t. Congress did and in June we’ll have elections for the House. I have put forward some constitutional reforms to the Congress which they probably will deny. 7 So I will go to the people but this is a good experience in democracy. To close I wish to thank you for inviting us, for receiving us. Be assured that we have the same goals, democracy and freedom. But a democracy with peace and order. (C)
President Reagan . We are so happy to have you here. We want to help because we believe in you and agree with what you are doing. And think that it is important for the three Americas: North, Central, and South. (U)

389. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 389. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M–86–20017 Washington, April 23, 1986
ECUADOR: The Febres-Cordero Administration and Its Challenges
Summary
During his first one and a half years in office, President Leon Febres-Cordero has demonstrated forceful, often controversial, leadership in implementing conservative policies that frequently have been closely aligned with US interests. He has been especially vigorous in his efforts to reorient and revive the economy. His market-oriented strategies achieved early positive results in both the external accounts and domestic recovery, but the rapid decline in world market prices for oil—Ecuador’s chief foreign exchange earner—now threatens to reverse many of the gains. Febres-Cordero has also launched a dedicated and successful drug enforcement campaign. He has had frequent battles with the legislature over his efforts to expand presidential powers—resulting in charges that he has dictatorial tendencies—and a recent military mutiny 2 tainted his administration with accusations of corruption. 3 His efforts to counter the terrorist activities of the fledgling guerrilla organization, Alfaro Vive Carajo, have lately met with some success due to police penetrations, but he faces growing opposition from leftist political groups likely to win seats in the 1 June Congressional elections.
[Omitted here is the body of paper.]

Defense Minister Luis Pineiros and Gen. Manuel Albuja, the army commander, resigned on Tuesday.
