Febres Cordero told his Army commanders that he would go home before violating his constitutional oath. He ordered the Army to subdue Vargas or replace him as President. The Army opted to support the constitutional order and Febres Cordero states democracy emerged strengthened in Ecuador.” (Reagan Library, NSC Records, Latin American Affairs Directorate, Country File, Ecuador (03/24/1986–01/15/1987))

390. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Carlucci) to President Reagan 390. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Carlucci) to President Reagan 1
Washington, undated
SUBJECT
Today’s Developments in Ecuador
Ecuadoran President Leon Febres-Cordero has been taken hostage by elements of Ecuador’s Air Force. 2 You may recall the successful State Visit last January we had with this pro-U.S., pro-free market Latin American President. 3
Along with the President, the Chairman of the Ecuadoran Joint Chiefs of Staff and the three chiefs of their armed services were taken hostage. These events occurred this morning at Taura Air Force Base near Guayaquil, Ecuador. The renegade Air Force elements are supporters of Air Force General Vargas, who staged a mutiny in March 1986 and is currently in prison. 4 This issue has become a contentious legislative/executive confrontation in Ecuador because the leftist-opposition controlled Congress has voted that Vargas be amnestied, but Febres-Cordero has refused to implement this citing the act unconstitutional.
The State Department has issued a strong statement in support of Ecuador’s constitutional democracy and urged the unconditional release of Ecuador’s democratically elected President. 5 We have requested the White House Press Office to make a similar statement.
In response to a request from the Government of Ecuador, US Ambassador to Ecuador, Fred Rondon, has authorized a US Air Force pilot to fly an Ecuadoran C–130 transport aircraft to ferry Ecuadoran Army Special Forces personnel to a secure area in Guayaquil. 6 Ambassador Rondon has stressed that this action will not take the US pilot to any scene of potential conflict. The Government of Ecuador has also requested a few US hostage rescue experts to advise them on how to proceed. 7 We are currently studying this request. 8

Tillman sent the memorandum to Carlucci for signature under cover of an undated memorandum, along with telegram 590 from Quito, January 16, in which Rondon described the transport arranged for the Ecuadorian Special Forces. Rondon commented: “This will convey strongest possible message to Ecuadorean army that we support army effort to rescue President and uphold constitutional order.”

391. Paper Prepared in the National Security Council 391. Paper Prepared in the National Security Council 1
Washington, undated
Ecuador Earthquake Update
To follow-up on your conversation on Monday with Ecuador’s President Febres-Cordero, NSC staff chaired a meeting to coordinate our response to the disaster and economic crises caused by last week’s earthquake in Ecuador. 2 The following is an update on USG efforts:
Disaster Relief : Between 20–60,000 Ecuadorans have been affected by the earthquakes. Under the direction of The Office of US Foreign Disaster Assistance (OFDA), tents, blankets and other equipment were sent to Ecuador immediately. We have encouraged the Government of Ecuador (GOE) to make an appeal to the UN Disaster Relief Organization so donor countries can make donations of funds to be used for local procurement of goods and services for the disaster relief effort. US Agency for International Development (USAID) specialists are on the ground to assess and assist the GOE with the human dimension of the disaster. Their efforts are proceeding effectively and expeditiously.
Economic Crisis : Rarely does a natural disaster destroy the economic lifeline of an entire nation. But that is precisely what has happened in Ecuador. Prior to the earthquake, Ecuador’s revenues from oil exports had fallen by half over 1986 because of the drop in oil prices, and Ecuador was unable to service its debt in January and February of this year. 3
The most significant damage is to 25 miles of the principal oil pipeline, which extends from jungle oil fields to the Pacific coast. The estimated cost of repairs exceeds $350 million, and Ecuador’s already limited ability to service the international debt will be further reduced. Two major bridges located in the difficult Andean terrain were also destroyed. A US Army Corps of Engineers team has tentatively assessed that the most optimistic estimate to repair the bridges is about four months. The economic loss has enormous consequences: the disaster may cost Ecuador as much as 2 percent of its GDP this year.
Early this week, Treasury officials contacted World Bank and Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) officials to discuss possible responses. We have been supportive of Ecuador’s request for an emergency loan from the World Bank. The IDB may be able to piggyback an emergency loan on the World Bank loan. Eximbank officials have indicated their receptivity to request from US firms for financing for US-sourced exports.
Once US evaluations of the scope of the disaster are complete, we will most likely consider sending a supplement request for Emergency Support Funds (ESF) to the Congress or develop a reprogramming. Either will obviously be problematical both within the executive and with the Congress. A supplemental will require offsets; reprogramming will require shifting funding from other regions. But we must assess the costs of not exercising a leadership role; it is doubtful that other countries will respond adequately if the US does not take the lead.
Political Repercussions : Indications thus far are that the dimensions of this crisis offer Febres-Cordero a unique opportunity to reinvigorate his presidency and rescue his reputation, so damaged after he was taken hostage in January. 4 If Febres-Cordero can put past rancors behind him and maximize the political “truce” that has developed because of the disaster, his presidency may recover. Time will tell. 5

A15) No memorandum of conversation for the March 9 phone call has been found. 392. Action Memorandum From the Under Secretary of State for Arms Control and International Security Affairs (Derwinski) and the Administrator of the United States Agency for International Development (McPherson) to Secretary of State Shultz 392. Action Memorandum From the Under Secretary of State for Arms Control and International Security Affairs (Derwinski) and the Administrator of the United States Agency for International Development (McPherson) to Secretary of State Shultz 1
Washington, April 30, 1987
SUBJECT
ESF for Ecuador After the Earthquake
ISSUE FOR DECISION
Whether to identify $20 to $25 million in FY 1987 ESF to reallocate to Ecuador to help offset the economic effects of the earthquake’s destruction of the oil pipeline.
ESSENTIAL FACTORS
Coming off a year in which declining oil prices had already seriously affected its fiscal revenues and foreign currency earnings, Ecuador has experienced a series of further economic catastrophes in 1987 including coffee price declines, floods, land slides and, most recent, the earthquake in the eastern part of the country which destroyed the country’s vital oil pipeline. Vice President Bush visited Ecuador following the earthquake and would like to see the United States take the lead in providing additional assistance. 2 The NSC is also interested and has chaired three interagency meetings designed to address the extent of the current financing crisis in Ecuador, identify existing sources of funding, and ascertain the possibilities for additional U.S. assistance. 3 Following the last NSC-chaired meeting, the Department prepared a background paper describing the current financial situation in Ecuador (Tab 1). 4
In our estimation, Ecuador faces a 1987 balance of payments financing gap of about $1 billion. Potential contributions from international financial institutions, commercial banks and Paris Club official creditors could provide $900 million, leaving about $100 million still unfunded. ARA believes that a U.S. contribution on the order of $30 million would help encourage provision of the remaining amounts from other donors such as the Japanese. (The USG has provided over $30 million in disaster-related assistance, but this did not deal with the balance of payments gap. We have also allocated $12.5 million in FY 1987 ESF to Ecuador, which was programmed before the earthquake and has yet to be obligated. Thus the $30 million would be additional to these amounts.)
In the NSC-chaired meetings, the Department and AID have taken the position that any decisions and recommendations on possible reallocations of existing foreign assistance funds to Ecuador should be made by you. We have discussed the possibility of providing additional PL 480 wheat; however, the Embassy opposes this option and we could also expect difficulties in the interagency process because it would replace likely commercial sales. We will also deobligate some $5 million in the Ecuador DA pipeline and the current year DA budget for reobligation for reconstruction purposes. Deobligation of DA from other countries in the ARA region and reobligation to Ecuador does not seem to be a likely possibility in this case.
The only remaining possibility is thus reallocation of unobligated balances remaining in FY 1987 ESF. Of the $3,550 million in ESF appropriated under the FY 1987 CR, $1,938 million remains unobligated as of April 1. However, of this amount, $1,711 million is either earmarked or in Central America, accounts which enjoy Congressional support. Of the earmarked funds, all but Cyprus ($15 million), Ireland ($35 million) and Uruguay ($12 million) represent our own highest priorities (Egypt, Pakistan, Philippines, etc.). Any effort to use earmarked money for Ecuador would require consultations under Sec. 614 authority. We doubt we could obtain Congressional support to break either Cyprus or Ireland (based on past experience) and any success with Uruguay is also doubtful, given last year’s experience.
The remaining balance is therefore $227 million, as shown in Tab 2. 5 Over 40 percent of the total is in Africa and another 23 percent in moderate Arab states (Jordan, Morocco, Oman, Mideast Regional). All of it has, of course, already been publicly allocated (some of it due to be obligated imminently) and any reductions will have repercussions with the countries concerned. Moreover, we still remain committed to provide $10 million ESF in support of the South Pacific fisheries treaty. Thus the outlook for available ESF is bleak.
Nonetheless, Febres Cordero will be bitterly disappointed if we cannot reciprocate his vigorous and principled support of policies we favor with the emergency ESF he seeks.
The decision, then, is whether the need to help Ecuador is great enough to merit creating problems elsewhere by reallocating money already committed to other countries. If you believe so, we suggest John Whitehead chair a meeting with the regional Assistant Secretaries and ourselves to sort out where the necessary reductions will be made. If you choose not to reallocate FY 1987 ESF for Ecuador, we will so inform the NSC and continue with our efforts to find other sources of assistance. The principal option for non-ESF assistance appears to be limited to PL 480 food aid.
RECOMMENDATIONS
That John Whitehead chair a meeting with the regional Assistant Secretaries and ourselves to determine how much and from where additional FY 1987 ESF will be reallocated to Ecuador. 6
Alternatively:
That we inform the NSC that the ESF situation is too tight to allow for reallocation to Ecuador and that we will therefore continue to seek other sources of additional assistance. 7
1 Source: Reagan Library, George P. Shultz Papers, Box 58C, Ecuador. Confidential. Sent through Whitehead. Drafted on April 16 and revised on April 28 and April 30 by Boyce; cleared by Gelbard and T, AID/PPC, AID/LAC, and EB. A stamped notation at the top of the first page indicates that Shultz saw the memorandum.
2 See footnote 5, Document 391 .
3 No minutes of these meetings have been found.

393. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 393. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M87–20046 Washington, July 29, 1987
Ecuador: The Danger of a Military Coup
Summary
Next year promises to be a difficult and crucial period for Ecuador’s strained democracy as balloting to elect its next president is scheduled to begin in January. We anticipate that political infighting between a resurgent left and President Febres-Cordero’s weakened government will escalate soon. Although we judge that the Ecuadorean military would prefer not to take power directly, the Army in particular has become increasingly enmeshed in politics—as Febres-Cordero’s most loyal defense—and therefore has gradually grown more politicized. Recent reporting from the Embassy [ less than 1 line not declassified ] suggests that top military leaders fear that the left—which they consider subversive and communist—is becoming increasingly powerful and disruptive.
We do not believe a military coup is imminent, chiefly because the majority of senior officers support Febres-Cordero and apparently see him as effective in countering leftist provocations. Frequent political clashes between the left and the government, however, are a virtual certainty from 10 August 1987 when Congress opens until the next government takes office in August 1988. If the military deems it necessary to step in to defend Febres-Cordero or to restore public order, particularly on a repetitive basis, the potential for a general or a group of senior officers to take power will grow.
[Omitted here is the body of the paper.]
1 Source: Central Intelligence Agency, Office of Support Services (DI), Job 90T00114R: Intelligence Publications Files (1986–1987), Box 1, Folder 38: ALA 87–20046, Ecuador: The Danger of a Military Coup. Secret; Noforn; [ handling restriction not declassified ]. Prepared at Gelbard’s request in the Office of African and Latin American Analysis and coordinated with DO.

394. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 394. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 1
Washington, February 9, 1988, 2151Z
1742. Subject: Meeting with Febres Cordero.
Ref: Quito 1738. 2
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. Summary. President Febres Cordero is very pessimistic about Ecuador’s future. He views Bucaram and Borja as the difference between cancer of the liver and cancer of the pancreas. He is determined to oversee fair elections and transfer power to his successor. He does not believe there is danger of a military coup at this time. End summary.
3. Ambassador met with President Febres Cordero for 45 minutes on February 9. Febres Cordero was extremely warm but gloomy about this country’s future.
4. He said that he had met with the military high command shortly after the election and, presumably, in answer to talk of a coup told the generals that there is absolutely no excuse at the present time for military intervention. He warned the military that a coup at this time would result in devastating consequences for Ecuador, which would be the subject of terrible international isolation. Febres Cordero believes that his military agreed with him.
5. He dismissed chances of a coup before August 10. He doubted that there would be any coup in 1988. He felt that the political and economic situation would have to deteriorate far more significantly before there was serious danger of military intervention.
6. Febres Cordero voiced his disregard for both Abdala and Borja. He felt that Ecuador is confronted by either a cancer of the liver or a cancer of the pancreas. What is the difference, he asked, when either one will kill you.
7. On Abdala, he described him as a mad man capable of anything. He stated that as mayor, Abdala was supported by the smugglers, narcotics traffickers and white-slavers of Guayaquil. He felt that Bucaram would intimidate Ecuador the same way he had intimidated and extorted Guayaquil’s businessmen. The President heaped even more contempt on Rodrigo Borja. He said that Borja has spouted socialism all his political life and is surrounded by leftists. Borja is a weak leader, Febres Cordero continued, and would not be able to resist the socialistic advice of his followers.
8. Febres Cordero foresaw a bloody campaign. He stated that Abdala Bucaram’s party will refuse to allow Borja to enter Guayaquil. This would anger Quito, which would seek to block Bucaram. Lives will be lost, the President stated, and “my government will be caught in the middle” trying to protect the candidates.
9. Whether it is Bucaram or Borja, it is going to be bad news for both Ecuador and the U.S. The U.S. is soon to lose the only real friend it has had in South America, Febres Cordero stated. Wait and see, he predicted either one of those two candidates is going to oppose your policies on debt and in Central America.
10. When the subject turned to Frank Vargas and the imprisoned commandos, Febres Cordero thought that Bucaram and Borja would release the commandos quickly. He doubted very much that the Army would lift a finger to prevent the release of the commandos, because the armed forces’ new leaders would be too busy trying to impress their new President.
11. What might lie in store for Frank Vargas was another matter, because Febres Cordero acknowledged that Vargas would threaten whatever President was foolish enough to reintegrate him into the armed forces.
12. In answer to Ambassador’s question on whether Ecuador might be headed for a new constitution (another way of asking whether a coup is in the cards sometime in the future), Febres Cordero felt it is likely. “That means that there is likely to be a presidential contest between you and Hurtado,” Ambassador stated questioningly. “No,” Febres Cordero answered, “I don’t think I would return.” He accused Hurtado of responsibility for the present constitution and said he is convinced that Hurtado will do all he can to return to power, which means that the present constitution will either have to be reformed or rewritten.
13. Comment: President Febres Cordero’s assessment of the current situation could not have been gloomier, he holds out no hope for a responsible government by either Bucaram or Borja. The first, he believes will be outrightly corrupt, while the second will subordinate everything to the socialistic theories of his Izquierda Democratica (ID) Party. Interestingly, he suspects that the military would go along with either of the 2 presidents, so long as their own nests are feathered appropriately.
14. In a separate conversation earlier in the day, Government Minister Moeller complained about Ecuador’s businessmen, who are in a mad scramble to raise prices because neither Bucaram nor Borja will permit such increases. He said that he is opposed to jailing businessmen who are fueling inflation, as some of the President’s advisors want, and he cannot believe that Febres Cordero would institute price controls. “We don’t know what to do” he lamented.
Rondon
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880114–0055. Confidential; Immediate. Sent for information to Guayaquil and Lima.
2 In telegram 1738 from Quito, February 9, the Embassy reported that Febres-Cordero had indicated that day “that he wished to send people he trusts to Washington urgently in order to brief the Secretaries of State and Treasury on the economic and political stability of Ecuador. The President was very pessimistic about the future of Ecuador but insistent that impartial elections will be held on May 8 and that he will transfer power to his democratically elected successor. He felt that the election campaign will be marked by bloodshed that will test sorely the country’s fragile democracy. He also feels beset by financial difficulties and is anxious for the U.S.’ understanding and help.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880113–0826)

395. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 395. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 1
August 11, 1988, 0612Z
Secto 15096. Subject: Memorandum for the President.
1. Memorandum for the President.
From: George P. Shultz.
Subject: My day in Ecuador, August 10.
2. Today I attended President Borja’s inauguration in Ecuador and sat through his two-and-a half hour inaugural address. 2 His speech was a bigger bomb than the one that hit my motorcade in Bolivia, 3 and Fidel Castro himself was sitting up there on the platform consulting his watch and muttering that it would never end.
3. During the day I had a series of interesting meetings. The Chilean Foreign Minister said their plebiscite was on track, but I told him we’d like to see it be truly fair, including fair access to TV for the opposition. I told Columbia’s Pres. Barco how impressed all Americans are with his brave fight against the deadly drug trafficker-guerrilla alliance. And Portugal’s Pres. Soares told me he had met with Castro and was moderately optimistic that a settlement in Angola might be forthcoming.
4. I also had a good private meeting with Borja and lunch with his economic team. 4 He and they seemed serious and well-intentioned, and I wished them the very best. The extreme leftist head of the Congress commissioned a mural for the hall where the inauguration was held. And the painter—another leftist who was Castro’s host there—included a Darth Vader-like figure with the letters “CIA” on his helmet. Borja apologized and noted he couldn’t control what Congress had done. And I told a press conference that the message of the mural was insulting to the US, but that if a bomb hadn’t driven us out of Bolivia, a mural wasn’t going to keep us away from this inauguration in Ecuador. 5 A lot of Ecuadorians agree, and one Quito paper editorialized today that “What happened is damnable because it shows in both the artist and the commission-giver a skewed knowledge of history—product of a generalized anti-American prejudice.”
5. But while Borja talked sense privately, his inaugural address seemed to collect every bad idea of the last fifty years. (Maybe that’s why it was so long: That’s a lot of territory to cover.) He began with a very good section on the crucial importance of democracy, but went on to a strange pastiche of intellectual pretension, economic illiteracy, and an endless recitation of statistics. By the time he finished, his popularity rating had probably dropped ten points.
6. One final point: Quito is smashingly beautiful. A city of hills and valleys 9,600 feet up in the Andes, it is clean, unmarred by visible poverty, and with enough of the old colonial heritage visible to remind you of Spain. Borja is a social democrat, and I left hoping he, like Mitterrand, will drop the ideology and do what works to help the people of that lovely land.
Shultz
1 Source: Reagan Library, George P. Shultz Papers, Box 22C, 1988 June–August, Memoranda for President Ronald Reagan. Secret; Immediate; Nodis; Eyes Only. Sent from the Delegation’s aircraft. Shultz travelled to Quito to meet with President-elect Borja, August 10.
2 In telegram 6140 from Quito, May 9, the Embassy reported the unofficial results of the May 8 presidential election with Borja winning by 190,000 votes. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880398–0684) In telegram 10567 from Quito, August 11, the Embassy sent an overview of Borja’s inaugural address, which “emphasized the theme ‘peace and unity’ but called upon the nation to make considerable sacrifice to pull Ecuador out of its ‘chaotic’ economic situation.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880705–0315)

396. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 396. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 1
Quito, August 11, 1988, 2238Z
10566. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with President-elect Rodrigo Borja.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary: President-elect Borja warmly welcomed the Secretary to Quito and expressed his hope for a close and productive relationship between our two countries. He stated that his administration would take a hard line against terrorism and narcotics, and that he welcomed continued mutual cooperation in these areas. Borja requested the Secretary’s assistance in avoiding a downgrading of Ecuador’s credit worthiness at forthcoming ICERC review, and asked for a 50 million dollar bridge loan to help the new government overcome a difficult foreign exchange crisis.
3. The Secretary expressed U.S. desire to continue the constructive relationship, and the willingness to reciprocate the Borja government’s approach toward that relationship. Regarding the 50 million dollar bridge loan, the Secretary noted that this is a decision for the Secretary of the Treasury, and approval would depend on having in place the necessary arrangements that would guarantee repayment of the loan. The Secretary noted that he would be making a public comment on the insult to the U.S. contained in the new mural at the Ecuadorian Congress, but that he would not allow the mural to deter him from attending the inauguration at the Congress. End summary.
4. The Secretary met with President-elect Rodrigo Borja for 50 minutes on August 10. Ecuadorian participants, in addition to the President-elect, were the incoming Minister of Foreign Relations Diego Cordovez, Minister of Government Andres Vallejo, Monetary Board President Abelardo Pachano, Central Bank Manager Jose Morillo, Secretary General for Administration Washington Herrera, the Deputy Minister for Foreign Affairs Mario Aleman, and Ambassador Jose Antonio Correa representing the Foreign Ministry’s Office of Protocol. The U.S. delegation included, in addition to the Secretary, Ambassador Holwill, Assistant Secretary Abrams, Assistant Secretary Redman, Deputy Assistant Secretary Gelbard, DCM Eisner (notetaker) and interpreter Van Reigersberg.
5. Borja initiated the conversation with a warm welcome to the Secretary and his presence at this democratic celebration of the people of Ecuador. He noted that those coming to the inauguration represented a wide spectrum of countries and ideologies, and their presence in Quito was reflective of the new Ecuadorian Government’s desire for pluralistic relations and its tolerance of diverse viewpoints. Borja continued that his government would seek the most cordial relations with the U.S. on the basis of a common democratic vocation and friendship. He looked forward to strengthening commercial ties, and to working out equitable solutions to conflicts in such key areas as foreign debt.
6. The Secretary expressed appreciation for Borja’s warm welcome, and delivered to the President-elect a letter from the President expressing good wishes for the new administration. 2 He noted that his presence here as a special representative of the President was in support of freedom, the rule of law and democracy. These were the values that guided the U.S., and they were the basis of any just society. Differences of opinion would always exist between the U.S. and Ecuador. Mutual respect would be a key: The U.S. will frankly express its views with the intention of promoting common interest and working through differences. As friends, we are prepared also honestly to agree to disagree.
7. The Secretary noted the positive and mutual effort of the U.S. and Ecuador in the fight against narcotics and terrorism. These forces can tear a country apart, as we have seen in Peru and Colombia. The U.S., the Secretary said, is willing to continue to help Ecuador in these areas.
8. Referring to the area of economic cooperation, the Secretary stated that he looked forward to the opportunity to discuss over lunch these issues with Borja’s economic team. 3 He was interested in their views, noting that the decisions made in the first few weeks of an administration are often critical to eventual success or failure. It is easier to make tough decisions during an administration’s early honeymoon period, and then to enjoy the fruits of these policies three years or so down the road. The Secretary noted that it was a different age in which we lived—an age in which information was the key. Recalling a recent conversation in Rio de Janeiro, he noted that open and democratic societies were those best able to take advantage of this reality.
9. The Secretary noted that the U.S. is prepared to reciprocate the warmth and quality of the relationship that the Borja government wishes. The U.S. will respect Ecuador’s decisions. The message conveyed by the international media to the American people—by the presence in Quito of Daniel Ortega and Fidel Castro and by the new insulting mural on the walls of the Congress—was of a government turning sharply to the left. The Secretary noted that, in his own statements to the press, he was expressing that Ecuador is a friendly, democratic country that believes in the same values of freedom. His visit was an opportunity to pay respect to those shared values. The Secretary noted that he had been advised not to go to Congress because of the offensive mural. Despite the insult, he would go to the inauguration to underline U.S. support for Ecuador’s democratic transition. The Secretary noted that he would have to make a statement to the press, however, regarding the insulting mural.
10. Borja stated that he had no doubt about the U.S. support for democratic regimes in Latin America. There were, however, three threats to democracy in the region. The Secretary had mentioned the first two—terrorism and drug trafficking. His administration would confront these threats with a strong hand, fully aware of the danger they pose to Latin American democracy. There was, however, a third danger not mentioned by the Secretary—the foreign debt. Borja stated that he had inherited a difficult domestic situation. He would need the U.S. comprehension and the Secretary’s personal help in working out a non-traditional approach that addresses the debt issue without destabilizing Ecuador and undercutting popular support for democracy.
11. Specifically, Borja stated that he had two requests to make of the Secretary:
— Ecuador hoped for his support to avoid a down grading of the country’s credit worthiness at the forthcoming inter-agency country exposure review committee review. A “value impaired” determination would cause serious problems for the new administration in its effort to renegotiate its debt with the private banks.
— Secondly, Borja noted that his administration needed a bridge loan of 50 million dollars. He was assuming office with negative foreign exchange reserves and with heavy food import needs. He stated that the problem, in part, was deliberately caused by the outgoing administration which wished to portray his government in the worse possible light by forcing a major devaluation of the local currency during his first weeks in office.
12. Borja noted that his party, the Democratic Left (ID), was a moderate leftist and social democratic organization with ties to the socialist international. He, himself, was a Vice President of SI. The U.S. could be assured that, notwithstanding the negative image in some of the press, his administration would follow a moderate and democratic program. His administration had no intention of nationalizing any private firms because it knew that this was inefficient and lowered both the quality and quantity of production. Rather, his party’s roots were firmly planted in the same soil as those of Mario Soares of Portugal and Felipe Gonzalez of Spain. The party’s support came from the lower classes of Ecuador, and the Democratic Left Party was a bulwark against radical, communist efforts to dominate the country.
13. The Secretary stated that Mario Soares and Felipe Gonzalez were very close and good friends. I am a pragmatic man, the Secretary continued, and, while I have my own philosophy, I have found that I can work best with those interested in solving common problems rather than indulging in dogmatic posturing. The Secretary noted that former Ambassador to Portugal Frank Carlucci had worked constructively with Mario Soares in benefit to both the U.S. and Portugal.
14. Borja expressed his pleasure in the Secretary’s having been “vaccinated” against the bad information on the Socialist International and its members. He noted that the continued existence of the ID was central to stability in Ecuador. The Secretary agreed and noted, looking around the room at the ID Ministers, that it looked to him that the party was in no danger of disappearing.
15. Recalling an earlier conversation with Felipe Gonzalez, the Secretary noted that the Spanish Prime Minister had told him how he had learned from Mitterrand’s mistakes in France. Gonzalez avoided the pitfalls involved in trying to translate a strong electoral victory into sweeping socialistic reforms. Gonzalez had taken a pragmatic program, and Spain was now booming economically. Mitterrand had had the courage to admit his mistakes, and to reverse course before it was too late.
16. Borja expressed his amazement that there were so little differences in the way he and the Secretary saw the world. He had expected many more differences, but now concluded that the only distinction between the two was whether golf or tennis was the better game.
17. Turning to the discussion of foreign debt, the Secretary noted that there was general recognition that this was a serious international issue. It had been a central theme during his meetings in Brazil, 4 Uruguay, 5 Argentina 6 and Bolivia 7 had been the subject of general remarks that he had made in Montevideo. 8 He would be pleased to provide to Borja’s economic team a copy of his remarks. The Secretary noted that, while countries would have to take tough measures, all the benefits of such reforms could not be restricted to creditors alone; incentives for Ecuadorians must also exist. At the same time, the Secretary stated the Borja regime is coming to power at a good time—the worse is over regarding the fall in oil prices and, hopefully, the nation would not suffer another damaging earthquake. Non-traditional exports were positive. With good economic management, Borja would be in a good position to argue for re-scheduling. Peru had started off by walking away from any solution to the debt problem and this had worked against a successful economic program. Working within the system, the Secretary stated that Borja should be in a good position to challenge the other members of the system to work with Ecuador in good faith.
18. Regarding the bridge loan, the Secretary noted that this was a matter within the competence of the Secretary of Treasury. As a former Secretary of the Treasury, he respected that Department’s preeminence in this area. The Secretary, however, stated that it would be helpful for Borja and his economic team to understand the concept behind a bridge loan. Fundamentally, it was a link between two piers, the latter pier an agreed and convincing program that guaranteed that the bridge loan would be repayed. As an example of a firm pier, the Secretary noted the recent Argentine agreement to undertake economic reforms that would release a pending World Bank loan. On the basis of this Argentine-IBRD understanding, the U.S. could offer a bridge loan to cover the period until the World Bank loan can be disbursed.
19. Borja expressed interest in the continuation of the economic discussion during the Secretary’s lunch with his economic team. He hoped that the Secretary could discuss further the length of the bridge. The Secretary said he looked forward to the discussion, but that the focus should be on the strength of the press not on the bridge’s length.
20. Borja noted that he would like to mention the “small and big” problem of the mural at the Congress. He had only received information of the insult to the U.S. at a late date and had taken all efforts within his power to change the mural. He had had some qualitative changes made, but not all that was desired. It was hard to work with an internationally prominent and difficult artist. Borja stated that he very much appreciated the Secretary’s attendance at the inauguration, and expressed hope that the mural would not cast a shadow over the good relations he desired with the U.S.
21. The Secretary took his leave of the President-elect, again noting his intention to emphasize to the press that his presence in Ecuador was evidence of the good and warm relations the U.S. wanted with a fellow democracy. The meeting Ended.
Holwill

397. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 397. Telegram From the Embassy in Ecuador to the Department of State 1
Quito, October 26, 1988, 2017Z
13627. Subject: Meeting with President Borja, October 25.
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. I met with President Borja on October 25 for an hour’s general conversation, ranging from bilateral questions and adjustment policy to whether Cubans would be involved in his literacy campaign. (They won’t be.) 2 He displayed an impressive grasp of Ecuadorian/Latin American realities, criticizing both Castro and Pinochet. He went on at length to explain his policies and his personal style.
3. I opened by admitting that we did not yet know how the October 24 ICERC debt review had come out (see septel) 3 and I congratulated Borja on the efforts of various ministers in responding to our concerns about his government’s debt policy. 4 I added that I looked forward to dealing with them regarding the adjustment program to assure that the gains made on August 30 would endure. Doing so would facilitate disbursement of the dols 6.5 million under the FY 87 ESF.
4. Responding to my comment that this effort was designed to cooperate rather than to “dictate” any policy to them, Borja explained—somewhat passionately and in considerable detail—that he was an unusually independent person who literally “could not accept” another person or another country telling him what to do. He claimed he had been this way “even with my father when I was four years old.”
5. Following this intense response, we touched the Texaco incident and I urged him to consult with us in advance on such matters in order to avoid frictions. 5 He comfortably acknowledged that the presentation of the decision had been botched by the GOE, and said they had learned the need for better communications. In any case, He said Texaco had been a good partner and he hoped the company would accept the GOE’s offer of a new “concession”. Comment: An extraordinary word from him, which he subsequently repeated. End comment.
6. We discussed how relations could be improved, and Borja expressed satisfaction that agrement had just been received for Jaime Moncayo as his Ambassador to Washington. I knew of his concern that we believed that 800 Cubans were to be imported for a literacy program. I preempted his denial of that report by explaining that I had traced down the story and had learned that a UNESCO mission on literacy had included two Cubans and two Nicaraguans. Borja thanked me for making this effort, saying that he has begun to see how such rumors were hurting the USG-GOE relationship. He emphatically said that “they’ve already left Ecuador.” He categorically rejected any foreign participation in the upcoming campaign itself. Borja seemed to want to dispel the idea that he was sympathetic to communism. He commented that he tried to be even-handed in his international dealings, “but frankly I don’t enjoy my contacts with Russians—their journalists always ask leading questions and their diplomats try to trap you.”
7. In another vein, Borja said that he had tried to persuade labor unions that the government was defending their interests with his anti-inflation program. “On the other hand, I wouldn’t deceive them” with exaggerated promises, so there will be a national strike (on November 24) “and we’ll hold the line on our policies.” I said that we had reported positively on the GOE’s tight and effective monetary policy; he responded that bank reserve requirements would be hiked again in coming weeks. He bitterly criticized the Febres Cordero government for tolerating massive dishonesty in its exchange-rate regime, and noted that the new rate mechanism resulted in a reduced spread between the trade and free rates (now about 80 sucres, down from over 250 three months ago).
8. Comment: I was surprised by Borja’s knowledge of the details of economic policy and his responsiveness on bilateral matters. This implies both that he is personally involved in the economic programs and that it is not likely to change greatly if there are personnel changes in the cabinet. Also interesting was his explanation of personal style; he approaches the office like a manager, not an autocrat. It was anything but “l’etat c’est moi.” He also said he would try to come to our election-night party, and obviously warmed to a bit of inside gossip on U.S. political developments.
Holwill

The economic emergency program announced in late August contains measures needed to begin stabilizing Ecuador’s economy. The tone of internal political discourse—often vitriolic in the past—has been softened, the GOE leadership has listened to divergent opinions, and Borja has demonstrated a commitment to reduce corruption in government and to respect human rights. He has also moved to establish cooperative executive-military relations, to stabilize the labor front, and to support the police in anti-narcotics efforts. At the same time, the new president has had to respond to strong pressures from radicals within his own party and from other extremist elements during his first sixty days.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880916–0108)

413. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State

398. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 398. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, July 28, 1981, 1600Z
2862. Subject: Ambassador Middendorf’s visit to Paraguay.
1. (Confidential—Entire text.)
2. Begin summary. During his July 22–24 visit Ambassador Middendorf was given an extraordinarily warm welcome as the first high-ranking representative of the Reagan administration to visit Paraguay. President Stroessner returned early from a trip to the Chaco expressly to receive him. In that meeting as in those with the Foreign Minister and several other senior GOP officials the Ambassador emphasized the high priority which the administration places on improved relations with Latin America including the Southern Cone and specifically Paraguay, our development plans and hope for support in the troubled Caribbean Basin, the need for cooperation against international terrorism, OAS budget concerns, and the importance of the private sector’s role in national and regional development. On all these subjects he was assured of Paraguayan support. Ambassador Middendorf also visited a major OAS/IDB rural development project, and met with a group of private businessmen. Press coverage of the visit was extensive and uniformly favorable. End summary.
3. Meeting with the President. President Stroessner returned early from a visit to the Chaco region especially to receive Ambassador Middendorf, accompanied by Ambassador Lane, for nearly an hour early on July 24. Ambassador Middendorf conveyed the warm greetings of President Reagan and Secretary Haig to the President and people of Paraguay, and their intention to give the highest priority to good relations with the countries of Latin America. The President expressed his great appreciation of these greetings, reciprocated them, and said that close relations with the U.S. are of historical importance to Paraguay. He recalled that Paraguay had joined with the U.S. in sending troops to the Dominican Republic in 1965 in its desire to help avoid another Cuba in Latin America. Paraguay is a homogeneous, firmly anti-communist country which seeks liberty with order. This approach has brought good results to the country which is developing rapidly. Ambassador Middendorf responded that the U.S. values its close, historical ties with Paraguay, appreciates its support in the Dominican crisis, and recognizes the rapid growth and stability of this country with a minimum of social disparities. He praised the export-oreinted, private sector orientation of Paraguay which has been emulated by other countries. In this connection he stressed the contribution that American companies like Allis-Chalmers (a major bidder on the Yacyreta hydroelectric project) can make in the development of Paraguay and as “goodwill ambassadors.” As illustrative of the new U.S. approach to the developing world he provided copies of his address on “The Reagan Administration and Economic Development in the Third World” and USTR’s “statement on U.S. trade policy” which the President said he would read carefully. 2
4. Ambassador Middendorf reviewed briefly for the President our hopes for cooperative action to help in the Caribbean Basin, 3 not as an anti-Castro effort but rather to strengthen the economies of that region. He said we hope for the support of Paraguay and other countries of the hemisphere in this effort. Stroessner replied “of course”, and went on to elaborate his strong views on the danger of communism and the need to deal with it firmly. Ambassador Middendorf said that we will keep GOP informed on Caribbean Basin planning and that we will wish to consult with Paraguay and other countries as it progresses.
5. Noting a reference by President Stroessner to communist support of terrorism, Ambassador Middendorf said that the U.S. believes strongly that terrorist attacks against independent countries must be stopped and that nations must cooperate to this end. The U.S. will do our share. Stroessner responded that this is necessary since communism and Moscow are against us. He added that the communists kill people and then invoke human rights to defend themselves as in Nicaragua and Cuba. He added that he supports human rights and that his government doesn’t kill anyone. Indeed it protects its citizens. As a result Paraguayans are better off. The only ones who criticize him are communists. He then mentioned once observing communist propaganda in the lobby of a hotel in San Francisco. Ambassador Middendorf commented that in a democracy like the United States such manifestations are of course permitted but the communists are a fringe group of no real importance.
6. Observing that the administration wants improved relations with the countries of the Southern Cone, Ambassador Middendorf referred to the change of policy on votes in the MDBs. 4 President Stroessner replied that his government is very pleased with the policies of the President and Secretary Haig. He went on to criticize the role of a former Ambassador (clearly Ambassador White) in Paraguay, particularly for his intimate association with the unrecognized opposition parties; while avoiding direct criticism of the former President, Stroessner then observed that some officials around Carter benefited communism. Avoiding any direct response to those remarks, Ambassador Middendorf said only that President Reagan and his administration have the fullest confidence in Ambassador Lane.
7. Ambassador Middendorf expressed U.S. appreciation for Paraguay’s support on efforts to reduce the OAS budget and to effect a better sharing of the organization’s costs, two thirds of which the U.S. now bears. Stroessner replied that our concerns on the budget are warranted, but that the U.S. must not relinquish its firm position of leadership on which small countries threatened by communism, like El Salvador, depend. He concluded the discussion by expressing his strong personal opinion that the Governments of South Africa and Taiwan deserve our support because they are firmly anti-communist. He said we must not support SWAPO and allow another Rhodesia. Ambassador Middendorf made no comment.
8. Meeting with Foreign Minister. In a meeting with Foreign Minister Alberto Nogues on July 23, Ambassador Middendorf stressed the high priority which the President and Secretary of State place on improved relations with Latin America. Nogues expressed his appreciation of this policy and said he very much hopes to call on Secretary Haig in Washington prior to the UNGA. 5 Middendorf promised to look into the possibilities for such a meeting but pointed out the scheduling problems. He then proceeded to deal at length with the issues of the Caribbean Basin Plan, international terrorism, OAS budget matters, and the importance of the role of the private sector in development. Nogues took note of the developmental as distinct from anti-Cuban thrust of the new initiative in the Caribbean, but stressed Paraguay’s particular concern with Cuba’s disturbing role throughout Latin America. He welcomed Middendorf’s promise of future consultations with the GOP as Caribbean Basin planning progresses, and expressed confidence that Paraguay will be receptive and supportive. On terrorism, Nogues agreed that international cooperation is indispensable—as is U.S. leadership. He said that the Stroessner government has sought to provide peace and stability to permit development. Despite past political differences, Paraguay has always been a loyal friend of the U.S. Paraguay shares and attempts to emulate U.S. democratic ideals, but it must be recognized that some countries are able to reach those goals faster than others due to special historical and political realities. When Ambassador Middendorf mentioned the recent change of U.S. voting policy in the MDBs as a recognition of human rights progress in Paraguay, Nogues coupled appreciation of that decision with the statement that Paraguay should really never had been included in the same category with the other Southern Cone countries in the first place since it has fortunately avoided their political problems and is perfecting its democratic institutions.
9. Referring to OAS projects in Paraguay, Ambassador Middendorf said the U.S. welcomes GOP efforts and support to reduce the OAS headquarters bureaucracy in order to continue sound programs in the field, and to reduce the U.S. share of the normal budget by 1 percent per year. He added that the administration is seeking a $1 million increase in voluntary contributions to the OAS this year. Nogues responded positively, and commented that the headquarter’s bureaucracy is like a cancer.
10. Ambassador Middendorf praised Paraguay’s emphasis on the private sector which has proved a model for development. Nogues agreed that the GOP’s philosophy favors the free market and private enterprise. Both were of the opinion that good business relations are important in international relations. Middendorf said he was pleased at the prospects for U.S. business in Paraguay, and emphasized that Allis-Chalmers is an important and influential company. Minister Nogues replied that Allis-Chalmers has excellent prospects of winning the turbine contract at the Yacyreta project, and that in Paraguay there is no complex against “Yankee imperialism.” Middendorf gave the Minister copies of his speech on “The Reagan Administration and Economic Development in the Third World” and the USTR statement on U.S. trade policy. The conversation concluded with a discussion of Paraguay’s potential as world breadbasket, its projects to increase production and transport facilities for soybean and other foodstuffs, and complementary OAS projects to benefit small farmers.
11. Meeting with businessmen. On July 23, Ambassador Middendorf met for coffee at the residence with twelve prominent local U.S. and Paraguayan business executives. He described the Reagan administration’s objective of maintaining close and friendly relations with Paraguay and all other nations in the region. He also explained the Caribbean Basin initiative, and emphasized the administration’s belief that the private sector should continue to play a leading role in the economic development of the region. See septel for report of the businessmen’s comments concerning the problems of doing business locally. 6
12. Visits to OAS/IDB project. In addition to briefings on OAS and IDB projects in Paraguay which he received at their respective local offices, Ambassador Middendorf made a field trip on July 23 to an integrated rural development project in the Department of Paraguari (about one hour’s drive southeast of Asuncion) which was designed by the OAS and will be financed by a $31 million IDB loan in cooperation with the GOP. The project is aimed at improving agriculture, developing small byproduct industries, and providing service infrastructure (roads, electricity, health, education) for some 60,000 people in the project area. Ambassador Middendorf spoke to both local and OAS/IDB officials to emphasize the importance of timely implementation, cost-control and the multiplier effect of private sector involvement to assure the future success of this and other projects.
13. Other activities. Other activities in Ambassador Middendorf’s crowded schedule included a supper hosted by Ambassador Lane at which presidential confidant and counselor Conrado Pappalardo presented a long and highly partisan account of Paraguay’s troubled political history. Ambassador Middendorf was able deftly to underscore the legitimacy of the non-communist opposition in democratic development and the seriously adverse impact of allegations of widespread corruption—whether well-founded or not—on Paraguay’s image abroad. At a luncheon in his honor hosted by the Foreign Minister and attended also by the Ministers of Interior, Industry and Commerce, and Education as well as prominent Colorado Party legislators, Middendorf heard further discourses on Paraguay’s political situation and economic prospects including its encouragement of foreign—especially U.S.—investment. In this regard the Minister of Industry and Commerce noted that no foreign enterprise has ever been expropriated in Paraguay. In response to reaffirmations of Paraguay’s steadfast anti-communist stance and friendship for the U.S., Middendorf again emphasized U.S. friendship for Paraguay, support for this country’s private sector emphasis in national development, and determination to deal with the threat of communism in Latin America. He again noted the important role that a non-communist opposition can play in a democratic society. In both these affairs, Middendorf reiterated U.S. support for Allis-Chalmers. In his final activity, Ambassador Middendorf attended the formal inauguration of the fourth international cattle show as the special guest of President Stroessner.
14. Press coverage. Ambassador Middendorf’s visit received wide coverage in the press, with front page photos and articles appearing in all major dailies. There was considerable conjecture before and during the visit as to its purpose and its meaning. An editorial in government-line La Tribuna published the day before Middendorf’s arrival stated that the visit showed “the importance that the U.S. gives to relations with our country” and that it was a reward for Paraguay’s constant support of U.S. foreign policy. It was also anticipated that from this improvement in political relations “mutual economic benefits” could follow. Other papers echoed the thought that the visit signaled an improvement in U.S.-Paraguayan relations. Speculation that the Ambassador was on a secret mission to orchestrate the isolation of Cuba was muted, probably as a result of his strong denial carried in wire service reports from Buenos Aires.
Lane

3 See Document 318, footnote 2 .
4 In telegram 2582 from Asuncion, July 8, the Embassy reported that Paraguayan officials had been informed of the U.S. decision to approve an MDB loan for Paraguay based on “progress noted in our 1980 annual human rights report of Paraguay.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810318–1024)

399. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Paraguay 399. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Paraguay 1
Washington, August 28, 1981, 0213Z
230020. Subject: Paraguayan Foreign Minister’s meeting with the Secretary.
1. (C) Entire text.
2. Summary. At a fifty minute meeting, August 25, Foreign Minister Nogues expressed President Stroessner’s and his own admiration for the President and Secretary, their satisfaction that relations are now much better than previously, and their hope that the United States will fully reciprocate Paraguay’s historic position as a faithful ally. The Secretary responded by emphasizing the convergence of our interests and viewpoints and discussing the Soviet/Cuban threat to the hemisphere. He also mentioned the residual problem of human rights and he urged that the GOP do what it can to ensure prompt award of the turbine contract for Yacyreta to Allis-Chalmers. In departing, the Nogues party commented almost as one that this meeting constituted a dramatic change from recent previous encounters. End summary.
3. Participation at the meeting included Lopez Escobar, Conrado Pappalardo and the GOP’s UN Ambassador for the Paraguayans, Gonzalez Arias, Ambassador Enders and ARA/SC Director Service for the Department. Nogues began by expressing his satisfaction and that of President Stroessner with the fact that this meeting was taking place and, more generally, with the course of US/Paraguay relations since President Reagan assumed office. Stroessner admired the Secretary both as a soldier and as a statesman. Nogues then gave a brief summary of the accomplishments of Paraguay under Stroessner. He suggested that Paraguay might well serve as a model for other Latin American countries and said his government was concerned that the Marxist threat, particularly in Central America, was preventing stable and peaceful development elsewhere in the hemisphere. Nogues concluded this introductory portion with the assertion that Paraguay has always been a faithful ally of the US and that it asks, indeed claims, a reciprocal friendship from us. He said he had made the same points to Secretaries Kissinger and Vance but had wanted to make this special trip to Washington to express personally to the Secretary the interest of his government in the closest possible relations.
4. The Secretary replied by noting that there is a great convergence of viewpoints and interests between Paraguay and the US. He said that he had been following events in Paraguay since the early 1960’s and that he is an admirer of Stroessner. The Secretary discussed briefly the Soviet/Cuban effort to take advantage of long standing economic and social problems in the hemisphere and assured Nogues that this administration is determined to prevent the success of those efforts. He noted in this respect that persons who have the wisdom and courage to speak out on the problem are often subjected to very harsh criticism from some sectors. The Secretary asked that Nogues convey to President Stroessner this administration’s absolute desire to move ahead in improving relations, noting that some steps have already been taken.
5. The Secretary then raised Yacyreta, urging that the GOP do whatever it could to ensure a decision prior to the expiration of the EXIM credit terms in September. He said this was important to both the Paraguayan and Argentine Governments in order to avoid extra costs; and he asked if there was anything we could do to help.
Nogues assured the Secretary that the GOP fully supports the Allis-Chalmers bid but that the matter has been tied up for more than a year because of problems in Argentina and because a decision must first be made on contracts for the civil works.
6. The Secretary described human rights as a “residual problem” which we will address as one friend speaking to another and through diplomatic channels. He said his own preference would be to use the term “personal liberties” rather than human rights because the very term human rights has unfortunately become loaded with emotional and political connotations. In addressing human rights matters, it is important that we not lose sight of the security concerns that the countries of this hemisphere share.
7. In response to a question from the Secretary on the extent of the security threat within Paraguay, Nogues noted that his country had been the first to break with Cuba, that the Cubans had assisted an attempted invasion twenty years ago from Argentina, that Havana was behind the Somoza assassination, and that propaganda continues, but that the GOP is on top of the situation. Stroessner firmly believes that the communists never sleep and that therefore he and others in the GOP are always on the alert.
8. The meeting concluded with reiteration of expressions of good will and certainty that relations will improve steadily. As they were departing, the Paraguayans commented that this meeting marked a very sharp contrast with the recent past. They were delighted with their reception and the length of the meeting.
Haig
1 Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S–I Records, Secretary Memorandums of Conversation (Memcons), Secretary Haig and Shultz, Lot 87D327, Secretary Shultz (Memcons) August 1981. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Drafted by Service, cleared in S, S/S, and S/S–O, approved by Enders.

400. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 400. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, November 11, 1982, 1445Z
5389. Subject: Ambassador’s luncheon meeting with President Stroessner.
1. (Confidential—Entire text.)
2. On November 10, 1982, President Stroessner lunched privately at residence with the Ambassador. This was his first visit to the residence since Ambassador Landau held his “despedida” in 1977; and, according to the President’s private Secretary, the first ever private luncheon with any Ambassador.
3. During this friendly, chatty luncheon, the subjects we discussed covered a wide range of issues such as the United Nations, OAS, other Latin American alliances (Stroessner is particularly disturbed with the non-aligned countries movement), the errors of the Yalta Agreement, a short conversation on the Bolivian threat to Paraguay (especially noting the communist cabinet members in Bolivia) plus comments on new Mariscal Estigarribia airstrip.
4. In the over-two hour meeting, I was impressed with President Stroessner’s remembrance of names and dates of historical events and military feats worldwide, from World War I onward. The President has a good political mind and is a charming and able conversationalist. He is an avid reader and has a wealth of great information on world affairs and world history. He particularly has the ability to recall military actions that have occurred world wide (in his story of his visit in the late fifties or early sixties to army posts throughout the USA, he recalled the names of the generals in command and in most cases told who succeeded them).
5. It is obvious that the President is quite alert, has no visible disabilities, except a tender right hand, and seems to be enjoying good health (after the morning and evening get toegthers for Stroessner’s birthday on Nov 3, longer-term diplomats here felt he looked better and more agile than two years ago).
6. President Stroessner indicated that he is a great admirer of President Reagan who, he feels, is constrained by Congress in fighting communism. For example Stroessner would like to see the United States back Taiwan more strongly.
7. The only point of possible friction in the conversation was the President’s observation that he felt the methods being utilized in this country were best for the Paraguayan people as shown by the peace and tranquility in the country. I did not push the item other than to state that he was well aware of our feelings on the state of siege and the “detenidos”. The President then stated he feels fascism is as evil as communism and he is a believer in democratic government. At this point he indicated a desire to invite members of the U.S. Congress to observe the February 6 elections in Paraguay.
8. Comment: Believe that this was an extremely useful first private contact with the President. Obviously, the President felt likewise as it was mutually agreed to follow up with frequent private meetings which should be beneficial to both parties. I plan to follow up this offer and initial exchange, if possible on a monthly basis, and will pursue subjects in depth during these meetings. It is interesting that about an hour after the end of the luncheon, President Stroessner called on direct residence phone without benefit of secretary to clear up several dates and names we both could not come up with at luncheon (Such as Mihajlovic of Yugoslavia after Yalta Conference). He then gave several names of authors of books I had expressed an interest in, and about an hour later his personal secretary arrived at the residence with two literary works on the triple alliance war which we had discussed.
Davis
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D820586–0589. Confidential; Priority.

401. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 401. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, April 27, 1983, 1945Z
1605. Subject: April 26, 1983: President Stroessner/Ambassador meeting.
1. (Confidential—Entire text.)
2. Summary: While main reason for invitation was supposedly to express gratitude for arrival of five-man flood evaluation team, it became evident that President wanted to express his discontent at present trend of press coverage by “ABC Color” newspaper. Other subjects discussed were record publicity, Laino expulsion, military and economic aid, and, of course, events in Bolivia. 2 End summary.
3. Flood conversation opened by expression of gratitude for our disaster relief funds allocated to Indian colonies along the Paraguay River plus profuse thanks for the fast cooperation in arrival and meetings with the five-man evaluation team. President mentioned great aid to Argentina by Vatican and others while only USA had come to Paraguay’s help thus far. Then stating that Paraguayans were tough and took their bad times and setbacks stoically and without tears as they had through history, the general then launched into a tirade against the negative criticisms of “ABC Color.”
4. “ABC Color” activities. Not only the Colorados have been discussing the consistent anti-government articles by “ABC” but even the general public and some opposition members have privately expressed concern at the frequency and vehemence of some “ABC” articles. Zuccolillo (Owner of “ABC”) has several times expressed concern at prospect of closing and as recently as a month ago Ambassador had expressed to Minister of Interior Montanaro the negative effects on Paraguay that such a closure would have. 3 However, immediately after expressing these concerns two weeks ago, “ABC” carried a Laino interview, which could hurt efforts to get Laino’s return approved and certainly strengthened displeasure of government.
5. President Stroessner first decried the unbalanced amount of anti-government criticism and seemed especially provoked by report on the ineffectiveness of the legislature and the negative comments on Paraguay’s participation in Itaipu. President stressed that criticism should be made on basis of grounds to improve conditions and not merely as a confrontation between press and government. Seriousness of his concern was the denunciation of Conrado Pappalardo’s sister, who is married to Zuccolillo, apparently to let Ambassador know such association would not affect his decision. He was also provoked that “ABC” did not carry his anti-communist interview of several days ago although a copy was provided to them.
6. Ambassador noted traditional right of free press and value of dialogue to keep all on their toes plus impressing upon Stroessner the vast negative effects of any closure on our mutual personal efforts over last nine months as well as the relations between our two countries.
7. Never could Ambassador extract a promise or statement from President that “ABC” would not be closed but received half-hearted agreement that nothing would happen during Ambassador’s trip to the US.
8. The mention of effecting our relations, brought the question of when we could give more military and economic aid to Paraguay. Ambassador stated the betterment of human rights in Paraguay was a step in the right direction but a newspaper’s closure would cancel any prospects.
9. Aid for Paraguay. President then repeated his previous remarks that we should grant Paraguay at least the same aid as Bolivia and again repeating the comparison of pro-communist and anti-US Bolivia to anti-communist and pro-US Paraguay. The theme “amigos con nuestros amigos” was once again heard.
10. Ricord. Once again Ambassador was requested to compile everything possible on information on the Ricord case, particularly if we have anything on statements by Ricord as to his associates in drug trafficking in Paraguay. 4 President pledged his support and cooperation to counteract the small drug trafficking in Paraguay.
11. Visit by Assistant Secretary Enders. 5 President stated his pleasure at Ambassador Enders’ visit for his “Cambio de Mando” on August 15. 6 (Upon leaving, Conrado Pappalardo also talked about Ambassador Enders’ trip for August 15 and stated he had urged that Mrs. Enders be sure to accompany her husband.)
12. Upon leaving, Ambassador reiterated his concerns over any closure of “ABC” and any prolonged expulsion of Laino and surprisingly, as opposed to Minister Montanaro’s remarks (Asuncion 1582). 7 President stated that plans were being studied for his return.
13. Comments: Several times during conversation Ambassador attempted to interject discussion on opening up the political process in Paraguay 8 and finally was told “lets’s meet on that upon your return.” 9
14. President Stroessner seems to be in excellent health, more alert and active than nine months ago. He also seems to be watching his weight and getting more rest than previously.
Davis
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830238–0557. Confidential.

Laino’s return was designed to test the genuineness of the Interior Minister’s claim that exiles would be allowed into Paraguay if they returned one by one. The GOP’s refusal to allow Laino to enter the country has provided the opposition with new ammunition to conduct its public relations sniping of the GOP.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830171–0246)
3 Memorandum of conversation for this meeting has not been found.
