6 No indication of an August 15 visit by Enders has been found. In telegram 3369 from Asuncion, August 16, the Embassy reported: “President Alfredo Stroessner was sworn in on August 15 for seventh term as President of Paraguay, capping three days of ceremonies. The general populace appears to have taken the President’s inauguration for another 5-year term as simply a matter of course, and virtually the only event which provided much occasion for comment and speculation was the installation of three new cabinet members.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830471–1013) In telegram 228836 to Asuncion, August 12, the Department instructed the Embassy to deliver a message from Reagan to Stroessner upon his inauguration: “Dear Mr. President: Please accept my congratulations and best wishes as you commence a new presidential term. The American people share my hope that the close friendship between Paraguay and the United States will continue in the years ahead. Sincerely, Ronald Reagan.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830463–0863)
7 In telegram 1582 from Asuncion, April 26, the Embassy wrote: “In Ambassador Davis’ first meeting with Minister Montanaro since Paraguayan group luncheon with Assistant Secretary Enders, points discussed at this meeting were present condition of human rights in Paraguay and possible Enders visit. Minister Montanaro was very pleased with his treatment in Washington and felt Ambassador Enders had conversed in a friendly and open manner with them. Minister was openly impressed by Ambassador Enders’ knowledge of Paraguay’s problems.” Additionally, the Embassy noted that Stroessner had “called off” Laino’s return and that “it was evident that only the president can made new decision to allow the return should it be requested.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830233–0634)
8 In telegram 3428 from Asuncion, August 19, the Embassy wrote: “The inauguration August 15 of President Alfredo Stroessner for a seventh term (1983–1988) occurred within the context of (A) discouraging economic and political developments and trends in Paraguay, and (B) a rapidly changing hemispheric and regional environment. The Embassy believes it timely, therefore, to propose a careful review of U.S. interests and objectives in Paraguay in the light of the current context in order to arrive at appropriate and agreed USG policy responses and courses of action.” Furthermore: “I must say I have been quite disappointed that I have not been more successful in my efforts to talk sense to the Stroessner government, to urge it to understand that its own interests lie in better observance of democratic rules. Maybe after about thirty years in office, President Stroessner just is not going to change. It is a pity, because by not loosening up, he is creating a dangerous situation for the future. I don’t think that we can afford to be identified with that wrong approach if we are going to have an influence on the situation later on. Fortunately, a lot of Colorados and even a lot of generals agree with me, or at least they say so.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830505–0170)
9 In telegram 281942 to Asuncion, October 1, the Department wrote: “Department welcomed Embassy’s review of developments and trends in Paraguay and related recommendations regarding USG policy objectives and agrees that a cautious shift in the balance of our policy objectives toward Paraguay is called for. However, in implementing the above strategy, we want to avoid, whenever possible, jeopardizing GOP cooperation on hemispheric or international issues and/or compromising access to and influence with key Paraguayans on domestic issues such a human rights, democracy and the economy. Periodic evaluation of the effectiveness and impact of this policy adjustment as it is implemented would be helpful to the Department in understanding the situation and tuning our course appropriately.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830571–0025)

402. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 402. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, May 18, 1984, 2203Z
2802. Subject: (C) Discussion with President Stroessner on U.S-Paraguayan relations.
1. (Confidential—Entire text.)
2. Summary: The Ambassador met May 18 with President Stroessner. An exchange on problems in our bilateral relations stemming from human rights problems in Paraguay dominated the discussion. 2 Stroessner was stung at the decision not to permit the visiting U.S. Army band and parachute team to March in the national day parade. He questioned whether the USG, with all its problems, really cared about this, or about the closure of ABC Color , which, as the Ambassador explained, prompted us to curtail the band’s activities in Paraguay. 3 The Ambassador stressed the importance the U.S. attached to the ABC problem and told the President that it would continue to affect our bilateral relations. They also exchanged views on the recent abortive visit by the Americas watch group, with the Ambassador stressing concern over the Paraguayan handling of the incident and Stroessner confirming assurances by other members of his government that the group could return to Paraguay if it wanted but would have to abide by Paraguayan laws including customs inspection. End summary.
3. The Ambassador met with President Stroessner May 18. In the course of a long conversation the two discussed strains in U.S.-Paraguayan relations which have arisen primarily as a result of recent human rights developments in Paraguay, and the U.S. reactions thereto. It was apparent that the President was most upset about the decision not to permit the visiting U.S. Army band and parachute team to march in the national day parade on May 15. He returned to this subject several times.
4. Stroessner argued that the U.S. had its hands full with problems all over the world—from the Persian Gulf to relations with China (over which he chided the U.S. for the apparent warmth of relations with the PRC). No one in the U.S. or the U.S. Government, he insisted, would have known or cared about the participation of the band in Paraguay’s National Day parade. Thus, he could not understand why this had been cancelled.
5. The President took the occasion to point to the participation by his “good friends” the Brazilians, who brought in two warships—whose crews marched in the parade—and sent a group of Mirage fighters to overfly the parade route. They also presented the Paraguayan Air Force with two reconditioned C–47 aircraft.
6. The Ambassador responded that the USG decision was made in reaction to the closure of ABC Color . He emphasized that it was his choice, not something forced on him by the Embassy staff (this to counter an assertion the President raised several times). Moreover, the Department fully endorsed the decision, although some in Washington suggested stronger measures. 4
7. The Ambassador noted that there had been strenuous efforts by certain members of the Paraguayan Government to turn the decision around but that this was not possible, given the importance we attached to the “ABC” incident. This led the President to request that in the future the Embassy deal on such matters with Foreign Minister Saldivar, and not with Conrado Pappalardo.
8. Continuing on the “ABC” matter, the President complained about the statement issued by the Embassy in the immediate wake of the closure. 5 Stroessner argued that he had consulted with the leadership of his party and the government and that they were unanimous in recommending closure of the paper in order to protect “the welfare and security of the Paraguayan people.” “ABC’s” owner, Aldo Zuccolillo, he insisted, couldn’t be controlled, and he had to be stopped for the good of the country. Again he argued that the U.S., with all its problems around the world, could not possibly care much about what happened to a newspaper in Paraguay and complained about U.S. “interference” in this matter.
9. The Ambassador responded that as far as the U.S. was concerned the “ABC” affair was not finished. He emphasized that the GOP’s action would continue to affect our bilateral relations, and that it would certainly affect the way in which the U.S. Congress would look upon future cooperation with Paraguay. The congressional reaction to the “ABC” closure, he noted, had only begun.
10. The Ambassador also underlined once again our concern over the handling of the recent visit by the Americas watch group, and our satisfaction at the GOP’s decision to invite them to return. He emphasized the stature of the organizations which the group represented, and that the members of the group were serious people who were well-connected and respected within the U.S. and abroad. He urged that the GOP look upon such a visit as an opportunity, not a threat. The President repeated the assurances given by others that the group could return to Paraguay if it wanted to, but insisted that they would have to abide by Paraguayan laws if they did. He asked that the Embassy continue to discuss such matters related to human rights cases with Interior Minister Montanaro.
11. Throughout the meeting President Stroessner was animated, affable and alert, and he showed no signs whatever of any problems with his health. While he was clearly concerned over problems in our relations, and particularly upset over the band incident, his manner was friendly throughout. While we doubt that he fully comprehends why we have reacted so strongly to the “ABC” closure, the band incident does appear to have brought home to him that the U.S. does attach considerable importance to human rights problems in Paraguay.
Davis

403. Action Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Motley) to Secretary of State Shultz 403. Action Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Motley) to Secretary of State Shultz 1
Washington, July 20, 1984
SUBJECT
A strategy to obtain the reopening of Paraguay’s only independent newspaper, ABC Color
ISSUE FOR DECISION
Whether to agree to a meeting with Paraguayan Foreign Minister Saldivar as part of a strategy to obtain the reopening of Paraguay’s only independent newspaper.
ESSENTIAL FACTORS
Almost totally isolated from the rest of Latin America, Paraguay’s government invites disdain and ridicule. President Stroessner and the Colorado Party continue to run the country as a private fiefdom. The Stroessner regime has traditionally been impervious to outside pressure in the area of human rights and the development of democratic institutions. Therefore any opportunity to influence the GOP in a manner consistent with our policy goal of more explicitly identifying ourselves with democratic ideals and processes should be considered worth pursuing. We have been offered the opportunity to effect the reopening of Paraguay’s only independent newspaper, ABC Color .
Since last March, when president Stroessner closed ABC for effectively “disturbing the peace”, we have carefully orchestrated a campaign to persuade him to permit the newspaper to reopen. In the process, the ABC case has become something of a cause celebre among press and human rights groups and the Congress (see attached documents). 2 Stroessner understands that there is no chance of improving relations with the U.S. so long as the paper remains closed.
President Stroessner’s Foreign Minister has now come to us with the outline of a deal. Essentially, it is that if we take measures to ease USG public pressure on Paraguay, Stroessner will consider permitting ABC to reopen in September. In addition, and implicitly part of the deal, Foreign Minister Saldivar has asked for a meeting with you. A Paraguayan Foreign Minister has not been received by a U.S. Secretary of State since Saldivar’s predecessor met with Secretary Haig in August 1981. 3
There could be a trap here. President Stroessner values our good opinion and friendship and is bothered by the extent of our protest, but he may just be looking for an opportunity to sell the GOP’s arguments justifying the paper’s closure. Saldivar may view a meeting with you as a means of gaining favorable publicity in Paraguay for the Government and for himself.
The risk is worth taking. If we do not follow up on this opportunity, there is no question but what ABC will remain shut. If ABC reopens, it will be a nice victory for the administration’s quiet diplomacy in the area of human rights, especially when viewed in conjunction with our successful efforts to achieve a decrease in the number of political prisoners, improved judicial processing, and a slightly more liberal, if still tenuous, approach to opposition political activities. If Stroessner does not keep his end of the bargain, your meeting with Saldivar can be defended as a final effort to get ABC Color back in business.
We wish to firm up the deal offered by the GOP, including getting reasonable assurances that if we were to ease up on public pressure and arrange a visit for Saldivar, ABC would be reopened. We will ask our Ambassador to seek assurance directly from Stroessner, if necessary. We would not wish to proceed under other circumstances, considering the depth of feeling in the Congress and the fact that elections are coming up in November.
Your contribution to this effort would be to agree to receive Foreign Minister Saldivar for a brief (20–30 minutes) substantive meeting. Minister Saldivar is prepared to come to Washington at your convenience, but he would like to come before the Colorado Party convention starts September 15. He wishes “to explain Paraguay’s case” on ABC . Since he is not a stimulating interlocutor, we would view the meeting as an opportunity for you to confirm to him our concern about the closure of ABC , our appreciation for human rights improvements in other areas and our desire to support democratic trends in the Southern Cone.
RECOMMENDATION:
That you authorize us to offer Saldivar a meeting with you at a mutually convenient time before September 15, providing we are satisfied that such a meeting will lead to the re-opening of ABC Color . 4
1 Source: Reagan Library, George P. Shultz Papers, Box 49G, Paraguay. Confidential. Sent through Armacost. A stamped notation at the top of the first page indicates that Shultz saw the memorandum.
2 Attached but not printed are a copy of the protest delivered on ABC Color ’s closure by Kilday to Martínez on April 6; House of Representatives Concurrent Resolution 309, May 17, that condemned the closure; a June 21 letter to Shultz from eleven Republican Senators; and a May 8 letter to Stroessner from three Republican members of the House Subcommittee on Human Rights and International Organizations.
3 See Document 399 .
4 Shultz wrote below the “Approval” or “Disapproval” lines: “how about doing this the other way around—guarantee a meeting when ABC Color is [granted permission to] reopen. G.” Brackets are in the original. A typed transcription of Shultz’s comments is dated July 24.

404. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 404. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, August 30, 1984, 1600Z
5204. Subject: (C) Ambassador’s August 29 meeting with President Stroessner: “ABC Color.”
Ref: Asuncion 5144 2
1. (Secret—Entire text.)
2. Summary: The Ambassador discussed the closure of “ABC Color” with President Stroessner on August 29. In exchanges that went on for an hour, the President repeatedly insisted that he did not want to talk about the “ABC” issue, while the Ambassador described the widespread U.S. concern over the matter and cautioned him about the continued problem it would pose for our bilateral relations. Out of the blue, Stroessner renewed his earlier requests for C–130 and F–5 aircraft for his air force. The Ambassador responded that this was precisely the kind of thing the U.S. could not do while “ABC” remained closed. Stroessner concluded by saying that he considered the matter to be a “dead issue.” End summary.
3. The Ambassador called on President Stroessner August 29 to discuss the problem of the continued closure of “ABC Color.” The Ambassador opened the discussion by describing his recent consultations in Washington, stressing the concern throughout the USG over the matter. He noted that this concern crossed party and ideological lines in the Congress, as indicated by the recent resolution on the subject. 3
4. President Stroessner interjected at this point that as far as he was concerned the “ABC” issue was something he did not want to talk about anymore. The Ambassador responded that it was important for the GOP to understand that the continued closure of “ABC” would mean continued problems in our bilateral relations. It would prevent the U.S. from deepening cooperation with Paraguay in matters affecting our mutual interests. Would the President give him a definitive answer on “ABC’s” future?
5. Sliding away from a direct response, Stroessner reiterated his desire not to pursue the discussion. He said the closure of “ABC” had been necessary, adding that for the sake of the Paraguayan people he could not afford to relax his grip. He pointed to Peru and Argentina as examples of what happened when there was “democracy without firmness.” He went on to recite a somewhat colored version of Paraguay’s chaotic political history during the first half of the 20th century, up until his accession to power. This could not be allowed to recur. He repeated that he did not wish to discuss the subject further.
6. The Ambassador asked if the GOP had any flexibility on the “ABC” issue. Perhaps “ABC” owner Zuccolillo might be willing to discuss conditions under which the paper could re-open. The President dismissed this possibility, leaving no doubt that he was not willing to treat with Zuccolillo. He added that U.S. complaints over the “ABC” matter were interference in Paraguay’s internal affairs, and said he was particularly stung by the Embassy’s public protest over the paper’s closure in March. No other country, he said, had raised the matter with him. Even the UK Deputy Under Secretary for Foreign Affairs, who called on him earlier this month, had agreed that it was an internal matter and had accepted the fact that the “ABC” question was now closed.
7. At this point, the President said there was something he wanted to raise. Paraguay, which had supported the U.S. faithfully over the years, needed U.S. help. He said the U.S. had promised to provide a C–130 aircraft to the Paraguayan Air Force back in 1971. He was still waiting. In addition, he said, his air force needed some F–5 aircraft, provision of which should cause the U.S. no difficulty since they were purely defensive. Defense Minister Martinez would be in touch with the Ambassador shortly to provide details.
8. The Ambassador said he hadn’t wanted to seem to be threatening but was surprised at this request, and at the apparent unwillingness of the GOP to listen to what he had been repeating over the past two years about how human rights issues affected our ability to provide military equipment. He said there was no prospect that the U.S. would be willing to sell such equipment to Paraguay as long as “ABC” was closed. Moreover, the closure placed even our modest IMET program in serious jeopardy in the Congress. This was exactly what he meant when he said that continued closure of “ABC” would limit possibilities for mutual cooperation.
9. The Ambassador added that as he had told Foreign Minister Saldivar, a meeting between Saldivar and the Secretary during Saldivar’s forthcoming trip to the U.S. would not be possible. The Embassy would, however, seek to arrange a meeting with Assistant Secretary Motley. The President said that this would be fine. 4
10. At this point, the conversation turned to other matters (which will be reported in separate messages). 5 As the Ambassador was leaving, however, the President reminded him of his desire for F–5s and commented philosophically that he remained in office not because he liked the job, but to protect his people against the kind of political upheavals which had convulsed Paraguay so often in the 20th century.
11. The President was calm and relaxed throughout the meeting, though he clearly did not relish the persistent discussion of “ABC.” We take his comments to be a definitive “no” to any re-opening of “ABC”. Our views on the U.S. response to this position will be the subject of a separate message. 6
Davis

The Foreign Minister argued for a meeting with the Secretary to present Paraguay’s side of the case, but avoided saying anything definitive about the paper’s future.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840547–0813)

405. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 405. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, August 22, 1985, 1706Z
4361. Subject: Meeting with President Stroessner.
Ref: Asuncion 4295 2
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary: During a lengthy and relaxed conversation, President Stroessner confided to me about his health, his attitude toward reelection in 1988, concerns about Bolivia, and the change in U.S. Ambassadors here. He displayed a vigor which belies public questions about the state of his health. End summary.
3. After having been informed by ForMin Saldivar on Monday that President Stroessner would be too busy to receive me this week, I was advised on Wednesday (August 21) that he wanted to see me the next morning. Thus, I had the opportunity for a lengthy tour d’horizon, which the President said he wanted.
4. Health: Stroessner raised this issue by pointing to a scratch on his hand and saying he wanted me to know it was from an accident, and that he was having no further skin problems. I noted that the city was abuzz with rumors about a possible gallbladder operation. He confirmed that his doctors told him he needed an operation, that although it was not urgent, it should be done soon and that he would have it here in Asuncion. He later returned to his health saying that he sometimes lacks pep (falta animo) in the morning when he wakes up, but that the thought that people are waiting to see him in his office gets him moving.
5. Reelection: Stroessner commented, in the context of speculation about his candidacy for reelection in 1988, that he lives in the present, not the future. He is interested in serving, he said, as long as “God, my health and the Paraguayan people allow me to.” (Comment: He later reiterated he would serve “as long as the Paraguayan people want me.” This is a switch from his past line that he would serve as long as the Colorado Party wanted him as its candidate. This may be an intended or unconscious reflection of the fact that he is currently facing some independent jockeying for position within the party. End comment.)
6. Contacts with Colorados: I knew from Interior Minister Montanaro that the President was interested in learning details of my contacts with various elements of the Colorado Party. Stroessner raised the issue anew and wanted to know why I would waste time meeting with what he described as an unimportant group of Colorado Party legislators, which, he pointed out, included people who were “not disciplined.” He knew I was going to have some of the “kamikaze” group (the Frutos clan) to lunch on Friday. 3
7. I said that it was important that I be able to inform Washington accurately on all political currents here. In that context I mentioned that I planned to lunch the following week with Mario Abdo Benitez (who is at the forefront of the opposite “militant” faction). The President asked, in the same tone as previously, “Why are you meeting with him?” I went through the same explanation again, probably to no avail. (Comment: This is a sensitive area for him. It is extremely clear that he spends a great deal of time U.S. Embassy watching. End comment.)
8. Change of Ambassadors: Stroessner said in terms of the upcoming change of Ambassadors that Ambassador-designate Taylor “could be valuable here.” He mentioned his narcotics program direction experience. I assured him that Taylor is a well-rounded career officer, not just a narcotics specialist.
9. Military assistance: Stroessner said that Paraguay could use the resumption of foreign military sales from the U.S. he spoke of the military imbalance between Bolivia and Paraguay and referred to the historical context of the Chaco War. The U.S. sale of a patrol craft to the Bolivians also came up, with Stroessner pointing out that the ship is now visiting in the Asuncion port. The threat is growing, he said. I used that opportunity to once again explain to the President that as long as his government continues to take actions such as closing the ABC Color newspaper and taking Radio Nanduti off the air 4 that there will be no disposition in Washington to resuming this kind of military assistance.
10. Exchange rate: As I was leaving, I remarked on the skyrocketing value of the dollar compared to the guarani. He replied abruptly that there would be no devaluation of the currency. I understand from other well-connected sources that the President has said he will not replace MinFin Barrientos. This conversation coincided with the unprecedented leap in the dollar-guarani exchange rate from about 1:800 on August 19 to 1:1050 and approaching 1100 on August 21. The President appeared not to understand that the outside world could and was devaluing the guarani.
11. Comment: Stroessner was the most relaxed and voluble I have seen him. He did not seem overly preoccupied by his health, beyond a normal focus on the aging process. A bit of bile entered the conversation in terms of my meeting with Colorado Party dissidents and the GOP’s handling of media criticism. In general, however, Stroessner’s vital signs show little indication that he is losing his grip although his economic blindspot remains as glaring as ever.
Davis

2 In telegram 4295 from Asuncion, August 19, the Embassy reported on rumors of gall bladder surgery for Stroessner. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D850588–0608)
3 August 24.
4 In telegram 2799 from Asuncion, July 12, 1983 the Embassy wrote: “The Paraguayan Government July 9 closed down a local radio station, Radio Nanduti, for 30 days, accusing the station of having ‘disturbed the public order.’”(Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D830395–0406) In telegram 7179 from Asuncion, November 8, the Embassy reported: “The Paraguayan authorities moved once again against Humberto Rubin, owner of the popular radio station Radio Nanduti. On November 6 the telecommunications authorities prohibited Rubin from working as an announcer. The move came on the heels of a meeting between Rubin and the Under Secretary for Information, Anibal Fernandez, who, according to Rubin, warned him that if he did not stop interviewing leaders of the unrecognized opposition parties and otherwise providing so much coverage to their activities, his station would have to close.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840716–0281)

406. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 406. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M 86–20034 Washington, July 9, 1986
PARAGUAY: Scenarios for Sudden, Unexpected Change
Summary
We believe that despite growing opposition activity in Paraguay, President Stroessner is in control, will be re-elected in 1988, and probably will remain in power until he dies, when the military and the ruling Colorado Party will choose a successor. Nevertheless, change in Paraguay could come about suddenly, leading either to a regime that initiates some democratic reforms or to right- or left-wing authoritarian rule more repressive than Stroessner’s. We believe that Asuncion will move toward democracy if the traditionalist faction of the Colorado Party, reformist elements in the military, or the opposition parties depose Stroessner. The President could, however, be ousted by rightist elements intent on protecting lucrative narcotics and smuggling interests or—in the longer term—Paraguay could fall prey to leftist subversion after a brief, chaotic democratic interlude. All these scenarios, in our view, are highly unlikely, but none can be discounted.
[Omitted here is the body of the memorandum.]
1 Source: Central Intelligence Agency, Office of Asian, Pacific, Latin America, and African Analysis, Job 04T00794R: Intelligence Publication Files—Production Case Files (1986), Box 1, Folder 43: Paraguay: Scenarios for Sudden, Unexpected Change. Secret; Noforn; [ handling restriction not declassified ]. Prepared at Gelbard’s request in the South America Division, Office of African and Latin American Analysis and coordinated with DO. Information available as of July 8 was used in the preparation of the memorandum.

407. Information Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Abrams) to Secretary of State Shultz 407. Information Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Abrams) to Secretary of State Shultz 1
Washington, January 5, 1987
SUBJECT
Paraguay: An Old Caudillo Faces the Future
SUMMARY: President Stroessner is struggling to contain opposition not only from frustrated sectors with increased political and economic expectations, but from within his own party, as well. He has resorted to his traditional method of applied political acumen, combined with selective repression, to contain this latest wave of opposition. END SUMMARY.
During the past few months, the GOP has taken the offensive against its moderate democratic opposition:
—An independent, privately-owned radio station has been all but driven off the air (and into bankruptcy) by government-sanctioned jamming and signal overriding;
—An increasing number of noncommunist opposition leaders have been jailed on spurious charges of sedition;
—Labor leaders have been arrested and publicly beaten for attempting to organize, and peacefully demonstrate and strike.
—Private meetings of the democratic opposition have been violently disrupted.
—Rural workers involved in legitimate land disputes have been physically mistreated by local police authorities.
President Stroessner, 74, has consistently defeated previous surges of opposition activity with combination of repression and personal political acumen. Moreover, he has been able to count on the support of the military and the 100 year-old Colorado Party. The situation is evolving, however, and today, the Colorado Party is suffering serious internecine conflict arising from disparate elements maneuvering for position in the “post-Stroessner” era. Deteriorating economic conditions are impacting adversely on the recently-expanded middle class, which previously looked to Stroessner to provide order and stability, but now is beginning to see him as an impediment to modernization.
The GOP thinks its visceral anti-communism should guarantee visible USG support and purports to be surprised when successive US Ambassadors do not deliver it. Ambassador Taylor’s contacts with the democratic opposition have incurred its wrath, as did our November 20 press statement calling on it to respect freedom of assembly and expression. 2 The FRG in Bonn and the EC in Asuncion have made démarches, as we have done in Washington. 3 Further, the GOP can no longer rely on the tacit approval of now-democratic Argentina and Brazil.
On the other hand, the GOP has benefited politically and financially from support from the Japanese Government. Paraguay, with a relatively large Japanese emigre community, is the largest Latin American recipient of bilateral Japanese aid. We have made the GOJ aware of our human rights concerns, but the GOJ has continued to devote a disproportionate amount of its assistance to Paraguay.
The opposition (both within and outside the Colorado Party) has identified the 1988 elections as a showdown, and can be expected to continue turning up the heat on Stroessner. We are not optimistic that increased internal and external pressure will cause Stroessner to moderate his policies, as he prepares for yet another term.
1 Source: Department of State, Bureau of Inter-American Affairs, Assistant Secretary Files, Special Collection—Iran Contra—Noriega, Lot 95D407, Chron (1987). Confidential. Drafted on December 15, 1986 by Bolton; cleared by Gelbard and Flower. Printed from an uninitialed copy.
2 In telegram 363989 to Asuncion, November 21, 1986 the Department forwarded the text of its November 21 press statement noting the detention of Paraguayan opposition leaders and urging the Paraguayan Government “to consider the release of these individuals as a demonstration of its respect for internationally accepted norms regarding freedom of assembly and speech.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D860890–0946)
3 In telegram 395249 to Asuncion, December 23, 1986 the Department noted that on December 18, Gelbard had given Martínez a démarche regarding Radio Nanduti and political repression in Paraguay. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D860975–0127)

408. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Paraguay 408. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Paraguay 1
Washington, February 12, 1987, 0520Z
41359. Subject: DAS Gelbard’s Conversation with Paraguayan Foreign Minister Saldivar.
Ref: State 930321 2
1. C—Entire text.
2. As mentioned reftel, Paraguayan Foreign Minister Saldivar telephoned ARA DAS Gelbard on February 10 just as Paraguayan Ambassador Martinez had entered Gelbard’s office to receive the USG’s formal protest against the teargassing of Ambassador Taylor. 3 Saldivar was clearly uncomfortable; he began with a lengthy, highly apologetic monologue, which gave the impression that the police officials who caused the incident had not been acting on the basis of instructions from higher authority. Saldivar repeatedly expressed his deep regrets for this incident. He noted his complete awareness of Gelbard’s previous conversations with Ambassador Martinez in the wake of Minister of Interior Montanaro’s statements against Ambassador Taylor. 4 In this regard, he stated that he had provided the guarantees of security for Ambassador Taylor and all Americans in Paraguay pursuant to Gelbard’s request.
3. Gelbard responded by covering essentially the same points as in his conversation with Ambassador Martinez (reftel). He expressed extreme concern about the direction in which the GOP was taking bilateral relations. If the police were not acting from higher level instructions, he asked rhetorically, does this mean that the Paraguayan Government’s control is breaking down? The USG has responsibility for the safety of its citizens and we had to wonder if we should alert American citizens to possible dangers which could befall them in Paraguay. Should we ask the American Davis Cup team to postpone or cancel the matches set for Asuncion in March? It was clear, at any rate, that the earlier statement of security guarantees given privately had had no worth. It was also clear that this incident represented a serious violation of the Vienna Convention, since the police were fully cognizant of the presence of Ambassador Taylor at the dinner and were in violation of some of the basic concepts of international conduct. Gelbard noted that in his visit to Asuncion last July GOP officials had spoken throughout of Paraguay’s vaunted “peace and tranquility;” it was clear that this does not exist and it was clear that the GOP was trying to make Ambassador Taylor a scapegoat for the complete range of Paraguay’s economic, political and social problems. 5 Gelbard said that it was imperative, at this point, in order to allay USG concerns for the GOP to make a public apology to Ambassador Taylor and to the Paraguayans present at the dinner and to repeat publicly the guarantees of security for Ambassador Taylor and all Americans in Paraguay. Gelbard concluded by saying that the USG wants good relations with Paraguay. Our Ambassador was acting in ways that were fully consonant with accepted international behavior, contrary to the GOP’s statements. At this point, we needed clear signs from the GOP that it wants good bilateral relations with US.
4. Saldivar pleaded for USG assistance in helping the GOP find a way out of this problem. He urged that we not “humiliate” them. Gelbard said that we certainly did not wish to do that and under the circumstances the public statement was a minimal request. Gelbard also noted that we, of course, expect the GOP and their associates in the Colorado Party to live up completely to the accepted standards of international behavior.
4. Saldivar closed by saying that he had spoken with Ambassador Taylor several times already and expected to work with him closely to resolve this extremely unfortunate incident.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D870110–0254. Confidential; Immediate. Sent for information to Buenos Aires, Brasilia, and Montevideo. Drafted and approved by Gelbard.
2 Not found.
3 In telegram 555 from Asuncion, February 10, the Embassy wrote: “Ambassador and Mrs. Taylor and other diplomats were tear gassed in Asuncion on February 9, while attending a reception at a private residence in the Ambassador’s honor hosted by a nonpartisan group called Mujeres por la Democracia (Women for Democracy) a group equivalent to our League of Women voters. The event occurred when the police threw up a cordon around the neighborhood to prevent invitees from attending the reception. After consulting with higher authorities, the police permitted diplomats to enter, after carefully checking their credentials. Then, after the well-heeled and smartly-dressed invitees plus onlookers being held on the periphery, whose number had grown to three hundred or more, did not disperse, the police called in their equivalent of a SWAT team and began launching tear gas at this group. Then, about a half-hour later, clearly aware of the presence of the Ambassador, his wife, and other U.S. and Argentine diplomats, who had previously identified themselves to the police and had been allowed to pass into the residence, the police launched a tear gas grenade from the adjoining property onto the rear patio where the diplomats, and those few Paraguayans who arrived before the police, were assembled.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D870445–0034)

Government contacts have apparently made up their minds in advance to view the visit in a favorable light and did not take umbrage at clear statements of the need for the GOP to begin a process of political opening. Opposition contacts were uniformly pleased with the private and public message they heard about the U.S. commitment to convincing the GOP to open the political process. Gelbard’s call for pragmatism was universally accepted. Press coverage was extensive, and most of the message delivered in private conversation also found its way in to the public domain even before the departure press conference. We expect to see no dramatic change in the mind-set of the GOP, but believe the visit will encourage the moderate forces in the Colorado party and the political opposition to echo his message with their own preoccupations over the future direction of the country.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D860572–0551)

409. Action Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Abrams) to the Under Secretary of State for Political Affairs (Armacost) 409. Action Memorandum From the Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs (Abrams) to the Under Secretary of State for Political Affairs (Armacost) 1
Washington, February 21, 1987
SUBJECT
Request to Call In Paraguayan Ambassador
Although you earlier declined 2 to meet with the Paraguayan Ambassador after the teargassing of Ambassador Taylor, I feel that the GOP’s inadequate—and even insulting—diplomatic note of explanation warrants your doing so now.
On February 18 we received the GOP’s formal response to our protest note over the incident. 3 It is inadequate in both tone and substance. In essence, it says Ambassador Taylor is lying, accuses him of interference in Paraguay’s affairs, and does not provide the apology we asked for, or the guarantee for Americans’ safety.
The GOP insists upon trying to separate its treatment of Ambassador Taylor from its bilateral relations with the USG. By raising this with Ambassador Martinez, you would confirm the seriousness of the incident and our demand that Ambassador Taylor—and the government which he represents—be treated with deference and respect.
Recommendation
That you agree to call in Ambassador Martinez. 4

410. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 410. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M 87–20056 Washington, September 15, 1987
PARAGUAY’S PUBLIC ENTERPRISES: DESTROYING ECONOMIC GROWTH
Summary
Paraguay’s large and inefficient state-owned enterprises, which expanded rapidly in the wake of the economic boom of the 1970s, have emerged as the country’s most significant economic burden and as a powerful magnet for massive corruption opportunities. Far from acknowledging growing criticism from domestic groups and international financial officials calling for dramatic spending cuts and the privatization of state firms, government officials are instead using the state entities to enhance their political control and increase their personal wealth. Despite some emerging pressure from the business sector, we see little prospect for reforms. We believe that the regime’s continued pursuit of statist economic policies and its pervasive corruption will boost Paraguay’s foreign debt, encourage capital flight, and nurture the country’s growing role as a regional smuggling and narcotics center. Over the longer term, these factors will likely contribute to political instability after President Stroessner’s death.
[Omitted here is the body of the paper.]
1 Source: Central Intelligence Agency, Office of Support Services (DI), Job 90T00114R: Intelligence Publications Files (1986–1987), Box 1, Folder 47: Paraguay’s Public Enterprises: Destroying Economic Growth. Confidential; Noforn. Prepared at Gelbard’s request in the South America Division, Office of African and Latin American Analysis.

411. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 411. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, April 25, 1988, 1844Z
1819. Subject: Ambassador Walters’ tête-à-tête with President Stroessner.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Background: The following is an account drafted by Ambassador Vernon Walters on his 15 minute tête-à-tête with President Stroessner on April 22. 2 It followed his hour and 15 minute meeting with the President (reported septel), which also included Major General Johannsen, two of Ambassador Walters’ staff and Ambassador Taylor. 3
3. Begin text: President Stroessner said he had asked to speak to me alone for a few minutes because he could speak more frankly that way. I said I also had some things I could say to him.
4. President Stroessner said that he was very disturbed that U.S. Government officials had accused senior Paraguayan officials of being active in drug trafficking. He had asked for names and specifics. He had received none. If the U.S. was not prepared to furnish the names, then they should refrain from such public accusations. He said if we would give him names and locations, he would take action against the drug traffic, if necessary, using the army. He felt that traffic in narcotics was a threat to every nation. Bolivia was a producer. Paraguay was not. I said that there were recent indications of two large shipments to Europe having transited Paraguay. Again he asked for names, dates and places.
5. President Stroessner said that he felt that Embassies were for the purpose of improving relations. Sometimes he felt ours in Paraguay was not helping this. I replied that he must understand that our policies were determined in Washington, and the Ambassador, like me, received his instructions from our government. These instructions were often the result of legislative action arising from a congressional perception of what was happening in Paraguay. If he wanted better treatment from us, he must help us to help him.
6. I said the Paraguayan people were grateful to him for the improvements in Paraguay and for stability and leadership he had provided. He could easily open things up in the area of the media. A hostile speech on the radio or a hostile article in the press were no threat to him, and would certainly improve the image of his country in the U.S. and in Congress. I said he was strong enough to allow some opposition. He replied that communists and others were trying to destabilize Paraguay and that was not in our interest. 4 I said that I agreed but that a little more flexibility in allowing freedom of expression would help.
7. I said another matter that helped give him a bad image abroad was the matter of political prisoners who had taken asylum in foreign embassies in Asuncion, and who were not allowed to leave the country. President Stroessner said that they were not political prisoners, but criminals. However he had decided to let them leave the country. He said the Argentines, who disliked his government, were willing to take them, and they would be allowed to leave the country “in a matter of days.” I told him that this was a wise move (I had previously reminded him that in 1976 he had released another political prisoner, Chase Sardi, at my request). 5
8. President Stroessner said that the present Argentine Government had armed, trained and sent agents across the border to stir up guerrilla activity in Paraguay. This had failed because guerrillas needed popular support and they had obtained none in Paraguay. He then attacked Argentina and Alfonsin for their hostility toward Paraguay.
9. Finally, he said that he appreciated the chance to speak frankly to me and the fact that I would speak frankly to him. He hoped I would remember what he had told me, and he would think over the things I had told him. End text.
Taylor

The future course and how the world would judge events in Paraguay can still be affected by the decisions of the GOP.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D870785–0833)

412. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 412. Paper Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA M 88–20036 Washington, May 6, 1988
Paraguay: Expanding Illegal International Activities
Summary
President Stroessner has deliberately fostered institutionalized corruption in Paraguay to help preserve his authoritarian control over the country. Corruption, which extends into every level of the bureaucracy, has facilitated the growth of a thriving underground economy. We believe, however, that Paraguay’s lawless atmosphere is now encouraging more ominous international illegal activities—involving passport sales, arms transfers, technology transfers, and counterfeit US currency—often with a narcotics connection. These operations are increasingly bringing together corrupt local officials and fugitive foreign criminals, and we believe that recent reporting only scratches the surface of the problem.
[Omitted here is the body of the paper.]
1 Source: Department of State, Western Hemisphere Affairs Records, Policy, Planning, and Coordination, Security Assistance Program Files, Lot 91D371, Paraguay, 1984–1988. Secret; Noforn; [ handling restriction not declassified ]. Prepared in the South America Division, Office of African and Latin American Analysis, and coordinated with DO.

413. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 413. Telegram From the Embassy in Paraguay to the Department of State 1
Asuncion, October 28, 1988, 1519Z
4704. For Asst Secretary Abrams only from Ambassador Towell.
Subject: Advancing U.S. interests in Paraguay.
Refs: A. Asuncion 4653, 2 B. State 317594, 3 C. State 345774, 4 D. State 344039, 5 E. State 351103 6
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. With a three-day official mourning period decreed following the death of Colorado Vice-President Montanaro’s mother, which forced a postponement of my courtesy calls on senior Paraguayan officials, I take this occasion to slow the pace, step back, take a breath, and send you an abbreviated look at where we are since I arrived in Paraguay, and what comes next. My preliminary views on Paraguay, and our perception here of what is to be done, are firmly grounded on my two extensive conversations with you before I left for post, 7 your guidance to me following my initial meeting with Foreign Minister Acevedo (Ref B), the Secretary of State’s precise letter of instructions to me (Ref C) and, finally the detailed reporting cable of Under Secretary Armacost’s meeting with the new Paraguayan Foreign Minister, which confirmed that we are all speaking to the GOP with one voice (Ref D). 8
3. Relations with Stroessner: You gave me the challenging task of re-establishing a relationship/rapport with the President for the purpose of advancing U.S. interests, especially emphasizing the 3–D triad of democracy, drugs and derechos humanos. My first shot was the long-delayed, formal credentials ceremony where I advanced the less confrontational drug issue out front, rather than highlighting the more emotionally charged themes of democracy and human rights at what Paraguayans viewed as a ceremonial, not substantive, event. When I was then invited to sit on the gilded couch with Stroessner for a brief chat, the press hovered around us with lights and cameras for a significant portion of our time together, and I judged it imprudent to hold a serious bilateral exchange with the chief of state live on the evening news. As I left the palace, I told a senior GOP aide that I would like to have, at an unspecified future time, another, informal, substantive discussion with the President to go over a number of serious issues, when the President’s health so permitted. The morning of the next working day, another senior presidential aide contacted me and said the President would see me as soon as I wished. This same invitation was confirmed to me directly again this morning through a third senior GOP aide. Unless you think I should slow the pace, I plan to take up that invitation shortly and use that meeting to continue to carry out my instructions to build an effective working relationship with Stroessner so that I can attempt to advance U.S. policy interests directly with a man with whom we have had no bilateral link for some time.
4. I have just come out of a meeting with the new Foreign Minister (septel) 9 where my aim was-under the rubric of getting a readout on his Washington meetings with you and under Secretary Armacost—to drive home the point (lest anyone is still not getting the message) that U.S. policy, including 3–D, is constant; that you say it in Washington and I say it in Asuncion; and that the policy is bi-partisan and won’t change after the election, nor with the arrival of the new, smiling boy-Ambassador in Paraguay. I judge my meeting with Acevedo as a tentative success in my approach of seeking to advance U.S. interests in a careful, balanced and professional manner. And as I was unhinging my jaw to suggest to Acevedo what you in Washington proposed to him—that he get President Stroessner publicly to say in Paraguay what he said at the UN about GOP opposition to drug trafficking—at that moment, without coaching, Acevedo told me he has already recommended to Stroessner that he say publicly that very thing in Asuncion. 10
5. I want to assure you that I am firmly on board with the 3–D triad, which is the policy of the United States of America. At the same time, I firmly believe that a constant drum-beat recitation of all three elements—and with equal weight and equal volume at all times and in every forum—is not the most effective way to advance our interests.
6. To put it bluntly, as Bob Gelbard did with Rose, 11 our policy of public confrontation with the GOP failed. My country team feels strongly that three years of public friction with the GOP did very little to advance the three D’s. To be sure, we have won points with the Paraguayan opposition, and that is certainly helpful with the U.S. Congress and the human rights groups you and I talked about. I do not believe, however, that the approval of Paraguayan opposition is not the foremost goal of U.S. foreign policy here; advancement of the 3–D’s is.
7. I do believe, as does my country team, that to be effective on the three D’s we must build a closer relationship with the GOP and its top officials. We are not proposing a shift in policy. We are advocating tempering our public criticism and strengthening our private dialogue. I believe that public criticism is a more valuable policy tool when it is used sparingly.
8. The Secretary’s instruction to me urges “balance” in conducting our foreign policy here. That is exactly what I have in mind. In the short term it may give the appearance to some skeptical outsiders of a change in policy or a weakening of our commitment to the three D’s. In the long term, however, I believe it will bear fruit. If through quiet diplomacy we can persuade the GOP to move to direct elections of municipal officials in 1990, to enact electoral law reform so that the atomized non participating opposition will unite and participate, or to allow the Christian Democrats to hold their convention, I believe these would be real milestones on the long road to democracy. More importantly, our building a relationship with Stroessner and those around him would position us well to influence the transition towards post-Stroessner democracy in this country.
9. Interestingly, in the conversations Rose Likins has had with the opposition 12 they have been the ones to advocate that we use more balance in our relationship with the GOP. The President of the Febrerista Party (Si Affiliate) told Rose that I should not visit the Casa del Pueblo—his party headquarters and scene of Clyde’s fall from grace with the GOP “because the USG has everything to lose and nothing to gain” from such an act, and that “You can’t get me out of jail if you don’t have a relationship with Stroessner.”
10. I am very much aware of the importance of maintaining our excellent relationship with the opposition. Even before I presented credentials, I used the pretext of Mike Shelton’s orientation visit here to meet with the wide variety of senior dissident Colorado and opposition figures well known in Washington, including Domingo Laino, Humberto Rubin, and Aldo Zuccolillo’s deputy at ABC Color . Realistically speaking, however, we recognize that real progress on at least two of the D’s may well not occur until Stroessner dies. Until then, we can try to chip away at the margins and prepare ourselves for the real work that will come in influencing the transition. Even then, changes are likely to come slowly.
11. In summary, I think we are on the right track here and, equally importantly, in tune with what you wish us to accomplish here. I have asked permission to come up to Washington to have Thanksgiving with my young daughter, and would like to meet with you and Mike Skol to review the situation and see if we need any major or minor course corrections. 13
12. As I am going to press with this, your 351103 is coming off the wire. If there are substantial points we have not covered above after we have digested it, I will be back in touch. Best regards. 14
Towell

7 No memoranda of conversation have been found.
8 See footnote 5 , above. In telegram 3309 from USUN, October 14, the Mission reported on Walters’ meeting that day with Acevedo, writing: “Ambassador Walters asked Acevedo if thought is being given to a likely successor to Stroessner. Ambassador Walters also encouraged Acevedo to make public GOP opposition to narcotics trafficking.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880917–0027) In telegram 342499 to Asuncion, October 20, the Department reported on Wrobleski’s October 17 meeting with Acevado “to discuss narcotics-related issues in Paraguay.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880931–0444)
9 See footnote 5 , above.
10 In telegram 3269 from USUN, October 13, the Mission reported on Acevado’s statement at the 43rd UN General Assembly, including that he “reported that the Paraguayan legislature is presently occupied with the sanction of a new law to repress drug trafficking.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880912–00014)
11 Not found.
12 Not found.
13 No memoranda of conversation have been found.
14 In telegram 4757 from Asuncion, November 2, the Embassy wrote: “I was summoned to the palace October 31 for a twenty-minute one-on-one meeting with President Stroessner with no press in evidence. Although he may be a sick man, I also found him to be a rambling, nostalgic, frail old man living more in the past than in the present. I told him I wanted a frank, open and productive dialogue with him and his government, and thanked him for seeing me so soon after presenting credentials. I said that U.S. policy has not changed as a result of my arrival, explained our policy acronym 3-D (drugs, democracy, and derechos humanos) and discussed in depth why support for democracy was a basic U.S. foreign policy tenet. Going carefully so I would be invited back again, I was surprised that he took this all in seemingly pleasantly and didn’t bother to give me his standard civics lesson on Paraguayan-style democracy versus chaos. He spoke of Paraguayan friendship for and support of the U.S. (in D.R., at the U.N. etc.,) and rambled on amicably, keeping me when I made various moves to leave after an appropriate period of time. After my drug pitch, Stroessner reacted in a bizarre way: he spoke of the Chaco War (‘where I was wounded’) and expressed his concern that the U.S. is arming Paraguay’s former enemy Bolivia in the course of our war on narcotics. After twenty-minutes of conversation, he seemed to tire. He walked me to the door and invited me to return for further conversations. I will.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880974–0102)
