444. Telegram From the Embassy in Uruguay to the Department of State 444. Telegram From the Embassy in Uruguay to the Department of State 1
Montevideo, August 10, 1981, 1447Z
2876. Subject: Ambassador Kirkpatrick visit to Uruguay: Conversation with President-designate Alvarez.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary. Ambassador Kirkpatrick had a very cordial and successful meeting with President-designate Alvarez. 2 They agreed that bilateral relations had improved greatly under the Reagan Administration and that a mutual basis had been established for future cooperation. They discussed the need to search for improved hemispheric formulas of economic cooperation, primarily in trade, not aid. Ambassador Kirkpatrick promised to look into the leather apparel issue on her return. Alvarez said Uruguay wanted to assist and contribute to the achievement of President Reagan’s laudable global objectives. In response to Amb. Kirkpatrick’s suggestion, he indicated, albeit with some caution, that Uruguay might play a modest but constructive role in Central America. Discussion of the Sinai MFO issue, also very positive, reported septel. 3 End summary.
3. Bilateral relations. Ambassador Kirkpatrick opened by telling General Alvarez that President Reagan and Secretary of State Haig had asked her to extend their best wishes. No area in the world was more important to this administration than Latin America. And few areas were as important. More high-level officials of this administration were knowledgeable and concerned about hemispheric problems than in any prior administration. No one in this administration would confuse Uruguay with Mexico. We recognized Uruguay as important and unique. Latin America was an area with many common problems but also great differences in individual situations and in national approaches to resolving problems. There was a long history of friendship between the U.S. and Latin America. Nowhere had it been stronger than in Uruguay, with our many common traditions. Noting that there had been a recent “interruption” in our traditional friendship, Ambassador Kirkpatrick said she wanted to convey the deep sense of embarrassment in the administration regarding certain policies of the recent past. We knew the situation Uruguay faced had been difficult and that the government was now moving back toward Uruguay’s traditional constitutional rule. We considered ourselves friends and partners, and were determined to act in the future as equals.
General Alvarez thanked the Ambassador deeply. He assured her that he, personally, and Uruguay as a whole, had always viewed the U.S. as a friend. Although the recent past could be characterized as one full of disappointments and letdowns, which sometimes were difficult to forget, he felt deeply encouraged, not only by the words of the Ambassador, but by the actions of the Reagan Administration. President-elect Alvarez asked the Ambassador to tell President Reagan that he wished him nothing but the best in striving to accomplish his objectives. Alvarez pledged Uruguay’s cooperation “in any way possible” to help achieve these goals. He said we should regard his commitment to contribute positively as a “blank check.” Uruguay was a country that wanted only to live peacefully, to work and pursue its well being. It was not embarked on an indiscriminate arming of its armed forces, since Uruguay’s main concern was internal defense. Uruguay had managed to subdue terrorist activity once and, if necessary, would find the way to do it again. All he really wanted to ask of the U.S. was our assistance in ensuring that Uruguay would have the opportunity to live and work in peace. The problems Uruguay faced, he said, were not insoluable. They could be overcome by plain hard work.
Ambassador Kirkpatrick expressed her understanding of General Alvarez’ comments and stated she was fully aware of Uruguay’s unfortunate experience with the Tupamaro guerrilla movement and that the communist-inspired group had temporarily disrupted Uruguay’s democratic processes and institutions.
4. Hemispheric problems. Ambassador Kirkpatrick explained U.S. concern with Soviet expansionism throughout the world. The threat was now greatest in the Middle East and Central America. The preservation of peace was everyone’s responsibility. She briefly described the Cuban buildup in Central America. Given Uruguay’s successful experience in overcoming foreign-inspired terrorism, she said, perhaps Uruguay was in a position to give advice to countries like El Salvador, Guatemala and Costa Rica. Alvarez quipped, “Then they’ll probably accuse us of exporting counter-revolution,” but he clearly took the point and nodded his understanding and appreciation.
5. Economic issues. General Alvarez related that in the past the biggest problems Uruguay encountered were due to the internal capitalization of certain sectors of the Uruguayan economy, based on commitments which subsequently were ignored. How could he encourage those previously hurt once again to make new investments, so necessary for a prosperous economy. Export income from meat and wool sales in 1980 was not even sufficient to cover the cost of Uruguay’s petroleum imports. Although Uruguay’s coffers now were full of foreign currency and precious metals, it was due mostly to the ingenuity of Uruguayans who had been able to cope with an almost untenable economic situation. There were serious issues. Alvarez said he would like very much to get together with a senior economic representative of the U.S. Government to discuss these issues in detail. (The Foreign Minister later confirmed that the President-elect considers economic issues to be at the forefront of Uruguay’s national agenda and critical to its foreign relations. “I’ve been pushing economic themes more than most people, “He said, “But Gen. Alvarez will push them twice as hard.”) 4
Ambassador Kirkpatrick stated that although knowledgeable about some of Uruguay’s economic problems, she had not been aware prior to coming here of Uruguay’s problems with the U.S. International Trade Commission regarding leather exports and subsidies. She promised to pursue this issue at the highest levels when she returned, including with her friends Bill Brock and Mac Baldridge. She could not guarantee results, but said she would certainly raise the issue and was optimistic, at least so far as increased U.S. understanding of Uruguay’s situation and possibly of concrete results.
General Alvarez, referring to Ambassador Kirkpatrick’s expertise in history and geopolitics, noted that while the U.S. Marshall Plan had satisfied our allies’ material needs, communism continued to spread, because we had failed to confront it ideologically, in the battle for men’s minds. Latin Americans, because of their heritage, were very proud. American aid would not satisfy their needs either. Mutual cooperation, as partners, were therefore an essential cornerstone to future relations. Ambassador Kirkpatrick agreed, adding that since many of these hemispheric problems are, in fact, common ones, we have to find common solutions. The new U.S. attitude of cooperation had already been manifested by our unilateral actions such as affirmative votes on loans for the Southern Cone countries in the international banking institutions, by our support in the UN Human Rights Commission, and by the efforts of the Reagan Administration to lift restrictions on arms sales related to Uruguay and others. A main obstacle remained in the U.S. Congress, but we were making extensive efforts to move ahead and to cooperate. Ambassador Kirkpatrick affirmed she favored a broader basis of mutual economic cooperation. Perhaps this process could best be started on a bilateral basis with multilateral agreements with other countries sharing our objectives to follow.
General Alvarez fully concurred with the bilateral approach, taking problems one at a time. If successful, he said, others would want to join.
6. The remaining part of the conversation concerned the Sinai MFO issue, reported septel. The meeting ended on a very pleasant note with both participants expressing their profound best wishes for the success of the other.
7. Alvarez press comment. Following the meeting, President-designate Alvarez called in the large number of journalists, who formed around the two principals in a roped-off area at the back of the large room. In a statement which received considerable press play and subsequent attention in political circles, Alvarez said, quote “On the formal aspects of our conversation I must tell you that after speaking with Ambassador Kirkpatrick I am filled with happiness because it means to me the coming together again with a brother; with the brother we all admire so much, which is the United States of America. To depart from formality, I want to take the opportunity, in the person of this distinguished and talented lady, to pay homage to American women, specifically to the mothers, the wives, and to the sons of man of a country that has the responsibility of preserving and defending democracy and liberty in the world.” Unquote.
Smith
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D81372–0002. Secret; Immediate. Sent for information to Buenos Aires, Santiago, and USUN. Kirkpatrick visited Montevideo August 4–5.
2 In telegram 2771 from Montevideo, July 31, the Embassy wrote: “Mission sources confirm that retired General Gregorio Alvarez has been selected as Uruguay’s next President by the Junta of General Officers (JOOGG).” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810358–1200)
3 In telegram 2875 from Montevideo, August 10, the Embassy reported Kirkpatrick’s discussion with Álvarez about possible Uruguayan participation in the Sinai MFO. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810371–1074)
4 No memorandum of conversation has been found.

445. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay and the Mission to the United Nations 445. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay and the Mission to the United Nations 1
Washington, October 9, 1981, 2104Z
27112. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with Uruguayan Foreign Minister.
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. Summary: Secretary Haig met Uruguayan Foreign Minister Valdes Otero September 24 at the UN. The first half of the cordial meeting was devoted to a long monologue by Valdes in which he set forth his impressions of improving U.S./Uruguayan relations plus thoughts on terrorism; the international situation; Sinai MFO; bilateral economic relations; and multilateral relations. The Secretary replied that present USG positions reflected a popular mood which would not change; we will not express public criticism on human rights; both countries see eye to eye on Marxism, subversion and terrorism; the U.S. people and Congress strongly support the Sinai MFO; and the USG will cooperate closely on human rights reports, the Sinai MFO and hemispheric security issues. End summary.
3. The Secretary met with Foreign Minister Valdes Otero in the Secretary’s suite at the UN Plaza Hotel. The conversation lasted approximately one hour and was noteworthy for the high degree of candor and good will evident on both sides. Besides the principals, those attending were Uruguayan Ambassador Pacheco, Director of Political Affairs Posadas, and Embassy Minister Sordo; on the U.S. side were U.S. Ambassador-designate Aranda, notetaker Alberti and interpreter Seidenman.
4. The Secretary noted that the USG was grateful to Uruguay for insisting upon a return to the democratic process, thereby serving as a model of encouragement for the rest of Latin America. And, he noted, we are particularly grateful for the GOU’s courageous decision to participate in the Sinai MFO. Ambassador Kirkpatrick, he said, brought to his attention some trade issues which are outstanding between the two countries. 2 With the Secretary’s support, Ambassador Kirkpatrick will call STR Brock to improve the situation.
5. Improving bilateral relations. Valdes Otero responded that as a sign of improving relations, during the past several months there have been a number of high level visitors from the U.S. 3 The interesting and important thing about those visits is that each has built upon the earlier visits to improve relations further and each has had its own important characteristics. Improvement in bilateral relations is particularly important because on multilateral relations issues we already see eye-to-eye. The GOU had found common ground with visitors such as Middendorf, Ambassador Kirkpatrick and General Walters. 4 With all the GOU had held long and productive meetings. The present meeting between the Secretary and the Foreign Minister was seen as a logical culmination and further evidence of warming of bilateral relations. Valdes then embarked upon a rather lengthy exposition of a number of agenda items (which the GOU had made available to us prior to the meeting).
6. Terrorism. The Foreign Minister said that we may have the impression that the GOU’s interests in world affairs are disproportionate to the country’s small-size, but Uruguay has gained a nearly unique experience from which many countries may profit. Uruguay was one-of the first nations to confront an early, very serious terrorist attack and to surmount it (he drew a distinction between surmount and conquer). It is interesting to remember, he said, that President Alvarez was the first Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff with responsibilities for combating the terrorist threat. The GOU had meager material resources but utilized intelligence in the struggle. The GOU does not want to publicize the means it used to combat terrorism since the terrorists also learn by experience and may profit from exposition of such means. Ambassador Pacheco, then President, was the first to enunciate the principle that one must never negotiate with terrorists; this principle has since been adopted by many countries.
7. International situation. Concerning the international situation, Valdes said, the GOU is worried about other countries in Latin America who are either Marxist, pro-marxist, or who have not yet learned the hard lessons about Marxism and continue with perhaps good intentions to assist Marxism. Some of these countries carry on bilateral trade with socialist states to such an extent that they become dependent on them. Uruguayans have a genuine and legitimate interest in the global situation.
8. Sinai MFO. Participation in the Sinai MFO was for Uruguay a very difficult decision. But having taken that decision the GOU is committed. The philosophic reasons for the decision are clear: future conflicts will not be limited to the Middle East but will affect other areas of the world; Uruguay believes that small countries are often in a better position to act usefully than are great powers.
Valdes Otero continued to describe what the Uruguayan decision on MFO means and what it does not mean. He said the U.S. Embassy in Montevideo tried to get him to explain the GOU’s decision but that he had deliberately saved the explanation for the Secretary: it is an act of confidence and faith which, if the MFO should fail, could prove to be an act of naivete. In making this decision the GOU had to confront and set aside serious concerns:
A. If there should be new elections and a change of administration in the U.S., where would the GOU be then? History is not very reassuring in this regard.
B. Within the present administration, can the Department of State control the unfriendly acts of other departments?
C. Beyond the executive branch, is the administration able to control unfriendly acts by the Congress?
D. What influence can the administration exert on press and public opinion?
We must, therefore, see that this decision was indeed an act of confidence and faith. It would be fatuous to believe that the Congress and the media will refrain entirely from criticizing the MFO; but the GOU does believe that a much more favorable public attitude could be achieved by steadfast adherence to a clear policy by the Reagan Administration. Valdes said that the GOU expects that their friendship will be returned by friendship and they would appreciate a public statement from the USG expressing support for Uruguay.
9. Bilateral economic relations. The USG should recognize that under no circumstances can or will the Uruguayan economy harm the U.S. economy. Uruguay has an open economy, a market economy and monetary stability. By the same token, the GOU does not want to be harmed by those persons “lacking a sense of proportion” who seek punitive measures against Uruguay. The GOU was pleased by Ambassador Middendorf’s idea that U.S. private-investment in Uruguay should be stimulated. This could be the U.S.’s greatest contribution to Uruguay.
10. Multilateral relations. The GOU would like to see instituted a regime of regular consultations on bilateral, regional, and Sinai MFO issues. The U.S. and Uruguay should support each other on regional and global issues, and in multilateral fora. This does not mean that Uruguay would subordinate its independent international policy. We have different positions on Law of the Sea, Iraq, and North/South issues. Valdes pointed out that Uruguay had not changed its well-known positions in these regards. Uruguay seeks USG assistance in closing out the Uruguayan case in the UN Commission on Human Rights, for example. GOU has already begun its effort in the UN Secretariat; it would appreciate the U.S.’s help toward this end.
11. The Secretary responded to Valdes’ concerns on the vagaries of the U.S. political process. He said that even if the Reagan Administration fails to win the next election (which the Secretary does not expect), it would—be misleading to predict a shift in this basic public attitude; the magnitude of the change of the U.S. people’s mood should not be underestimated.
12. Concerning the U.S. position on human rights, the Secretary assured Valdes that this administration will not, as a matter of principle, rely on public criticism to achieve its human rights objectives. If there is need for criticism, and there may well be, these criticisms will be brought to the GOU’s attention quietly and frankly, in the spirit of friendship.
13. Regarding the convergence and coordination of policies, especially policies concerning the common global threat, the Secretary said the GOU can be sure that its participation, in the Sinai MFO will be most helpful. But even without MFO we know that both countries have a common view toward Marxism, subversion and terrorism. Naivete will not solve this problem which in fact will be with us for a long time to come because the Soviets and their friends will never cease probing for our weak spots. This fact serves for us as an ongoing and unifying bond which will endure because the motive and need for it will continue.
14. The U.S. people and Congress will consider the Sinai MFO as a positive and favorable development, which will only redound to the credit of those participating. The USG is endeavoring to expand the base of contributors to give the MFO a broader character. Talks held this week with both Begin and Sadat give the USG some cause for confidence that the Camp David process and the autonomy negotiations will succeed. Nations with the courage and vision to participate in the Middle East peace efforts will be judged favorably by history and will never incur discredit.
15. The GOU knows where the U.S. stands concerning human rights reports. Recent visitors to Uruguay including Ambassador Kirkpatrick, General Walters and Ambassador Middendorf are all of the same view, as is Ambassador Enders. The Secretary urged the Foreign Minister to call to his attention any exceptions in the USG he might encounter. He then assured Valdes of the closest U.S. cooperation and coordination not only on the Sinai MFO but on the overall hemispheric security issue. The U.S., he said, is losing patience with Fidel Castro and is also concerned by the apparent Nicaraguan slide into Marxist totalitarianism. We desire to work jointly with other Latin American Governments and to profit from the Uruguay experience in countering subversion.
16. In closing, the Secretary noted that our new Ambassador to Uruguay had been hand-picked to serve as an extension of this administration’s views. He expressed his admiration and gratitude for Uruguay’s courage and said that Ambassador Pacheco should feel free to call on him at any time.
17. Ambassador-designate Aranda assured the Uruguayan delegation that the Secretary a few days earlier had expressed exactly the same views to him and to Ambassador Boeker (FSI), and pointed out that these were not views enunciated especially for the Uruguayan audience. Mr. Aranda assured the Uruguayans of his belief that Uruguay may in the future emerge as the major success story for the Reagan Administration’s Latin American policy.
Clark

446. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 446. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 1
Washington, November 3, 1983, 0959Z
313724. Subject: Meeting with Colorado Party Leader Dr. Julio Sanguinetti.
1. (C—Entire text).
2. Summary: On October 11 HA Assistant Secretary Elliott Abrams met with Colorado Party Leader Dr. Julio Sanguinetti. Sanguinetti reviewed recent political developments in Uruguay and commented on the now suspended political negotiations with the military. 2 He expressed concern for the problems created by the personal ambitions of President Alvarez. 3 Sanguinetti commented that the practice of maltreatment and torture of political detainees continues, although he believes that the number of incidents has declined. He stated that a majority of the leaders of both political parties believe that the proscription of a few prominent politicians such as Wilson Ferreira should not be permitted to block an accord with the military. Sanguinetti opined that US policy toward Uruguay has been good and the US should not act in a manner that would affect its ability to influence the GOU. Sanguinetti said that many political prisoners have been imprisoned for periods disproportionate to the seriousness of their crimes, and they should be released, whereas others would be in jail for their serious crimes under any government. He also said that he did not consider the human rights organization Servicio Paz Y Justicia to be subversive, but said he keeps his distance from them because some of their leaders had previously expressed sympathy for the Tupamaros. End summary.
3. Colorado Party Secretary General, Dr. Julio Sanguinetti, met with HA Assistant Secretary Elliott Abrams and HA officers on October 11. After a brief statement by Abrams expressing US support for the democratization process in Uruguay, Sanguinetti said that after ten years of problems in the area of human rights under this military government there have been some recent improvements. Many problems continue, however, particularly in the areas of freedom of the press, freedom of association and mistreatment of detained individuals. Sanguinetti added that human rights is part of the general problem in Uruguay. When the democratization process is brought to a successful conclusion, the question of human rights will automatically be resolved.
4. Sanguinetti reviewed developments in Uruguay since the defeat of the 1980 draft constitution. He said that the current talks between the military and the political parties regarding amendments to the constitution, broke down on July 5 because the military insisted on retaining a number of prerogatives, such as the right to cordon off an area to search for suspects and the right to invade homes at night. There were a total of twenty-four proposed amendments that were unacceptable.
5. Sanguinetti said that the parties expected that, as part of this dialogue with the military, the number of people proscribed would decrease; instead the number of proscriptions has increased. This made it impossible for the political parties to continue with the dialogue or to resume it once it was stopped. The military had tried to get the political parties to resume talks, and some senior military officials visited Sanguinetti recently at his home just before his departure for the U.S. in an effort to convince him to resume the discussions, but Sanguinetti explained to the generals that the atmosphere generated by the proscription of Eladio Fernandez and by the closing of the weekly publications made this impossible. Sanguinetti said that the military did not seem to understand that proscriptions were an important issue and that these actions made it impossible for the political parties to justify to their rank and file a return to the political dialogue. Sanguinetti continued that before a dialogue can resume, the military must take actions such as ending Constitutional Act No. 7, which allows the government to fire any public employee for political reasons, and ending the practice of closing newspapers. He continued that the political parties are willing to run the risk of antagonizing the military in order to obtain some concessions before returning to the bargaining table.
6. Sanguinetti said that the worst thing the military can do would be to interrupt the political process. This would do to the Uruguayan military what the Malvinas defeat did for the Argentine military (inferring that they would be totally discredited in public opinion).
7. Sanguinetti then reviewed the differences between President Alvarez and the rest of the military. He said that Alvarez is personally responsible for the recent banning and the closing of the journals, and he takes a far harder position than do the rest of the senior officers in the military. According to Sanguinetti, 90 percent of the problem in reaching agreement with the military is a result of the personal ambition of President Alvarez, and Alvarez himself encourages the other 10 percent of the problem which is caused by military fear of change. Since some officers have been tried in Argentina, the military has a natural fear, encouraged by Alvarez, that the Uruguayan political parties would try to do the same thing once democracy is restored in Uruguay. The political party leaders have talked very frankly with the military saying that they do not intend to take any revenge for previous actions, but Alvarez has told them that the parties may not be able to control their rank and file.
8. Asked to go into some more detail on the prevalence of torture, Sanguinetti said that torture has not been eliminated but the number of incidents has decreased. He said that dictatorships generally create situations in which there is little respect among the police for the people. Moreover, there are certain police groups which use torture in their fight against terrorists. Since terrorism has declined, the use of torture has declined as well. Nevertheless, two months ago a group of youthful communist activists started to organize themselves and were arrested and placed before a military court. This was wrong because they were neither terrorists nor members of the military and therefore should have been tried by civil authorities. Sanguinetti stated that they were definitely maltreated while in the custody of the military, thereby demonstrating that torture still does occur.
9. On the subject of Wilson Ferreira, Sanguinetti said the Ferreira question is one of the most serious problems to be resolved between the politicians and the military. The military are insistent that they will not accept his return to political life. Sanguinetti believes that the Colorado Party as well as a majority of the Blanco Party, including the leadership of the party, are in agreement that the question of the political status of one or two people like Wilson Ferreira cannot be allowed to stand in the way of reaching an agreement with the military on a return to democratic government. Sanguinetti added that he recently had met with Ferreira in Bolivia and, not surprisingly, Ferreira did not agree. Ferreira believes that the Colorado Party would prefer to have him outside the country because it would then be easier for the Colorados to win their election. Sanguinetti differed, saying that he beat Ferreira in 1971 and it would be easier to beat him again then to run against Ferreira’s stand-in, who could exploit the political issue of Ferreira’s proscription. Asked about the Colorado Party leader, Jorge Batlle, Sanguinetti said that like Ferreira, Batlle remains banned, but he does live in Montevideo and despite legal proscriptions against him does participate normally in internal Colorado Party activities. He cannot, however, be a candidate in the 1984 elections. Asked whether he thought the proscription against Batlle might be removed, Sanguinetti replied that if the proscription against Juan Ferreira remained in effect, for purposes of balance the proscription against Batlle would probably remain in effect. Sanguinetti continued that he has told Wilson Ferreira’s son, Juan, that it might be best for ten people to remain proscribed than for the democratization process to be torpedoed as a result.
10. Asked for his opinion of U.S. policy and U.S. public statements on the movement toward democratic government in Uruguay, Sanguinetti responded that he thought the U.S. position has been a good one. The U.S., he said, should act in a way that will not affect its ability to influence the GOU. Sanguinetti then recalled that under President Carter the U.S. used to criticize the GOU publicly. This angered the military but made the people happy. With the election of President Reagan the military were happy, and in fact the Navy Commander said that the Carter nightmare was over. The Uruguayan military were then surprised at the position of the Reagan Administration. In particular he considered a U.S. statement issued after the August 2, 1983 suspension of public political activity to be excellent. 4 Although the military prohibited the daily newspapers from publishing it, the influential weekly political journals, which operate in a legally nebulous area, published the statement, and the government did not retaliate. Sanguinetti added that at times the political parties would like the U.S. to exert more pressure, but they realize that the U.S. cannot do so without losing its influence with the GOU.
11. Asked about the status of political prisoners in Uruguay, Sanguinetti said that he divides them into three categories. The first consists of typical political prisoners, such as General Seregni, who was a leftist candidate for President in 1971. He was later arrested and accused of participating in a public disturbance in 1973 and of being a traitor because, as a military officer, he joined the Communist Party. Sanguinetti said that Seregni’s crime warranted a maximum of 6 months in prison, maybe even a year if it were a very serious disturbance, but certainly not 8 years in prison. A democratic government would let people like Seregni out immediately, and it would be far better for the military to begin this process while they are still in office.
12. The second category consists of those people who would be imprisoned under any government because of their having participated in violent acts such as terrorism. These cases, however, should be reviewed and the number reduced to the bare minimum. The third group consists of prisoners who may have belonged to subversive organizations but who either did not receive a fair trial or who have already served their sentences. A democratic government would have all these cases reviewed and liberate most of these people.
13. Asked to comment on the cases of Massera and Hiber Conteras, Sanguinetti said that he is very familiar with Massera and in fact served in the Congress with him. Massera is a communist mathematician who attended Berkeley and is now 65 years old. Sanguinetti opined that he clearly falls in the first category. He has never been accused of any crime other than of being a member of the Communist Party which was a legal party when he was a member.
14. Sanguinetti said that he was not very familiar with the Conteras case. He knows that Conteras was a writer and a journalist, but although he read some of his material he never met him. He believes that Conteras would fall in the third category and a judge should review his case.
15. On the subject of human rights groups, Sanguinetti said that the recently formed Human Rights Commission is composed of important and prestigious democratic personalities such as Dr. Reta, a former university professor and specialist in penal law, who is no longer allowed to teach at the university. This newly formed group has not yet done anything, but it will be important simply because of the prominence of its members.
16. Regarding Servicio Paz y Justicio, Sanguinetti said it is composed of people who have taken a more confrontational position. The Colorado Party shares their general views but has reservations about the group because some of them, such as Father Perez Aguirre, were sympathetic to the Tupamaros during the terrorist period. Although they were not then nor are now a subversive group, Sanguinetti said he has kept his distance from them because of their political leanings.
Shultz

447. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 447. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 1
Washington, October 10, 1984, 0307Z
299847. Subject: Maeso/Motley UNGA bilateral.
1. Confidential (Entire text).
2. Summary: Foreign Minister Maeso had no real agenda for his October 4 bilateral with Assistant Secretary Motley, but his message was clear: You’ll miss us when we’re gone. Maeso noted the warmth of our bilateral relations in recent years 2 but cautioned that the US could expect relations to cool in the future, regardless of who wins the election. Maeso warned that Cuba will help revivify the left in Uruguay and that this will contribute to more strident criticism of U.S. policy in Central America, among other things. He noted that the Blanco Party is already moving to the left under the leadership of Wilson Ferreira’s son, Juan Raul. 3 According to Maeso, the natural custodians of democracy in Uruguay are the armed forces, and he urged that the US enhance relations with them. Maeso characterized negotiations with the IMF as going badly and asked the US to “talk to the IMF” on Uruguay’s behalf. As he said good-bye, Maeso invited the USG to send observers to the November elections.
3. On October 4, 1984 Assistant Secretary Motley, accompanied by the desk officer, met with Foreign Minister Maeso, Uruguayan UN Ambassador Juan Carlos Blanco and MFA Foreign Policy Director Alvaro Alvarez. The Foreign Minister initiated the conversation by noting that this would be his last UNGA and then commented on the state of U.S.-Uruguayan relations. In the hour long conversation that followed, most of which was guided by a few questions from Ambassador Motley, Maeso made the following points:
Our bilateral relations will cool: Maeso predicted that with the help of conservative former President Pacheco, the Colorados will win in November. No matter who wins, however, the US should expect cooler relations with Uruguay as the country repudiates the current regime and looks toward Europe for support. Maeso urged the US to be patient. He believes that institutional and political ties forged between our countries in recent years are now too strong to be entirely undone or forgotten.
Relations with Brazil will cool: Maeso expects the next government to tilt towards Argentina. He asserted, however, that his government’s efforts to strengthen ties with Brazil would endure, in part because Brazil would not permit otherwise. 4
The left will gain ground in Uruguay: Maeso warned that after the elections relations are likely to be restored with Cuba, which will cause problems for Uruguay and the U.S. alike. Cuba will abet the left in its efforts to extend its influence internally. In foreign policy this will lead to more strident criticism of U.S. policy in Central America among other things. According to Maeso, there are 1,500 Uruguayans with Tupamaro connections in Cuba. GOU intelligence services indicate that for the last eight months former Uruguayan guerrillas and sympathizers have been drifting back towards home. Maeso is particularly concerned that the left will be able to play a spoiler role in the Uruguayan Parliament, where he does not expect moderates to control more than 40 of the votes even if the Colorados win. (The remaining 60 would presumably be composed of leftists drawn primarily from the majority faction of the Blanco Party and the leftist coalition Frente Amplio.)
Maintain ties with the military: The Foreign Minister urged that the U.S. enhance relations with the armed forces, which he characterized as perhaps not up to the job of governing but not corrupt and certainly not tainted with human rights abuses as in Argentina. He does not believe that the Uruguayan military will be infiltrated by the left as has happened elsewhere in Latin America because in Uruguay the armed forces are “the natural custodians of democracy.” Six months after the inauguration, Maeso predicted, people will longingly remember the tranquility afforded by the current government.
Assistant Secretary Motley noted that he and Ambassador Aranda shared the view that we should make an effort to shore up the confidence of Uruguay’s military and demonstrate that there are rewards in returning the country to its democratic traditions. The Foreign Minister agreed and praised Ambassador Aranda for his warmth and the exceptional way he has integrated himself into the Uruguayan scene.
We need help from our friends: In response to queries about the IMF negotiation, Maeso noted that negotiations are going badly. Despite sound economic policies and a responsible posture on regional debt, Uruguay now finds itself alone—“like Adam without Eve”—as it tries to negotiate with the IMF. Vegh is demoralized and angered by the IMF’s refusal to consider less tangible, but equally important, social and political factors in negotiating a new stand-by agreement. A failure to gain IMF approval will undermine moderates in the elections and increase anti-American feeling, the Foreign Minister warned. He then asked for the U.S. to “talk to the IMF” on Uruguay’s behalf.
Ambassador Motley noted that the IMF staff is stretched thin but is gradually coming to understand the importance of the socio-political factors to which Maeso referred. For that reason it is essential that the GOU continues its talks with the fund, as difficult and frustrating as this may be. As for talking to the fund, Motley pointed out that contrary to common belief, we do not own it and indeed must be very circumspect in our dealings with its officials. The most anyone could do would be to address the Uruguayan case at the margins and in the context of broader concerns. This we could and would do.
4. As the meeting closed Maeso said he would not attend the Panamanian inauguration on his way home but would visit Costa Rica instead. He also extended an invitation for the USG to send observers for the elections in November. Uruguay, he noted pointedly, has a tradition of welcoming serious observers who do not come with preconceived notions. 5
Shultz

Mr. Sanguinetti’s election Sunday in Uruguay’s first presidential elections since 1971 provided the vindication of this strategy: two outspoken political leaders were banned as candidates, but the dictatorship should end on March 1.” (Alan Riding, “Peacemaker For Uruguay: Julio María Sanguinetti,” New York Times , November 27, 1984, p. 448. Article in Latin America Review 448. Article in Latin America Review 1
ALA LAR 85–003 Washington, January 18, 1985
Uruguay: Sanguinetti and the Military
The Uruguayan armed forces will turn over power to President-elect Julio Sanguinetti on 1 March, ending more than 11 years of military rule. Predominantly conservative and anti-Communist, the military strongly favored Sanguinetti’s centrist Colorado Party over its center-left and leftist rivals in the November 1984 election. Sanguinetti has indicated that he will generally pursue cautious policies to avoid angering the armed forces. Military leaders, however, may be displeased with some steps he is likely to take in the areas of foreign policy and the military budget.
According to various sources, some officers have remained opposed to the restoration of civilian rule, but most support it out of desire to shed responsibility for the country’s worsening economic difficulties. The armed forces have also been influenced by the return of democracy in neighboring Argentina and other South American countries.
The Commander in Chief of the Army, Gen. Hugo Medina, has been a key figure in the transition process. [ less than 1 line not declassified ] Medina has strongly supported Sanguinetti, believing him to be the only politician capable of leading the country through a smooth transition. Medina, said to be highly respected within the armed forces, reportedly is working hard to convince fellow officers that they have no reason to fear civilian rule under Sanguinetti. [ 4 lines not declassified ]
Areas of Agreement
Implementation of the 1984 Accord . Sanguinetti apparently plans to honor the agreement that he and his party reached with the armed forces in August 1984, paving the way for the restoration of civilian rule. The accord—which was strongly opposed by the Colorados’ main rival, the center-left Blanco Party—provides for limited military participation in the new government during its first year in office. For example, it calls for the formation of a National Security Council, composed of both military and civilian members, that will serve in an advisory capacity. It also grants the armed forces a voice in appointments of senior military officers. A constituent assembly will be convened later this year to work out constitutional reforms that would replace these transitional arrangements.
Amnesty for Political Prisoners . According to a variety of sources, the military supports Sanguinetti’s approach to the sensitive issue of amnesty for political prisoners. While the Blancos and the leftist Broad Front coalition, Uruguay’s third main party, advocate a general and unconditional amnesty for all of the approximately 500 people imprisoned on political grounds, Sanguinetti favors immediate release only for those detained since the 1973 military takeover. The cases of those jailed earlier on charges of involvement in far-left guerrilla activity against elected Uruguayan governments will be considered by the courts on an individual basis. Military acceptance of Sanguinetti’s policy on this issue is reflected in the outgoing regime’s recent decision, reported by the US Embassy, to free many of the post-1973 political prisoners before leaving office. 2
Punishment for Human Rights Abuses . Many members of the Blanco and Broad Front parties have demanded that the new government use civilian courts to prosecute military officers accused of committing human rights violations during the period of military rule. Sanguinetti and the Colorados, however, will not pursue civilian prosecution of these officers, but will leave the question of trials to the military courts, according to US Embassy reporting. This is the approach that military leaders have insisted upon.
Economic Policy . We believe the Sanguinetti government also will pursue economic policies acceptable to the military as it confronts a four-year-old recession, inflation that is 70 percent and rising, 15 percent unemployment, and a $5.5 billion foreign debt. Sanguinetti and other Colorado leaders have indicated that they are not planning any sharp departures from current economic policies. According to US Embassy and other reporting, 3 the new government will try to stimulate private industry and preserve the outgoing regime’s free-market approach. Although the Colorados may seek greater government control over the banking sector, they do not favor bank nationalization as advocated by the Blancos and the Broad Front. The Colorados plan to look to the United States and other Western countries for aid and trade concessions.
Labor . Sanguinetti’s policy toward the restive labor unions is also likely to meet with the approval of the anti-Communist military leadership. His goal is to weaken the strong influence that Uruguayan Communists have long wielded within the labor movement. His plan, which he has discussed publicly, is to seek legislation providing for secret-ballot voting in strike calls and elections of union leaders. Sanguinetti apparently believes that such a reform would lead eventually to a more moderate and more pluralistic labor movement. According to US Embassy reporting, however, the new government may delay introducing its planned labor legislation to give moderates within the unions time to get organized. 4
Areas of Potential Disagreement
Although a variety of reporting indicates that Sanguinetti for the most part will follow policies palatable to the armed forces, for political reasons he may take some foreign initiatives that would not be well received by military leaders. Moreover, budget considerations may force him to cut military spending below the levels sought by leaders of the armed forces.
Foreign Policy . According to US Embassy [ less than 1 line not declassified ] Sanguinetti’s foreign policy will emphasize ties with the United States, Western Europe, and Uruguay’s South American neighbors. Nevertheless, [ less than 1 line not declassified ] he also intends to reestablish diplomatic relations with Cuba, expand cultural and commercial ties with the USSR, and possibly upgrade relations with Nicaragua by permitting Managua to open an embassy in Montevideo. In addition, we believe he may periodically criticize US policy in Central America
Sanguinetti recognizes that the military would be displeased by such actions. He probably reasons, however, that military commanders generally support him and are unlikely to challenge his diplomatic initiatives early in his administration. He apparently is more concerned with keeping leftist political opponents off balance through measures that reflect an independent foreign policy.
Military Budget . Because of the financial squeeze facing Uruguay, the new government almost certainly will closely examine military spending. It is likely to impose restrictions on military wage increases and on purchases of new equipment and supplies. Sanguinetti also has said publicly that he will gradually reduce the number of military personnel, which has nearly doubled to 68,000 in recent years. Although these policies run the risk of sparking military resentment, Sanguinetti has indicated that he will proceed as cautiously and flexibly as possible and look for ways to soften the impact of cuts.
Outlook
We believe that the new government’s overall policy mix will be accepted by the armed forces, at least during the early stages of civilian rule. Moreover, Sanguinetti is an adroit, careful politician who is likely to pursue his policies with prudence and flexibility. He will also be careful to appoint commanders who favor keeping the military out of government and focused on professional pursuits.
The danger of a military move against Sanguinetti will grow if he proves unable over time to ease Uruguay’s economic troubles and bring labor unrest under control. A resurgence of leftist-led political violence stemming from a prolonged period of ineffectual civilian rule would further heighten the chances of military intervention.

449. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Mission in Geneva 449. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Mission in Geneva 1
Montevideo, March 2, 1985, 0324Z
Secto 4020. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with Uruguayan President-elect Sanguinetti.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary: During one-hour meeting with President Sanguinetti Secretary congratulated Sanguinetti on the return to democracy in Uruguay, and promised our support. Sanguinetti made special plea for support in economic area and for help in closing UNHCR books on Uruguay. 2 He said Central America was special problem, and GOU policy would be based on quest for peace and justice. He expressed concern that Central America, and especially Ortega’s request to meet with Secretary in Uruguay, not become an issue in our relations.
3. Sanguinetti opened the meeting by explaining the importance of this day to Uruguay. Uruguay has the oldest democratic tradition in Latin America, and the re-establishment of democratic institutions is important not only here but throughout the region. But democracy would not solve all problems, especially the economic ones. The policies of the new government would be moderate. He anticipated good relations with the U.S. and asked for support and understanding, especially in the economic area. He emphasized the need for investment and market access, but acknowledged the legal constraints on our policies. He noted that Uruguay’s economy had declined dramatically in the past three years, and it would be difficult for the people to accept new austerity programs. He made a commitment to seek agreement with the IMF, World Bank and commercial banks. He emphasized that to pay debt Uruguay must export, to export, Uruguay must have investment.
4. In foreign policy, Uruguay would not depart from traditional lines. The GOU would join no bloc, support peace and maintain relations with other nations on a non-ideological basis. Agreement had been reached to re-established relations with Venezuela, and discussions have started with Cuba. He recognized the risks of relations with Cuba, and would establish clear limits on their presence and activities in Uruguay.
5. On Central America, the GOU would support international law and peace. Ortega of Nicaragua seeks relations with GOU, seeks a meeting with U.S. in Montevideo. The GOU does not want to mediate between the Nicaraguans and U.S. in any meeting in Montevideo.
6. In his response, the Secretary noted the importance of March first as the day democracy returned to Uruguay. He promised U.S. support and understanding because of the special importance of the return to democracy in Latin America for President Reagan and the USG. The Secretary recognized the special importance of economic recovery to the preservation of democracy, agreed growth was the answer to the debt and recession problems, and reiterated our promise of support. He said there were limits on what we can do, but offered to cooperate in any possible way. Most important to recovery, however, were GOU policies.
7. The U.S. is the most open market in the world, continued the Secretary. Citing figures on the increase of Uruguayan exports to the U.S., he said we are already doing a lot. We are not protectionist, but have some problems in special areas, such as textiles.
8. The Secretary delivered a letter from President Reagan inviting President Sanguinetti to make a state visit to the U.S. later this year. 3 He explained that the invitation was not only important as a symbol of our support for Uruguay, but also would enable Sanguinetti to convey a message to U.S. businessmen and investors. He promised to do everything we can to make the trip a success.
9. Sanguinetti expressed appreciation for the invitation and reiterated his interest in good relations with the U.S.
10. In the area of human rights, Sanguinetti said the problem was solved for Uruguay. He said the issue of human rights in Uruguay was being considered this week in Geneva by the UNHCR and asked for our support in closing the books on Uruguay. He said the matter was urgent because if no decisions were reached this week, Uruguay would be maintained on the UN’s problem list for some time to come. He asked for our support.
11. Returning to the problem of Nicaragua, the Secretary said we had received no request for a meeting in Montevideo. He said we would meet if requested, but emphasized he was here in Montevideo to celebrate the return of democracy, not to meet with Ortega. The presence of Ortega in Montevideo on this occasion was ironic because Nicaragua was one of the few exceptions to the return of democracy in Latin America. With respect to relations with Cuba, the Secretary said this was a Uruguayan decision. He warned of the risks, however, and noted that Cuba uses embassies as a base for interfering in the internal affairs of the host country. He offered to provide information on this if requested.
12. In closing, the Secretary reiterated our support for democracy in Uruguay. We want to find ways to help the new GOU within our limitations. He said the U.S. wanted to work with the GOU on specific ideas for cooperation in the economic area, and said consultations could begin soon. Sanguinetti responded the GOU was working on specific ideas as well and looked forward to discussions.
13. As the meeting broke up, Assistant Secretary Motley returned to the problem of Nicaragua. He said we had received no request for a meeting, the U.S. delegation and the Nicaraguan delegation were in the same hotel, and we would be ready to meet provided the request didn’t come too late. Foreign Minister Iglesias said he would convey this message to the Nicaraguans.
Shultz

450. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 450. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 1
March 2, 1985, 2137Z
Secto 4032. Subject: Memorandum for the President: My talks with the Uruguayan leaders.
1. Secret: Entire text.
2. Memorandum for: The President.
3. From: George P. Shultz.
4. Subject: My talks with the Uruguayan leaders.
5. In three separate sessions in Montevideo this morning I met with the Vice President and Congressional leaders, the new economic team, and the Foreign Minister. 2
6. All seem to be over their post inauguration euphoria. Justifiably proud of their return to democracy, they are sobered by the difficulties of making it work in the face of serious economic deterioration. The Finance Minister impressed me with his very persuasive recitation of the current situation, and the long downhill slide Uruguay has seen in the last ten years. The GDP has declined 15 percent in the last three years and real wages are one-half of ten years ago. Uruguay was an early leader in establishing a welfare state. The Uruguayan leadership recognizes that it has a lot of internal housekeeping to do and (a refreshing change) that direct foreign capital investment (as opposed to loans) is the needed ingredient to invoke growth. Good intentions notwithstanding, they have a significant challenge ahead.
7. The Foreign Minister appears as capable as the Finance Minister but in a more open, effusive way. He’s well schooled in the international economic arena and I think they’ll be a strong team.
8. In summary, in my initial evaluation of the new government I count as pluses their aim to see direct foreign investment as a needed boost, a capable foreign policy and financial team, and a generally moderate albeit independent foreign policy. Among the minuses, the hint that Sanguinetti likes to appeal to leftist elements among his constituencies and the attendant dangers of posturing being turned into policy, as in the recognition of Cuba, announced the day of the inaugural. 3
Shultz

451. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 451. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 1
New York, September 25, 1985, 2317Z
Secto 19025. Subject: The Secretary’s meeting with President Sanguinetti of Uruguay.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Summary: The Secretary’s meeting with President Sanguinetti addressed the domestic situation in Uruguay, where political conditions have stabilized but labor unrest is on the rise, and the country’s foreign debt and trade problems. 2 Sanguinetti emphasized the need to increase trade and expressed concern about protectionism, especially on the part of the EC, whose agricultural subsidies are undercutting Uruguayan exports. Sanguinetti expressed gratitude for U.S. support for Uruguay’s debt rescheduling and other efforts to help, including the recent modest cheese quota and congressional authorization for dols 30 million in economic assistance in FY 86 and FY 87. The Secretary said he hoped the Congress would appropriate the funds needed to implement the authorization. Foreign Minister Iglesias renewed his proposal for informal private talks with selected leaders on the debt and trade issues; the Secretary described the complexity of the current situation and said the U.S. was carefully studying how best to proceed.
3. On Central America, Sanguinetti said the new Contadora support group was intended to clarify public understanding of the Contadora process and that his government was pressing the Nicaraguans on the issues of Soviet influence and internal democracy. The Secretary asked for continued Uruguayan cooperation on UN issues. Sanguinetti reiterated his desire to accept President Reagan’s invitation for a state visit to Washington; 3 the Secretary explained that the President’s operation and recovery had required changes in his schedule and the visit had to be rescheduled. Tone of meeting was cordial throughout. End summary.
4. Participants: The Secretary met with President Sanguinetti of Uruguay at 9:00 a.m., Tuesday, September 24, at the Uruguayan UN Mission. The Secretary was accompanied by USUN Ambassador Walters, ARA Assistant Secretary Abrams, ARA/SC Director Durkee note taker, Deputy Press Spokesman Redmond, White House fellow Karen Galatz, and Stephanie van Riegersberg interpreter. President Sanguinetti was accompanied by Foreign Minister Iglesias, UN Ambassador Lupinacci, and Ambassador to the US Luisi.
5. Situation in Uruguay: After opening pleasantries, Sanguinetti offered six-month assessment of developments since last discussion in Montevideo at March inauguration: 4
— Country was stabilizing, with democratic institutions functioning and political parties cooperating;
— Main domestic problem was labor unrest, but this was understandable after twelve years of repression;
— Internal dynamic was somewhat unbalanced, with business sector too discouraged and labor leaders too aggressive;
— GOU working to restore balance, first by getting agreement on external debt, which has been accomplished through negotiations with IMF, World Bank, and private banks, with next priority to ease domestic debt problems.
6. U.S. support for refinancing: The Secretary noted that the US had tried to be supportive of Uruguay’s debt rescheduling; Sanguinetti expressed appreciation for the U.S. efforts.
7. Trade problems: Sanguinetti went on to stress GOU concerns over foreign trade problems, which he described as more important than debt problems. Uruguay’s export earnings were down 10 percent so far in 1985; worse yet, prices of Uruguay’s major export products still declining, thanks to European subsidized competition. He had spoken same morning to French Foreign Minister to explain problem and to insist that EC had to make changes, but no solution in sight. As example, he mentioned that Brazil was offered EC meat at dols 600 per ton, a price that was about half the market price, and Uruguay simply could not compete under such conditions; the EC had 900,000 tons of meat in storage, and that overhang depressed the market.
8. U.S. support on trade: The Secretary agreed that European agricultural policies caused serious distortions and encouraged Sanguinetti to continue to make that point to EC leaders. He noted that the U.S. had opened up the U.S. cheese market to Uruguay through a modest quota, and added his hope that the Congress would provide the appropriation needed to implement the authorization for ESF for Uruguay. Sanguinetti expressed understanding of the difference between authorizations and appropriations.
9. Congressional visits: The Secretary said we had encouraged congressional leaders to visit Uruguay and were pleased that Senator Lugar had returned with very positive impressions. 5 Sanguinetti agreed that such visits were very useful to gain clear understanding of complexity of managing transition following long period of dictatorship. Radical rhetoric was to be expected in such conditions, but Uruguayan voters had demonstrated moderation. This tendency was reinforced by positive Argentine example.
10. New GATT round: The Secretary expressed appreciation for GOU support for new GATT round, noting that best defense against protectionist pressures was good offense in favor of opening markets to trade. The U.S. still offers world’s most open market, and we want other countries to open theirs as well, in order to provide quote level playing field unquote. Sanguinetti said GOU had similar view of objectives but was concerned about pace of events. The situation in Latin America could change quickly, as in Mexican and Peruvian cases, and it was important not to lose more time.
11. Informal debt dialogue: Iglesias recalled discussion in Montevideo regarding possible informal, private discussions about approaches to trade and debt problems, noting that the U.S. had concentrated on strengthening the international financial institutions. This was important but more needed to be done, since new money infusions from private banks were declining sharply. Quiet, informal discussions could help clarify thinking on new approaches and improve coordination. The Secretary said he understood the point and USG was working hard to find practical solutions. Respective roles of IMF and IBRD had to be clarified. Public perception of lead role of IMF led to misconception that debt problem could be resolved through monetary adjustments, when in fact debt problems needed to be addressed in ways in which World Bank was key factor. Debt-ridden countries did not need more debt but rather more equity, which meant that more had to be done to attract capital, especially capital from the affected countries that had gone abroad. These were complex questions, and the U.S. continued to study how best to address them.
12. Central America: Recalling Ambassador Shlaudeman’s recent visit to Montevideo and other capitals of the Contadora support group, the Secretary asked for Uruguayan views of Central American situation. 6 Sanguinetti replied that GOU supported the Contadora process and wanted new support group to be helpful. New initiatives were sometimes misunderstood, as when the Cartagena group was initially misperceived as a debtors cartel, but Contadora support group was intended to clarify public understanding in Latin America of Central American issues. For GOU, avoiding armed conflict in Central America was domestic issue as well as foreign policy problem. The GOU has pressed Nicaragua, most recently in discussions with VP Ramirez, on two key points: First, Soviet influence is very negative; second, there must be progress toward internal democracy. Ramirez understands these points, but the commandantes have the power. The GOU understands the U.S. position that there will be no military intervention but U.S. will support those forces putting pressure on Sandinistas. It was very important that such activities be perceived as consistent with international principles and standards of conduct, since small countries were often forced to depend on such principles more than major powers.
The Secretary expressed appreciation for the Uruguayan comments and said we wanted to stay in close touch through Ambassador Shlaudeman.
13. UN affairs: The Secretary explained that the U.S. took seriously what happened at the UN, for which reason we had assigned one of our best Ambassadors. We appreciate past cooperation with the GOU and looked forward to working closely with them at this session of the UNGA.
14. State visit: Sanguinetti said he had very much appreciated President Reagan’s kind words on receiving the credentials of Ambassador Luisi. 7 He reiterated his desire to accept the invitation to visit Washington. The Secretary said we were looking forward to that visit and explained that the President’s unexpected surgery and need to recuperate had disrupted his schedule. The President has recovered well, but the visit has had to be rescheduled for later.
Shultz

452. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 452. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA 86–10001 Washington, January 1986
[Omitted here is a title page.]
Uruguay: Challenges to Democracy
Julio Sanguinetti, who in March 1985 became Uruguay’s first civilian President in 12 years, is facing growing challenges as he attempts to govern the smallest of South America’s fledgling democracies. Although Sanguinetti initially enjoyed the support and cooperation of opposition party leaders and the military, much of that support is dissipating as economic problems and attendant pressures mount:
• The deteriorating economy—Uruguay registered 3.5-percent negative growth in 1985—is, in our view, Sanguinetti’s most serious long-term problem. High inflation and unemployment, coupled with a decline in exports and a foreign debt burden of $5 billion, have convinced the President to implement unpopular austerity measures recommended by the IMF. The recently signed agreement with the Fund enables Uruguay to reschedule its debt, but its call for belt-tightening also provides organized labor and leftist parties with a cause to rally opposition to the government.
• Labor unrest is Sanguinetti’s most critical near-term challenge. Labor is becoming increasingly militant, and violent confrontations with the government are growing in frequency and scope. Sanguinetti’s dialogue with labor has so far failed to reduce tensions, and recent government actions indicate that the President has now decided to take tougher steps to control strikes and labor violence.
• Sanguinetti’s relations with the political left have also grown more confrontational, and in recent months he has countered obstructive parliamentary maneuvers by the leftist Broad Front coalition and given the police free rein to control leftist-inspired riots and demonstrations. We expect increased agitation by the Broad Front and by Communist-dominated student groups. Another potentially serious development, in our view, is the reappearance on the political scene of the far-left Tupamaro guerrilla movement. Although some Tupamaro factions have pledged to participate in electoral politics, others have stated their intention to resume violence.
• Another concern of Sanguinetti is the recent increase in Cuban activity in Uruguay, according to the US Embassy. 2 The Embassy [ less than 1 line not declassified ] report that Cuba is cultivating ties to labor and leftist leaders, including the Tupamaros. Although Cuban involvement to date has been very low-key so as not to upset recently restored diplomatic ties to Uruguay, any signs of growing instability or government ineptitude might spur greater Cuban activity.
The military is carefully monitoring the domestic political situation and is worried by the labor unrest and the resurgence of the left. In our view, however, the officer corps’ greatest concern is that the President might accede to leftist demands for Argentine-style trials of military personnel accused of human rights abuses under past military governments. [ 2 lines not declassified ] Sanguinetti, however, has demonstrated a keen awareness of military sensitivities, has generally consulted with the services before taking action on controversial issues, and probably is aware of the limits of the military’s tolerance.
We believe the Sanguinetti government has a slightly better than even chance of remaining in power over the coming year. The President’s more assertive policies have strengthened his political standing; polls show that he remains Uruguay’s most popular public figure. Although Sanguinetti will continue to face economic problems, labor unrest, and leftist agitation, we expect that he will be able to hold civilian opposition to manageable levels. Moreover, the President will in all likelihood retain the support of the armed forces—primarily by resisting pressure for widespread human rights trials.
We cannot rule out, however, that Sanguinetti will revert to the erratic and indecisive style of his first months in office, thereby emboldening labor and the left to intensify antigovernment agitation. If the economy deteriorated dramatically and the President appeared to lose control of events, factions of the Tupamaros might return to armed struggle. Under such circumstances, hotheads in the military—particularly junior officers of an ultranationalist and vaguely anti-US bent—could attempt a coup. The chances of a successful military intervention, in our view, would increase substantially if Sanguinetti attempted to placate the left by permitting human rights prosecutions of military officers.
A strong, stable, civilian government in Montevideo would clearly buttress the US goal of promoting democracy throughout the region. A military takeover, however, could produce a highly volatile regime that, at least initially, might adopt populist anti-US rhetoric and policies. A coup in Uruguay would underscore the vulnerability of Latin America’s young democracies.

453. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 453. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA 86–20029 Washington, June 6, 1986
URUGUAY: Prospects for Sanguinetti
Summary
President Julio Sanguinetti, Uruguay’s first civilian President in 12 years, enjoys strong popular support. Sanguinetti is pragmatic, moderate, and generally friendly to the West, although he has expanded ties with the Soviet Bloc. The President has skillfully handled a number of sensitive issues since he took office in March 1985, capitalizing on the electorate’s preference for orderly reform and politics by consensus. He successfully negotiated a controversial IMF agreement and has maintained open relations with both the political opposition, which controls Congress, and the armed forces. Sanguinetti has fostered cooperation between his Colorado Party and its chief political rival and has made some inroads dealing with the country’s faltering economy, but he faces several tough challenges in the coming year. He has yet to make the structural reforms—such as pruning the overblown public sector and diversifying exports—that might lead to prolonged economic growth. Labor unrest continues to generate political tensions—the Communist-dominated union confederation has called for a general strike during Sanguinetti’s visit to Washington—and sap industrial productivity. Also, the armed forces remain nervous as they await the Supreme Court decision regarding possible trials of officers for human rights violations committed under the previous military regime.
[Omitted here is the body of the memorandum.]
