454. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Poindexter) to President Reagan 454. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Poindexter) to President Reagan 1
Washington, June 16, 1986
Meeting with Uruguayan President Julio Maria Sanguinetti
I. PURPOSE
To support Uruguay’s return to democracy, to strengthen bilateral relations, to bring Uruguay more firmly into the West, to explain our policies on Central America and terrorism.
II. BACKGROUND
President Julio Sanguinetti, Uruguay’s first civilian President in 12 years, is a serious, articulate statesman who is pragmatic, moderate and generally friendly to the West and the United States. Uruguay’s almost century-long democratic tradition was interrupted when the military seized power in 1973 following several years of economic decline, labor agitation and urban guerrilla (Tupamaros) violence. Uruguay’s political leaders have traditionally espoused a social democratic philosophy which led to an extensive social welfare system that eventually became unsustainable and an economy dominated by the state which grew in inefficiency over the decades. One only has to drive through the lovely capital city, Montevideo, to see how tarnished Uruguay’s reputation as the “Switzerland of Latin America” has become: once handsome homes and buildings now shabby and crumbling, boarded-up small stores and factories, youth idle in the streets. President Sanguinetti’s bold and skillful leadership has bolstered his political position but large challenges remain. You invited him for a state visit in a letter delivered to him by Secretary Shultz at Sanguinetti’s inauguration in March 1985 as a concrete gesture of support for Uruguay’s return to democracy, a theme which we anticipate will dominate this state visit. 2
Return to Democracy and Consolidation : President Sanguinetti and his Colorado Party played an active role in the transition to democracy by helping negotiate a face-saving formula for the military to relinquish power by allowing it limited participation in the government. The Uruguayan electorate remains pluralist; there are three major parties, the traditional Colorado and Blanco parties, and the newer Broad Front, a coalition of leftist groups spanning the ideological spectrum from democratic socialism to Marxism-Leninism. A “National Accord” between the three parties signed last April has been badly eroded over the past two months, making Sanguinetti’s ability to maintain opposition support in the Congress difficult. Although rebuilding the economy is his principal long-term challenge, labor unrest is the greatest immediate problem. Aggressive leftwing union leaders and communists called over 200 strikes and organized 223 new unions in the first six months of the Sanguinetti administration. Labor leaders are planning a general strike on June 17, the day of your meeting with Sanguinetti. Sanguinetti has also had to walk a fine line between meeting demands from the left and appeasing an apprehensive military, which is concerned about leftist demands for Argentine-style trials of officers for alleged human rights abuses committed under the military regime. Long-term prospects for stability in Uruguay are uncertain at best. During your meeting with Sanguinetti, you could show sympathy for the challenges he faces in consolidating democratic rule in Uruguay.
The Economy and US Assistance : 3 During his first year in office, President Sanguinetti implemented modest free market reforms and austerity measures that enabled Uruguay to obtain an IMF-supported stabilization program and arrange for new commercial loans. Cheaper oil imports and lower interest rates will probably generate economic growth this year of roughly two percent while the government’s anti-inflationary wage policies will hold down price increases. Despite these accomplishments, Montevideo has not implemented the basic reforms needed to make the Uruguayan economy internationally competitive: pruning back the public sector and overblown welfare programs, giving concrete production incentives to private entrepreneurs, and diversifying exports. Domestic and congressional opposition will make it difficult for him to address these problems.
The Uruguayan government has publicly stated its desire for foreign investment and since 1985, we have been negotiating with them on what would be the first Bilateral Investment Treaty (BIT) in South America. The BIT will in all likelihood not be ready for signing during President Sanguinetti’s visit and has become a sensitive issue in Uruguay. The Foreign Minister has requested that we not publicly mention the BIT but you could privately encourage a BIT as an important step in attracting US investors to Uruguay.
Trade issues are the primary irritant in relations. Uruguay appreciates that we are its largest customer, but is deeply concerned about protectionism and falling prices for its products. They are especially concerned about the negative impact of the export provisions of the Farm Act, particularly the Export Enhancement Program (EEP) and the rice and dairy programs. You can tell President Sanguinetti that there were sections of the Farm Bill which you did not support, and that the Executive Branch has formulated its programs to minimize the harm caused to non-subsidizing exporters. You can tell him as well that we believe the new trade round is the best hope to iron out problems in agricultural trade and that we are pleased that Uruguay will host the GATT Ministerial in September. You might mention that we expect to join together in our concern about EC agricultural policies, and that we would appreciate their support on services.
The US will provide Uruguay $14.4 million in Economic Support Funds (ESF) and this agreement will be signed during the state visit. We have a small MAP (military assistance) program that will assist President Sanguinetti to demonstrate to the military that democracy yields some rewards. President Sanguinetti is not aware that we have arranged for you to show our support for Uruguay with some small gestures that you may choose to mention during the meeting:
— a program which Sanguinetti just requested to provide top business and academic agricultural experts to consult on modernization is available,
— a longstanding countervailing duty issue regarding leather wearing apparel can be lifted,
— the temporary cheese quota granted to Uruguay in 1985 has been slightly increased and is a permanent quota,
— Trade Development Program funds for feasibility studies related to development projects are available as well.
Foreign Policy and Central America : Besides maintaining Uruguay’s traditionally close relations with the United States and Western Europe, Sanguinetti is paying greater attention to regional issues. Its position of leadership in the Cartagena Group on the debt issue has permitted it to exert a moderating influence. It has played a minor role in the Contadora Support Group. Uruguay has also strengthened relations with the Soviet bloc and established relations with Cuba after a 21-year hiatus in order to placate Uruguay’s domestic leftists.
Sanguinetti and his government appear to be particularly fearful of a US invasion of Nicaragua because of the impact they believe this would have on domestic Uruguayan politics. They oppose US assistance to the contras. You can reassure him that the United States has no intention to intervene militarily in Central America. You can point out that South Americans talk to you about democracy, but seem to draw the line when it comes to supporting a democratic outcome in Nicaragua. You might suggest to Sanguinetti that if he cannot openly support the resistance, you hope he might refrain from publicly opposing our efforts to assist them.
The Soviets, Terrorism and Libya : We want the new Latin American democracies to feel that they are part of the “inner circle” of Western democracies by discussing high priority issues with them. You can explain to Sanguinetti that improving our relationship with the Soviets is a long-term task and that we are prepared to be patient. Sanguinetti will most likely welcome being better informed about your decision on Interim Restraint based on Soviet noncompliance on arms control treaties. You can assure him that the US will continue to exercise utmost restraint while protecting strategic deterrence and working for reductions in strategic arsenals on both sides.
Sanguinetti publicly criticized our military actions against Libya. You might explain to him that we are making concentrated efforts with our allies to combat state-supported terrorism and because we share Uruguay’s belief that international cooperation is necessary to successfully combat terrorism. Irrefutable evidence indicates Sandinista support to Colombia’s M–19 in Palace of Justice incident.
Short One-on-One : Your Ambassador in Uruguay, Judge Malcolm Wilkey, suggested that a short one-on-one with Sanguinetti about Central America, after the Oval Office meeting concludes and the participants are walking to the departure area, will help him understand the significance of this matter to the US government and underscore our determination to see democracy flourish in Nicaragua. You might emphasize to him once again your belief that US military intervention in Central America will not be necessary if the democracies of this hemisphere work together to produce a democracy in Nicaragua. You could explain that our national security concerns about Nicaragua extend to concerns about the security of South America’s new democracies because of Sandinista expansionist tendencies.
[Omitted here are a list of participants, a press plan, and a sequence of events.]
Tab A
Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 4
SUBJECT
State Visit of Uruguayan President Julio Sanguinetti
I. WHERE DOES OUR RELATIONSHIP STAND?
○ A year after Uruguay’s peaceful return to its democratic traditions, our relations with pragmatic President Sanguinetti and his government are excellent.
○ A century-old statist tradition, popular Uruguayan skepticism about the U.S. and a leftist majority in parliament circumscribe Sanguinetti’s actions. — Uruguayan public opinion is generally more critical of USG policies than that of the GOU, especially on economic issues and Central America.
○ Unequivocal USG support for Uruguay’s return to democracy, frequent contact with political leaders, and concrete gestures of support such as $14.4 million in ESF assistance have improved communication between our governments and also improved our public image in Uruguay.
○ Uruguay is deeply concerned about trade issues and protectionism. — It needs trade to service its external debt and build support for Sanguinetti’s free market economic program, but its principal exports of meat, woolen textiles, and leather goods are highly import sensitive.
— It appreciates that the United States has become its largest customer, over traditional markets in Argentina, Brazil and the EC.
○ Uruguay will host the GATT Ministerial in September. We are both concerned about EC agricultural policies. We want GOU support for including services on the agenda.
○ Uruguay favors democracy in Central America, but strongly opposes support for the Nicaraguan resistance.
○ The GOU has exerted a moderating influence on the regional debt question through Foreign Minister Enrique Iglesias.
II. WHAT DO WE WANT?
○ Success for Sanguinetti and a mature relationship with his friendly, democratic government.
○ Greater understanding of our policies on Central America and terrorism.
○ Continued sound economic policies and responsible behavior on the debt question.
○ Support for a comprehensive agenda for the new trade round, to include services, intellectual property, investment and agriculture.
III. WHAT DOES SANGUINETTI WANT?
○ Greater market access for its exports.
○ Not to have Central America dominate the visit.
○ To identify areas of agreement and potential cooperation.
○ Agriculture high on the agenda in new trade round.
○ Foreign investment to help promote economic growth.
○ Military assistance to demonstrate to the Armed Forces the benefits of democracy.
○ Technical assistance in the area of agriculture and strengthening the private sector.
IV. WHAT CAN BE ACHIEVED FROM THIS VISIT?
○ Engage Sanguinetti in a more personal relationship with USG officials.
○ Demonstrate support for Uruguayan democracy and Sanguinetti’s responsible economic program.
○ Give Sanguinetti a good hearing on trade issues and seek GOU support for a comprehensive agenda in the new trade round that includes both services and agriculture.
○ Heightening of Sanguinetti awareness of importance we attach to Nicaragua, our unhappiness with South American democracies’ role so far.
○ Share with Sanguinetti our concerns about Libya and Syria and hopes for East/West relations.
○ Sign an ESF agreement, a Transportation Technical Assistance Agreement, and encourage completion of negotiations on a Bilateral Investment Treaty.

Uruguayan democracy is healthy, thanks in large part to the cooperation of the opposition political parties and the military. Although the economy is still sluggish, Sanguinetti has reversed decades of statist stultification and set Uruguay on a sensible path toward economic growth.” Whitehead noted “the fact is that Uruguayan exports to the US have increased by 70 percent since 1982; we are now Uruguay’s leading export market; and we import twice as much from Uruguay as we export to them. How, I asked the Foreign Minister, can we then be accused of protectionism? 455. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 455. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 1
Washington, June 19, 1986, 0443Z
193388. Subject: The Secretary’s meeting with President Sanguinetti.
1. (C—Entire text.)
2. Summary: The Secretary met President Sanguinetti June 16 for a cordial overview of the topics to be addressed during the state visit. Sanguinetti made clear that the GOU wanted to use the visit as political boost and asked for US help on trade matters to consolidate the return to democracy. He noted that he would avoid being drawn into public debate on US aid to the Contras. Sanguinetti asked for working level discussions to consider possible trade agreements covering specific sectors, such as textiles, leather goods, or dairy products (Finance Minister Zerbino later added services to this list). The Secretary noted that the US is already Uruguay’s largest market and that textile trade is particularly sensitive, but said we could explore possible areas of mutual benefit. Sanguinetti also mentioned GOU interest in military assistance and in a new route for Pluna, the state airline. At the Secretary’s request, Sanguinetti reviewed the situations in Paraguay, where he hoped there could be an evolution within the Stroessner regime, and Chile, where he foresaw serious problems if Pinochet continued to take a hard line and if the democratic opposition failed to present a clear alternative for the future. End summary.
3. The Secretary called on President Sanguinetti at the Madison Hotel, June 16 at 4:15 P.M. to review the main points to be addressed during the state visit. President Sanguinetti was accompanied by Foreign Minister Iglesias, Finance Minister Zerbino, Ambassador Luisi and members of the presidential staff. Accompanying the Secretary were Assistant Secretary Abrams, AID Director McPherson, Ambassador Wilkey, ARA/SC Director Durkee (notetaker) and Stephanie Van Reigersberg (interpreter).
4. GOU overview of visit: Sanguinetti began by sketching out his approach to the visit, explaining first that he wished to project it as a political event in order to avoid confusion or expectations on economic issues that might be exploited by his opponents. Secondly, he said that he would not get drawn into public debate over the issue of U.S. aid to the Contras, although he was prepared to discuss Central American issues in private.
5. Trade issues: Thirdly, within the framework of very good bilateral relations, he saw trade issues as central and therefore wished to explore how to increase trade with the U.S. He suggested discussions at the working level to consider free trade agreements covering specific sectors, given the likely difficulty of working out a comprehensive free trade agreement such as that between the U.S. and Israel. When the Secretary asked what sectors he meant, Sanguinetti said they would have to be analyzed but that for Uruguay the most beneficial would be textiles, woolens, hides, leather goods; the U.S. obviously could export many more products than Uruguay. Subsequently, Finance Minister Zerbino noted that services (banking and informatics) were also possible candidates. While this matter was being explored, he also hoped for consideration of increasing Uruguayan shares in those markets regulated by quotas.
6. Pluna route request: Sanguinetti also mentioned that discussions were underway to consider changes in civilian air services, and he wanted the Secretary to know that the route request for Pluna was significant as part of the GOU plan to increase the role of the private sector by making Pluna a joint venture.
7. Military assistance: Sanguinetti expressed satisfaction with the state of civilian-military relations, which were vital for the stability of democratic government. He believed that the Army was in reasonably good condition and would benefit from continued U.S. training, but he asked for material assistance for the Air Force and Navy, whose equipment was very old.
8. The Secretary responded that the U.S. viewed the re-emergence of democracy in Uruguay as very important for the region, and we wanted the visit to be a celebration of that achievement. He added that we attached similar importance to promoting democracy in Central America. Regarding trade, he expressed satisfaction at the increase in bilateral trade and hoped it would continue to grow. If it were possible to identify specific sectors of mutual benefit, all the better, but he noted that any trade agreements must be based on two-way flows, adding that textile trade was particularly sensitive. The Secretary went on to point out that the GOU would have a good platform from which to promote freer trade when it hosts the GATT Ministerial meeting in September. He concluded that the U.S. appreciated the effort by Sanguinetti and Zerbino to strengthen the economy and wanted to help. Sanguinetti responded by expressing appreciation for US help and hoped that Uruguay could demonstrate that democracy and economic growth went hand-in-hand, a hope the Secretary said he shared.
9. Review of Paraguay and Chile: In response to the Secretary’s question about Uruguayan views of Paraguay and Chile, Sanguinetti commented that the two countries had very different histories and situations. Paraguay he described as still a 19th century country, a big ranch which needed a strong foreman. In his view, change in Paraguay must come from within the regime, because the opposition was too weak and too divided to offer an alternative. Unfortunately, Stroessner, like all dictators, wanted to hold on forever, and this was dangerous. If he could step back at the end of his current term and let others of his party take over, he could be safe while gradual changes occurred. But if he insisted on staying, he would become even less able to deal with problems and the opposition might become radicalized.
10. With regard to Chile, he said Pinochet was still strong but constant struggle with the opposition was eroding his base of popular support. At the same time, the communists were getting more aggressive and violent. He believed that a reasonable solution depended on strengthening the democratic center, helping it to overcome divisions and to present a clear alternative. Sanguinetti said he had tried to convey two points to Chile: First, that as in Uruguay, it was possible for Pinochet to leave power and not wind up like Somoza or Duvalier; second, the opposition cannot just insist that Pinochet must leave without explaining who would replace him. In the end, the Chilean majority, as in Uruguay, would avoid the extremes of either Pinochet or the communists if the democratic parties could show that they had a reasonable plan to govern afterwards.
11. The Secretary thanked Sanguinetti for his views and said he hoped there would be further opportunities for discussions during the visit.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S–I Records, Secretary Memorandums of Conversation (Memcons), Secretary Haig and Shultz, Lot 87D327, Secretary Shultz (Memcons), October 1985–present. Confidential; Immediate; Limdis. Sent for information Priority to Santiago and Asuncion. Drafted by Durkee; cleared by Gelbard and Abrams and S/S–O and S; approved by Shultz. Sanguinetti made a State visit to Washington June 16–19; he then visited New York, June 19–20.

456. Memorandum of Conversation 456. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, June 17, 1986, 10:35–11:50 a.m.
SUBJECT
Meeting Between President Reagan and Uruguayan President Julio Sanguinetti (U)
PARTICIPANTS
U.S.
The President
The Vice President
Chief of Staff Donald Regan
Secretary of State George P. Shultz
National Security Advisor John M. Poindexter
Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs Elliott Abrams
Ambassador Malcolm Wilkey
Jacqueline Tillman, NSC
Uruguay
President Julio Maria Sanguinetti
Foreign Minister Enrique Iglesias
Finance Minister Ricardo Zerbino
Ambassador Hector Luisi
President Reagan . It is a genuine pleasure to welcome you to the White House. Supporting democracy in this hemisphere is a top priority of my administration, and we are particularly happy about the return to democracy in your great country. I’m sure you must have an agenda of things you’d like to discuss. (U)
President Sanguinetti . Our agenda is brief but important. The first thing is to inform you, Mr. President, regarding the great and unique efforts we undertook to have a peaceful outcome in our transition back to democracy. Things are now going well and we think our example is of great value in terms of South America. We are convinced a peaceful transition is the only way for those left who don’t have a democracy yet, that they can follow our path. Conditions are never identical but a search for a peaceful solution that gives room to all sectors of the society is the key. (C)
The second thing is fulfilling public expectations once the democracy is won. It is not easy to consolidate democracy, to manage the accumulated problems of 12 years when our democracy was repressed. To reorder the economy, to lower the deficit, to improve exports, to reduce inflation, all at once! This is all especially difficult when a country is having difficulties with international trade. I am emphasizing the subject of trade in this visit. We have always felt where the foreign debt is concerned that the real subject was trade, not debt. We have said this in the Cartagena meetings, and at first, we were not always understood. The real problem is for the economies in the region to grow, and for that to happen, trade has to grow. This is especially important in a small country like ours. Our domestic markets are small, so we have to export. (C)
We have been looking at this subject from a number of vantage points. Uruguay has supported the idea of the new GATT conference. We have many arguments with our friends, some of which are our neighbors and who have slightly different approaches than ours. We tell them that it is very important to see the situation as it actually is. Our country has suffered a great deal from protectionism from the EC. There has been a great deal of harm not only in the EC, but in third country markets. The sale of subsidized commodities at below market prices is almost impossible to compete with. In addition to this problem is the response of the US today to this practice. It hurts us with rice, beef, grains. We have to offset the damage done by these practices. We are working to achieve a more open and free world market. (C)
Bilateral US-Uruguay trade is greatly improved—a good deal and progress can continue. We have entered into some agreements with Argentina and more recently with Mexico and Brazil regarding negotiations on specific markets. The idea we wish to pursue with the US is to see if we can study the issue and see if there is a way which would allow us free trade in specific sectors. Perhaps some sectors could be open to free trade in both directions, food stuffs, prepared feed, textiles, leather apparel, and services as well. The fundamental issue is to explore support for the idea in the short term, to work to promote a more open trade system that would open the way in a different direction. In the short term, we need the quotas that we do have with the US to be doubled. I’ve spoken to the Secretary of State about this and a few other items: a task force on agriculture, civil aviation, military assistance which we can channel with relative ease. 2 (C)
Uruguay is a small, moderate country. We are in between large countries like Argentina, Brazil, Paraguay, Chile. We had a mature transition, are proceeding with economic sanity. Our first goal was the health of the country, moving later to economic expansion on the basis of free trade. We could demonstrate to the region that we are a model to imitate. We are in a region full of problems. Chile is in an upheaval. Argentina is somewhat turbulent. Things are going well in Brazil, but Brazil is a country that tends to look inside itself. We have a special relationship with Brazil, they listen to us the most. Paraguayan political instability is beginning and playing a constructive role is important. This is why we are convinced that in Uruguay we can demonstrate a great factor of stability. (C)
Relations with our armed forces are good. It was not easy, the transition in Uruguay. We were a century old democracy, a peace loving people. For 100 years the army was not in politics. Then in the 60s and 70s, the intellectual guerrillas, with no popular base in the country, forced the armed forces out of the barracks. We suffered a lot, it was a national shame, a disgrace. We are working now to incorporate the military into our civic life. We do not want a humiliated army, like in Argentina. A humiliated army is dangerous. We are succeeding in reintroducing the army into the life of the country. We are receiving extraordinary cooperation from the Generals. Well, this is how we see things. (C)
President Reagan . Like you, six years ago I inherited a difficult economic situation. I know how difficult it is to change the direction of a national economy and reduce the size of the government. So I am very sympathetic. But I can assure you it is worth the effort. You and your economic team, especially Finance Minister Zerbino, have accomplished much during the last year. I commend you on your responsible policies. I hope we can sign a Bilateral Investment Treaty. This would be a strong signal to US investors about your economic intentions. Someone once said, a country grows great with imports and people. I am pleased that you will sign the Economic Support Fund agreement during your visit. (C)
Now, I know there has been a recent piece of legislation which has caused you some problems. The Farm Bill. I opposed our Congress on parts of this bill, but the Congress had its way. I believe the GATT round offers us the best chance to deal with agricultural trade issues and fully support you on this. I hope your government will support the inclusion of services. It has taken us a long time to get the Europeans to go along with the new GATT round. We made some progress in Tokyo. (C)
I regret that there is nothing I can do about our Export Enhancement Program for the moment, but I have tried to find other ways we might be helpful. I am pleased to respond favorably to your request to make available a team of agricultural experts from the business and academic communities to consult on modernization and other issues. The countervailing duty on leather wearing apparel can be lifted. Your temporary cheese quota will be made permanent, and we are prepared to fund feasibility studies for projects designed to support economic innovation and development. (C)
On another issue. The Nicaraguan opposition, armed and unarmed, is struggling for the same political rights which you have insisted on in your own country. We attach great importance to the views of the four democratic governments in Central America. They understand the Nicaraguan threat. President Azcona of Honduras was very clear during his visit here that there can be no peace without democracy. Arias of Costa Rica has said the same. The present Contadora draft would not ensure democracy in Nicaragua. It needs a timetable to make sure they comply. The Nicaraguan resistance is genuinely democratic. They are ready for a dialogue. They have said three times they would lay down their arms and engage in a dialogue. They have been turned down each time by the Sandinistas. We are seeking to make the freedom fighters have enough leverage to bring on negotiations. (C)
To switch to another issue, we have made some progress in US-Soviet relations but improving the relationship is a long-term process and we just have to be patient and determined. But I believe Gorbachev will honor his agreement to a summit this year in the US. Non-compliance of the Soviets on arms control agreements is a major problem. My decision on Interim Restraint followed years of unsuccessful efforts to improve Soviet performance in this area. We will continue utmost restraint but on a more realistic basis than Salt II. We hope the Soviets will act more constructively, including at the Geneva arms reduction negotiations. There is room for some optimism. I believe Gorbachev is the first Russian leader that has actually ever proposed eliminating nuclear weapons. Our great hope is that we can eventually eliminate all nuclear weapons, so the world can be a safer and more peaceful place. We share your belief that international cooperation is necessary to combat terrorism. We made some gains in that regard in Tokyo. 3 (C)
President Sanguinetti . Some agreements were made? (U)
President Reagan . On terrorism? (U)
President Sanguinetti . Yes. (U)
President Reagan . Yes. We agreed to an exchange of intelligence but especially working on the states that sponsor terrorism. It is pretty hard to catch the individual terrorist. So we need to get to the governments where actual support for terrorists is being given. In the case of Libya, we had irrefutable proof that they were behind the Berlin bombings, where two of our men were killed. With this evidence in hand, it was necessary to let them know we knew. It has had a good effect so far. In Tokyo, we talked about jointly initiating economic measures, expelling people from the so-called People’s Bureaus. They don’t call them Embassies, they call them People’s Bureaus. (C)
On trade. We are opposed on protectionism and will take actions where there are cases of unfair denial of markets or subsidizing. We are striving for free and fair world trade. We recently took some actions with our neighbors to the north. It seems that over the last few years, they had developed a monopoly here so we’ve taken some temporary actions aimed at building our domestic industry. There was the element of subsidy on the other side. (C)
President Sanguinetti . How do you see the difficulties in Mexico? (C)
President Reagan . Well, we want to help them with the banks and want them to get out of the hole they are in. George, do you want to contribute something? (C)
Secretary of State Shultz . Mexico is our close neighbor. The President has gone out of his way to establish close relations with our neighbors. One index of this effort is that he has met with his counterparts in Mexico and Canada more than anyone else. So it has been a big effort. The Mexicans have severe economic problems but they are solvable if they would go down the path you’ve been talking about. We talked about this when we met in Cartagena. The Mexicans need to create the conditions to bring back the money that has fled; there is something like 60 billion of Mexican money outside the country. This is the essence of their problem. We don’t appreciate at all Mexico’s role in Central America. They take the Nicaraguan’s part and work on treaty arrangements that give the advantage to them and work against the Central American democracies in the region. So we have differences about this that we argue about in our many meetings. And they are always voting against us in the UN. So there are some points of tension. But until the capital returns, the conditions in the country are going to remain difficult. The more you can say to them about this the better. We spoke about Paraguay and Chile yesterday. I believe the President would be interested in your views. (S)
President Sanguinetti . Yesterday, we talked about Chile and Paraguay. There are very different situations. Paraguay is a very isolated country and has been since colonial days. It is just an enclave there. [ less than 1 line not declassified ] In the first one and a half centuries of its history, there are four names, Sanchez, Lopez, Lopez and Stroessner. So that is the context that one has to think about Paraguay. It is not like Chile, Uruguay, Argentina. The Southern Cone countries are European countries; the Andes are Spanish-Indian countries, and the coastal countries are Latino-African. [ less than 1 line not declassified ] This is the time to find a political solution there before there is a real problem. But you have to find a political solution inside the regime, not outside. The opposition is very weak, there are no leaders. But inside the regime there are some possibilities. The difficulty is to convince Stroessner and his people to do this. They have absolute power, but Stroessner is in a decline. It could be that the next elections could be freer, that someone in his party could win, and he could be a “father figure” from his home. But you have to look inside for change. (S)
In Chile, there are two groups to convince: Pinochet and the opposition. Chile is a distinct situation. It too has a long democratic tradition and a high cultural level. But they have also had a Marxist experience and this has conditioned the people. Twenty percent of the people support Pinochet. Eighty percent are against him. But that eighty percent don’t want change for change’s sake. They are asking, what is coming next? They don’t want another Marxist. The great weakness is that there is a communist opposition who is using violence. There is a center but there is no unity and too many leaders. There is not one person that can stand above the rest to find a solution. Pinochet is weakening, about that there is no doubt. If your regime is based on law and order but there is no law and order there is no reason for a dictatorship. That is what is happening to Pinochet. (S)
We talk to the opposition on an ongoing basis. I gave a press conference recently and all the Chilean press was there and I talked about Uruguay and our experience to give a clear signal. I said, “there are two feelings present during transitions: the fear of those who are leaving power and the impatience of those coming to power.” The problem is to manage those feelings. I talk sometimes about a swimming pool. You don’t dive into a pool with no water. The opposition asks is there water in the pool, but some are saying, we need two feet of water, others say, we have to have ten feet. And the military have to know they can swim too. And Pinochet has to believe there is more for him than Somoza’s fate, to end up assassinated in Paraguay. If not, there will be a fight to the death. I tell the opposition they have to go one step at a time. If they try to jump up three steps at a time, they will fall down. We spent two and a half years arguing with our military. There was a plebiscite in 1980, a year of debate after that, parties were organized but there were obstacles all the way. But we made it clear to the military that we are at the table and we were staying at the table. Also, and most important, we convinced the military that they had a future. It isn’t easy. (S)
Another case is Central America. We share the same objectives, peace and democracy. But there is no clear, common strategy. The important thing is to win the war, not just the battle. We don’t see things exactly your way. We believe force reinforces Ortega’s position, and the military people gain in strength as well. The question is how to set up the political option so the political solution becomes dominant because it is clear that this can only be solved within a political scenario. No military victory is possible. So we are trying to force Ortega to come to a negotiated position. (S)
Secretary of State Shultz . But you have to have strength to back up negotiations. (C)
President Sanguinetti . But you have to support a negotiated solution to prevent Ortega from becoming more consolidated militarily. (C)
President Reagan . But there is great erosion in support for the Sandinistas, even in the military. They have had thousands of deserters. They are waiting to join the freedom fighters but need supplies and help. I met recently with a Nicaraguan Monsignor who told us that Ortega had stepped up repression. He told us that the people of Nicaragua want what was promised to them, the promises to the OAS of democracy, respect for human rights and so forth. We are concerned that they will become a threat to all of the Americas once they are consolidated. Because their policy is an expansionist policy. They have not abandoned the dream for world communism. They were more open about this earlier, when Mr. Ortega talked publicly about revolutions with no borders. And history has not given one example of a communist state that has ever given up total power. Faced with enough force, they would have to give up their stranglehold, to go out and try to win votes, to take their chances in a democratic system. Their power today comes from the barrel of a gun. I also met with three Nicaraguans recently, two were former Sandinistas who turned away from the Sandinistas. The third was a Creole minister from a Protestant church. The Sandinistas came and arrested him because he was a minister preaching the word of God. And they tortured him, and cut off his ears. Then one day in prison, another prisoner told him he had to escape because they were going to kill him. So he did escape. All this suffering because he was a minister. (C)
And I agree that there is a danger in Chile. I believe if it were only a matter between the Chilean people and Pinochet it could be worked out. But there is a third force that is not democratic and whose tactics is to keep the other two fighting and look for their chances. Well, I must be messing up your schedule by keeping you here. (C)
Finance Minister Zerbino . Mr. President, I wanted to mention just briefly once more the economic theme. We are enjoying some relief from lower oil prices and interest rates. But the projections are somber. Exports decreased in value in spite of the fact that volume is up 5 percent. Our principle commodities are down ten percent of normal export prices. So, as I said, projections are somber. We share similar objectives with the US. It will be some time before we can develop substitutions for agriculture. Rice is a major problem which is affected by the Farm Bill. The drop is more than one-half of one percent of GDP. Translate one-half a percent of GDP to yours and you will understand that this is a very significant amount. This is why our President is emphasizing the trade area and our desire to be closer to the US. We need short term measures to overcome our problems in international trade. (C)

The meeting took place in the Oval Office. Tillman sent the memorandum to Poindexter under cover of a June 18 memorandum.

457. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 457. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 1
New York, September 30, 1987, 0516Z
Secto 15048. Subject: UNGA: Secretary’s meeting with Uruguayan Foreign Minister Iglesias.
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. Summary: The Secretary met with Uruguayan FM Enrique Iglesias on September 28 in New York City during the 42nd UNGA. 2 The meeting centered on regional and debt issues. The Foreign Minister acknowledged the Secretary’s positive impression of internal Uruguayan economic and political development. The Secretary urged that Uruguayans proceed with caution on fisheries negotiations with the USSR. Iglesias warned that unless the Chilean opposition ceases its internal bickering, Pinochet could ultimately successfully—and, by default, legitimately—maintain himself in power. Iglesias also foresaw worsening relations with Paraguay both because of increasing intransigence on the part of the Colorado Party, and an increasingly negative public opinion of Uruguay being formed as a result of a child custody dispute. The Secretary urged Iglesias to encourage his Contadora colleagues to insist that the Nicaraguan Government negotiate a cease fire directly with the democratic resistance prior to November 7.
3. On debt, Iglesias saw rising interest rates as the primary threat to recovery and urged increased concessions from US commercial banks; the Secretary pointed to Chile as a country not afraid to develop and implement creative debt management schemes. The Foreign Minister was accompanied by his UN Ambassador Paolillo and UN Minister Fisher. Assistant Secretaries Abrams and Redman, Deputy Assistant Secretary Gelbard, and Country Officer Bolton (notetaker) also participated. End summary.
4. Domestic political situation: Shultz commented favorably on Uruguay’s apparent success in its democratic transition, encouraging the military’s positive attitude, and developing responsible economic programs. The Secretary said the GOU’s success was all the more remarkable given the potentially turbulent influence of Uruguay’s neighbors, Argentina and Brazil. Iglesias praised President Sanguinetti’s fundamental goal to establish political and economic stability in Uruguay that would help insulate it from its neighbors’ policy swings. Although sufficient signatures may have already been gathered to bring the de facto amnesty law to a referendum, Iglesias predicted the law would survive a vote. For economic progress to continue, Iglesias said the commercial bank creditors will have to narrow the interest rate spread. Uruguay, he continued, was being penalized for having responsibly gone to the banks two years ago, rather than delay as did Argentina who renegotiated a rate much more favorable than Uruguay’s.
5. USSR/Fisheries: In view of the imminent Shevardnadze visit, the Secretary inquired about the status of the fisheries agreements already initialed by the GOU and USSR and cautioned Iglesias that despite recent developments in the arms control field, the Soviets should not be underestimated. Iglesias assured the Secretary that the Uruguayans will be cautious and that the initialed agreements are in fact under review. Iglesias claimed the GOU is actually interested in the port services agreement, rather than in the agreement allocating fishing zones since the waters under consideration are already experiencing ecological strain. Iglesias confirmed to the Secretary that he would be in Uruguay for FM Shevardnadze’s visit. He noted that Cuba would be the last stop, which indicated to him that the USSR is sending a clear message that it has no intention of consulting with its surrogate prior to Shevardnadze’s travel.
6. Regional: Argentina: Iglesias sees the Alfonsin government as excessively discouraged by the recent Peronist election gains. During President Sanguinetti’s recent visit, Iglesias said his President used the Uruguayan situation (the Colorados lack a parliamentary majority) as an example of minority government that can succeed and encouraged Alfonsin to be prepared to work with this new generation of Peronists. After all, he continued, genuine democratic development requires that an alternative to the incumbent government exist. Iglesias expressed deep concern over what he sees as the increasing influence of the military in Argentine political life; to avoid a similar phenomenon in Uruguay, Iglesias explained that even token MAP assistance would increase the rate of the Uruguayan military’s integration into the democratic political society. The Secretary cited our budget constraints, but alerted Iglesias that we were looking for end-of-year funds for FY 87 and had budgeted both Uruguayan IMET and MAP for FY 88.
7. Regional: Brazil: Iglesias expressed confidence in Brazil’s ability to revive its economy. He described Brazil as being under less economic stress than Argentina because of its greater resources which in turn gives it greater flexibility.
8. Regional: Chile: The Secretary, interested in encouraging democratic reforms during a time of increased political awareness preceding the plebiscite, asked the Foreign Minister for his analysis of the Chilean political and economic situation. Iglesias cited the democratic opposition’s continued inability to rally around one effective, serene candidate as almost a guarantee that Pinochet may in fact be able to maintain himself in power legitimately. When asked by the Secretary to explain further, Iglesias described a scenario in which Pinochet could lose the plebiscite, but among a field of bickering, shrill opposition candidates in an electoral campaign, eventually emerge as the candidate of choice by default.
9. Regional: Paraguay: Iglesias predicted that Uruguay’s bilateral relations with the entrenched and inflexible GOP would worsen as a result of a child custody case just beginning to attract Uruguayan public attention. He said it was unlikely that the GOP would return the child, whom the GOU suspects is the offspring of Uruguayan citizens who disappeared in Argentina during the “Dirty War,” but the emotional nature of the issue is sure to inspire resentment among Uruguayans. Iglesias said the recent Peronist gains in Argentina have made the GOP more intransigent and less likely to cooperate with Uruguay or Argentina as the GOP sees the Peronists as almost natural allies.
10. Debt: Iglesias predicted that calls for capped interest rates will increase markedly during the next few months. He cautioned that adjustment programs based on rising interest rates are doomed to failure and therefore it is incumbent upon the commercial banks to consider additional concessions, such as capped interest rates even though losses may result. In response to the Secretary’s inquiry on debt equity swap schemes in the region, Iglesias said that Uruguay and Argentina are developing proposals, while Brazil is reluctant to begin since debt equity swaps would require politically-difficult denationalization. Chile, however, has managed to discount about 20 per cent of debt successfully. The Secretary immediately used the Chilean success as an example of how much a country can do itself to manage its debt if it has initiative and courage.
11. Central America: Iglesias expressed overall satisfaction with the progress of the Central American peace process and looked forward to Uruguay’s participation in the verification committee. The Secretary strongly criticized the Nicaraguan Government for refusing to negotiate with the democratic resistance to implement a cease-fire prior to November 7. Shultz described how essential a cease-fire is to the Guatemala accords, and that a successful one can only emerge from direct negotiations between the two parties and cannot be imposed from above even at the last minute. He assured Iglesias that the democratic resistance was ready to talk and strongly urged Iglesias to use his influence to bring pressure to bear on Managua to negotiate a cease-fire with the democratic resistance, if the Guatemala accords are to have any hope for success.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D870804–0111. Confidential; Immediate. Sent for information Immediate to Buenos Aires, Brasilia, Montevideo, Santiago, and Asuncion.
2 In telegram 173311 to Montevideo, June 6, the Department reported on Shultz’s June 2 meeting with Iglesias who “expressed optimism about the political and economic situation in Uruguay, particularly over progress in reintegrating the Uruguayan military into civilian society. He asked for US military assistance to help this process and focus the military on professional tasks.” Shultz indicated “that the US would make a special effort to try to help Uruguay with its modest military needs.” They “also discussed Soviet overtures to reach a fishing and Aeroflot agreement package with Uruguay. Iglesias said the GOU is weighing the political risks and economic benefits. The Secretary encouraged him to use caution in reaching any such agreements with the Soviet Union. Iglesias also touched on Uruguay’s concerns regarding an economically viable US route for its national airline, Pluna.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D870440–0867)

458. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 458. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 1
August 4, 1988, 2319Z
Secto 15030. Subject: Memorandum for the President: Uruguay, August 4.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Memorandum for the President.
From: George P. Shultz.
Subject: My day in Uruguay, August 4, 1988. 2
3. The old line about Uruguay is that when either of its (comparatively) huge neighbors, Argentina or Brazil, sneeze, Uruguay catches cold. Right now, though, they are sneezing and sniffling and Uruguay is managing to stay pretty healthy.
4. Certainly Uruguay is ahead of both Brazil and Argentina in opening up its economy and in restoring a relaxed, totally open democratic system. It is a good example of the latter that Pres. Sanguinetti invited to his luncheon for me the leaders of all the opposition parties—and they all came. 3
5. Sanguinetti is, as you will recall from his state visit in 1986, a very perceptive and equally charming man. He has managed Uruguay’s transition to democracy brilliantly, and travelled the world to bring recognition to his small country. He has a realistic view of the Sandinistas, wants to be helpful on Panama if there’s anything constructive Uruguay can do, 4 and offers some real insights about countries as varied as Chile and the Soviet Union. Here, as in Argentina, the return to democracy has brought to the fore a team of very impressive leaders. The consolidation of democracy seems secure, and if they can succeed with their economic plans 5 they’ll give some meaning to the old description of Uruguay as the Switzerland of Latin America.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, N880006–0059. Secret; Immediate; Nodis. Sent from aboard the delegation’s aircraft. Shultz travelled to Montevideo to meet with Sanguinetti and senior Uruguayan officials, August 4. He also gave an address to businessmen and bankers and gave a news conference; see Department of State Bulletin , October 1988, pp. 2–20.
2 In telegram 4953 from Montevideo, August 5, the Embassy transmitted the text of Shultz’s August 4 press conference in Montevideo. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880683–0333)
3 No memorandum of conversation has been found. In telegram 5057 from Montevideo, August 9, the Embassy reported on Shultz’s August 4 meeting with Uruguayan political officials at the Ambassador’s residence. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880695–0318)
4 In telegram 4985 from Montevideo, August 5, the Embassy reported on Shultz’s August 4 meeting with Barrios Tassano in which they discussed Panama, writing: “After both the Secretary and Barrios Tassano had explored every development in the U.S.-Noriega imbroglio, the situation was left that Barrios Tassano and Sanguinetti would use their best efforts to obtain from the Noriega regime a statement of the terms and conditions on which Noriega would depart, and would keep the Ambassador in Montevideo fully informed of any developments.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880683–0717)
5 In telegram 5042 from Montevideo, August 9, the Embassy reported on Shultz’s August 4 meeting with Zerbino in which they discussed “market-oriented measures undertaken by the Sanguinetti administration; Zerbino’s thoughts on the Uruguay Round; Zerbino’s apprehension regarding the U.S. trade bill; the seventh replenishment of the IDB; and the upcoming bilateral textile negotiations in Washington.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880695–0027)

459. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 459. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Uruguay 1
Washington, August 15, 1988, 2333Z
265225. Subject: Secretary Shultz’s meeting with President Sanguinetti, August 4, 1988.
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. Summary: Secretary Shultz and Uruguayan President Sanguinetti met for over an hour August 4 in Montevideo. 2 Sanguinetti discussed Uruguay’s consoliation of its democratic transition and intention to stick with its successful economic program in spite of renewed strikes aimed at forcing policy changes. He urged the Secretary to provide more military assistance to help in the reinforcement of Uruguay’s democracy. The Secretary expressed strong support for what Sanguinetti and Uruguay had accomplished and noted that we had provided some equipment and considerable training. However, financing was a limiting factor, but we would look into additional used equipment. On Panama, the Secretary expressed appreciation for FM Barrios’ efforts, while stressing the need for Noriega to make a written proposal. 3 He also described the current Panamanian situation. On Central America, Secretary Shultz contrasted the many positive developments among the four democracies with the continuing downward slide and repression of the Sandinistas. Sanguinetti agreed on the dismal Nicaraguan picture and both analyzed the Salvador and Honduran situations. Finally, the Secretary analyzed developments in the USSR and the PRC. End summary.
Uruguay’s Transition
3. President Sanguinetti began with a tour d’horizon of the Uruguayan situation. He said Uruguay still had problems but was handling them well. The unions were difficult and were again creating difficult problems as in 1986, with lots of strikes. They were pushing hard for a changed economic policy, but the GOU would not go along and would stick to current policy; in any event, the unions had not paralyzed the country as they would like. Exports were up 60 percent from 1985 when his government began. Inflation was still a problem because the fiscal deficit was still too large. Politically, Uruguay was doing well, but it was now paying the price of a successful transition and was back in a political “spring” with the rampant strikes. It was not good for politics to keep a tough economic policy. Sanguinetti observed that later, at lunch, there would be present a cross-section of all political leaders, including the left. Sanguinetti related that he himself had called Gen. Liber Seregni, leader of the left coalition Ecuador for President Borja’s inauguration, although Seregni could only accept the trip to Quito “on a personal basis.” While this showed Uruguay’s sense of political “fair play”, the left continued to accuse Sanguinetti of being a friend of the military and the imperialists. Sanguinetti added that the situations of his neighbors—Brazil and Argentina—was troubling. They could not control inflation in Uruguay with instability on both borders.
4. Secretary Shultz responded that Sanguinetti had done an excellent job. He recalled the country’s situation at the time of the inauguration; now, democracy was strong and a success was assured. It was not a transition anymore but continuity. The economy had advanced; compared to others in Latin America or elsewhere it looks quite good. The GOU’s policies have worked and as it pushes further it will work even better. The transition for the military had also been accomplished well. The Secretary mentioned that the USG had tried to help with training and some equipment, but was not able to provide all the equipment Uruguay would like. There were no restrictions, simply our own financial constraints.
5. Sanguinetti said the GOU was not asking a lot, only for used military equipment. Some additional help would be greatly appreciated and highly useful. For example, the A–37 used aircraft had made a real difference with the Air Force. It was not easy for the military to make the transition from power to power, so help was important.
6. The Secretary agreed that the USG had done all the Air Force requested. For the Army, the USG would look into possibilities for surplus equipment. The Navy was more difficult to help because ships were expensive, hard to find, and the PE the GOU wanted was used in anti-narcotics programs. IMET funds were somewhat more available, especially with end-of-year possibilities. Ambassador Wilkey added that this issue was a high U.S. priority; some USG offers had been declined though. Congressman Hyde, as a result of his recent visit, had asked for a list of Army and Navy equipment. 4 Everyone agreed the GOU list was reasonable.
Panama
7. Secretary Shultz mentioned that he and FM Barrios had had a good private talk on the current situation. Uruguay had handled this in an excellent manner. It was right for Uruguay to be careful and to get a written commitment from Noriega. Barrios and the Secretary had agreed a low-key approach was best. Everyone wanted to mediate issues involving the USG and we needed an authoritative channel; Uruguay was it. If the situation developed to the point where an agreement might be possible, the USG might have to get involved directly because of legal aspects. Overall, the Secretary stressed, the Foreign Minister’s and Foreign Ministry’s professionalism had been impressive. On Panama itself, it was unacceptable that a country was under control of the drug trade and the USG would not stop trying to get Noriega out. Delvalle’s removal through a 15-minute meeting was in essence a bloodless coup, so civilian government did not really exist. Regarding U.S. sanctions, the Secretary acknowledged that some assets had been frozen, but private investors and financial institutions see political instability as the main problem to keeping funds in Panama. With an economy based almost completely on services (banking, shipping, insurance, tourism), the political instability has a major economic result. At this point, the Secretary concluded, a transition government headed by Delvalle or someone else is required, but not Solis Palma.
Central America
8. Turning, at Sanguinetti’s request, to Central America, Secretary Shultz noted that many good things have happened: Instead of one democracy, there are now four, even if in various stages of fragility; the emergence of the rule of law is very important; there is positive, low inflationary growth. The negative side is that the Nicaraguan people are taking a terrible beating: Real per capita income has been halved; inflation is now either 2,000 percent (our estimate), but either means an end to the money economy; there are large flows of arms from the USSR and the Sandinistas continue to supply guerrillas in El Salvador. He noted that Argentina was also concerned with Sandinista links to guerrillas. The recent additional repression was troubling since it gives us all a belief that they will not change absent pressure. What is healthy is that the four democracies are trying to solve their own problems, in part, with significant U.S. economic aid, which we hope to institutionalize. The Secretary added that he had now tried to accelerate our diplomacy. The press was trying to treat the meetings in Guatemala badly, but they had gone quite well and he would return August 9.
9. Sanguinetti agreed it was fundamental to get the four Central American democracies to solve their own problems. Until he visited Nicaragua and the other Central Americans in December 1987 he had not realized how bad the economic situation was. Managua looked like a neutron bomb had exploded: Buildings but no people in the streets. He had not realized the degree of poverty and deprivation. Sanguinetti noted that he was a politician and looked for action signs: Nobody was applauding Ortega and there was no joy in the streets. Thus, Nicaragua is no longer a model and is losing support among its neighbors.
10. On El Salvador, the Secretary described President Duarte as a valiant man. After the assembly elections last year, Arena looked the favorite for the presidential election, but Chavez Mena has developed a good chance. The people are committed to an honest political process, the economy is giving and inflation is under control in spite of the earthquake and guerrillas. Duarte, because of his dedication to the democratic process and rule of law, among other issues, has made an immense contribution, although economic reform is his weak suit. Turning to Honduras, the Secretary noted that they were about to have their third election. In Guatemala, there had almost been a coup and we are trying to shore up the democratic process, although sometimes it is difficult to help them. Nevertheless, Guatemala is the relative heavyweight in the region historically. Overall and unfortunately, people are reluctant to speak up regarding the Soviets’ heavy arms shipments. Arias.
11. Sanguinetti agreed that he admired Duarte greatly; it was a miracle what he had accomplished to organize a democracy. Guatemala was complicated, but going reasonably well. Assistant Secretary Abrams urged greater South American support for the four democracies which felt abandoned by South America’s democracies, and mentioned that Sanguinetti’s trip had helped in this regard. Unfortunately, the South Americans had backed down in the CIVS and supported the Sandinistas against the four democracies. Sanguinetti acknowledged that Abrams’ point was fair and true, but they were now in close contact with the democracies. This in itself was a good justification for keeping the group of 8 going, so that Uruguay could influence bigger countries like Mexico.
Global Themes
12. Secretary Shultz said that prospects for U.S. relations with the USSR are reasonable, although we needed to maintain a certain skepticism. What they were trying to do internally was particularly difficult, although he said he was more optimistic than some about their ability to affect internal reform. The U.S. and USSR could now talk about most problems, but not Central America. We have worked heavily with them on Southern Africa, Middle East consultations, Afghanistan, arms control and chemical and nuclear weapons. We were now starting talks on ballistic missile technology, which is one of the three most destabilizing problems extant, along with nuclear and chemical weapons.
13. U.S.-Chinese relations were also better. Both of those countries were trying to change internally, but going about it very differently. China, from the bottom up and with agrarian reform, was an instant success by providing incentives. Deng Xiao Peng told me the success is so obvious people now want more reform and they are moving it into larger enterprises, utilities and even political reform. In contrast, the Soviets seem to be doing it the opposite way, from the top down with the bureaucracy fighting it since it is not in the Russian tradition. The Chinese, differing from the Soviets, have a strong entrepreneurial tradition. Gorbachev economic reform has not taken place yet. The Secretary mentioned that Premier Ryzhkov, who is running the economic side, complained to him about central planning and described its stupidity. The Secretary added that he did not expect the Soviets to do much on foreign trade until this internal market is reformed.
14. Sanguinetti commented that, in his view, Eastern Europe and the internal nationalities question, which were in a way the same issue, would prove Gorbachev’s toughest. Greater freedom in the system will allow much faster growth among certain nationalities—Armenians and Balts, for example—but not among Russians. So there were many problems around the corner. Shultz
Whitehead

469. Memorandum of Conversation
Washington, November 17, 1981, 11 a.m.

460. Memorandum From the Executive Secretary of the Department of State (Bremer) to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 460. Memorandum From the Executive Secretary of the Department of State (Bremer) to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 1
Washington, March 24, 1981
SUBJECT
Visit of Venezuelan President Herrera
Venezuelan President Luis Herrera Campins has been advised of President Reagan’s invitation to visit Washington April 13. 2 The Venezuelans are delaying their acceptance until we can clarify what the visit will involve. We strongly recommend that it be a state visit, or at least one that is comparable in style.
Venezuela is one of the three countries in Latin America most important to the United States, not only because of its petroleum but also because of its active and positive political role in Central America and the Caribbean. Venezuela pursues foreign policy goals in the hemisphere which generally coincide with ours, seeking to compete effectively with Cuba in the Caribbean area and strongly supporting the government of El Salvador.
Previous Venezuelan President Carlos Andres Perez made a state visit in June 1977. 3 Any significant disparity between the treatment accorded President Herrera and his predecessor, who is from the opposition party, would be widely commented on, and damaging politically, in the lively competition between the two major parties of Venezuela’s vigorous democracy.
President Herrera is Venezuela’s Chief of State. We believe we can design a state visit along the lines of the attached illustrative schedule 4 which would take little more of President Reagan’s time than a working visit, yet would indicate to the Venezuelans and all Latin Americans the importance this Administration attaches to our relationship with Venezuela, with consequent benefits to our relations.
Venezuelan Ambassador Perez Chiriboga planned to go to Caracas on March 24 to consult with his government on acceptance of the invitation and planning for the visit. He is delaying his trip until we are able to be specific on the outlines for the schedule. We would therefore appreciate a response as soon as possible.
L. Paul Bremer Executive Secretary 5
1 Source: Reagan Library, Executive Secretariat, NSC Country File, Latin America/Central, Venezuela (02/28/1981–04/03/1981). Confidential. Fontaine sent the memorandum to Allen under cover of a March 25 memorandum in which he noted that arguments for a full State visit “are cogent and convincing,” adding: “But the matter has already been decided. There will be full State visit for President Herrera.”
2 In telegram 72867 to Caracas, March 21, the Department instructed the Embassy to invite Herrera on Reagan’s behalf to visit Washington on April 13. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810135–0150) In telegram 2965 from Caracas, March 23, Luers reported that he had conveyed the invitation to Foreign Minister Zambrano who asked for details about the proposed visit. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810135–1102)
3 See Foreign Relations , 1977–1980, vol. XXIV, South America; Latin America Region, Document 336 .
4 Attached but not printed is an illustrative schedule.
5 Kelly signed for Bremer above Bremer’s typed signature.

461. Memorandum From Roger Fontaine of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 461. Memorandum From Roger Fontaine of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 1
Washington, March 30, 1981
SUBJECT
IG Meeting on Venezuela, March 27, 1981 (Chaired by John Bushnell) (U)
The cancelled Herrera visit robbed the IG of its reason for being, but we met anyway. It became a general seminar of US-Venezuelan relations with two issues discussed. 2 (C)
First, possible F–16 or FX sales to Venezuela. State representatives expressed skepticism of Venezuela’s need for the F–16 while DOD representatives argued that Venezuela’s full and equal partnership with the US in the Caribbean gave it the right to request what it wanted with the expectation of getting them. Final decision awaits our arms sales policy. (C)
Second issue involved the final settlement of oil companies’ expropriation by the Venezuelan government. Half the compensation remains in an escrow account subject to Venezuelan legal claims which are currently going through the Venezuelan courts. Until now, the US has had no policy and the matter has dragged on for five years. Therefore, a subgroup of the IG will write a paper over the next three weeks sketching out the options. 3 (S)
Finally, Deputy Secretary Eaton told the IG that no mention of the aborted Venezuelan visit should be made. Leaking of it would cause some embarrassment, etc. Since 25 people were present, leaking seems inevitable. (S)
1 Source: Reagan Library, Robert H. Lilac Files, Arms Transfer: Country File, 1981–1983, AT: Venezuela [February 1981–March 1981]. Secret. Sent for information. A copy was sent to John Treat of the National Security Council staff. An unknown hand wrote and underlined “JC & BN have seen” at the bottom of the page.
2 In a March 30 memorandum to Venezuela IG members, Bushnell reported the March 27 interagency meeting on Venezuela. (Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Subject File, Venezuela [February 1981–March 1981])
3 Not found.

462. Memorandum From Roger Fontaine of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 462. Memorandum From Roger Fontaine of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 1
Washington, April 15, 1981
SUBJECT
The President’s Letter to Venezuelan President Herrera Campins (U)
At Tab B is Secretary Haig’s memorandum summarizing President Herrera’s letter to the President and the current status of relations with Venezuela. Secretary Haig notes our commonly held views vis-a-vis Cuba and the Duarte government in El Salvador. The Secretary also recommends that we hold up notification re the sale of F–16s to Venezuela until the exchange of the Presidents is complete. (C)
At Tab A is the President’s response to President Herrera, which covers the pertinent issues including a gentle critique of President Herrera’s proposal that Latin America be a “zone of peace,” a concept that implies the US should have no responsibilities in the region. 2 At Tab I is your memorandum to the President recommending that he sign the letter to President Herrera Campins. 3 (C)
NOTE: Foreign Minister Zambrano of Venezuela indicated a strong interest in a September meeting of the two Presidents. This was not an “official request” but it was proposed to Ambassador Luers. (C)
RECOMMENDATION
That you sign the memorandum at Tab I. (U)
The President’s reply at Tab A has been cleared with the speechwriters. (U)
Tab B
Memorandum From Secretary of State Haig to President Reagan 4
SUBJECT
Letter from Venezuelan President
Venezuelan President Herrera’s letter to you regretting that he cannot visit in April gives a frank presentation of Venezuelan views on hemispheric affairs. 5 Venezuela pursues foreign policy goals in the hemisphere which largely coincide with ours, including strong support for the Duarte government in El Salvador. Still, we believe an important reason for Herrera’s decision not to accept your invitation at this time was concern about having to situate himself in public vis-a-vis U. S. policies in Central America and the Caribbean.
Venezuela recognizes its profound self-interest in countering Cuba in the Caribbean area and actively opposes Cuban destabilization. U.S. and Venezuelan interests coincide in this respect. It is in our interest to encourage, influence and work with Venezuelan activism in the region. However, in his letter Herrera also repeats the idea he has been developing publicly that Latin America must be a “zone of peace without foreign interference.” That is simply not realistic, and we should tell him so, courteously and warmly.
I recommend that you send the attached reply to Herrera. Meanwhile, we should hold up notification of our decision to sell Venezuela F–16A’s (desirable in itself) until your exchange is complete. 6
Attachment 2
Excerpt of a Paper Prepared in the Department of State 7
3. Embassy Comment on Venezuelan President’s Letter to the President : Ambassador Luers believes that Venezuelan President Herrera’s letter to the President, which the Venezuela ambassador in Washington delivered to State Department last week, constitutes an effort to explain why the April 13 date for a visit was not acceptable; advises the U.S. on Venezuelan actions in Jamaica; and expresses Venezuelan views on regional affairs and how they differ from U.S. policies. Luers believes the heart of the message is the last part in which Herrera indicates that problems in Central America and the Caribbean must be solved by Latin Americans in a “zone of peace” without foreign powers introducing tensions through “bloc conflicts.” In this part of the letter, Venezuela is warning us that an effective U.S. policy in this hemisphere should involve broad development programs, non-intervention, less emphasis on Cuba and East/West issues and not promoting rightist governments. Nonetheless, Luers believes the Herrera letter should be taken as an effort to open a candid dialogue between friends. What Herrera and his advisers need is some personal exposure to the administration. As Latins they need to touch and feel the Reagan administration. The personal and tactile values will play a large role in narrowing our differences. (Caracas 3528, PSN 12734) 8 (C)
