463. Memorandum From Secretary of State Haig to President Reagan 463. Memorandum From Secretary of State Haig to President Reagan 1
Washington, May 13, 1981
SUBJECT
F–16A Aircraft for Venezuela
The Venezuelan Air Force has officially requested planning and review data for the possible purchase of up to 24 F–16A fighter aircraft. 2 We should not provide these data unless we are prepared in principle to approve a sale of such aircraft. A sale to Venezuela would represent the first introduction of US aircraft of this level of capability into the Latin American region, though F–16s have been sold to or authorized for a number of NATO allies, Australia, Korea, Spain, Sweden, Austria, Egypt, and Israel. Should we not approve this request, Venezuela might turn to the French for advanced Mirages.
Prior to mid-1980 the F–5E was the most capable fighter the US would sell in the Latin American region. Last year the marketing of relatively more advanced intermediate export fighters (F–5G and F–16/79) was approved for a number of Latin American countries, including Venezuela. The F–16A would take us a step beyond the F–5G and F–16/79 class in terms of capability, principally because of the F–16A’s more powerful engine.
Venezuela is one of our leading oil suppliers and trading partners, with which we need to maintain close relations. For its part, Venezuela is concerned about growing Cuban military capabilities, and sees a need to replace its aging Mirage III and CF–5 aircraft with more advanced aircraft.
Our willingness to sell F–16As to Venezuela might enhance a number of US policy objectives aimed at insuring security and stability in the Caribbean and Central America. These include maintaining and perhaps increasing Venezuelan security and economic assistance to El Salvador and Jamaica; the supply of Venezuelan military equipment, training, and credit to Caribbean nations; a Venezuelan role in defense of Caribbean sea lanes of communication; and closer interaction between the US and Venezuelan Armed Forces.
Approval, however, would make it difficult to turn down subsequent requests for F–16 aircraft from other major friends in the Latin American region. For example, if we are successful in getting Congress to repeal the current ban on military sales to Argentina, that country might be interested. It would be particularly difficult to turn down a request from Colombia, since the US has traditionally maintained an even-handed approach on arms transfers to that country and Venezuela. The two nations view each other as potential adversaries because of a border dispute in the Gulf of Venezuela. However, the high cost of the F–16A might make it unattractive to Colombia and most other potential customers in the region. Given budgetary stringencies, we do not anticipate that Foreign Military Sales financing will be available for the purchase of advanced aircraft in the region. Since Colombia is less able to pay cash than Venezuela, there could be some Colombian resentment, though we do not believe it would be serious enough to warrant disapproval of the Venezuelan request.
Approval of the F–16A for Venezuela would be consistent with the Administration’s forthcoming formal policy statement on conventional arms transfers, which is now being reviewed on an interagency basis before submission to you for approval. The policy statement will emphasize our interest in meeting the legitimate defense needs of our allies and friends. It will also include references to a recipient’s absorptive capacity and economic situation, neither of which poses a problem in the case of Venezuela. The policy statement will make reference to regional stability, which we feel would not be endangered if we maintain an even-handed approach on possible subsequent requests for the F–16A from other major Latin American countries.
I recommend that you approve in principle the sale of up to 24 F–16A aircraft to Venezuela. Defense and ACDA concur. 3
1 Source: Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Subject File, Venezuela [05/19/1981–05/25/1981]. Confidential.
2 In telegram 3254 from Caracas, March 28, the Embassy reported: “The Venezuelan Air Force (VAF) has officially requested planning and review data for the F–16A fighter,” noted the “sale of the F–16A to Venezuela requires a major policy decision by the administration and, we believe, consultations with congressional leaders,” and recommended “that an administration decision on the sale of the F–16A be made prior to providing the VAF with P&R data on that aircraft.” (Reagan Library, Executive Secretariat, NSC Country File, Latin America/Central, Venezuela (02/28/1981–04/03/1981)) In telegram 3281 from Caracas, March 28, the Embassy transmitted additional reporting on the Venezuelan Air Force data requests regarding the F–16A. (Ibid)
3 There is no indication of Reagan’s approval or disapproval.

464. Memorandum From Robert Schweitzer, Roger Fontaine, Robert Kimmitt, and Chris Shoemaker of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 464. Memorandum From Robert Schweitzer, Roger Fontaine, Robert Kimmitt, and Chris Shoemaker of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) 1
Washington, May 19, 1981
SUBJECT
F–16 Aircraft for Venezuela (C)
Attached is a memorandum from Secretary Haig (Tab A), which recommends approval of the sale of F–16A aircraft to Venezuela. (C)
We are very much worried about this action. To be sure, State and Defense are aboard. Venezuela wants this particular aircraft very badly; no other bird will satisfy because of the hard sell the Air Force has done, to include personal involvement by General Allen, the Air Force Chief of Staff. (C)
Because of the oil lever and the fact the Venezuelans were persuaded by our Air Force to forget about the KFIR and the MIRAGE in favor of the F–16, the decision to buy the F–16 is unalterable in Venezuelan minds. Were we to non-concur, there would be heavy policy damage. The Venezuelans plan to purchase only some 24 F–16s; no real change in the military balance will occur with such a modest number. (C)
We had hoped to market the lower performance FX to Third World countries, rather than our first-line aircraft. Northrop and General Dynamics developed the FX (a generic term to describe Northrop’s F–5G and General Dynamics’ F–16/79) for this purpose. This approach might have worked, but as Northrop complains, every time they enter a market area set aside by USG policy for this aircraft, they find the US military has sold the F–16 to the prospective buyers. Northrop may well press a complaint through the political channel. Unfortunately, the lower performance FX is unattractive to foreign airmen. (C)
We have a similar problem arising in Pakistan where the Paks appear to be on the way to using $2.5 Billion of hard-found Foreign Military Sales (FMS) credits to buy high-performance airplanes instead of the kind of less glamorous defensive arms they need. (C)
The Carter philosophy of refusing to sell sophisticated technology to friends in the Third World only drove those friends to foreign markets where they purchased the same technology with less quality and at higher prices. In the course of these actions we alienated the Latins and lost influence with them. (C)
Some other salient facts:
— There is no real threat that would occasion the need for such sophisticated technology. Were Cuba or the Soviet Union to “attack” Latin countries, the US would be obliged by the Rio Treaty and our own self-interest to defend them; hence the sophisticated technology the Latins need for their least probable threat is already in place without cost to them.
— The real threat to Latin America is not classic invasion by Cuba, but subversion; the best way to meet this form of aggression is to preempt the Communists with social, economic, and political reforms.
— Venezuela is the only Latin nation which in any sense is able to afford the money, but a single visit to any barrio of Caracas would convince a visitor they do not have the money for F–16s either.
— Although other Latin nations cannot afford the F–16s, we can be sure that the Venezuelan purchase will unleash demands of their neighbors for the same aircraft in equal or greater numbers. Colombia, which is a fairly sure bet to come in early, and others will plead their very real financial plight and ask for generous terms, low prices or low-cost leasing. Honduras would probably ask for outright grant aid. There is recent cable traffic proposing that the F–16 be sold to Peru; (there already is a proposal by the USAF Vice Chief of Staff to put the Peruvian AF Chief of Staff into an F–16 cockpit this week: “we have to do it because we did it for the Venezuelans”—and all without a national policy). The foreign credit ramifications for OMB in next year’s budget are potentially great. State and DOD will be preoccupied unnecessarily in long negotiations dealing with requests. However, there is a wider point which troubles us even more: once more the President is being placed in a narrow box where the only alternative to approving the sale at this time is to rupture our relationship with an important oil supplier and friend as well as lose the sale to France or Israel. (C)
Accordingly, this issue should be discussed at the next NSC meeting. In addition to resolving the policy issue, NSC discussion could stress the need for all services to receive prior approval for future attempts to sell expensive, sophisticated systems throughout the world. Unless the President provides the guidance, there is every indication sales of sophisticated aircraft in particular, and other systems will continue to be pushed in the Third World. At the end, the only option for the President will be the kind of forced choice he is faced with in this situation. The problem is serious, even grave, and has more than a disciplinary implication if the President is to be sole organ of foreign policy as the Constitution prescribes. (C)
Ambassador Luers told Frank Carlucci on May 15 2 that time is of the essence, but OSD sources agree with us that this issue can be delayed a week. One reason for Venezuelan urgency is that the current chief of staff of the Venezuelan Air Force is retiring in July. His replacement does not want to purchase F–16s; he wants A–7s and A–10s instead, either of which makes more sense. (C)
RECOMMENDATION
That you sign the memorandum to the President at Tab I, seeking approval for discussion of the Venezuelan F–16 sale at the next NSC. 3 (C)
Tab I
Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (Allen) to President Reagan 4
SUBJECT
F–16 Aircraft for Venezuela (C)
Al Haig recommends approval of the sale of F–16 aircraft for Venezuela (Tab A). Cap Weinberger agrees. Both argue that considerable policy damage with an important oil supplier would occur if we did not approve this sale. Disapproval would also mean that the Venezuelans would probably place an early order for either the French MIRAGE or Israeli KFIR, neither of which they like or want. (C)
Because this sale reverses a policy against first-line fighter sales to Latin America that goes back at least to the Nixon Administration, I asked the Secretaries to provide the memorandum for your review. (C)
We had hoped to induce the Latin nations to procure the lower performance, less expensive FX aircraft. However, the FX is unattractive to well-educated and professional airmen in Third World countries, and our Air Force has pursued a vigorous policy of selling sophisticated aircraft like the F–16 without prior interagency deliberation or adequate consideration of the real needs of recipient states in the Third World. You need to provide guidance, because other cases (Peru and Colombia, and others) will arise, with some nations wanting generous terms. Accordingly, I propose that you direct full consideration of this issue at the next NSC meeting. (U)
RECOMMENDATION
That we discuss this issue more fully at the next NSC prior to final approval. 5 (U)
Tab A Memorandum from Secretary Haig 6

(Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Subject File, Venezuela [04/16/1981–04/30/1981])

465. Memorandum of Conversation 465. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, May 20, 1981, 4–5 p.m.
SUBJECT
Vice President’s Meeting with Venezuelan Minister of Internal Relations
PARTICIPANTS
VENEZUELA
Rafael Andres Montes de Oca, Minister of Internal Relations
Gonzalo Garcia Bustillos, Minister of the Secretariat of the Presidency
Ambassador Marcial Perez Chiriboga
U.S.
The Vice President
Secretary Haig
Assistant Secretary-designate Thomas O. Enders
Ambassador to Venezuela William H. Luers
Charles Tyson, NSC
Roger Fontaine, NSC
Stephanie von Reigersberg, Interpreter
The Vice President welcomed the visitors and said that President Reagan would say, if he were present, that the United States has great respect for the way Venezuela is handling its international affairs and for Venezuela’s democratic system. He said we identify with what Venezuela is doing. Some wonder what this new U.S. Administration is doing. In the human rights area we know Venezuela is concerned. We want Venezuela to know we care about human rights and reform programs. We believe that more attention needs to be given the human rights violations that occur in countries like Cuba. We support Duarte in Salvador like Venezuela does. We can learn from the Venezuelan model. We want to make clear we are concerned about Communism.
Minister Montes de Oca said Venezuela believes human rights violations must be condemned wherever they occur. But Marxists seem more able than democracies to appear as defenders of human rights, while the Marxists are in the process of trampling human rights. We do not have an effective way of competing in the information field. Democracies must devise a better system of providing information to persuade the world or else we can lose the vital war of public opinion.
In the case of El Salvador, for example, said the Minister, President Herrera’s Government has gone to the media repeatedly to defend its program and support Duarte against the strong criticism from the Venezuelan left and Social Democrats. This open debate in a free society has helped people understand. But still the left in its propaganda is able to use people and institutions which are not Marxist. We must defeat this effort and find ways to attract the “useful fools” away from the Marxists.
The Vice President described the real information problems the U.S. government faces here and in Europe over policy toward El Salvador. Minister Montes de Oca stressed that the U.S. and Venezuela must talk more about how more effectively to orient public opinion. In El Salvador we must focus public attention on the election process that will lead to a more stable democratic life there.
The Vice President then said that the U.S. goal is to improve relations with as many countries as possible in the hemisphere. We will have a different style from the previous administration in handling human rights violations and will try to work quietly with friendly countries toward improvement of the situation.
Minister Montes de Oca replied that this U.S. objective is desirable. He believes it should be accompanied by support for two important interests of nations that are often seen as separate routes. We should on the one hand support strongly the evolution of political democracy and liberty by demonstrating, more in our actions than in our talk, the force of liberty in defense of human rights. At the same time we must promote social progress and economic development, in order to be on the side of people struggling against poverty. In this way we can promote integral democracy and thus most effectively fight Marxist insurrection. The Minister said he was delighted, in his talks with Secretary Haig, to learn that the U.S. also strongly supports the need for such integral development—political democracy and economic development must go together. 2
The Vice President asked about the Minister’s views on Cuban-supported guerrilla intervention in Colombia. The Minister said that the M–19 began as a serious, bold home grown terrorist group which earned, as such terrorist groups often wrongly do, a reputation for being populist. Fidel then penetrated this group and gave it support. The M–19 made the mistake of all guerrilla movements everywhere of writing everything down. The Colombians found some documents which enabled them to capture many key M–19 leaders. The Minister said that the difficult economic/social situation in Colombia makes it a logical object of Marxist insurrection. But he believes that Fidelismo can be fought there effectively as elsewhere in the hemisphere. The Minister spoke of two other examples of ways to fight Fidelismo.
In Costa Rica, a tradition of near Athenian democracy exists without an armed force. The strengthening of this democratic system alone has not immunized that country, however, against Marxist insurrection. The Costa Rican leaders realize they must develop an internal security force, and they have told the Venezuelans they plan to do so following the next Costa Rican elections.
Jamaica is another example of how to defeat Fidelismo, said the Minister. The election process in Jamaica resulted in the defeat of Manley and his Cuban friends. We, after talks and cooperation with the United States, have won an important victory in Jamaica. If we continue to work together, when we finish we will have the best example of how to respond to the Marxist threat—democracy and economic development together.
The Vice President said the U.S. fully agrees with the need to cooperate to help Jamaica now and described President Reagan’s initiative in asking David Rockefeller to engage the U.S. private sector in the effort.
The Vice President then turned to the problem of the tax claims against oil companies stemming from nationalization. 3 He said we have heard from the oil companies about these problems and we hope that we can resolve this one potential problem that exists in our close friendly relationship. He said he hopes that there can be progress toward resolving the issue and believes that Ambassadors Enders and Luers will have more to say on that subject later.
Then Secretary Haig said that the discussions the U.S. has had this week with the Venezuelan visitors have been precious and said we are grateful to President Herrera for sending them to Washington to discuss these important issues. We are sorry that President Herrera’s visit did not work out earlier, but we are certain that when the visit does take place it will be very successful.
Minister Montes de Oca said he wanted to reply to the Vice President’s remarks on the oil issue. He said, first, the petroleum nationalization was carried out seriously and under law in a manner that made it an example of comity and justice among nations. Secondly, he said, many of the claims are before the courts, and as the U.S. knows, in democracies there is separation of powers which poses limitations on the Executive branch. But, he added, Venezuela is disposed to discuss this matter with the Ambassadors consistent with our friendship with and respect for the United States. The Minister said he was pleased the Vice President had raised this delicate matter not in a formal way and not making a major point of it.
The Minister concluded by conveying his best wishes to President Reagan and expressing the Venezuelan Government’s pleasure with the work of Ambassador Luers in Venezuela. 4
1 Source: Reagan Library, Roger W. Fontaine Files, Subject File, Venezuela [05/19/1981–05/25/1981]. Confidential. Drafted by Luers; cleared by Enders on May 21. The meeting took place in the Roosevelt Room. In a May 15 information memorandum to Haig, Enders noted that in the interest of “achieving some degree of Latin American consensus on foreign policy issues,” Herrera had called for “a meeting of all Latin American and Caribbean countries” and Zambrano was “trying to arrange a more modest initial meeting with Mexico, Brazil and Argentina.” Enders recommended that Haig not take a position on the subject during his meeting with Montes de Oca and García. 466. Minutes of a National Security Council Meeting 466. Minutes of a National Security Council Meeting 1
Washington, undated
Arms Transfer Policy & F–16As for Venezuela
Mr. Allen opened the discussion by stating that the Secretaries of State and Defense submitted a proposed arms transfer policy statement, and that the NSC Staff prepared a draft National Security Decision Directive predicated on the Secretaries’ statement. 2 The NSDD makes a clean break with PD–13 3 and establishes a positive, flexible, and pragmatic approach toward arms transfers. Consideration of the policy statement also provides a good opportunity to discuss the sale of F–16A fighter aircraft to Venezuela.
Secretary Weinberger stated that there was agreement on the arms transfer policy statement. On Venezuela, he noted that there are MIG–21 and 23 fighters in Cuba, with airfields being built in Nicaragua and Grenada, thereby giving an expanded arc of capability to unfriendly air forces. This new arms transfer policy will permit consideration of all factors involved in a decision of this sort, rather than basing decisions on preconceived notions about arms transfers. 4
The President asked whether this decision might lead countries less able to pay for F–16s to request such aircraft for political reasons.
Secretary Weinberger responded that such requests could be received, and that they would be reviewed in the light of the new arms transfer policy.
The President then asked for a description of the differences between the FX fighter and the F–16.
General Jones responded that both the F–5G and the F–16/79 are very capable planes, but that the F–16 variant has an edge in capability and has a better image than the F–5G.
The President then asked whether it were true that the F–5G could outperform the F–16 in some categories and was it easier to maintain.
General Jones responded in the affirmative, noting that many F–5s have been sold around the world, some as part of coproduction ventures, and that they are cheaper, more easily maintainable aircraft.
The President asked how the F–5G compares against the Mirage.
General Jones responded that the advanced Mirage would be a good competitor for the FX, which includes both the F–5G and F–16/79, while the older Mirage aircraft are not as capable.
The President then remarked that, given these very positive features of the F–5G, we should promote actively both the F–5G and F–16 in future sales efforts.
Secretary Haig then noted that we are really discussing two issues: the policy statement and F–16As for Venezuela. The policy statement is a long overdue revision of the Carter policy. It lays out, on page 2, the important issues that will be considered on an interagency basis prior to decision, including threat and absorptive capacity. Under Secretary Jim Buckley will oversee implementation of the policy at the interagency level. If a case raises significant policy issues, it will be forwarded to the President or the NSC for approval, in a paper that lays out the pros and cons of the particular sale. The policy itself should go out over the President’s signature, with State doing both briefing and background work.
Mr. Allen then asked whether Jim Buckley had already given a preview of this policy both to the Hill and to industry.
Secretary Haig responded that he had, noting that industry was very supportive because they thought that the Carter policy was misguided.
The President agreed, noting that if we ever have to accelerate defense production, we would not have the capability to do so as matters now stand.
Secretary Haig reported that consultations had been undertaken on the Hill. House members, reflecting the Democratic majority, urged the use of more restraint-oriented language, while those in the Senate were more enthusiastic about the forthcoming nature of the policy. In sum, he foresaw no major problems on the Hill.
Mr. Allen stated that an NSDD revoking PD–13 would be forwarded to the President.
Secretary Haig then turned the group’s attention toward the proposed sale of F–16A aircraft to Venezuela, noting that the F–16A is more capable than the F–16/79.
General Jones agreed, saying that the F–16A is a high performance aircraft substantially more capable than the F–5G.
The President asked exactly which plane was being proposed for Venezuela.
General Jones responded that it was the high performance F–16A.
Secretary Haig stated that if the F–16A were not approved, the Venezuelans indicated that they would purchase Mirage aircraft from France. At this point, we are only providing details to the Venezuelans so that they can make a final decision on whether to purchase the aircraft.
Mr. Allen asked whether the decision was required before the Herrera visit.
Secretary Haig suggested that if the President were to approve the State-Defense-ACDA recommendation, we could provide the details now and, if the Venezuelans are interested, the final decision to sell could be announced when the President sees Herrera.
Mr. Allen then asked the cost of an F–16A as compared to the FX aircraft.
General Jones responded that both the F–16A and the F–16/79 would cost approximately $30 million per aircraft, including support. The F–5G would be cheaper.
Secretary Haig then noted that this decision would have implications elsewhere in the world, particularly in Pakistan, which is also interested in F–16.
The President then remarked that anyone objecting to the sale should make his objections known.
Secretary Weinberger stated that it might be better to approve the sale before the Herrera visit so that it would not look as if the decision had been extracted from the President. It would also make for a better atmosphere at the meeting with Herrera.
Secretary Haig said that advance approval would also avoid making the President look like an arms merchant trading weapons for bilateral concessions.
The Vice President then asked what threat would be countered by this sale, since the new arms transfer policy makes clear that threat is a primary consideration.
Secretary Weinberger responded that primary threat for Venezuela is MIG aircraft that are based in Cuba and that perhaps would be based in airfields in Grenada and Nicaragua.
Secretary Haig said again that if the Venezuelans did not receive approval to buy the F–16A, they would turn to the Mirage.
Secretary Weinberger stated that the F–16/79 would probably be sufficient to counter the threat to Venezuela, but since the Venezuelans are good friends and can pay for the F–16A, we should approve the sale.
Mr. Schneider agreed that the Venezuelans can afford the aircraft but noted that the other countries [ less than one line not declassified ] cannot. He suggested, therefore, that a policy be developed that would take the recipient’s financial situation into account before considering a sale of first-line fighters. Also, if one reason for making the sale is to reduce unit costs and warm the production base, we should consider the full range of first-line fighters, including the F–18.
The President remarked that this approach made good sense.
Secretary Haig noted that we will breach “the sound barrier” with this first-line sale to Latin America, and that we may be approached by other “have” nations ([ less than one line not declassified ] for example) once the decision has been made. The fact is, however, that we have been lagging and the Europeans have been gaining. Thus, when new requirements develop, we will assess them against the new policy guidance.
Mr. Allen said that arms transfers raising significant issues should be considered good topics for the NSC advance notice calendar.
Mr. Meese underscored that this would be the first introduction of first-line fighter aircraft into Latin America.
General Jones said that if we were “free agents” on this issue, we would probably prefer to sell the FX to Venezuela. However, we were not free to do so in this case because of European competition.
Mr. Meese remarked that national prestige was also involved.
Mr. Allen stated that, in the guidance accompanying announcement of this decision, there should be some reference to our not desiring to spur an arms race by virtue of this sale.
Secretary Weinberger agreed, noting, however, that we should not establish a policy of selling first-line aircraft only to those who could pay, because there may be cases in which we wish to provide such aircraft to poorer nations.
Secretary Haig said that State and Defense would prepare the necessary guidance to accompany this decision.
Mr. Baker asked whether the guidance would state only that the decision to provide details had been made.
Secretary Haig said it would be better to say that the President has approved the sale in principle.
Mr. Allen said that memoranda to the parties concerned would be prepared.
Mr. Baker asked whether we should defer announcing the decision if Herrera were to visit in July.
Secretary Weinberger thought not, since we should avoid the appearance of the sale being extracted from the President.
Secretary Haig agreed, saying that advance approval would also provide a better atmosphere for the visit. 5

467. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 467. Intelligence Memorandum Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
PA 81–10302 Washington, August 1981
[Omitted here are title pages.]
Venezuela-Latin America: Reaching for a Unified Foreign Policy
Summary
Venezuela’s continuing attempts to convene a foreign ministers’ meeting with Brazil, Argentina, and Mexico—proposed as the first step toward formulating a coordinated foreign policy—reflect the Herrera administration’s concern over what it perceives as the East-West thrust of the emerging US policy for Latin America. Fearing that the region could become the setting for bipolar conflict, Caracas is trying to focus and project Latin American uneasiness before Washington settles on its course.
The four nations, however, hold widely divergent political views and pursue some dissimilar goals. Although they share the region’s general uneasiness over the potential effect of superpower interference on security interests and economic development, their differences—at any multilateral meeting—would color discussions of Central America, Cuba, and US policy.
Thus, on balance, talks among the four would offer a forum for reiterating traditional Latin American positions on nonintervention and self-determination—positions that, while couched in global terms, would be directed primarily at the United States. But on the issues fundamental to a unified foreign policy, a meeting is likely to be more revealing in underscoring regional disharmony than in establishing bases from which to proceed.
[Omitted here is the body of the memorandum.]
1 Source: Central Intelligence Agency, Office of Support Services (DI), Job 82T00565R: Production Case Files (1981), Box 1, Folder 23: Venezuela—Latin America: Reaching For a Unified Foreign Policy. Secret; Noforn; [ handling restriction not declassified ]. Prepared in the Office of Political Analysis and coordinated with DO, the Office of Economic Research, and the NIO for Latin America. Information available as of July 16 was used in the preparation of the memorandum.

468. Memorandum of Conversation 468. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, November 16, 1981, 5:35–6:35 p.m.
SUBJECT
Venezuelan President Herrera Campins and Secretary Haig Bilateral
PARTICIPANTS
United States
The Secretary
Ambassador William H. Luers
Assistant Secretary Thomas O. Enders
Samuel F. Hart (Notetaker)
Venezuela
President Luis Herrera Campins
Foreign Minister Zambrano
Minister of the Presidency Garcia Bustillos
Minister of Finance Ugueto
Minister of Defense Lael
Minister of Agriculture Zapata
Ambassador Perez Chiriboga
Ambassador Cardozo
Corina Balzalo
The following substantive issues were discussed during this meeting:
NORTH/SOUTH ISSUES
Herrera Campins said that at Cancun expectations had been created that the North/South global negotiations would go forward. 2 It is important to consolidate what has been achieved by the setting and implementation of partial, concrete goals. One such place to begin would be assistance by the industrialized countries to the developing countries in food and agriculture, a theme that was flagged at Cancun. Another possibility would be the development of a special Inter-American Development Bank Trust fund which would help the private sector. Since Cancun should be seen as a defeat for the Soviet Union, we must take advantage of the opportunity now before us. Secretary Haig responded that since Cancun President Reagan has begun steps to implement his commitment on agriculture. This involves consultations with other countries. Furthermore, Ambassador Kirkpatrick has been instructed to explore informally with all interested nations how we should go forward with the global negotiations. The experience at Cancun had a profound impact on President Reagan despite the difficulties. The US is optimistic that the spirit of Cancun will be an historic achievement. President Reagan is very much engaged and will welcome President Herrera’s observations.
THE CARIBBEAN BASIN INITIATIVE
Secretary Haig then moved to a discussion of the status of the CBI. A successful regional effort strengthens global negotiations. Not enough preparatory work had been done before the last ministerial meeting to give the CBI real shape. After January 1, President Reagan will announce important new efforts by the US in trade, investment and economic assistance. This should be kept confidential because the Congress and other interested groups have not yet been fully consulted. Our efforts reflect our concern about the Caribbean and Central America and we are convinced that the poor and hungry there need help in solving their problems, not just words. Unless we can relieve the social crisis, our security assistance will fail. If President Herrera can express concern about the economic crisis in the region while in the US, this could help convince the US Congress to provide the resources. Of course, it would also be useful for President Herrera to mention the security threat and aggression in the region. We hope that at a minimum President Herrera will not find it necessary to say anything which would hamper our efforts to get economic and security resources.
EL SALVADOR
President Herrera noted that he believed time was on the side of democracy in El Salvador. He asked about some pessimistic statements attributed to the Secretary. The Secretary responded that we have a fine line to walk in painting a grave enough picture to get resources from the Congress while at the same time not making it seem so grave as to discourage our friends. The government of El Salvador must understand that it has to go forward with elections. President Herrera commented that the military and right must receive no encouragement to interfere with free elections. His information was that even the hardest liners on the Salvadoran Right are beginning to accept this need. Secretary Haig reiterated our commitment to the election process. He added that it would be useful if the Venezuelan military could share the benefits of its anti-guerrilla experience in the 1960’s with the Salvadorans. We are worried because the Salvadoran military only reacts now and they should be taking initiatives as Venezuela did.
NICARAGUA
Secretary Haig said that we have become increasingly concerned in recent weeks about Nicaragua. The Nicaraguan Government is systematically eliminating its opposition. It is important that the other nations of the region appear as concerned about this as we are. We appreciate the Venezuelan reaction of diplomatic disapproval toward the Sandinistas and welcome Venezuelan ideas on how to deal with the problem. President Herrera said he was also concerned about Nicaragua and had made a declaration on the subject, as had Acting President Montes de Oca. While there is severe pressure on the democratic forces, information indicates that the time may be approaching when the democratic countries of the hemisphere can undertake a political step to reverse the trend there. The GRN is susceptible to international pressure. Nicaragua is not lost; the door must be kept open for those Nicaraguan forces which are fighting for pluralism. We should not leave them alone. For example, help must be given to the press which is being strangled economically and to free radio stations to keep them operating. Venezuela realizes that what happens in Nicaragua and elsewhere will have its effect throughout the continent.
F–16’s
The Secretary said that we had gone ahead with the notification to Congress of the F–16 sale to Venezuela on Thursday, November 12, and that we expect approval of this without major difficulties because Venezuela is such a highly regarded democracy. 3 We are also committed to try to deliver the first aircraft before President Herrera leaves office.
OIL EXPROPRIATION/COMPENSATION
This issue, Secretary Haig noted, is one of the few bilateral problems between us. It would be useful if President Herrera could help find some solution.
FRIENDSHIP BETWEEN OUR COUNTRIES
It was mentioned by Secretary Haig that sometimes friends dwell too much on their areas of difference and too little on their many areas of agreement. We deeply value our friendship with Venezuela. Because of Venezuela’s democratic history and its influence in the region, it can do much that we cannot. We don’t want to burden Venezuela with a requirement of endorsement of our policy. It is more important that our values converge. We sincerely welcome advice and criticism from Venezuela and President Reagan is looking forward to fuller exploration of several of the subjects discussed above in his talks with President Herrera. President Herrera said that one of the attributes of friendship is that one can talk about everything frankly.

Goldberg signed for Haig. The meeting took place at Blair House. In telegram 10292 from Caracas, October 30, the Embassy sent briefing information for Herrera’s visit. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D810514–0990) Herrera made a State visit to Washington, November 16–19.
2 According to the President’s Daily Diary, Reagan met with Herrera October 21 at the Cancun Summit conference on North-South issues. (Reagan Library, President’s Daily Diary) No memorandum of conversation has been found.
3 See Document 466 . In a November 6 action memorandum from Enders, Fairbanks, and Burt, Buckley approved their recommendation that Congress be informally notified on November 12 of the F–16 sale to Venezuela. (Reagan Library, Jacqueline Tillman Files, Subject File, Venezuela 11/07/1981)

469. Memorandum of Conversation 469. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, November 17, 1981, 11 a.m.
SUBJECT
President Reagan’s Meeting with Venezuelan President Luis Herrera Campins
PARTICIPANTS
The President
The Vice President
The Secretary of State
Mr. Richard V. Allen
Ambassador William H. Luers
President Luis Herrera Campins
Foreign Minister Zambrano Velasco
Minister of the Presidency Garcia Bustillos
Ambassador Marcial Perez Chiriboga
President Reagan welcomed President Herrera. He said that we are concerned about the deteriorating situation in Central America and are persuaded that we cannot permit Marxist subversion there to be successful. We are also doing everything we can to bring about elections in El Salvador and we support President Duarte. We would like to hear President Herrera’s ideas on what more we can do.
President Herrera thanked President Reagan for the warm welcome that he has received. He said that all of Latin America has a great concern about what is happening in Central America. In Venezuela there is great expectation about President Herrera’s visit to Washington and about these talks because the US and Venezuela both are democratic nations which try to project these values in their foreign policy. In the Caribbean and Central America there are two types of action where we should work jointly. One action is through government and private cooperation. We should try to help the nations of that region to overcome the difficulties that they face. President Herrera said he hopes that Mexico, the United States, Canada and Venezuela will achieve concrete results from what was begun at Nassau. 2 We cannot allow the social/economic problems and the injustices in the region to be exploited by Communist forces to subvert these nations. President Herrera said we must, however, be conscious of the need not to act in such a way that creates problems for other countries in the area not already affected by Communist subversion. He said that the most delicate point which the President felt he should make concerned the great fear in Latin America that the principle of non-intervention not be violated. In its search for security, the U.S. must proceed with prudence and great wisdom or else the search could affect us all negatively and damage world peace.
President Herrera said that one other example of how we can work together is the case of Jamaica, where Fidelist tactics received their major setback. We will be able to deliver a second noble blow to Communist subversion if we succeed in finding a democratic way out in El Salvador. The strongly-held collaboration between the United States and Venezuela could help this effort significantly. We have to demonstrate that Communist subversion in El Salvador does not today have the strength it had before. Also secondly, we must be certain that the forces of the Right do not think they can count on support from the U.S. to carry out a coup against the existing government.
President Reagan said that he can personally assure President Herrera that the U.S. considers both the Right and the Left as extremists and that the U.S. is working hard to support Duarte in his efforts to maintain a central government. The President said that the U.S. and Venezuela are thinking more similarly perhaps than President Herrera realizes. He said that history has made Latin American countries suspicious of the Colossus of the North. This is precisely why the United States has proposed the Caribbean plan which should be worked out with the cooperation of other nations. The President said he also agreed fully that the Jamaica example has a great importance. If together we can show other nations that through our programs we mean not to interfere in their internal affairs but we mean to allow nations to find democratic ways to eliminate social injustice, then we will have accomplished a great deal. The President said that Venezuela is important in this process. We fully intend to work with Venezuela and learn from them. On the other hand, the President added, he is concerned that time is not on our side. We see a significant military arms buildup in Nicaragua which is threatening Nicaragua’s neighbors.
President Herrera said that he would like to recall an important event that is relevant to the situation in Nicaragua. In the last months of the Manley Government in Jamaica, the opposition party asked President Herrera to cut off petroleum shipments and economic assistance to the Manley Government. President Herrera said that he reviewed this request and decided to continue the assistance to Manley. The next time President Herrera saw PM Manley he assured Manley that he would fulfill his commitments to ship oil and provide some assistance. But President Herrera also said that there is a political problem that in Jamaica could be resolved by free elections. After the discussion, Manley promised to hold elections and set an appropriate date for them. President Herrera then told the opposition to make themselves ready for the elections since he believed Manley would agree to hold them. The elections were finally held and, of course, the opposition won. This is reality not theory.
The President, turning to Nicaragua, said he has grave concerns about the problems there: La Prensa , the large Cuban presence, the difficulties of the private sector and the trade unions. However, the President said, he believes this is a moment in which international political pressure can be brought to bear on the Nicaraguan Government by democracies, particularly Latin American democracies, that could lead to an opening up in Nicaragua. The Nicaraguans are having serious problems economically including liquidity problems. They now are beginning to realize that they must do something. Internally it is essential that pluralist Nicaraguan forces that need support receive it; such as the political parties, the church, the press, the trade union movement and human rights organizations. The President said he believes that there is a possibility of changing the Council of State into a more important body—with characteristics of a legislature. The President said that, as long as there is a chance for pluralism in Nicaragua, it is the obligation of the governments of the hemisphere to try to achieve an opening and to work toward expanding the forces of pluralism. That is why Venezuela continues to have cordial relations with the Junta and very friendly relations with the parties and other groups of the opposition.
President Herrera said that he realizes that it is difficult to learn much from a reading of the press which often distorts events. He said he believes security is a goal that every nation must try to achieve in its way. However, with the interdependence that exists in this hemisphere, and, indeed in the world, the unilateral search for security by one nation could damage the peace of the entire world and bring about insecurity for us all. As concerned as Venezuela is about events in Nicaragua, Salvador and Cuba, the President said he wanted to permit himself, as a friend of the United States, to urge that, in its search for security, the United States Government take steps that are very well thought out in advance because searches for security that are not well thought out might lead to the destruction of peace throughout the world.
President Reagan said that that is precisely why we are placing such emphasis on a political solution, through elections, in El Salvador.
President Herrera said that he was pleased to hear this. It must be clear, he added, that Salvador has become a neuralgic point for world Communism and Communist forces have set a victory in Salvador as a point of honor for them. Therefore, President Herrera said the defeat of Communism in Salvador will have worldwide significance that would have impact in favor of democracy throughout the world.
President Herrera said democracies have special qualities. For example, after the French/Mexican Communique, democratic and non-democratic nations in the hemisphere got together to ensure that further support did not develop for this damaging document. By acting quickly and concretely, we minimized the effect and turned the Communique into a negative factor. We believe that there is much ground for political action on the part of democracies.
President Reagan replied that the U.S. had agreed and welcomed Venezuela’s actions and believe that it was an unfortunate statement that French and Mexicans had made. He said the U.S. seeks a political solution in Salvador through elections. But he added we also know that the other side is building up its forces, strengthening and arming the forces of subversion. President Herrera replied that he believes that there are ways to dissuade or deter the forces of subversion from being successful. President Reagan said we would be very interested over the next two days in hearing President Herrera’s thoughts as to how this could be accomplished.
The two Presidents then moved from the Oval Office into the Cabinet Room to meet the larger delegations.
1 Source: Reagan Library, Executive Secretariat, NSC Subject File, Memorandums of Conversation—President Reagan (11/19/1981–11/27/1981). Secret. In a November 12 memorandum to Reagan, Allen wrote that “it is vital that you discuss fully and frankly with President Herrera Campins the situation in Central America” and that “above all, President Herrera Campins must understand your determination to keep democracy and the hope for democracy alive in the region, and that we will not permit Cuban interventionism to succeed. American firmness on this question—particularly—in your direct, private talks with the Venezuelan President—will help strengthen his resolve despite internal Venezuelan pressure to do otherwise.” (Reagan Library, WHORM: Subject File, Republic of Venezuela, CO171, 033898)

470. Memorandum of Conversation 470. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, November 17, 1981, 11:30 a.m.
SUBJECT
President Reagan’s Expanded Meeting with Venezuelan President Herrera Campins
PARTICIPANTS
U.S.
The President
The Vice President
The Secretary of State
The Secretary of Defense
Mr. Edwin Meese, III
Mr. Richard V. Allen
Ambassador William H. Luers
Assistant Secretary of State Thomas O. Enders
Mr. Roger Fontaine, NSC
VENEZUELAN
President Herrera Campins
Foreign Minister Zambrano Velasco
Finance Minister Ugueto Arismendi
Defense Minister Leal Puchi
Minister of Energy Calderon Berti
Minister of the Presidency Garcia Bustillos
Minister of Agriculture Zapata
Ambassador Perez Chiriboga
Ambassador Cardozo
Ambassador Sosa Rodriguez
Admiral Lanz Castellanos
President Reagan briefly reviewed for the enlarged group what he and President Herrera had discussed in the Oval Office. The President then asked Secretary Haig what might be covered in this meeting. The Secretary said that Secretary Weinberger might wish to discuss the F–16’s. 2 We should also touch on the residual petroleum nationalization issues and on the follow-up to Cancun. 3 The Secretary said President Herrera might also have some matters to raise.
Secretary Weinberger said that the Reagan Administration has agreed to the sale of F–16’s to Venezuela, but for tactical reasons the proposal was not submitted to Congress until recently, so that this sale would not become confused with other arms sales problems. Now we believe that Congress will approve the sale. The Congress is, however, an independent body and one can never be certain. Secretary Weinberger said that we will make strong arguments and we are confident we will see it approved by Congress. We will also try to speed up delivery of some of the aircraft as much as possible.
President Reagan asked whether President Herrera would like to bring up other issues. President Herrera said that there are three main issues, aside from bilateral questions between our two governments, that he believes should be discussed during his visit to Washington:
— First is the concrete and rapid implementation of the discussions of the four nations at the Nassau Meeting.
— Second is an effective search for a democratic way out in Salvador.
— And third is the follow-up to Cancun on global negotiations.
These three issues relate to the support for democracy in the world. The first two relate directly to democracy. If we can be successful we will achieve a worldwide blow against Communism. On the follow-up to Cancun, democracies should come up with some proposals that will demonstrate that democracies are more creative and more able than other systems to deal with the changing world. He said that these three topics can be discussed at later meetings. President Herrera thanked Secretary Weinberger for his comments on the F–16.
President Reagan concluded the meeting by saying that in the Wednesday meeting the two Presidents should discuss Cancun and bilateral issues. 4

471. Memorandum of Conversation 471. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, November 18, 1981, 11:30 a.m.
SUBJECT
President Reagan’s Wrap-up Meeting with Venezuelan President Herrera Campins
PARTICIPANTS
The President
The Secretary
Mr. Richard V. Allen
Ambassador William H. Luers
President Luis Herrera Campins
Minister of Foreign Affairs Jose Alberto Zambrano Velasco
Minister of the Presidency Gonzalo Garcia Bustillos
Ambassador Marcial Perez Chiriboga
President Herrera said that he listened to the President’s speech that morning from the Press Club on arms limitations and was left with a magnificent impression. 2 He said the entire delegation had listened to it in Blair House with much sympathy. He said it would make a great contribution to the relaxation of tension in the world. The Venezuelans welcomed this initiative taken at such a difficult moment in international relations. The President congratulated President Reagan most sincerely and cordially on the speech. He said the only thing he regretted was that because the Venezuelans had already scheduled the noon luncheon at the Press Club, President Reagan had to speak in the morning.
President Reagan thanked President Herrera for his kind remarks.
President Reagan then said we are going forward on our Caribbean plan and we should have some specific ideas ready by the first of next year. He said we are also ready to move ahead on global negotiations on the basis of the terms agreed to at Cancun. The President said that he thought that the Caribbean plan would provide some showcases on how, in practical ways, the ideas we have in mind might work in developing relations between the industrialized and developing countries. The President said he would welcome President Herrera’s ideas.
President Herrera thanked President Reagan for mentioning the global negotiations in his morning speech. This reference, he said, will contribute greatly to the attitudes of the countries in the developing world which will recognize that the highest authority in the United States has expressed himself publicly again in favor of moving forward. President Herrera said he had been talking that morning with his principal adviser on these issues, Ambassador Perez Guerrero, who is optimistic that we can begin soon on these negotiations. He said there was no desire on the part of the Third World nations, who have a majority in the United Nations, to impose their will on the minority—the industrial nations. The developing nations seek a consensus approach to these very important issues.
President Herrera said that a beginning could be made by discussing issues relating to agriculture and food or even trade and energy, leaving until later the more delicate issues such as monetary reform. President Herrera also agreed with President Reagan that if the four Nassau nations could design programs for economic cooperation and place into action these programs for the development of the Caribbean region, this would be evidence that specific results from the North/South relationship are possible. President Herrera said he believes that in the Caribbean we should take advantage of Canada’s special connection with the English-speaking islands. He said the Canadian Ambassador who recently presented his credentials in Venezuela had stressed Canada’s desire to cooperate closely with the US and Venezuela on this Caribbean initiative. Herrera said that we must continue to maintain contacts on this matter at the political level and at the technical level. He reiterated his interest in including private and public sector elements in the program.
President Reagan said that we are moving well along now on these matters. He asked President Herrera how he would like to move forward on elections in El Salvador and to help our friend Duarte. President Reagan also asked what were the possibilities that steps could be made to press some elements of the FDR into the electoral process.
President Herrera responded by saying there were a number of things that must be done. First, we must make certain that the Duarte Government has enough sense of stability that it is and can be confident in implementing the elections. In order to assure the stability of Duarte, we must support the Junta against all forces from the Right and from the Left. President Reagan replied that we did everything we could during the visit of President Duarte to demonstrate to all that we support his central government against both extremes, on the Left and on the Right. Duarte met with members of Congress and the press and he impressed upon them his task and problems.
President Herrera continued by saying that a second objective should be to promote broader participation in the process from other groups, even those that are not now political parties, and assure all opposition elements that they will have adequate time to prepare for the elections to the constitutional assembly. We must promote political contacts with Ungo and other social democrats to try to infuse them with more enthusiasm for this electoral process.
Thirdly, President Herrera said, we should encourage international supervision of the electoral process in El Salvador to demonstrate the serious democratic intention of the Duarte Government. Organizations, such as the OAS and other international bodies, could participate in the supervision. The Duarte Government has indicated it welcomes this approach. President Herrera then said, we should discourage those who are pressing for the participation of the guerrillas or subversives in the electoral process. No government would be able to accept the inclusion of terrorists in the government or in the armed forces as a precondition for elections.
President Reagan said that he agreed with those points. But, he said, the problem we are facing today is that the moderates who are in the FDR refuse to join the process and are not inclined to separate themselves from the guerrillas. President Reagan agreed that there is no way the terrorists should be included in the government. If, however, we are to attract moderates on the Left to support the elections, Venezuela would probably be far more effective than the United States in this endeavor.
President Herrera said Venezuela has identified some elements that would be useful in this regard. For example, the Herrera Government has held discussions with the Venezuelan social democratic party, Accion Democratica. Accion Democratica (AD) has close working relations with other social democratic parties. Herrera’s Government has tried to motivate AD to establish contacts with visiting members of the Salvadoran Junta. During some recent visits to Venezuela these relations have become quite cordial.
President Herrera said there is one card that has not been played but that might be considered. We should approach the left wing of the German social democratic party—the followers of Willy Brandt through the German liberal party on which the coalition government of Helmut Schmidt depends. He believes that if we could convince the liberals to influence the social democrats in Germany we could perhaps move the SD’s to encourage the social democrats in El Salvador to participate in the electoral process.
President Herrera said finally that subversion threatens to destabilize a country both economically and politically. The political situation in El Salvador depends very much on the economic situation. We should not forget, therefore, the need over the coming months to keep the economy as healthy as possible.
President Reagan said he wanted to make clear that, in bringing about a settlement in El Salvador, we must be careful to steer clear of elements that could be permanent destructive factors in the society. The President used the example of Laos, where we persuaded a government to join with hard core Communists in a coalition. The hard core Communists eventually became the government of Laos.
Secretary Haig said that there was one more issue that the Treasury Department had raised with President Herrera 3 —the question of the claims against the nationalization of petroleum companies. The Secretary said that he believes we should continue to discuss this problem to try to put it behind us. President Reagan agreed and the meeting disbanded.
The two Presidents then went to make their press statements. 4

472. Memorandum From Constantine Menges and Oliver North of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) 472. Memorandum From Constantine Menges and Oliver North of the National Security Council Staff to the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) 1
Washington, December 21, 1983
SUBJECT
Secretary Shultz’ visit to Venezuela, Brazil, and El Salvador
Attached at Tab I is a memo from you to the President concerning the proposal by Secretary Shultz that he lead the delegation to the inauguration of President-elect Lusinchi, and subsequently visit Brazil and El Salvador as had been planned in October. The memo from Shultz to the President is attached at Tab A.
Your memo endorses the proposal of Secretary Shultz. It also informs the President that there are two factions within the just-elected social democratic party of Venezuela—one led by the President-elect with a strong labor union base which is realistic about the communist threat in Central America, and the other led by former President Carlos Andres Perez, which has been naive in expecting positive results from coalitions with the communists in Central America. 2
We think it important that, between now and the visit of Secretary Shultz, we send one or two senior officials to meet with President-elect Lusinchi in Caracas, Venezuela for an exchange of views on the situation in Central America and our objectives. Yesterday, a leading member of the Venezuelan private sector and friend of the President-elect, urged that this be done in the course of a meeting on another subject with [ less than one line not declassified ] and Menges. 3 The weeks before President-elect Lusinchi assumes the responsibilities of office is an ideal time to provide him and his key advisors with the factual information and background briefing materials which can prepare the way for constructive results when Secretary Shultz and his group arrive.
You had approved my travel with Secretary Shultz to Brazil and El Salvador in October, and I would like to request that you approve my travel with the Secretary on this trip to Venezuela, Brazil and El Salvador. As an observer at the various meetings with our Ambassadors, I believe such a trip would add to my effectiveness here at the NSC.
RECOMMENDATION
That you sign and forward the memo to the President, and that you approve my travel with the Secretary on this trip and suggest this to him. 4
Tab I
Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) to President Reagan 5
SUBJECT
Secretary Shultz’ visit to Venezuela, Brazil and El Salvador
Issue
Venezuela will inaugurate its sixth consecutive democratically elected President Jaime Lusinchi on February 3, 1984, and Secretary Shultz suggests that he lead the delegation and then continue on to visit Brazil and El Salvador.
Facts
Dr. Lusinchi and his Social Democratic Party won the Venezuelan elections by a large margin and want to establish a close working relationship with us. The President-elect has told us that Venezuela will continue to stress the importance of democracy in Central America as part of the Contadora negotiating process. 6 Venezuela must also reschedule its $35 billion foreign debt and cope with severe economic problems triggered by the fall in price of its major export, oil.
In Brazil, Secretary Shultz wants to demonstrate symbolic support to follow-up on your visit of last year and to assist both the continuation of the democratization process and the orderly resolution of its economic problems—especially the continuing refinancing of its $90 billion foreign debt. 7
In El Salvador, Secretary Shultz wants to follow-up the Vice President’s successful visit of December 11, and have a regional strategy meeting with U.S. Ambassadors from the area.
Discussion
The just-elected Social Democratic Party of Venezuela has two factions—one led by the President-elect with a strong labor union base which is realistic about the communist threat in Central America, and a second group, led by former President Carlos Andres Perez, which has been naive in expecting positive results from coalitions with the communists in Central America. It is very important that the U.S. provide the new President-elect with timely information and support so that his group will shape Venezuela’s foreign policy in the Caribbean region in a constructive way. Secretary Shultz’s visit can be an important part of that process. His visit to Brazil and El Salvador—delayed by the October tragedy in Lebanon and our rescue mission in Grenada—also can make a positive contribution.
Recommendation
That you approve the request of Secretary Shultz to visit Venezuela, Brazil and El Salvador in February 1984. 8
Tab A
Memorandum From Secretary of State Shultz to President Reagan 9
SUBJECT
My Visit to Venezuela, Brazil and El Salvador
Venezuela’s sixth democratically elected president, Jaime Lusinchi, will be inaugurated between February 2 to 4. With your approval I would like to lead the delegation to the inauguration. As part of the same trip I would also hope to visit Brazil and El Salvador as planned in October.
The winner by a landslide, Lusinchi wants to establish a close working relationship with us. Under his leadership Venezuela will continue to stress the importance of democracy in Central America and in the Contadora process. Lusinchi also must tackle immediately the rescheduling of Venezuela’s $35 billion foreign debt and a severe economic downturn triggered by the fall in oil prices.
In Brazil, we will conclude our report on the five Working Groups which you and President Figueiredo created a year ago. 10 Brazil wants and badly needs external support, both financial and symbolic. Its economy is severely constrained by its external imbalance and its political democratization has created a sense of uncertainty. The Brazilian government has been concerned that the US would not give sufficient importance to bilateral ties. Without a definitive show of external support, the country might not have sufficient self-confidence to resolve its economic problems in an orderly fashion and to conclude successfully its political democratization. While in Brazil, I also plan to meet with our Ambassadors in South America.
In El Salvador I will follow up George Bush’s successful visit last week with a full court press on the GOES to make and announce essential changes in several key areas that should strengthen our hand with the Congress. I also plan to review regional strategy at a meeting with our Ambassadors from the area.
I recommend you authorize me to lead the U.S. delegation to Venezuelan President-elect Lusinchi’s inauguration.

473. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 473. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA 84–10082 Washington, January 1984
[Omitted here is a title page.]
Venezuela: Challenges Facing the New Administration
Venezuela’s new President Jaime Lusinchi will face serious economic and political challenges when he takes office on 2 February 1984. Despite a solid majority of the presidential votes and majority control of the bicameral legislature, we do not believe that he will succeed in easing his country’s financial difficulties or reviving the recession-ridden economy during his first year.
Lusinchi is by most accounts [ less than one line not declassified ] without well-formulated views on political and economic issues. The US Embassy believes that the magnitude of his victory represents more a repudiation of incumbent President Herrera and proof of the organizational capability of Lusinchi’s Democratic Action Party (AD) than a vote of confidence in the President-elect’s qualifications for the job.
We and the US Embassy believe that his announced preference for a consensus approach to government will hinder his efforts to establish his authority over a divided ruling party, an influential labor sector that believes it will receive favored treatment from his administration, and unusually uncooperative opposition parties. Indeed, the combination of a humiliated and divided Social Christian Party (COPEI) and the AD’s impressive victory—the largest since democratic government was restored in 1958—could undermine the traditional spirit of cooperation and shared economic and social objectives between the country’s two dominant, centrist parties.
We believe Lusinchi is likely to follow moderately expansionary economic policies, which are traditionally characteristic of Democratic Action administrations. This seems to us implicit in his statements both before and after the elections and would represent a compromise among various and competing economic advisers. Although reduced export earnings, which have fallen by over 30 percent from the 1981 peak, and pressures from international lenders will force Venezuela to implement some austerity, we believe Caracas feels relatively self-assured by its growing foreign exchange reserves, which have risen to over $11 billion, and its ability to cut imports by 40 percent since last February. Because of these two factors—and traditionally ardent nationalism—Caracas is unlikely to risk the political costs of seeking a formal IMF agreement. Lusinchi may instead seek an unofficial IMF “endorsement” of a self-imposed austerity program or some arrangement that would gain the Fund’s stamp of approval with minimal conditionality.
In the short term this could be a viable political and economic strategy, but neither it nor other feasible options are likely to be sufficient to move Venezuela out of recession over the next year. More importantly, Lusinchi’s probable course will only postpone—and intensify—the structural adjustments the government would have to make to assure stable economic growth over the longer run. The government also will pay other costs: growing inflationary pressures, greater erosion in investor confidence in Venezuela’s economy, and increased unemployment. Minor policy adjustments, especially to protect against any significant losses of labor backing, are likely.
Venezuela’s outlook—tough political sledding for President Lusinchi and continued economic stagnation—will adversely affect traditionally close relationships with the United States. Lusinchi rejects any idea of a special relationship between Caracas and Washington. If he continues his circumspect approach to foreign policy—and preoccupation with economic problems may incline him to do so—the foreign affairs arena will be left more open for the aggressive former President Carlos Andres Perez to implement his Third World views. Perez, in any event, is likely to be a growing power in the administration as he marshals forces for a run at the presidency in 1988.
On the economic side, we believe that continued import and foreign exchange controls will sharply reduce US exports to Venezuela in 1984. Although we believe it is highly unlikely, Caracas could decide to carry out its threats of continued debt repayment moratoriums and even debt repudiation if it sees no progress in negotiating debt relief with foreign creditors. This would especially hurt US creditors, who hold 48 percent of Venezuela’s $36 billion external debt. We believe that such an action, if successful, could set a precedent that other debtor nations might be inclined to emulate.
[Omitted here is the table of contents, a map, and the body of the Assessment.]

474. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 474. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the White House 1
February 3, 1984, 1542Z
Secto 2023. Subject: Sec-Pres Caracas February 1–2.
1. (Secret—Entire text).
2. Memorandum for the President from George P. Shultz.
3. Visit to Venezuela, February 1–2, 1984. The inaugural ceremonies here were the center piece of the day, well attended and promising. President Lusinchi’s inaugural address was moderate and statesmanlike. I had an excellent private meeting with the Central American core four Foreign Ministers and bilaterals with the Presidents of Argentina and Bolivia. 2
4. A dozen heads of government and more than 70 delegations, including numerous representatives of the Soviet bloc, marked Venezuela’s global reach. Behind the fanfare, however, this is a country aware that it is in deep economic trouble but which remains committed to Western values and hemispheric stability.
5. Lusinchi’s inaugural address was statesmanlike and confident. In domestic matters, he stressed the need to put Venezuela’s finances in order, making an appeal for the support of all parties for the “years of penitence” that lie ahead because of Venezuela’s debt. He made no cheap shots at the banks.
6. On foreign affairs, Lusinchi was equally moderate. On Central America, he named no names, but made clear that Venezuela would continue to support democracy. Indeed, in what could be construed as an indirect warning to the Nicaraguans, he stressed that support for democracy elsewhere is a principle enshrined in the preamble to Venezuela’s Constitution.
7. I will be seeing Lusinchi for breakfast February 3.
8. The highlight of my private meetings was breakfast with the Foreign Ministers of Costa Rica, El Salvador, Honduras, and Guatemala. Though we had word of some personal and tactical tensions among them, we readily reached agreement on the need for close coordination to keep pressure on Nicaragua in the Contadora process. And although the civilians are generally not up on the military side, they seemed aware of the special importance of cooperation against the Salvadoran guerrillas before the elections. The two areas we need to work on are first, once again, to get the resources to do the job, both economically and militarily, and [garble] convince the Guatemalans that we are serious and that they will only hurt themselves if they stay aloof from the common cause.
9. Nicaragua’s Ortega was in town telling everyone who would listen that we were the source of all evil and that Kissinger had secretly recommended an invasion of both El Salvador and Nicaragua. That line is wearing a bit thin even down here. I took advantage of my press conference 3 to point out that Ortega’s imagination was playing tricks on him, but that he had every reason to be worried after the way the Sandinistas had betrayed Nicaragua.
10. My bilaterals with the Presidents of Bolivia and Argentina were less productive. Bolivia’s Siles stressed his country’s economic problems, which are [garble] and not all his fault, and said he would do better in the future. I said we understood and supported his role in returning Bolivia to democracy, but were concerned by the drug trafficking and the growing influence of the left. We will see.
11. Argentina’s Alfonsin was one of the media heroes here and obviously a man to watch. But my meeting with him was cut short by Caracas traffic and ceremonies. The Falklands issue remains high on the Argentina agenda, as does the debt issue, and Alfonsin seems to be looking for political hay on both. Alfonsin insisted that a NATO military base on the Falklands would lead to a Soviet response. I assured him that we are not considering turning the Falklands into a NATO base. Time ran out before we could get further. Alfonsin remembered very warmly the Vice President’s visit for his inauguration. 4
Shultz

475. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the White House and the Department of State 475. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the White House and the Department of State 1
Rio de Janeiro, February 4, 1984, 2045Z
Secto 2035. Subject: Memorandum for the President: Meeting with President Lusinchi, Feb 3.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. To: The President.
From: George P. Shultz.
3. President Lusinchi was visibly moved by your letter of congratulations and accepted in principle our invitation to make a State visit May 15. 2
4. Lusinchi is a veteran politician with good instincts. But he faces massive problems and is not fully informed on their wider implications. We need to move quickly to consolidate our ties, and I am arranging an interagency follow-on to brief him on Central America. 3
5. Three themes dominated my private meeting with him. They were Venezuela’s economic difficulties, Central America, and how to strengthen our cooperation on both.
6. Lusinchi wants to play a role on Central America. He is convinced that Cuba is an enemy of Venezuela quite as much as of the United States, and wants to be supportive on Grenada. But his information on Nicaragua and El Salvador comes more from the political left than his own military and intelligence communities. We agreed that our Ambassador George Landau would coordinate a continuing exchange of information beginning with a visit to Caracas by a combined State-Defense intelligence team. Details for Cap’s, [ less than one line not declassified ] and Bud’s consideration will follow separately.
7. Lusinchi brought up Venezuela’s economic troubles persistently. He knows that he has a lot to do himself, but is eager for our help in debt rescheduling and with the banks. He also specifically mentioned EXIM, CCC credits, and the high cost of the F16 fleet they bought from us. I told him we wanted to be constructive, the bankers were aware of Venezuela’s problems but our influence over them was limited. I added that Venezuela could improve the climate by settling the problem of oil compensation. Lusinchi agreed and may take action on this long-lingering issue.
8. While we are not in a position to offer much concrete assistance to Venezuela, I offered to arrange consultations over the full range of Venezuela’s economic problems. I will talk to Don Regan and others about this when I return to Washington. Venezuela’s economic problems are so deep they will require very careful management to prevent social unrest and a negative impact on our relations. Both State and Treasury will have to sharpen their focus on these issues. At the same time, as in the case of Argentina, its resource base is such that sound management can bring stability and growth in a reasonable time.
Shultz
