476. Telegram From the Embassy in Venezuela to the Department of State 476. Telegram From the Embassy in Venezuela to the Department of State 1
Caracas, May 4, 1984, 2230Z
3921. Subject: President Reagan’s telecon with President Lusinchi of Venezuela of April 4, 1984. 2
1. (Confidential—Entire text.)
2. During President Reagan’s call to President Lusinchi, thanking him for Venezuela’s assistance with the Argentine debt package, President Lusinchi indicated that he had some thoughts on the overall Latin American debt crisis, and that he would convey them through me. Subsequently, President Lusinchi and I discussed this matter. Today he sent me a note on the subject through the Minister of Finance. A translation of the note is as follows:
3. Quote. The Government of Venezuela is very concerned about the increase in interest rates in the United States and the existence of expectations of further increases in the near future, which make extremely onerous the servicing of debt contracted with the international banks by Latin American countries. In some cases it may make it impossible to fulfill the obligations contracted. For the stability of the international financial system, and the recovery of the economy of these countries, it would be advisable to have preferential fixed interest rates established in agreements which are entered into for refinancing the debt. 3 End quote.
Landau
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840292–0585. Confidential; Immediate. Also sent Immediate to the White House and the Department of the Treasury.
2 See Document 64 .
3 In a May 9 memorandum to McFarlane, Burghardt and Menges noted: “Venezuela has $35 billion in outstanding debt. The public sector portion—$27 billion—is up to date, but the private sector portion is $800–900 million in arrears. The GOV has decided that it will not enter into an IMF agreement or Paris Club arrangement and, instead, will unilaterally reschedule the private debt—a very unusual approach. Treasury is particularly concerned about how some $200 million in ExIm Bank claims will be covered and has threatened negative actions. But there appears to be general agreement (even in Treasury) to avoid punitive measures as long as the GOV demonstrates its seriousness in dealing with the debt problem. The Lusinchi government is a marked improvement over its predecessor on this score.” (Reagan Library, Executive Secretariat, NSC Country File, Latin America/Central, Venezuela (02/03/1984–12/17/1984))

477. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) to President Reagan 477. Memorandum From the President’s Assistant for National Security Affairs (McFarlane) to President Reagan 1
Washington, December 1, 1984
Subject: Meeting with Venezuelan President Lusinchi.
I. PURPOSE
To reaffirm the friendly bilateral relationship based on shared democratic values. To review the situation in Central America, clearly explain our policy there and seek constructive cooperation from Venezuela in obtaining a Contadora treaty which assures the implementation of democracy in Nicaragua and preserves our ability to help El Salvador.
II. BACKGROUND
Venezuela made the transition to democratic government in 1958 and then faced ten years of Cuban-supported subversion and guerrilla warfare (1959–69). President Lusinchi is a member of the social democratic party ( Accion Democratica ) which has alternated in power with the more conservative Christian democrats. During the last decade Venezuela used its oil wealth and democratic institutions to provide leadership in the Caribbean and Central America. President Lusinchi has an especially close relationship with the social democratic leaders of Costa Rica, the Dominican Republic and Argentina. He understands the need to defeat Cuban-supported subversion and terrorism and among the Contadora nations (Colombia, Panama, Mexico) it is Venezuela which has given the strongest support to Duarte in El Salvador and shared our views about the need for a good Contadora treaty. Lusinchi distrusts the Sandinistas, is skeptical of their elections and refuses their request for economic assistance. We should use this state visit to build on these positive attitudes in order to obtain more active Venezuelan cooperation in Central America. President Lusinchi needs to be encouraged to take the lead away from Mexico among the four Contadora countries and bring about a new Contadora treaty draft which meets the legitimate security needs of Central America by: (1) including the provision that the security portions of the treaty enter into force simultaneously with the finding by the OAS that Nicaragua has implemented its 1979 commitment to hold genuinely democratic elections; (2) provides for an effective verification structure which is governed by a majority vote of the five Central American countries; and, (3) preserves our ability to help El Salvador.
We view the Venezuelan “political solution” of the 1960s with their communist guerrillas as a model for Central America since it combined military attrition of the guerrillas, with offers of amnesty and future participation in elections for those guerrillas who laid down their weapons and ended subversive cooperation with Cuba. There is progress along these lines in El Salvador but the communist threat will continue until Nicaragua implements its 1979 democratic commitments to the OAS and that is why Venezuela needs to take the lead away from Mexico in the Contadora process. You can assure President Lusinchi that our Central America policy continues to have the four mutually reinforcing elements of support for democracy, economic improvement, active diplomacy and security assistance to threatened governments and that the US opposes the violent right as well as the violent left.
Venezuela welcomed the rescue of Grenada and is contributing to the democratic process there. President Lusinchi will be concerned by our recent intelligence that Cuba and the Soviet Union are encouraging what is likely to be a pro-Soviet coalition government in its neighbor Guyana, which has been the object of a significant Soviet-bloc Cuban penetration effort for some years. Venezuela is also concerned about the consequences of the far left penetration of the Bolivian government but at the same time opposes a military coup there. We should suggest increased cooperation in the Caribbean and countries which border Venezuela in order to simultaneously accomplish two objectives which we share: (1) the strengthening of genuinely democratic institutions through timely action and assistance; and, (2) preventing and countering Soviet-bloc Cuban and Nicaraguan subversion.
In the economic area, oil-rich Venezuela entered into a period of crisis when the price of oil dropped. President Lusinchi has given the economy priority and has succeeded in rescheduling his country’s public sector debt on favorable terms without an IMF program. His strategy is based on reducing government spending, increasing employment and business confidence. Venezuela is not seeking economic assistance from the US. One issue of concern to us is Venezuela’s tardiness in dealing with private sector debt, which affects our Export-Import Bank. We also would like Lusinchi to seek prompt resolution of an old irritant in our relations—compensation claims by US companies dating from the 1976 nationalization of the oil industry. President Lusinchi has a strong interest in combating the illegal narcotics market and has cooperated on this with us and other countries.
[Omitted here are a list of participants, a press plan, and a schedule]
1 Source: Reagan Library, NSC Records, Latin American Affairs Directorate, Miscellaneous Issues File, President Lusinchi/Venezuela. Secret. Prepared by Menges. Copies were sent to Bush, Meese, James Baker, and Deaver. An unknown hand wrote at the top of the page: “President has seen.” Tab A, a memorandum from Shultz, was not found attached. Tab B, Talking Points, are attached but not printed. Tab C, a biography of Lusinchi, was not found attached.

478. Memorandum of Conversation 478. Memorandum of Conversation 1
Washington, December 4, 1984, 10:50–11:50 a.m.
SUBJECT
Meeting with President Jaime Lusinchi of the Republic of Venezuela (U)
PARTICIPANTS
The President
The Vice President
Secretary of State George P. Shultz
Robert C. McFarlane, Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs
Langhorne A. Motley, Assistant Secretary for Inter-American Affairs
George Landau, U.S. Ambassador to Venezuela
Harry W. Shlaudeman, Ambassador-at-Large and President’s Special Envoy for Central America
Constantine Menges, National Security Council
Roger Robinson, National Security Council
President Jaime Lusinchi
Foreign Minister Isidro Morales Paul
Ambassador Valentin Hernandez
Minister of the Presidency Simon Alberto Consalvi
Presidents Reagan and Lusinchi met briefly in the Oval Office and then joined the group in the Cabinet Room at 10:50 a.m. (U)
President Reagan welcomed the President of Venezuela and made a joke about finding talking points in his coat pocket for a previous royal visitor. 2 (U)
President Lusinchi expressed his appreciation for the generous and warm welcome. (U)
President Reagan said that the United States is honored to have President Lusinchi as a visitor. Among the issues for discussion a very important one is Central America, where U.S. policy continues with four mutually reinforcing elements: support for democracy; economic help to improve living conditions; security assistance; and, active diplomacy. The United States would feel much more comfortable if Venezuela would take the leading position among the four Contadora countries in an effort to define a workable treaty. (C)
President Lusinchi said Venezuela is willing to do more but must be careful and prudent. Last week there were discussions with Ambassadors Landau and Shlaudeman about the Contadora process, and it seems that in the last few days there are new international possibilities for Central America in the attitude of the Contadora countries and the United States. I agree with what you said in your earlier welcoming remarks 3 that the elections in Nicaragua were not true elections, but it is a political fact which strengthens in some way the Nicaraguan government and gives it—in the view of some governments—international legitimacy which it did not have before. There should not be a communist regime in Nicaragua; and there can be an opening of the regime because, unlike Cuba, it is not an island or the producer of one crop. I believe the Nicaraguan leaders are communist and want to create a communist state, but there are more possibilities in Nicaragua. I believe the Nicaraguan leaders will not be able to impose communism and that contradictions will appear. At the same time, there cannot be a military solution, since that would have negative side effects. In the discussions with Ambassadors Landau and Shlaudeman, we talked about the creation of the constituent assembly in Nicaragua as providing some way for the democratic opposition to participate in drafting a new constitution. If there had been fair democratic elections in Nicaragua, I think the Sandinistas would have lost and there certainly are opportunities now for political possibilities to open up the Sandinista regime. What is your view? (C)
President Reagan said I believe we have a situation in Nicaragua like that in Eastern Europe. The government is Marxist-Leninist, it does not care what the people think, and it can hold onto power. If the people were free to speak they would want the government that was promised by the original revolution—a genuinely democratic government. But, just like Castro did in Cuba to the democratic groups which believed him and united to overthrow a rightist dictator, the Sandinistas have betrayed the democratic Nicaraguans who joined with them against Somoza. I do not think that any of us who believe in democracy can allow the Sandinistas to consolidate themselves and continue as a center for the subversion of other countries. That is why pressure has to be kept on Nicaragua. This is just what you did in Venezuela—you kept the pressure on the communist guerrillas while providing amnesty for those willing to lay down their weapons and participate in their democratic elections. (C)
President Lusinchi said the most difficult international problem is the Nicaraguan export of subversion and alliance with the USSR and Cuba, and, in this respect, we must impose pressure on the Sandinistas. The Contadora draft Acta can be modified, the observations of the other Central American countries can be taken more into account. We agree fully with you that it is not permissible to tolerate the export of the Nicaraguan revolution to other countries nor can we allow Nicaragua to become the focus of military imbalance in the region. Nicaragua says it does not have offensive military equipment. They told us recently they would be willing to start international inspections. They say the Soviet ships did not carry supersonic or offensive weapons. A Nicaraguan official told us recently that they would permit Venezuela to have complete information for this trip to Washington so we could share this with you and the State Department, but Nicaragua has not sent this information to us. (C)
President Reagan said I can give you an example of how difficult it is to inspect what is coming into Nicaragua. Some time ago a Soviet-bloc ship was in the Panama Canal with a cargo which included military helicopters and we said so publicly. The Sandinistas denied this and said they would invite the international press to inspect the ship’s cargo when it docked. The only press they invited was the Cuban press, and we don’t put much faith in what they report. Concerning the recent ships we suspected might have MIG jet fighters, it did seem strange that this ship went all the way around South America instead of going through the Panama Canal. They know we can often tell what’s on the ship if it goes through the Panama Canal. Right now we still do not know for sure whether there were MIGs, yes or no? I doubt that Nicaragua would give any of us a real look at the weapons they have. If all of us stay firm and indicate what we want is a restoration of the democratic institutions that the revolution was designed to achieve, then, the other things would follow and work out well. (C)
President Lusinchi said yes the Sandinistas made those original promises of democracy and a mixed economy. We have complained publicly in a telegram to Nicaragua that they are not carrying out those democratic promises. We are certainly willing to continue making efforts to compel Nicaragua to fulfill its commitments. In the Contadora treaty and outside we identify ourselves with your views; there is no disagreement between us. (C)
President Reagan said if we keep pressing for democracy then things will work out in Nicaragua. The first two drafts of the Contadora treaty did not meet the Contadora 21 objectives. Those drafts left some big gaps which Nicaragua could use to accomplish its purposes. A former Sandinista official said in a newspaper article a few days ago that Nicaragua is actually an occupied country with Cuba and the Soviet bloc being the occupation forces. That person pointed out that if you take the 9,000 Cuban and Soviet-bloc personnel and the 250,000-man army which the Sandinistas have said they want to build in Nicaragua and translate that in proportion to the U.S. population, it would be the same as though in the U.S. there were 300,000 Cuban military and security advisors, 300,000 Cuban technical advisors, 300,000 Soviet-bloc personnel, and as though we had an army of 25 million. (C)
President Lusinchi said you are right. We have a full coincidence of views. If we use most of the suggestions made by the Central American countries, we could force the Contadora draft to be acceptable. I am optimistic that we can propose a modified Contadora treaty that will provide for democratization and the other goals. (C)
President Reagan said, in our view if Venezuela, rather than some other country, took a leading role in producing the draft Contadora treaty, we would feel better about it. (C)
President Lusinchi said yes, but we must be discreet. We do not want to go against the sensibilities of any other country. We do want to arrive at a better Contadora treaty, and, you can be certain that this will be our behavior. If we combine this with the Manzanillo talks, which are well-led by Ambassador Shlaudeman, then there may be a better chance for a solution. Another situation of interest in Latin America is the Southern Cone where the signature of the treaty between Argentina and Chile will definitely help the democracy in Argentina. 4 And, we have to take into account that in Chile there has been a strengthening of opposition to the democratic transition. I think that U.S. action with discretion would help the democratic transition to occur in Chile. There is an opportunity for this democratic transition based on the Christian Democratic Party and certain parts of the Socialist Party, not including those elements which are cooperating with the communists. A dramatic situation also exists in Bolivia where a number of political groups are cornering President Siles with their irrational behavior. The President himself is acting somewhat irrationally, and I have information that he is thinking of committing suicide. The government is opposed by communist groups and communist trade unions. Elements of the military are pulling in different directions, and the economic situation is the worst ever. Perhaps we can find a way to send additional food supplies to Bolivia because the economic situation is dramatically serious. Some military personnel are trying to overthrow Siles, but what will come after? Some of the armed forces are penetrated by the narcotics traffickers, and they would use the power of government to increase the narcotics problem. (S)
On drug trafficking, we have prepared some information items for you. The situation in Venezuela is more serious than many people think, and we are concerned not only about Venezuela but also about Latin America as a whole. There is an increasing connection between drugs and subversion and terrorism as we are seeing in Bolivia, Ecuador, Colombia and Peru. Vice President Bush showed (in Quito, Ecuador) documents and photos illustrating the relationship between drugs and Nicaragua and Cuba. 5 Perhaps we should take the Cuban government into the international court because of its connection with the illegal drug traffic. This is all part of the internationalization of subversion. For us it is a matter of legitimate self defense, and we would like you to help us with all the knowledge you have about methods to fight the drug traffic. The chief of the Venezuelan Navy recently went to Puerto Rico for naval maneuvers and told me there are useful opportunities for cooperating with the United States Navy. There is a U.S. program, IMET (International Military Education and Training), through which you give support to military students from Latin America, but we have been excluded along with Mexico, due to a lack of U.S. funds. Before, there was a budget of $1 million for 153 Venezuelan students. Now, we are able to send only 20 students on a budget of $150,000. Colombia received $750,000, and we would like to train more of our military through this program and train our pilots for the F–16 fighter aircraft. (C)
Turning to foreign trade, you have been handling the foreign trade laws of the U.S. well, but we think it would be helpful if you could adapt several aspects of this law to the Venezuelan situation. Also, we ask the United States to support Venezuela in obtaining financing from the Inter-American Bank, the World Bank and commodity credit. We have important priority needs which we have explained to those organizations, and we would welcome your support. We want to exchange views with you and the State Department on the international debt problem. We have successfully refinanced our debt, and urge that the United States take the lead to readjust interest rates, which, although they have gone down recently, might go up again in the future. (C)
Yesterday I received your letter on EXXON and the refinery in Aruba. 6 You are correct in your approach, and I would like to see if we could find a way to contribute to economic development in those islands since they are right off our coast. Due to lack of time, I won’t discuss specific solutions but, together, we should be able to find solutions. I will be leaving an Aide Memoire with you which will give you details. 7 (C)
President Reagan noted that the U.S. is very eager to cooperate with Venezuela on narcotics issues. On Chile, I shall leave the discussion to you and Secretary Shultz. About the EXXON refineries, I agree that solutions must be found. On interest rates, we’re doing everything possible so that they do not go up again. (C)
Secretary Shultz said that he would have to leave soon in order to be at the State Department to welcome President Lusinchi for lunch. (U)
President Lusinchi said, perhaps I talk too much, but Latin Americans like to talk and Spanish does not lend itself to synthesis as does English. We are totally willing to be of assistance in having harmonious, friendly, and cooperative relations. We are admirers of your country and of you, personally, and we believe that the second Reagan administration will be fruitful for the United States and its friends. (U)
President Reagan commented that there are no problems which cannot be solved if we talk to each other rather than talking about each other. (U)
President Lusinchi said, at the risk of embarrassing the individual, I want to tell you how pleased we are to have Ambassador Landau as your Ambassador. He is doing an excellent job and knows our country well, and we hope you keep him in Venezuela for the entire second term. (U)

I slipped into my topcoat. When time came for my opening remarks I took my notes out of my pocket & horror of horrors it was a speech welcoming ‘His Royal Highness’ the Duke of Luxemburg. I couldn’t imagine how they could have given me the wrong speech. I stared to ad lib & then remembered I’d worn my top coat last on the day of the Grand Duke’s arrival. Today’s speech was in my suit coat pocket. I made a switch & got away with it pretty well.” (Brinkley, ed., The Reagan Diaries , volume I, January 1981–October 1985, p. 403)

479. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassies in Venezuela, Bolivia, France, Spain, Chile, the United Kingdom, and the Federal Republic of Germany 479. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassies in Venezuela, Bolivia, France, Spain, Chile, the United Kingdom, and the Federal Republic of Germany 1
Washington, December 6, 1984, 2322Z
360395. Subject: Meeting between Secretary and President Lusinchi, December 4, 1984: Chile, Bolivia and Bilateral issues.
1. (C—Entire text).
2. Summary. In addition to Central America and the Caribbean (Guyana, Aruba), Secretary and President Lusinchi discussed a number of bilateral issues as well as Chile and Bolivia. 2 Secretary shared Venezuela’s concern over developments in both these countries. The Secretary had strong praise for Venezuela’s progress on public debt negotiations, urging resolution of the private debt question as well as an improvement in the investment by settlement of the oil nationalization claims. Lusinchi put in a plug to have a Venezuelan replace Ortiz Mena as President of the Inter-American Development Bank.
3. Secretary Shultz called on President Lusinchi during latter’s December 3–5 State visit. Foreign Minister Morales Paul, Minister of the Presidency Consalvi, Finance Minister Azpurua and Ambassador to U.S. Hernandez were with Lusinchi. Ambassador Shlaudeman, Assistant Secretary Motley, Ambassador Landau and ARA/AND Director Rondon accompanied Secretary.
4. Chile.
The Secretary told President Lusinchi that we shared the concerns the Venezuelan President expressed over Chile and Bolivia earlier in the day to President Reagan. 3 In Chile, the Secretary stated that our policy has three objectives: to support a return to democracy, to let Pinochet know privately and publicly that we oppose the recent wave of repression and to encourage the moderate opposition to stay away from the communists. If the moderates align themselves with the extreme left, they will lose the support of moderate elements elsewhere in the world, the Secretary commented. In addition, the Chilean moderates would give Pinochet a handle with which to beat them. The Secretary added that we do not want to see the communists supplant Pinochet. We do want to give Chilean centrists a chance to express their views.
5. Secretary Shultz expressed concern over French and Spanish efforts with the extreme left in Chile.
6. President Lusinchi opined that Chilean Socialists such as Altamirano and Almeyda have little weight. He thought that the centrists are getting stronger in Chile. The Secretary urged that the United States and Venezuela stay in close touch on Chile.
7. Bolivia.
We have provided lots of support for Bolivia, the Secretary stated, but as things stand now the Siles government has to take measures we are able to support. Assistant Secretary Motley noted that American help to Bolivia has run at the rate of about dols 100 million a year. He stressed that the economy is weak in Bolivia and failure to adopt effective economic measures is exacerbating the political situation.
8. Narcotics.
The Secretary welcomed narcotics cooperation between the United States and Venezuela. He pointed to the interest of both the President and Mrs. Reagan in narcotics control. He asked to be kept informed if the United States is not forthcoming in the field of narcotics.
9. IMET.
Secretary Shultz referred to President Lusinchi’s request to President Reagan for a greater American contribution to the IMET program between the United States and Venezuela. The Secretary said we want to be helpful and will double our program this year, from dols 50,000 to dols 100,000.
10. Venezuelan steel.
The Secretary expressed surprise that Venezuela is concerned over its steel exports to the United States. He commented that Venezuela is a relatively small exporter and its sales have not drawn great attention in the United States, where we are more concerned over steel sales by large producers. Secretary Shultz cautioned, however, that there can be no guarantee that there might not be concern in the future. Lusinchi explained that his government is simply anticipating possible problems.
11. Debt Negotiations.
You must have a genius for a Finance Minister, if you can resolve your debt problems without recourse to the IMF, the Secretary commented. He expressed the hope that Venezuela would be able also to clear up the question of private indebtedness. Lusinchi thanked Shultz for his words of encouragement and the goodwill of the United States.
12. Investment climate: Oil nationalization claims.
The Secretary observed that it is fundamental to get people to invest in Venezuela. In this connection, the Secretary urged resolution of the oil compensation issue. He recalled that he raised this matter with President Lusinchi in Caracas at their breakfast in February. 4 He felt that a settlement would not be very costly for Venezuela and would impact very favorably on investor attitudes. He urged President Lusinchi to work with Ambassador Landau to find a solution.
13. Inter-American Development Bank.
President Lusinchi also put in a plug to have a Venezuela citizen replace Antonio Ortiz Mena as President of the Inter-American Development Bank. The Secretary noted that Ortiz Mena has been retiring for a long time and said the United States would look at a Venezuelan candidate with interest.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840782–0523. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Drafted by Rondon; cleared by Kilday and McKinley and S/S–O, and ARA/SC in draft; approved by Shultz.
2 In telegram 359904 to multiple diplomatic and consular posts, December 6, the Department reported on the Caribbean issues discussed by Shultz and Lusinchi in their December 4 meeting. (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D840782–0242) A memorandum of conversation for Shultz’s December 4, 4:30 p.m. meeting with Lusinchi on Central America in the Presidential Suite of the Madison Hotel is in the Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S Records, 1984 Nodis and Exdis Memorandums, Lot 94D91, Nodis Memorandums, December 1–31, 1984.

480. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 480. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 1
New York, October 2, 1985, 0750Z
Secto 19061. Subject: Secretary’s Bilateral with President Lusinchi October 1.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary. In a lengthy conversation with Secretary Shultz October 1, Venezuelan President Lusinchi gave assurances that his government is not deceived by the Nicaraguans but has rather consistently warned GON leaders against interfering in their neighbors’ affairs. 2 The Secretary reviewed US views on a final Contadora agreement and pointed out the Core Four’s objections to Contadora’s recent report to the UNSYG. The Secretary gave Lusinchi advance notice of the possible meeting with Ortega and the upcoming meeting with the Soviets. Discussion of the current COM vacancy in Caracas is reported septel. 3 End of summary.
3. Secretary met morning October 1 with Venezuelan President Lusinchi in his suite at the St. Regis Hotel, New York. FonMin Consalvi and Ambassador to the US Valentin Hernandez were most prominent among the many Venezuelans present. Others on US side were A/Sec Elliott Abrams, Spokesman Bernard Kalb, Stephanie von Reigersberg (interpreter) and ARA/AND Phil McLean (notetaker).
4. Lusinchi began with personal compliments for the Secretary’s skills. He then delivered a lengthy exposition of Venezuelan views on Central America. He said the Venezuelan position is to promote dialogue and achieve democratic solutions in each of the Central American States. Pursuing that peace has been the GOV’s main activity at this UNGA. In a region easily influenced by outside forces, the Nicaraguans are acting as Soviet pawns. The kidnapping of President Duarte’s daughter is an idiocy which postpones any possibility of making progress on peace in El Salvador and may lead the guerrillas into a final confrontation. Until intelligence confirmed the leftists’ role, the GOV suspected it was a rightist provocation. The GOV is contacting UNGO and others to see what it can do. The situation in Costa Rica is also dangerous. At least in Guatemala the crisis is easing. Mejia is acting reasonably and, he noted with pleasure, the US is encouraging progress. He cautioned the US not to overemphasize the region’s “negative factors”, apparently a reference to the Contras who he claimed are ineffective because of their ties to the Somozistas.
5. The Secretary began his reply by noting our appreciation of Venezuela’s candid and realistic appraisals. The US supports Contadora and wants to see it come to a positive conclusion, but because of the track record of communist governments the agreement must include simultaneity, verification and a schedule of provisions for national reconciliation. Without these elements, any agreement would not bind the Nicaraguans at all. On the other hand, if the US undertakes to stop aid, it will stop aid. The decrease in US influence would leave the communists in the driver’s seat. The Secretary said he had just talked to the four Central American Foreign Ministers who say they will challenge an inaccurate and biased report they say the Contadora countries are going to submit to the UNSYG.
6. FonMin replied that none of the countries have indicated displeasure to the GOV, though there had been meetings with two of them just that day. The report, already submitted to the UNSYG, is a simple recounting of the Contadora process up to this point without any evaluation or intention to favor any side. It does outline the three issues which remain to be resolved, i.e. armaments, follow-up on security systems and reconciliation. Lusinchi interjected to assure the Secretary that the GOV consistently tells the Nicaraguans there is no role from them in solving their neighbors’ problems, either by invoking “revolutionary solidarity” with the Salvadorean rebels or supporting “autonomous groups” in Costa Rica.
7. The Secretary concluded by raising three topics briefly. First, the GOV may hear that Amb. Schlaudeman will be meeting with Ortega. The purpose will be to say the Manzanillo talks can only be re-started if a national dialogue under church sponsorship is underway. Second, later this month A/Sec Abrams will be meeting with the Soviets to discuss Central America and the Caribbean. The purpose is to tell the Soviets that their activities in the region are unwelcome and unwarranted. The GOV should know the US will make no deals with the Soviets. Third, Puerto Rico is not a colony. The only difference among the Puerto Rican people is whether they should seek statehood or remain with their present status. This subject has no place in the UN as a decolonization subject. The Venezuelans gave no reaction to these last points.
8. At the conclusion of the discussion the Secretary raised the current COM vacancy in Caracas. That discussion is reported septel.
9. Department please pass to Caracas, San Salvador, Managua, Guatemala, San Jose and Tegucigalpa.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, [no n number]. Secret; Immediate; Nodis.
2 In an October 1 memorandum to Reagan, Shultz wrote: “Venezuelan President Lusinchi assured me today that his government is not deceived by the Sandinistas and will not tolerate their interference in Costa Rican or Salvadoran affairs. The Venezuelans are doing what they can with their Salvadoran guerrilla contacts to encourage the release of Duarte’s daughter. Lusinchi heard me out as I conveyed your support for Otto Reich’s nomination to be our next Ambassador in Caracas. He told us of his predecessor’s bad experience with President Kennedy’s Ambassador, Teodoro Moscoso, a person who seemed to have badly rankled the Venezuelans. In the end, Lusinchi said the AFL-CIO had now recommended Otto’s qualifications, and in view of our traditional friendship, his government will agree to the nomination when his name is submitted.” (Reagan Library, Executive Secretariat, NSC Records, System File, System II, Case 91018)

481. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Embassy in Venezuela 481. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State and the Embassy in Venezuela 1
Palo Alto, California August 8, 1986, 1750Z
Secto 16015. Subject: Secretary Shultz’s Meeting with Venezuelan President Lusinchi, August 7, Bogota - memorandum of conversation.
1. C—Entire text.
2. Summary. Secretary Shultz and Venezuelan President Lusinchi met the morning of August 7, prior to the Barco inauguration, for an hour long discussion of Colombia, Central America, Haiti, Puerto Rico, and Jamaica. On Colombia, both expressed the need to fortify Colombia’s efforts in the face of the serious narco-terrorist threat to its democratic institutions. They agreed that President Barco is in a good position to play a positive role for Colombia’s future. On Haiti, at the Secretary’s request, Lusinchi agreed to announce some economic assistance (petroleum and food) next week as a means of encouraging movement toward democracy, but was reluctant to do all that Venezuela is eventually prepared to do until a democratic transition is clearer and more advanced. He also urged multilateral cooperative efforts to educate Haitians on developing democratic institutions. On Central America, the Secretary stressed Nicaragua’s increased isolation and, while Lusinchi agreed that pressure must be kept on them, he urged acceptance of a political solution. He also characterized the Sandinistas as “stupid” and “immature,” and agreed on the need to bolster the four Central American democracies. On Puerto Rico, the Secretary strongly opposed any UN resolutions, but Lusinchi and Consalvi gave no promises. Lusinchi also pressed for greater U.S. contacts with Michael Manley in view of Lusinchi’s expectation that Manley stands in good chance of returning to power and the consequent need to work together to moderate his behavior. Lusinchi was accompanied by FM Consalvi, Finance Minister Azpurva and the GOV Ambassador to Colombia. With the Secretary were A/S Abrams, A/S Kalb, Ambassador Gillespie, NSC staffer Tillman and DAS Gelbard (notetaker). End summary.
3. Colombia. The Secretary and President Lusinchi began by expressing their mutual pleasure at being able to attend the inauguration of yet another democratically elected president in Latin America, especially in a country with such a long democratic tradition. Lusinchi noted that in spite of Colombia’s serious narco-terrorism and the tragedies involving the Palace of Justice and the Ruiz volcano, there was a significantly higher than normal level of participation in this year’s Colombian elections. This would greatly fortify Colombia institutionally, a fact very important to Venezuela because of their close bilateral ties. Virgilio Barco, according to Lusinchi, is a great believer in democracy and can be trusted. He described Barco as steady and as one who “does not change with the wind”; this was particularly important for Colombia at this time. Lusinchi stressed that we all needed to help defend Colombia. Any collapse would be destructive “in all senses” to all of Latin America. Geopolitically Lusinchi felt it was the region’s most important country, even more so than Bolivia. As a result, he said, Castro was undoubtedly making significant plans to undermine Colombia. Lusinchi described the internal subversion as serious, with the terrorist-drug link even more dangerous. However, he felt that the Colombian military had now resolved to fight this. The linkage between the terrorists and drug traffickers had caused this to happen; their alliance had thus been a serious mistake and would lead to the destruction of both. The Secretary expressed total agreement with Lusinchi’s analysis and said that U.S. was fully prepared to cooperate and collaborate with Barco’s government. 2
4. Haiti. Secretary Shultz expressed the view that Haiti was very important to both the U.S. and Venezuela, with the mutual hope that something positive would emerge. The U.S. had been providing an increased flow of aid and encouragement to Namphy, who has been cleaning house politically, has announced a date for elections, is getting out with the people and, overall, is heading in the right direction. However enormous problems exist and he, (the Secretary) plans to visit Haiti next week to express U.S. support. He noted that the U.S. and GOV have the most potential influence and effect on the GOH’s behavior; some eastern Caribbean countries also have influence, but they do not have the resources to affect Haiti’s behavior. He asked about the GOV’s intentions regarding aid, so he would know in anticipation of his trip. 3
5. Lusinchi stated the GOV’s clear intention to help Haiti for both moral and political reasons. But Venezuela wants to condition its aid on movement to democracy as rapidly as possible. In this, we needed to block Castro, who already has a plan to send his people into Haiti. Lusinchi said that we made mistakes with Nicaragua and Cuba and cannot afford to make an error here. Therefore, we needed to be certain of a transition to democracy. Secretary Shultz agreed wholeheartedly, saying this was why economic assistance had to be presented in the context of Namphy moving towards democracy: The more he does, the more forthcoming donors would be. Thus, we did not need to provide all aid now, but enough to provide incentives.
6. Lusinchi agreed that he had confidence in Namphy but not in all members of the GOH. The GOV was encouraging the Haitian exiles they knew and also the church to be helpful. Haitians were all, he said, a bit crazy, so the transition had to be done carefully. Lusinchi acknowledged the GOV could help by giving petroleum and surplus agricultural products. In response to the Secretary’s request that some be announced next week so that the Secretary could supplement it with a U.S. aid announcement, Lusinchi agreed to announce such GOV aid. Lusinchi also offered to invite young Haitians to Venezuela for military training and other programs. He proposed that in conjunction with the Dominican Republic and other countries, meetings could be held with moderate Haitians (such as Manigot) to orient them towards accomplishing a peaceful transition to democracy.
7. Central America. Secretary Shultz noted that everyone is increasingly aware that Nicaragua is the Central American problem. The Sandinistas are increasingly isolated and we all need to help the four Central American democracies. He expressed his understanding of the GOV’s reluctance to help the Nicaraguan opposition, but said it was necessary. As the USG considered the negotiating process, we have tried to listen to and report the views of the four impressive, recently elected Central American presidents. Lusinchi replied that he continued to believe Central America could only be resolved politically. The situation was deteriorating rapidly and the Sandinistas were behaving stupidly. He cited as an example their “absurd” fight against the Catholic Church and the Protestants, which was a real mistake since both have enormous popular support. Other blunders, such as closing “La Prensa” and Ortega’s travels to Havana and Moscow, showed they always did the wrong thing and are very immature, even to the point of using outmoded communist rhetoric. When the Secretary stressed the need to keep the pressure on, Lusinchi agreed but urged avoiding the “big stick” interventionist approach. Lusinchi concluded by saying that his relations with Nicaragua were not good, but the GOV was helping the four democracies.
8. Puerto Rico. The Secretary emphatically stated that the UN had no business getting involved in Puerto Rico; it is gross interference in U.S. internal affairs. Countries were shifting their views, but every year there was a UN resolution on Puerto Rico and we do not like it. Lusinchi replied that Venezuela had a consistent policy of support for self-determination and acceptance of Puerto Rico’s commonwealth status. Consalvi added that the issue would probably disappear after this year with the expected demise of the De-Colonization Committee. However, it was hard for the GOV to change its traditional position while the committee still existed. They tried to be fair, he added and consulted the Puerto Rican Government first.
9. Jamaica. Lusinchi said he was worried that Seaga is losing public support because of the economic situation and it is extremely difficult to change the Jamaican reality. Michael Manley is thus in a good position to move back into power and Lusinchi urged that Venezuela and the U.S. should improve relations with Manley now so he does not consider all of us enemies. He had no formula to propose, but we need to avoid rejecting him outright; he was not rejecting Seaga now either, but felt we had to be careful. Abrams noted that he would be in Jamaica in two weeks and would meet with Seaga and open a dialogue with Manley. The Secretary stressed that the main issue is to improve the economy; they had had bad luck with bauxite, but have not faced up to their other problems.
10. Ambassador Reich. In closing, Lusinchi expressed great pleasure with the new U.S. Ambassador. He said that Reich was doing all the right things, consulting closely with him and his government on a wide range of issues, getting to know many people and travelling extensively. He felt Mrs. Reich was also making an excellent contribution to the U.S. presence and efforts in Venezuela. The Secretary agreed that the Ambassador was outstanding and said he was pleased to get this report.
Shultz
1 Source: Reagan Library, George P. Shultz Papers, Box 23A, Memcons 1985–1986. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. Sent for information to Port au Prince, Bogota, Kingston, and the Contadora Collective. Shultz visited Bogota, August 6–7, to attend Barco’s inauguration. See Document 349 .
2 In telegram 9488 from Caracas, October 1, the Embassy reported on Crowe’s visit to Caracas and his September 29 meetings with Venezuelan military leadership and Lusinchi. The Embassy noted: “Both political and military leaders are concerned about instability in the region, are focusing on the threat of narcoterrorism, and are anxious for greater U.S.-GOV military cooperation.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D860748–0073)
3 Shultz visited Port-au-Prince on August 15 to meet with Haitian President Henri Namphy.

482. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 482. Intelligence Assessment Prepared in the Central Intelligence Agency 1
ALA 86–10051 Washington, December 1986
[Omitted here is a title page.]
Venezuela: Struggling With Declining Oil Revenues
Key Judgements
The unexpected depth of the oil market collapse in 1986 has set back President Jaime Lusinchi’s efforts to stabilize the Venezuelan economy. In response to the steep decline in oil revenues, Lusinchi has urgently introduced new adjustment measures aimed at checking the economy’s slide and bolstering the nation’s debt service capacity. The President’s efforts, however, are unlikely to provide more than temporary relief to an economy suffering from overdependence on oil revenues and an excess of government intervention. In our view, the Venezuelan economy is likely to remain in recession at least through the 1988 presidential election—a situation that could result in a defeat for President Lusinchi’s conservative, pro-US Ortodoxo faction of the ruling Democratic Action party. According to US Embassy and press reporting, Lusinchi is already under attack from his political opponents for his management of the economy. Continued recession is also likely to negatively affect US interests by depressing demand for US products and by limiting Venezuela’s capacity to provide economic assistance in the Caribbean Basin.
We expect Lusinchi to succeed in protecting the nation’s capacity to meet its servicing obligations on the external public debt. International bankers are likely to acquiesce in Venezuela’s request that most of the principal due through 1990 be rescheduled—providing Venezuela insurance against the risk that oil prices might descend again to $10 per barrel. To conserve foreign exchange, Lusinchi has imposed new restrictions on imports and has devalued the bolivar. These measures, and Venezuela’s relatively large foreign exchange reserves, should allow Caracas to meet its external payments obligations over the next three years, even if oil revenues remain depressed.
We believe, however, that success will continue to elude Lusinchi in his efforts to extricate the economy from a recession that has persisted for more than eight years. The economy’s problems extend far beyond a temporary inadequacy of foreign exchange. According to the US Embassy, the principal impediments to growth are deficient institutions and policies that over several decades have resulted in a pervasive presence of the state in the economy—a presence that Lusinchi has not aggressively attacked. His failure to liberalize the economy, his abrupt policy reversals on such issues as the external private debt, and the uncertainty over oil revenues rule out, in our view, a significant revival of private investment in the medium term. Because the oil revenue shortfall also constrains government and consumer spending, we see no plausible engine to pull the economy out of its doldrums. A growing danger is the possibility that Lusinchi, out of frustration, will try to spend Venezuela out of recession. We concur in the IMF judgment that such a strategy would provide only a transitory stimulus to the economy—while risking a sharp increase in inflation that would handicap future efforts to generate self-sustained economic growth.
The moribund economy has become the principal issue in domestic politics and may play a determining role in presidential succession. With campaigning for the 1988 election already under way, COPEI, the Christian democratic opposition, has made modest gains in public opinion polls by stridently assailing Lusinchi’s economic management. COPEI’s chances to seize the presidency will continue to improve as the economy deteriorates, but we believe COPEI will fall short in the 1988 election—unless Democratic Action, the ruling social democrats, splits over the selection of a candidate. Within Democratic Action, Lusinchi’s Ortodoxo faction is being challenged by maverick ex-President Carlos Andres Perez, who also hopes to capitalize on Lusinchi’s problems with the economy. Although Perez is fighting an uphill battle against the party machine, his chances are boosted by his immense following among party rank and file and by the Ortodoxos’ inability to settle upon a candidate to succeed the popular Lusinchi, who cannot seek a consecutive term in 1988. Perez’s bitter relations with the Ortodoxos suggests the possibility that—if his bid falls short—he might refuse to endorse the party’s nominee or might even accept a third-party nomination.
The economic crisis poses no threat to the stability of democratic government over the medium term, in our judgment. Public acceptance of Venezuela’s political institutions remains strong and the radical left is almost nonexistent. Labor peace is virtually assured by the intimate relationship between Democratic Action and the Confederation of Venezuelan Workers—the nation’s dominant labor organization. Over the longer term, however, the inability of the economy to generate sufficient jobs for the nation’s rapidly expanding labor force could become a destabilizing factor.
US interests will be adversely affected by Venezuela’s continuing foreign exchange crisis and economic recession. Over the medium term, US exports to Venezuela will continue to be limited by Venezuelan trade restrictions and chronic recession, while US creditors—who hold about one-third of Venezuela’s $35 billion external debt—are almost certain to experience continued difficulties in collecting principal and interest payments from Venezuelan borrowers, especially in the private sector. Venezuelan economic assistance to fledgling democracies in the Caribbean Basin—usually coincident with US interests—has been trimmed substantially from precrisis levels and is unlikely to recover soon. US-Venezuelan bilateral relations will probably be strained if the US increases its trade barriers to Venezuelan oil, steel, and aluminum exports—or if the failing Venezuelan economy contributes to the election of Perez, whom we believe would once again use the presidency to project himself internationally as a spokesman for Third World causes.

483. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 483. Telegram From Secretary of State Shultz’s Delegation to the Department of State 1
New York, September 26, 1987, 0227Z
Secto 15035. Subject: Secretary’s meeting with Venezuelan President Lusinchi.
1. (C—Entire text).
2. Summary. President Lusinchi concurred with the Secretary’s exposition of urgency of Nicaragua challenge and of uncertain prospects that Guatemala Accord can lead to acceptable solution: a Cuba-like presence in Nicaragua is dangerous and unacceptable to both countries; Sandinista promises alone are insufficient; ceasefire is impossible without negotiations with the resistance; and Contras are best insurance of reasonable follow-through to Guatemala. Lusinchi did not agree, however, that Mexico is acting consciously against U.S. interests in this area. Bottom line from the Venezuelan: U.S. policy is correct and Venezuela itself must be “vigilant ... categorical” with the Nicaraguans.
3. Referring repeatedly to the dangerous narco/terrorist situation in Colombia and its effect on his own country, Lusinchi agreed that the spreading problem must be dealt with internationally. He praised U.S.-Venezuelan anti-drug cooperation, and thanked USG for assistance during recent flood emergency. Lusinchi noted strong Venezuelan economy, good oil and other development prospects, and ongoing excellent relations with U.S. banking community; he asked, though, that USG provide “a little political push” to accelerate private sector and multilateral agency cooperation. The Secretary said that Venezuela’s international financial record merits respect and support.
4. The Secretary praised Venezuela’s efforts to help Suriname and deal with Guyana, suggested more aid for Haiti. Lusinchi agreed on common U.S./Venezuelan stake in Caribbean and outlined his country’s assistance to several nations, including Haiti. He added his concern about Libyan activity in the region, suggesting U.S. and Venezuela work together on that problem. End summary.
5. Secretary Shultz talked 22 September with Venezuelan President Jaime Lusinchi in latter’s New York hotel suite. Accompanying Lusinchi were Foreign Minister Consalvi and Ambassador to the U.S. Hernandez. On the U.S. side: Ambassador to the U.N. Walters, Assistant Secretary Redman, ARA DAS Gelbard and ARA/AND Director Skol (note-taker).
Nicaragua
6. The Secretary began with Central America. The U.S. would like to see the Guatemala Agreement turn out to be a workable one. The U.S. posture is to help the people involved put the pieces together. But much work has to be done. One example: there are currently no active negotiations between the Nicaraguan Government and the resistance with regard to a ceasefire, yet a ceasefire is called for by November 7. The Secretary insisted that a ceasefire is a complicated thing to negotiate, that it can’t simply be declared, and that the people doing the actual fighting must come to agreement on the terms.
7. The major problem for U.S. interests revolves around the question of armaments, the Cuban/Soviet presence. These are a direct threat to us. The Soviets have a major base in Cuba; from there they collect electronic intelligence, they fly missions on our coasts, they service their ships. We do not intend to see established a similar security threat in Nicaragua, enabling the Soviets for example to do on our west coast what they are now doing on the east coast.
8. The Secretary added that some of the specific positions in the Contadora drafts were helpful in areas where the Guatemala Agreement is vague or postpones key decisions into the future. What we want is to see peace in Central America, to see democracy in Central America, and we have to work together to achieve those goals. To that end, we believe strongly that one of the reasons the Nicaraguan communists were ready to sign the agreement was the existence on the ground of the capabilities represented by the resistance; they are the best insurance policy that there will a follow-through on the Guatemala Agreement. At the same time, if in fact the agreement’s goals are achieved, our funding for the resistance can be used for humanitarian purposes. Unfortunately, we see a grouping—Nicaragua, with Cuban and Mexican support—working in ways contrary to our interests. In response to Lusinchi’s quick question about the inclusion of Mexico in the list, the Secretary insisted that though Mexico is our neighbor and we are working hard and successfully to bolster the bilateral relationship, Mexico’s international policy remains a serious problem for us. He added that he has said the same thing directly to the Mexicans.
9. Lusinchi said he agreed with the Secretary, with the exception of his characterization of Mexican policy. There indeed must be close follow-up to the Guatemala Agreement. It would be “naive, suicidal” to trust Nicaraguan intentions just because certain declarations had been made. Lusinchi indicated he had said just that to the Sandinistas, as to Foreign Minister d’Escoto in Caracas. The Nicaraguans have to “open up,” fulfill their commitments, disconnect their ties to the Soviets. It is not RPT not permissible to have a base in Nicaragua along Cuban lines.
10. With regard to Mexico, Lusinchi suggested that policy toward Nicaragua reflected nothing more than traditional Mexican adherence to the non-interference doctrine, that Mexico was not RPT not in fact skewing its support in favor of Nicaragua. He noted that in his visit to Mexico, he saw nothing “suspicious” in Mexican attitudes. The Mexicans, for example, were not changing their suspension of oil shipments. Mexico, he insisted, had “well-refined” perceptions of the situation. It would be “naive, stupid” for anyone to underestimate Cuban and Soviet intentions in the area.
11. Lusinchi agreed on the need to be vigilant against the Nicaraguans, noted again that Venezuela had been “categorical” in its approaches. He added that “We believe that your position is a good position, the right one, the right course.” He recalled the expression, “Pray to God, but make sure you take action yourself.” To which the Secretary related a story from President Reagan on the need to take action: About the God-fearing man caught in a flood, who rejected one rescue boat, saying he would rather trust his faith; then another boat, and finally a helicopter, each time stating his trust in God. After he drowned he went to heaven and complained to God about being deserted. The Lord replied that he had in fact sent “two boats and a helicopter.” 2
Narco-Terrorism
12. The Secretary recalled USG assistance during the recent flood disaster, noting that the U.S. is a true friend of Venezuela. 3 Lusinchi thanked the Secretary for the quick help provided through the Embassy in Caracas. He then turned to “even more important” aid—U.S. assistance in the anti-drug campaign. He noted excellent cooperation with DEA and cited the challenge presented to Venezuela by the seriousness of the narcotics and terrorism situation in Colombia. “We end up paying the price,” even though Venezuela itself does not have a drug or guerrilla problem. He agreed with the Secretary’s point that, whatever the origin of the drug threat, it does quickly spread, and therefore must be dealt with on a genuinely international basis. Lusinchi stressed Venezuelan resolve and uniqueness—not only Colombia, but Ecuador and Peru faced overwhelming threats from drug traffickers and terrorists. Venezuela has a solid, diverse democracy, unpenetrated by drug corruption as in Colombia and Ecuador. He said that Ecuadorean President Febres Cordero had even told him that he constantly had to change troops on the border because of how quickly they were compromised by drug money.
Economic Relations
13. Lusinchi said that, although Venezuela remained free of guerrilla and narcotics activity, economic and social stability had to be maintained in order to keep it that way. He asked that the USG help with the private sector and with multilateral financial organizations to accelerate financial cooperation with Venezuela. He cited past excellent relations with Paul Volcker, just concluded favorable talks with Morgan and other U.S. banks, and word—from the bankers—that Alan Greenspan was favorably inclined. Lusinchi added that prospects for aluminum and new, light oil investment are excellent, and reiterated Venezuela’s “special case” status vis-a-vis its neighbors. The Secretary replied that the international financial record of Venezuela merited respect and support and that he was certain that Venezuela would be able to develop fine relations with Alan Greenspan. Lusinchi noted, however, that “a little political push” from the USG would certainly help.
The Caribbean
14. The Secretary praised Venezuela’s efforts vis-a-vis Suriname and Guyana, noting that we had a common stake in democratic development in the Caribbean. He added that Haiti remained shaky and could use some additional Venezuelan assistance. Lusinchi replied that Venezuela was helping Haiti. With arms and troop housing, and would continue to provide assistance, as it was as well in Jamaica, the Dominican Republic and elsewhere. Trinidad is an example of a country which needed not so much more money, but help with better economic planning. He opined that only the U.S. and Venezuela were really doing anything useful in the region (although Consalvi noted that, from his observations, the Grenadians felt that the U.S. was “neglecting” them). The Secretary told Lusinchi that the two countries should maintain close contact on the subject of Caribbean assistance.
Libya
15. Lusinchi raised Palestinian and Libyan activity in the area, citing Venezuela’s capture of a Palestinian and Libyan. The former, a “major terrorist” was turned over to the U.S. The Libyan, and the documents captured with him, revealed that Khaddafi was “not just crazy,” but dangerously active in the Caribbean. He had been traveling from Suriname to Panama and was involved in a “more dangerous” variation of Castro’s plans for the subversion of the black Caribbean. Lusinchi suggested that the U.S. and Venezuela should confer on the Libyan threat, and on what to do about it. The Secretary agreed.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Executive Secretariat, S/S Records, 1987 Nodis and Exdis Memorandums, Lot 94D432, Exdis Memorandums, August 1–December 1987. Confidential; Immediate; Exdis. 484. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Venezuela 484. Telegram From the Department of State to the Embassy in Venezuela 1
Washington, October 20, 1988, 2312Z
343588. Subject: The Reagan-Lusinchi Pull-Aside.
Ref: (A) 88 Caracas 10236, 2 (B) 88 State 336400 3
1. Confidential—Entire text.
2. Summary. As expected (Caracas 9556), President Lusinchi used President Reagan’s September 26 pull-aside to comment on Peru’s plight. 4 Lusinchi also expressed concern for Haiti. The Venezuelan Chief Executive, in a talkative mood, commented on U.S. elections, Venezuelan elections, changes in the Soviet Union and his travels to Eastern Europe. Given the nature of the encounter—a reception—President Reagan did not respond to any specific points and there were no action implications. End summary.
3. President Reagan took President Lusinchi aside at a U.S.-hosted reception for heads of delegation attending UNGA to tell him that he was a bulwark for the cause of democracy in a key Latin American country. Lusinchi assured President Reagan that the transition period in Venezuela would be uneventful. Lusinchi described Carlos Andres Peres (CAP), his likely successor, as a strong anti-communist. Comment: Lusinchi clearly sought to assure the USG of the GOV’s continued interest in exercising regional leadership and in cooperating with the U.S. End comment.
4. Lusinchi manifested his interest in the region’s fledgling democracies by discussing Alan Garcia. The Secretary, also present, said Garcia had squandered a good opportunity to help his country at the beginning of his administration. Lusinchi admitted that Garcia was immature, but insisted there were no alternatives; we should support Garcia. Lusinchi said that Venezuelan military leaders were in contact with their Peruvian counterparts at the request of Garcia.
5. Haiti was another topic of concern for Lusinchi. He said Avril was a good man and, again, urged U.S.-Venezuelan cooperation in Haiti. Lusinchi expressed specific concern for Haitian exile communities in Venezuela and Cuba. Comment: We note that Lusinchi’s positive disposition to Avril contrasts with the views of other Venezuelans (Caracas 10081), who are reserving judgment on the September 17 coup. 5 End comment.
6. Ref B. responds to Ref. A points not discussed above.
Shultz
1 Source: Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880935–0429. Confidential; Immediate. Sent for information to Maracaibo, Lima, Port-au-Prince, and the Interest Section in Havana. Drafted by Yale; cleared in NSC, ARA/AND, ARA, CAR, CCA, P, S/S, and S/S–O; approved by Abrams.

3 In telegram 336400 to Caracas and the OPEC Collective, October 14, the Department reported on Gil’s September 28–29 meetings with Assistant Secretary of State for Economic and Business Affairs Eugene McAllister and Deputy Assistant Secretary of State for Energy and Resources Policy John Ferriter: “Gil’s main message was Venezuela’s need for more foreign investment inflows and debt rescheduling to balance an expected 33 percent drop in oil revenues this year.” The Embassy also noted: “Reporting cable on Gil’s September 29 meeting with DOE Secretary Herrington is at DOE for clearance.” (Department of State, Central Foreign Policy File, Electronic Telegrams, D880916–0509)

485. Telegram From the Embassy in Venezuela to the Department of State 485. Telegram From the Embassy in Venezuela to the Department of State 1
Caracas, December 30, 1988, 2035Z
6123. Subject: Ambassador’s call on President Carlos Andres Perez.
1. Secret—Entire text.
2. Summary: Ambassador met with President-elect Carlos Andres Perez (CAP) on December 28 to follow up on issues discussed at CAP’s meeting with President-elect Bush in Washington on December 13. 2 Issues discussed included:
— CAP’s perception of the Bush-CAP meeting;
— Panama;
— Nicaragua;
— Oil prices and possible SPR sales by Venezuela to the U.S.;
— Narcotics cooperation;
— CAP’s inauguration and possible participation by Castro and Ortega;
— The occidental claim;
— Bilateral trade;
— Tenure of current U.S. Ambassador to Venezuela;
— CAP’s travel plans;
— Debt; and
— Trade and investment, including barter arrangements.
These issues will be reported by septels. 3 Throughout the meeting CAP was short on specifics but appeared very pleased by his conversation with President-elect Bush. End summary.
3. General perception of CAP-Bush meeting: CAP spoke at length and very warmly about his meeting with President-elect Bush. He used a number of adjectives such as “warm,” “open,” “knowledgeable,” “sensible,” “understanding,” and “strong” to describe President-elect Bush. He was, as we have heard from various sources in the CAP team, obviously pleased with the personal treatment accorded him by Vice President Bush, including the “extra” unexpected gestures by the Vice President such as meeting CAP at the driveway, accompanying him and presenting him to the press corps, and finally taking him personally to his limousine and waiting until the departure of CAP’s motorcade. This Embassy has heard from various sources that the Venezuelans were extremely impressed by the courtesies accorded to CAP by the person they consider to be the most important man in the world after January 20, 1989. 4
The Ambassador has been told that CAP has stated, “He (Bush) treated me as an equal,” not only in the courtesies extended but in truly listening to CAP’s opinion and eliciting his suggestions and recommendations.
4. Embassy believes that, although differences of opinion are bound to emerge between the U.S. and Venezuelan administrations which are to take office on January 20 and February 2 respectively, the CAP-Bush meeting has created an opportunity for close cooperation, at least in the short term, on extremely important hemispheric issues.
Reich
1 Source: George H.W. Bush Library, Bush Vice Presidential Records, Office of National Security Affairs, Donald P. Gregg Files, OA/ID 19874–015, Country Files, Venezuela–1988. Secret; Immediate. Printed from a copy that indicates the original was received in the White House Situation Room. An unknown hand wrote “GOOD VIBES!” in the right-hand margin.
